Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Westminster Confession of Faith  
	  A commentary by A.A. Hodge
	  A comprehensive guide to the Confession's 33 chapters
    The Importance of Scripture  
	  The Bible is the only and all-sufficient rule of faith and practice
	  It is the word of God, and its authority is intrinsic and binding
    Interpreting Scripture  
	  Understanding the Bible belongs to men, not God
	  Men must interpret Scripture separately and combine its teachings into a consistent whole
    The Role of Human Creeds and Confessions  
	  They are not a substitute for Scripture, but rather a helpful guide
	  They are the result of the collective wisdom of the Church
    The Development of Church Doctrine  
	  The Church has advanced gradually in interpreting Scripture and defining doctrine
	  Attention has been focused on one doctrine at a time, in different ages
    The Purpose of Creeds and Confessions  
	  To provide a consistent and harmonious system of doctrine
	  To assist individuals in understanding and applying Scripture
    The Relationship between Scripture and Creeds  
	  Scripture is the ultimate authority, and creeds are secondary
	  Creeds are not a replacement for Scripture, but rather a helpful tool
    The Importance of Collective Wisdom  
	  The collective wisdom of the Church is more reliable than individual judgment
	  The tried and proved faith of the Church is more trustworthy than private wisdom
    The Value of Human Creeds and Confessions  
	  They are valuable in proportion to how well they teach what Scripture teaches
	  They are a helpful guide, but not a substitute for Scripture
    The Significance of the Westminster Confession  
	  A comprehensive guide to Reformed theology and doctrine
	  A valuable resource for understanding the Bible and its teachings

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Purpose of Creeds and Confessions  
	  To mark, disseminate, and preserve the attainments made in the knowledge of Christian truth
	  To discriminate the truth from the glosses of false teachers
	  To present the truth in its integrity and due proportions
	  To act as the basis of ecclesiastical fellowship among those in harmony
	  To be used as instruments in the great work of popular instruction

•   Necessity of Creeds and Confessions  
	  Necessary in all ages and branches of the Church
	  Useful when not abused
	  Provide a clear and accurate statement of the results of new attainments in gospel truth

•   Boundaries of Creeds and Confessions  
	  Bind the consciences of men only so far as it is purely scriptural
	  Bind those who have voluntarily subscribed the Confession
	  Do not bind those who have not subscribed

•   Membership and Office-Bearing  
	  A Church has no right to make anything a condition of membership that Christ has not made a condition of salvation
	  Membership involves a credible profession of the true religion
	  Office-bearers must protest to believe in the truth and wisdom of the constitution and laws of the Church

•   Ecclesiastical Discipline  
	  Provision must be made for ecclesiastical discipline
	  Public teachers in the same communion must not contradict one another
	  The one must not pull down what the other is striving to build up

•   Formularies and Public Authority  
	  Formularies must be prepared, representing the common consent
	  Clothed with public authority for the instruction of the members of the Church
	  Especially for the instruction of children

•   Scriptural Basis  
	  The matter of Creeds and Confessions binds the consciences of men only so far as it is purely scriptural
	  The form in which the matter is stated binds those who have voluntarily subscribed the Confession

•   Harmony of Sentiment and Cooperation  
	  All harmony of sentiment and all efficient cooperation in action would be impossible without a common understanding of the truth
	  A Church has no right to make anything a condition of membership that Christ has not made a condition of salvation

•   Original Synod of the American Presbyterian Church  
	  The original Synod of our American Presbyterian Church in 1729 adopted the Westminster Confession of Faith and Catechisms as the doctrinal standards of the Church

•   Adoption of the Westminster Confession  
	  All the ministers of the Synod present, except one, gave their assent to the Westminster Confession of Faith and Catechisms
	  The Confession was adopted after proposing all the scruples any of them had to make against any articles and expressions in the Confession of Faith

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Westminster Confession and Catechisms  
	  The Assembly of Divines at Westminster agreed on the Confession and Catechisms in 1646.
	  The Confession and Catechisms were declared to be the confession of faith, except for some clauses in the 20th and 23rd chapters.
    1788 Synod Decisions  
	  The Synod ratified and adopted the Form of Government and Discipline in 1788.
	  The Synod approved and ratified the Directory for Worship.
	  The Synod approved and ratified the Westminster Larger and Shorter Catechisms with a small amendment.
    Standard of Doctrine and Worship  
	  The Synod ordered that the Directory and Catechisms be printed and bound with the Confession of Faith and the Form of Government and Discipline.
	  The whole document was to be considered the standard of doctrine, government, discipline, and worship.
    Ancient Creeds  
	  The ancient Creeds express the common faith of the whole Church.
	  They relate to fundamental principles of Christianity, especially the Trinity and the Person of the God-man.
    The Apostles' Creed  
	  The Apostles' Creed was not written by the apostles, but was formed by common consent.
	  It was adopted by particular churches and used in the reception of members.
	  It was appended to the Shorter Catechism in the first edition published by order of Parliament.
    The Apostles' Creed Text  
	  The Creed is as follows: "I believe in God the Father Almighty, Maker of heaven and earth."
    The Nicene Creed  
	  The Nicene Creed is formed on the basis of the Apostles' Creed.
	  It was contributed by the great Council held in Nice in Bithynia in A.D. 325.
	  It was added to by the Second Ecumenical Council.
    Other Creeds  
	  The text mentions other Creeds, but they are not specified.
    Creeds and Confessions  
	  The text provides a brief and general history of the principal Creeds and Confessions of the Christian Church.
	  The Creeds and Confessions are grouped according to the order of time and the churches that adhere to them.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Nicene Creed  
	  Was formulated at the Council of Constantinople in A.D. 381
	  Was later modified by the Council of Toledo in A.D. 569
	  Is the Creed of the whole Christian Church, with the Greek Church rejecting only the "filioque" clause
    The Nicene Creed's Content  
	  Affirms the existence of one God, the Maker of heaven and earth
	  Affirms the divinity of Jesus Christ, the Only Begotten Son of God
	  Affirms the Holy Spirit as the Lord, the Giver of life
    Heretical Opinions and the Church's Response  
	  The Church had to provide additional definitions and muniments of the truth due to heretical opinions
	  Nestorianism was condemned by the Creed of the Council of Ephesus in A.D. 431
	  Eutychianism was condemned by the Council of Chalcedon in A.D. 451
    The Definition of the Person of Christ  
	  The Church defined the faith as embracing two natures in one person
	  This definition is received and approved by the entire Church
    The Athanasian Creed  
	  Was composed after the death of the great theologian Athanasius
	  Affirms the unchangeable faith of the whole Church on the Trinity and the duality of natures in Christ
    The Athanasian Creed's Content  
	  Affirms that Jesus Christ is both God and man
	  Affirms that Jesus Christ is perfect God and perfect man
	  Affirms that Jesus Christ is one Christ, not two persons
    The Reformation and the Church's Response  
	  The Church of Rome responded to the Reformation by calling the Council of Trent (1545-1563)
	  The Council of Trent produced the Doctrinal Standards of the Church of Rome
    The Doctrinal Standards of the Church of Rome  
	  Were produced in opposition to the progress of the Reformation
	  Affirm the authority of the Pope and the sacraments
    The Reformation and the Protestant Churches  
	  The Protestant churches responded to the Reformation by producing their own confessions and creeds
	  These confessions and creeds will be discussed in the following sections

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Roman Catholic Church  
	  The Council of Trent's decrees and canons form the highest doctrinal rule for the Roman Catholic Church.
	  The decrees contain positive statements of doctrine, while the canons explain and condemn opposing Protestant doctrine.
	  The Roman Catechism, prepared by Pope Pius IV in 1556, explains and enforces the canons.
	  The Tridentine Confession of Faith was imposed on all priests and candidates in the Roman Catholic Church.

•   Papal Standards  
	  Different papal bulls and private writings have been set up as standards of the true faith by the authority of popes.
	  Examples include the Catechism of Bellarmine (1603) and the bull Unigenitus (1711).
	  The theology taught in these papal standards is Arminianism.

•   Greek Orthodox Church  
	  The Greek Orthodox Church emerged from the Eastern half of the ancient Church.
	  It is considered the "orthodox" Church due to its possession of the original creeds defining the doctrine of the Trinity and the Person of Christ.
	  Greek theology is imperfectly developed beyond the ground covered by these ancient creeds.

•   Greek Orthodox Confessions  
	  The Greek Orthodox Church possesses a few confessions of more modern date, including:
		- "The Orthodox Confession" of Peter Mogilas (1642)
		- The Confession of Gennadius (1453)

•   Lutheran Church  
	  The Lutheran Church is one of the two main families of Protestant churches, the other being the Reformed Church.
	  The Lutheran Church includes all Protestants in Germany and the Baltic provinces of Russia who adhere to the Augsburg Confession.

•   Lutheran Symbolical Books  
	  The Augsburg Confession (1530) is the oldest Protestant confession and the ultimate basis of Lutheran theology.
	  The Apology (Defence) of the Augsburg Confession (1530) was prepared by Melancthon and subscribed by Protestant theologians in 1537.
	  The Larger and Smaller Catechisms (1529) were prepared by Luther for preachers and teachers and for the instruction of youth, respectively.
	  The Articles of Smalcald (1535) were drawn up by Luther and subscribed by evangelical theologians in 1537.
	  The Formula Concordiae (1577) was prepared to settle controversies in the Lutheran Church regarding the relative activities of divine grace and the human will in regeneration.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Lutheran Confessions  
	  The Lutheran doctrine is stated in a confession that is more scientific and thoroughly developed than in other public symbols.
	  This confession is acknowledged only by the high Lutheran party.
    Reformed or Calvinistic Churches  
	  The Reformed churches include those in Germany, Switzerland, France, Holland, England, Scotland, and America.
	  They subscribe to the Heidelberg Catechism and other confessions.
    Reformed Confessions  
	  The Second Helvetic Confession (1564) is a widely accepted confession.
	  The Heidelberg Catechism (1562) is a confession of faith for the Reformed churches in Germany and Holland.
	  The Thirty-nine Articles of the Church of England (1551, revised 1562) are Calvinistic in doctrine.
    Other Reformed Confessions  
	  The Canons of the Synod of Dort (1618-1619) are a true and authoritative exhibition of the Calvinistic System of Theology.
	  The Confession and Catechisms of the Westminster Assembly are the common doctrinal standard of Presbyterian churches.
	  The Westminster Assembly's Confession and Catechisms are also approved by Congregationalists in England and America.
    Specific Confessions  
	  The Second Helvetic Confession is adopted by Reformed churches in Switzerland, Poland, Hungary, Scotland, and France.
	  The Heidelberg Catechism is the confession of faith for the Reformed churches in Germany and Holland.
	  The Thirty-nine Articles are the doctrinal standard of the Episcopal churches in England, Scotland, America, and the Colonies.
    Synod of Dort  
	  The Synod of Dort was convened in 1618 to settle questions brought into controversy by the disciples of Arminius.
	  The Canons of the Synod of Dort were received by all Reformed churches as a true and authoritative exhibition of the Calvinistic System of Theology.
    Westminster Assembly  
	  The Westminster Assembly produced a confession and catechisms that are the common doctrinal standard of Presbyterian churches.
	  The Confession and Catechisms of the Westminster Assembly are also approved by Congregationalists in England and America.
    Savoy Confession  
	  The Savoy Confession is a confession produced by the Congregational Convention in 1658.
	  The Savoy Confession is very nearly identical to the Confession and Catechisms of the Westminster Assembly.
    Calvinistic System of Theology  
	  The Calvinistic System of Theology is a widely accepted theological system.
	  The Canons of the Synod of Dort and the Confession and Catechisms of the Westminster Assembly are authoritative expressions of this system.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Westminster Confession  
	  The Westminster Confession is a small document
	  Modern Independents have largely abandoned its use in favor of the Assembly's Catechisms
	  The Confession and Catechisms have been endorsed or adopted by all New England Assemblies
    Historical Adoption of the Confession  
	  The Cambridge Platform (1647-1648) and the Boston Confession (1679-1680) adopted the Confession
	  The Saybrook Platform (1708) also adopted the Confession
    Authority of Creeds and Confessions  
	  All human creeds derive their authority from Scripture
	  The great body of Christians has a responsibility to gather and adjust their teaching on various subjects
	  Creeds and Confessions are useful for expressing and preserving the opinions of the Christian Church
    Purpose of Creeds and Confessions  
	  The first purpose is to express and preserve the opinions of the Christian Church
	  The second purpose is to provide a standard of faith for the Church
	  The third purpose is to bind the consciences of men
    Binding of Consciences  
	  The matter of Confessions binds the consciences of men on the ground of Scripture
	  The form of Confessions binds on the ground of the representative body of the Church
    Terms of Admission to the Church  
	  Private members are admitted to the Church on the terms of faith in the Confession
	  Preachers and rulers are admitted to office on the terms of faith in the Confession and a demonstration of their ability to teach and lead
    Adoption of the Westminster Confession  
	  The Westminster Confession and Catechisms were first adopted as the standard of faith by the Westminster Assembly in 1646
	  The General Synod adopted the Confession in 1788
    Early Creeds  
	  The Apostles' Creed originated in the early Christian Church
	  The Nicene Creed was produced by the Councils of Nicaea (325) and Constantinople (381)
    Development of Creeds  
	  The Creed of the Council of Ephesus (431) and the Creed of the Council of Chalcedon (451) were developed to address heretical tendencies
	  The Creed falsely attributed to Athanasius was actually written by a later author
    Doctrinal Standards  
	  The Church of Rome has its own set of doctrinal standards
	  The Greek Church has its own set of doctrinal standards
	  The Lutheran and Reformed churches have their own set of doctrinal standards

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Heidelberg Catechism  
	  Composed by Ursinus and Olevianus
	  Appointed by Frederick III, Crown Prince of the Palatinate
	  Standard of the Presbyterian Church of Scotland for nearly 100 years

•   Thirty-nine Articles of the Church of England  
	  Prepared by bishops of the Church in 1562
	  Revision of "The Forty-two Articles of Edward Sixth" (1551)
	  Standard of the Church of England and Episcopal Church of America

•   Synod of Dort  
	  Convened by the States General of the Netherlands in 1618-1619
	  Composed of representatives of all Reformed churches except France
	  "Canons" of high authority among Reformed churches
	  Standard of the Church of Holland

•   Westminster Confession and Catechisms  
	  Drawn up by a national assembly of divines and civilians in Westminster, England (1643-1648)
	  Standard of faith for Presbyterian and Congregationalist churches
	  Not adopted by the Congregationalists of England, but partially adopted by those in New England

•   Origin of Confessions  
	  Most Reformed and Lutheran confessions were composed by single authors or small groups of theologians
	  Examples: Augsburg Confession (Luther and Melancthon), Second Helvetic Confession (Bullinger), Heidelberg Catechism (Ursinus and Olevianus)

•   Old Scotch Confession  
	  Composed by a committee of six theologians, led by John Knox
	  Appointed by the Scottish Parliament
	  Standard of the Presbyterian Church of Scotland for nearly 100 years

•   Reformation in Scotland  
	  Received its first impulse from Patrick Hamilton's return from the Continent (1528)
	  A purely religious revolution, led by John Knox and Andrew Melville
	  The Church of Scotland framed its Confession of Faith and First Book of Discipline in 1560

•   Independence of the Church of Scotland  
	  Maintained its independence from civil dictation and integrity as a Presbyterian Church until after King James' ascension
	  Often temporarily destroyed by English influence and the increased power of the throne

•   Westminster Assembly  
	  Convened by the Long Parliament in Westminster, England (1643-1648)
	  A large and illustrious national assembly of divines and civilians

•   Adoption of Confessions  
	  The Westminster Confession and Catechisms were not adopted by the Congregationalists of England, but partially adopted by those in New England
	  The Synod of Dort's "Canons" were widely adopted by Reformed churches, but not by the Church of Scotland.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The National Covenant and Solemn League and Covenant  
	  The Scotch nobility, clergy, and people signed the National Covenant in 1638
	  The Solemn League and Covenant was signed in 1643 by the Scotch General Assembly, the English Parliament, and the Westminster Assembly
	  The Covenant aimed to preserve the Reformed religion in Scotland and reform religion in England and Ireland

•   The Reformation in England  
	  The Reformation in England had two phases: a genuine work of grace and a political and ecclesiastical revolution
	  The genuine work of grace was introduced by the publication of the Word of God and the English translation of the Bible

•   Key Figures in the English Reformation  
	  Cranmer, Ridley, Hooper, Latimer, and Jewell were true Reformers of England
	  They were evangelical and Calvinistic, in sympathy with theologians and preachers of Switzerland and Germany

•   The Role of the Bible in the Reformation  
	  The English Bible, published in 1526, played a crucial role in the Reformation
	  It helped to popularize the Protestant faith and render the nation's heart permanently Protestant

•   The Impact of the Politico-Ecclesiastical Revolution  
	  The politico-ecclesiastical revolution introduced by Henry VIII and confirmed by Queen Elizabeth impeded the Reformation
	  It arrested the constitutional changes in the Church and subjected all questions of doctrine to the sovereign's control

•   The Act of Supremacy  
	  The Act of Supremacy made the sovereign the earthly head of the Church
	  It enabled Elizabeth to repress the constitutional changes in the Church and maintain her power

•   The Struggle between the Puritan and Court Parties  
	  The struggle between the Puritan and Court parties became more intense and bitter during the reigns of James I and Charles I
	  The Court party adopted Arminianism, which prevailed among the parasites of arbitrary power and the votaries of a churchly and sacramental religion

•   The Denial of Reform and the Act of Uniformity  
	  The denial of reform and the Act of Uniformity led to the repression of dissent and the robbing of the people of every trace of religious liberty
	  This led to the extension of the royal prerogative and the constant resort to arbitrary measures and acts of violence

•   The Impact on Civil Liberties  
	  The denial of reform and the Act of Uniformity led to the trampling of civil liberties underfoot
	  The people were subjected to arbitrary measures and acts of violence

•   The End of the Reign of Charles I  
	  Charles I's reign was marked by the struggle between the Puritan and Court parties
	  The eventual outcome of this struggle is not mentioned in the text, but it is implied that it had significant consequences for the Church and the nation.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Long Parliament  
	  Was formed in November 1640
	  Was a significant body in English history
	  Was known for its independence from the king

•   Parliamentary Reforms  
	  Abolished the Court of High Commission and the Star Chamber
	  Ordained the abolition of the office of archbishop and bishop
	  Abolished the framework of prelate government

•   The Assembly of Divines  
	  Was formed in June 1643
	  Was tasked with settling the government and liturgy of the Church of England
	  Was also responsible for clearing the doctrine of the Church from false aspersions

•   Composition of the Assembly  
	  Comprised 121 divines, 10 lords, and 20 commoners as lay-members
	  Included men of all shades of opinion on Church government
	  Had a diverse range of representatives, including Episcopalians, Presbyterians, Independents, and Erastians

•   Attendance and Participation  
	  Only 60 members attended the first day
	  Average attendance was between 60 and 80 members
	  The majority of members were Presbyterians, with only 5 prominent Independents

•   The Five Dissenting Brethren  
	  Were a group of 5 prominent Independents, led by Dr. Thomas Goodwin and Pastor Philip Nye
	  Possessed considerable influence in hindering the Assembly's work
	  Received support from politicians, the army, and Oliver Cromwell

•   The Erastians  
	  Were a group that held that Christian pastors are simply teachers and not rulers in the Church
	  Believed that all ecclesiastical and civil power rests exclusively with the civil magistrate
	  Were represented in the Assembly

•   The Role of the Scotch General Assembly  
	  Sent delegates to Westminster, including Alexander Henderson, George Gillespie, and Samuel Rutherford
	  Had a significant influence on the Assembly's work

•   The Solemn League and Covenant  
	  Was condemned by the king
	  Was a significant factor in the withdrawal of Episcopalians from the Assembly

•   The Legacy of the Assembly  
	  Had a significant impact on the development of the Church of England
	  Laid the groundwork for the eventual establishment of Presbyterianism in England

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Organization of the Westminster Assembly  
	  The Assembly was organized by Thomas Coleman, John Lightfoot, and John Selden
	  The Parliament sympathized with them, and the prolocutor was Dr. Twisse
	  The Assembly met in Henry the VII's Chapel and later in the Jerusalem Chamber

•   Initial Work of the Assembly  
	  The Assembly was tasked with revising the "Thirty-nine Articles"
	  They were also directed to consider a discipline and government for the Church

•   Preparation of the Directory of Government, Worship, and Discipline  
	  The Assembly worked on the Directory from October 1643 to near the close of 1644
	  They were delayed by controversies with Independent and Erastian factions

•   Preparation of the Confession of Faith  
	  A committee was appointed to prepare the Confession, consisting of Pastor Drs. Gouge, Temple, and Hoyle, and others
	  The committee worked on the Confession and Catechisms simultaneously

•   Completion of the Confession of Faith  
	  The Assembly resolved to finish the Confession first, and then construct the Catechism on its model
	  The finished Confession was presented to Parliament on December 3, 1646

•   Completion of the Catechisms  
	  The Shorter Catechism was finished and reported to Parliament on November 5, 1647
	  The Larger Catechism was finished and reported to Parliament on April 14, 1648

•   Ratification of the Confession and Catechisms  
	  The Confession of Faith, Directory of Public Worship, and Larger and Shorter Catechisms were ratified by the Scottish General Assembly
	  The Scottish General Assembly approved the work as soon as it was concluded at Westminster

•   Establishment of the Presbyterian Church in England  
	  The Long Parliament established the Presbyterian Church in England experimentally on October 13, 1647
	  The Church was to be in place until the end of the next session of Parliament

•   Supersession of the Presbyterian Church  
	  The Presbyterian Church was superseded by Cromwell's Committee of Triers
	  The Presbyterian ministers were ejected in mass by Charles II in 1662

•   Dispersal of the Assembly Members  
	  Many members quietly dispersed and returned to their homes after the completion of the Catechisms
	  Those who remained in London were engaged in examining ministers for ordination or induction into vacant charges

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Westminster Assembly  
	  Met from 1643 to 1649
	  Held 1,163 sessions
	  Changed into a committee for conducting trials and examinations in 1649
    Dissolution  
	  Committee broke up in 1652
	  Oliver Cromwell forcibly dissolved the Long Parliament
    Confession of Faith  
	  Adopted by the original Synod in North America in 1729
	  Received as the standard of faith by Presbyterian churches in Scotland, England, Ireland, and America
    Rejection of Erastian error  
	  Excluded from the Confession
	  Views on church establishments led to rejection of certain passages
    Adopting Act  
	  Declared that the Synod did not receive certain passages in the Confession
	  Rejected the idea of a controlling power of civil magistrates over synods
    Revisions and amendments  
	  Revised and amended in 1787
	  Prepared for the organization of the General Assembly in 1789
    Altered passages  
	  WCF 20.4: "They may be proceeded against by the censures of the Church and by the power of the civil magistrate"
	  WCF 23.3: "The civil magistrate may not assume to himself the administration of the Word and sacraments"
	  WCF 31.2: "As magistrates may lawfully call a synod of ministers and other fit persons to consult and advise with about matters of religion"
    Current status  
	  Adopted as the doctrinal part of the Constitution of the Presbyterian Church in America in 1788
	  Still stand to this day
    Relationship to other churches  
	  Confession of Faith and Catechisms used by Congregational bodies of Puritan stock
	  Not adopted by Lutheran and Reformed churches (question 1)
    Significance  
	  Highly reverenced and used as a means of public instruction
	  Standard of faith for Presbyterian churches in Scotland, England, Ireland, and America

Here are the 24 questions summarized in 10 bullets with sub-bullets:

    The Westminster Assembly

	  How was the Assembly organized? (15)

	  How did they proceed to frame the Catechisms? (18)
	  What was the action of the Long Parliament touching the work of the Assembly? (19)

	  What was the ultimate fate of the Presbyterian establishment in England? (21)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The existence of God can be known through reason and nature  
	  The light of nature and the works of creation and providence are sufficient to enable men to ascertain the fact that there is a God.
	  This knowledge is sufficient to render men inexcusable for not acknowledging God's existence.

•   Three false opinions have been entertained about human knowledge of God  
	  Opinion 1: Extreme Rationalists deny the existence of a world beyond the natural one and claim that all knowledge is confined to the facts of our experience.
	  Opinion 2: Some Christians claim that no true and certain knowledge of God can be derived from the light of nature in the absence of a supernatural revelation.
	  Opinion 3: Another opinion is not explicitly stated in the text, but it is implied to be a middle ground between the first two opinions.

•   The assumption of Extreme Rationalists is disproved  
	  Men of all nations, ages, and cultures have discerned the evidences of God's presence in nature and providence.
	  The works of nature and providence are full of design and can be scientifically explained by recognizing the intention behind them.
	  Conscience implies accountability to a personal moral Governor and has led men to recognize God's existence.

•   The assumption of Extreme Rationalists is disproved (continued)  
	  The fact that men have discerned God's presence in nature and providence is not limited to unenlightened individuals, but also includes some of the first teachers of positive science.
	  The works of nature and providence can be explained by recognizing the intention behind them, which is a form of design.

•   The opinion of some Christians is disproved  
	  The direct testimony of Scripture (Romans 1:20-24; 2:14-15) contradicts the opinion that no true and certain knowledge of God can be derived from the light of nature.
	  Many conclusive arguments for the existence of a great First Cause can be drawn from the facts of nature alone.
	  All nations, even without a supernatural revelation, have possessed some knowledge of a God.

•   Natural religion has given birth to natural theology  
	  Even in the absence of a supernatural revelation, natural religion has given birth to a considerable natural theology.
	  The most enlightened of the heathen have developed a natural theology based on their understanding of nature and providence.

•   The existence of God can be known through reason and nature (continued)  
	  The fact that men have discerned God's presence in nature and providence is a universal and indestructible element of human nature.
	  Conscience implies accountability to a personal moral Governor and has led men to recognize God's existence.

•   The existence of God is not limited to supernatural revelation  
	  The existence of God can be known through reason and nature, even in the absence of a supernatural revelation.
	  Many conclusive arguments for the existence of a great First Cause can be drawn from the facts of nature alone.

•   The process of drawing conclusions from natural sources is legitimate  
	  The fact that the argument for the existence of a great First Cause remains unanswerable shows that the process by which the conclusions are drawn from purely natural sources is legitimate.

•   The existence of God is a universal and indestructible element of human nature  
	  Conscience implies accountability to a personal moral Governor and has led men to recognize God's existence.
	  The existence of God is a universal and indestructible element of human nature, regardless of cultural or scientific background.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Distinguishing between natural and supernatural knowledge  
	  Natural knowledge can be obtained through reason and observation of nature
	  Supernatural knowledge requires a revelation from God

•   The limitations of natural theology  
	  The natural theology of modern Rationalists owes its excellence to Christian revelation
	  Natural theology is insufficient to lead men to a knowledge of God's being, nature, and purposes

•   The Deist and theistic Rationalist view  
	  They believe that the light of nature is sufficient to lead men to knowledge of God
	  They see supernatural revelation as only an illustration and enforcement of natural truths

•   The insufficiency of natural knowledge for salvation  
	  Scripture teaches that natural knowledge is insufficient for salvation (1 Cor. 1:21, 2:13-14)
	  Natural knowledge cannot teach men about God's remission, substitution, sanctification, and restoration

•   The history of nations without revelation  
	  Nations without revelation have held incomplete and erroneous truths
	  Their faith has been uncertain, and their lives have been immoral

•   The necessity of supernatural revelation  
	  God has made a supernatural revelation of himself to a chosen portion of humanity
	  This revelation is necessary for men to attain a knowledge of God and his will for salvation

•   The commitment of revelation to writing  
	  God has committed his revelation to writing in the Sacred Scriptures
	  The Scriptures are the exclusive source of supernatural revelation

•   The absolute necessity of supernatural revelation  
	  A supernatural revelation is necessary for men to attain salvation
	  Natural religion alone is insufficient to lead men to a knowledge of God and his will

•   The probability of supernatural revelation  
	  The character of God as revealed by natural religion makes it probable that he would give a supernatural revelation
	  God's benevolence and intelligence lead us to expect that he would provide a revelation adequate to human needs

•   The anticipation of supernatural revelation  
	  Man's moral and religious nature craves a clearly revealed rule of duty and communion with God
	  God's intelligence and benevolence lead us to anticipate that he would provide a supernatural revelation to meet these needs.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Revelation to Humanity  
	  God has given a revelation to humanity through various means
	  This revelation has been made in diverse manners, including theophanies, audible voices, dreams, visions, and prophetic inspiration
    The Christian Scriptures  
	  The Christian Scriptures are a collection of writings that contain God's revelation to humanity
	  The main departments of evidence for the Christian Scriptures include:
		- The Old and New Testaments as genuine and authentic historical records
		- Miracles recorded in the Scriptures as established facts
		- Explicit prophecies already fulfilled in the Scriptures
		- The perfection of the moral system taught in the Scriptures
		- The supernatural intelligence and adaptation of the Scriptures to all human characters and conditions
		- The spiritual power of Christianity
    The Holy Scriptures  
	  The Holy Scriptures, or the Word of God written, contain all the books of the Old and New Testaments
	  The books of the Old Testament include:
		- Genesis to Malachi
	  The books of the New Testament include:
		- The Gospels, Acts, and Epistles of Paul, Peter, John, and others
    The Canon of Scripture  
	  The canon of Scripture includes all the canonical books of the Old and New Testaments
	  The books commonly called Apocrypha are not part of the canon of Scripture and have no authority in the Church of God
    Scripture Proof Texts  
	  The following passages are cited as proof texts:
		- Luke 16:29, 31
		- Ephesians 2:20
		- Revelation 22:18, 19
		- 2 Timothy 3:16
		- Luke 24:27, 44
		- Romans 3:2
		- 2 Peter 1:21
    The Authority of Scripture  
	  The canonical books of the Old and New Testaments are divinely inspired and given to us as an authoritative rule of faith and practice
	  The books commonly called Apocrypha are of no more authority than any other human writings.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Establishing the Canon  
	  The Old Testament and New Testament are collections of inspired writings given by God to his Church during different dispensations.
	  The canon is determined by examining the evidence that each book was written by the inspired prophet or apostle whose name it bears.

•   Types of Evidence  
	  Internal evidence: language, style, and character of the matter contained in the book.
	  External evidence: testimony of contemporaneous writers, universal consent of contemporary readers, and corroborating history from independent credible sources.

•   Genuineness of Old Testament Canon  
	  Christ and his apostles endorse the Jewish Scriptures as genuine and authentic.
	  They quote from the separate books and divisions of the Jewish Scriptures as the Word of God.

•   Jewish Canon Endorsed by Christ and Apostles  
	  The Jewish canon endorsed by Christ and his apostles is the same as the one we have today.
	  The New Testament writers quote almost every book in our copies, and no others.

•   Evidence for New Testament Canon  
	  Early Christian writers agree in quoting the books we receive as of apostolic authority.
	  The early Church Fathers provide catalogues of the books received as apostolic, which agree perfectly on most books.

•   Early Translations of the Scriptures  
	  The Peshito, or early Syriac translation, agrees almost entirely with ours.
	  The Vulgate, prepared by Jerome in A.D. 385, was based on the Italic or early Latin version and agrees entirely with ours.

•   Internal Evidence  
	  The language and idiom in which the books are written.
	  The harmony in all essentials in the midst of great variety in form and circumstances.
	  The elevated spirituality and doctrinal consistency of all the books.

•   Practical Power of the Books  
	  The books have practical power over the consciences and lives of people.
	  They have been recognized as Scripture by both Jews and Christians since the time of Christ.

•   Universal Consent  
	  The testimony of the early Christian writers uniformly agrees with that of the ancient Jews as to every book.
	  Both Jews and Christians have separately kept the same canon and agree perfectly on the genuineness and authenticity of every book.

•   Historical and Critical Proof  
	  The evidence for the canonical authority of the Sacred Scriptures is of the same kind as that relied upon by literary men to establish the genuineness and authenticity of other ancient writings.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Apocrypha  
	  The Apocrypha refers to ancient writings that are not part of the sacred canon
	  These writings have unfounded claims to a place in the canon
	  Examples include Tobit, Wisdom, Judith, Ecclesiasticus, Baruch, and the two books of Maccabees

•   Reasons for rejecting the Apocrypha  
	  They were never part of the Hebrew Scriptures
	  They were never quoted by Christ or the apostles
	  They were not accepted as authoritative by the early Fathers
	  They were only accepted by the Roman Church after the Council of Trent

•   Characteristics of the Apocrypha  
	  They make no claim to inspiration
	  They disclaim inspiration in their contents
	  Some consist of childish fables and inculcate bad morals

•   The Canon of Scripture  
	  The canonical Scriptures were divinely inspired
	  They are given to us as an authoritative rule of faith and practice

•   The Nature of Inspiration  
	  Inspiration is a supernatural influence of the Spirit of God
	  It accompanied the sacred writers uniformly in what they wrote
	  It directed them in all they wrote and secured the infallible expression of it in words

•   The Effects of Inspiration  
	  All written under inspiration is the very Word of God
	  It is infallible truth and of divine authority
	  This infallibility and authority attach to both the verbal expression and the matter of the revelation

•   Evidence for Inspiration  
	  The Scriptures assert it of themselves
	  God authenticated the claims of their writers by accompanying their teaching with signs and wonders

•   The Importance of Inspiration  
	  It is impossible for God to unconditionally command belief except to truth infallibly conveyed
	  Wherever God sends his "sign," there he commands belief

•   The Old Testament Writers' Claim to Inspiration  
	  They claimed to be inspired by God
	  They spoke in the name of God, prefacing their messages with "Thus saith the LORD"

•   The Authority of Scripture  
	  The Scriptures are an authoritative rule of faith and practice
	  They convey with absolute accuracy and divine authority all that God meant them to convey

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  The Authority of Scripture  

    The Old Testament's Inspiration  
	  The Old Testament is quoted as infallible in the New Testament (e.g. Matt. v. 18, John x. 35)
	  The New Testament writers attribute quotes to God or the Holy Spirit (e.g. Heb. iii. 7, Acts ii. 17)
    The New Testament's Affirmation of Old Testament Inspiration  
	  The New Testament explicitly affirms the inspiration of the Old Testament (e.g. Luke i. 70, Heb. i. 1)
	  The New Testament writers quote the Old Testament as authoritative (e.g. 2 Tim. iii. 16, 1 Pet. i. 10-12)
    The Apostles' Quotations and Claims  
	  The apostles quote the Old Testament as infallible (e.g. Matt. ii. 15-23)
	  The apostles claim to have the Spirit and speak with plenary authority (e.g. Acts ii. 33, 1 Cor. ii. 13)
    The Authority of Scripture  
	  The authority of Scripture depends on God, not on human testimony or the Church (2 Peter i. 19, 21)
	  The internal evidences of a divine origin in the Bible are conclusive (e.g. unity of design, supernatural intelligence)
    The Role of the Church  
	  The Church's testimony is not the ultimate source of divine knowledge (Romish heresy)
	  The Church is a product of the Spirit through the instrumentality of the Word
    Internal Evidences of a Divine Origin  
	  The Bible presents phenomena of a supernatural intelligence (e.g. unity of design, 66 separate books by 40 authors)
	  The Bible's efficacy, majesty of style, and scope are arguments for its divine origin (e.g. 1 Tim. iii. 15, 1 John ii. 20, 27)
    The Role of the Holy Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit bears witness to the truth and divine authority of Scripture in our hearts (e.g. John xvi. 13, 14, 1 Cor. ii. 10-12)
	  Our full persuasion and assurance of Scripture's truth and authority come from the inward work of the Holy Spirit (e.g. 1 John v. 9, 1 Thess. ii. 13)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Perfection of Scripture  
	  Exhibits perfect freedom from errors of its production
	  Displays marvelous knowledge of human nature
	  Offers original and luminous solutions to human problems
	  Provides an exalted view of God, his law, and moral government
	  Offers a practical and beneficent system of morality

•   The Influence of Scripture  
	  Has unparalleled power over the human conscience
	  Extends and persists in its influence over communities of men
	  Is the direct work of the Holy Spirit on human hearts

•   The Role of the Holy Spirit  
	  Opens blinded eyes and gives sensibility to diseased hearts
	  Gives assurance through spiritual experience
	  Enables believers to test Scripture through experience

•   The Sufficiency of Scripture  
	  Contains the whole counsel of God for His glory and man's salvation
	  Is either explicitly stated in Scripture or deduced by good and necessary consequence
	  Nothing is to be added to Scripture, whether by new revelations or traditions

•   The Necessity of Inward Illumination  
	  The Spirit of God is necessary for the saving understanding of Scripture
	  Some circumstances require the light of nature and Christian prudence

•   Scripture as a Complete Rule  
	  Embraces the whole of supernatural revelation
	  Is sufficient for all practical necessities of men and communities
	  Is a perfect system of doctrine and principles for life

•   The Design of Scripture  
	  Professes to lead us to God
	  Must teach us whatever is necessary for that end
	  Assumes all the prerogatives of completeness

•   The Completeness of Scripture  
	  Teaches a perfect system of doctrine and principles
	  Has been proven by diligent study and practice
	  Is complete in all elements of a perfect rule of faith and practice

•   The Impossibility of Incompleteness  
	  The more diligent men study the Bible, the less they believe it is incomplete
	  The Bible teaches a perfect system of doctrine and principles

•   The Authority of Scripture  
	  Is the direct work of the Holy Spirit on human hearts
	  Is a complete rule of faith and practice
	  Is sufficient for all practical necessities of men and communities

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. No new revelations of the Spirit are expected  
	  The Bible is a complete and all-sufficient rule of faith.
	  The New Testament does not refer to any further revelation before the second advent of Christ.
	  No pretended revelations since the apostles have borne the marks of a supernatural revelation.

  2. Traditions of men cannot supplement Scripture  
	  The Scriptures do not ascribe authority to any supplementary rule.
	  Christ rebuked the practical observance of tradition in the Pharisees.
	  Tradition is indeterminate, obscure, and fragmentary.

  3. The Roman Church's traditions are inconsistent with Scripture  
	  Many Roman Catholic traditions are of modern origin.
	  They are inconsistent with the clear teaching of Scripture and the opinions of high authorities in the Church.

  4. Personal spiritual illumination is necessary for understanding  
	  Man in a state of nature is carnal and unable to discern spiritual truths.
	  Spiritual illumination opens the mind and heart to the spiritual discernment of Scripture.

  5. Spiritual illumination differs from inspiration  
	  It conveys no new truths, but opens the mind to the appreciation of existing truths.
	  It is an element in regeneration common to all children of God.
	  It is private and personal, not public.

  6. The Scriptures are a complete rule of faith and practice  
	  They lay down general principles, but do not descend into details.
	  They leave men to apply principles in the exercise of their natural judgment.

  7. The Holy Spirit guides in practical matters  
	  The sanctifying influences of the Holy Spirit guide men in adapting principles to changing circumstances.

  8. Liberty is allowed within the limits of Scripture  
	  Men are allowed to apply principles in the light of experience and changing circumstances.
	  This liberty is limited to the strict interpretation of Scripture principles.

  9. Not all things in Scripture are plain or clear  
	  Some things are necessary for salvation, but not all are equally clear.
	  The necessary truths are clearly propounded in Scripture.

  10. The Bible is the ultimate authority  
	  It is the complete and all-sufficient rule of faith and practice.
	  It is the final authority in matters of faith and practice.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. The Sufficiency of Scripture  

  The Scriptures are sufficient for man to know in order to salvation or practical guidance in duty.
	  This is affirmed by Scripture (2 Pet. 3:16, Psalm 119:105, 130)
  The Scriptures are designed for the personal use of both the learned and the unlearned.
	  Protestants affirm this, while Romanists deny it.

  II. Interpretation of Scripture  

  Every essential article of faith and rule of practice can be clearly learned from Scripture.
	  Protestants affirm this, while Romanists deny it.
  Private and unlearned Christians may safely interpret Scripture for themselves.
	  This is supported by Scripture (2 Tim. 3:15-17, Acts 17:11, John 5:39)

  III. Perspicuity of Scripture  

  The Scriptures are affirmed to be perspicuous (clear).
	  This is supported by Scripture (Psalm 119:105, 130, 2 Cor. 3:14, 2 Pet. 1:18-19)
  If the Scriptures are not perspicuous, they must mislead and falsify their pretensions.
	  This is a logical consequence of their claim to be a divine law and guide to salvation.

  IV. Experience and Scripture  

  Experience has uniformly proved the truth of the Protestant doctrine.
	  Churches that have disseminated the Scriptures among the people have conformed to their teaching in faith and practice.
	  Churches that have locked up the Scriptures have departed from them in letter and spirit.

  V. The Original Languages of Scripture  

  The Old Testament was written in Hebrew, and the New Testament in Greek.
	  These languages are the absolute rule of faith and ultimate appeal in all controversies.

  VI. Translation of Scripture  

  The original languages of Scripture are not known to all people.
	  Therefore, they are to be translated into the vulgar language of every nation.
	  This is supported by Scripture (Matt. 5:18, Isa. 8:20, Acts 15:15, John 5:39, 46, 1 Cor. 14:6, 9, 11, 12, 24, 27, 28, Col. 3:16, Rom. 15:4)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Preservation of Scripture  
	  The sacred text has been preserved in a state of essential purity.
	  The oldest existing Hebrew manuscripts date from the 9th or 10th century, while the oldest Greek manuscripts date from the 4th to 6th century.

•   Methods of Establishing the True Text  
	  Ancient manuscripts are collated and compared to establish the true text.
	  Quotations from the apostolic Scriptures found in the writings of early Christians are used to verify the text.
	  Early translations into other languages, such as the Samaritan Pentateuch, Greek Septuagint, and Latin Vulgate, are also used.

•   The Infallible Rule of Interpretation  
	  The infallible rule of interpretation of Scripture is the Scripture itself.
	  When there is a question about the true and full sense of any Scripture, it must be searched and known by other places that speak more clearly.

•   The Supreme Judge of Controversies  
	  The supreme judge by which all controversies of religion are to be determined is the Holy Spirit speaking in the Scripture.
	  All decrees of councils, opinions of ancient writers, doctrines of men, and private spirits are to be examined and judged by the Scripture.

•   The Authority of Scripture  
	  The authority of the Scriptures as the ultimate rule of faith rests alone in the fact that they are the Word of God.
	  The Scriptures are complete as a revelation in themselves and are not to be supplemented or explained by light drawn from any other source.

•   The Relationship Between Scripture Sections  
	  The different sections of the revelation mutually supplement and explain one another.
	  The Holy Spirit who inspired the Scriptures is the only adequate expounder of his own words.

•   The Role of the Holy Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit is promised to all the children of God as a Spirit of light and truth.
	  Christians are to study the Scriptures in dependence upon the guidance of the Holy Spirit.

•   The Protestant Doctrine  
	  The Protestant doctrine is that the Scriptures are the only rule of faith and practice.
	  There is no body of men qualified or authorized to interpret the Scriptures or to apply their teachings to the decision of particular cases.

•   The Romish Doctrine  
	  The Romish doctrine is that the Papal Church is the infallible teacher of men in religion.
	  The Church authoritatively determines what is Scripture, tradition, the true sense of Scripture and tradition, and the true application of the rule to every particular question of faith or practice.

•   The Importance of Scripture  
	  Scripture is the ultimate authority in matters of faith and practice.
	  Christians are to study the Scriptures using all the helps of true learning to ascertain their meaning.

Here are the 40 questions with 10 bullets and sub-bullets:

    The Authority of Scripture  
	  1. What propositions are affirmed in the first section?
		- The Scriptures are the only authoritative voice in the Church.
		- Creeds and confessions bind only those who voluntarily profess them.
	  2. What is the first stated false opinion as to the capacity of men to attain to a knowledge of God?
		- That men can attain to a knowledge of God through reason alone.
	  3. How is it proved to be false?
		- Because the Scriptures refer us to no standard or judge in matters of religion beyond or above themselves.
    The Sufficiency of Scripture  
	  4. What is the second false opinion stated?
		- That the Scriptures are not sufficient for the guidance of individuals.
	  5. How is it proved to be false?
		- Because the Scriptures are complete and perspicuous.
	  6. What is the third false opinion stated?
		- That Christians need the authority of the Church to interpret the Scriptures.
	  7. How is it proved to be false?
		- Because all Christians are commanded to search the Scriptures and judge both doctrines and professed teachers themselves.
    The Means of Understanding Scripture  
	  8. How can it be shown that a supernatural revelation from God to man is antecedently probable?
		- Through the existence of the Bible and its claims to be a supernatural revelation.
	  9. By what means was such a revelation at first given?
		- Through the prophets and apostles.
	  10. How has it since been embodied and transmitted?
		- Through the writings of the prophets and apostles.
    The Canon of Scripture  
	  11. How may the fact that the Christian Scriptures contain such a revelation be proved?
		- Through the evidence of the Bible itself.
	  12. What propositions are taught in the second and third sections?
		- The Old and New Testaments are part of the canon of Scripture.
    The Old and New Testaments  
	  13. What is the Old Testament?
		- The collection of writings that make up the Hebrew Bible.
	  14. What is the New Testament?
		- The collection of writings that make up the Christian Bible.
	  15. By what principles are we to determine whether or not a book has a right to a place in the canon of Scripture?
		- Through the principles of inspiration and apostolic authorship.
    The Apocrypha  
	  16. How is the genuineness of all the books received by Protestants in the Old Testament established?
		- Through the evidence of the Hebrew text and the early Christian fathers.
	  17. How is the genuineness of the books of the New Testament proved?
		- Through the evidence of the early Christian fathers and the manuscripts.
	  18. What are the Apocrypha?
		- The collection of writings that are not part of the canonical Bible.
	  19. How can it be proved that they are no part of Sacred Scripture?
		- Through the lack of apostolic authorship and the evidence of the early Christian fathers.
    Inspiration and Authority  
	  20. What is inspiration?
		- The process by which God guides the writers of Scripture.
	  21. What are the effects of inspiration, and how far do they extend in the case of the Scriptures?
		- Inspiration extends to the entire Bible, but not to the individual writers.
	  22. State the evidence that the Scriptures are inspired.
		- Through the evidence of the Bible itself and the early Christian fathers.
    The Church and Scripture  
	  23. Show that the authority of Scripture does not rest upon the testimony of the Church.
		- Through the evidence of the Bible itself.
	  24. What are the internal evidences which authenticate the claims of Scripture?
		- The unity and coherence of the Bible.
	  25. How does the Holy Ghost bear witness to the Scriptures?
		- Through the witness of the Holy Spirit in the hearts of believers.
    The Completeness of Scripture  
	  26. What is meant by the affirmation that the Scriptures as a rule of faith and practice are complete?
		- That the Bible contains all the necessary guidance for believers.
	  27. How may it be proved?
		- Through the evidence of the Bible itself and the early Christian fathers.
	  28. Prove that no additional revelations of the Spirit are to be expected during the present dispensation.
		- Through the evidence of the Bible itself and the early Christian fathers.
    Traditions and Reason  
	  29. Prove that traditions of men are not to be admitted.
		- Through the evidence of the Bible itself and the early Christian fathers.
	  30. How does spiritual illumination differ from inspiration?
		- Spiritual illumination is the work of the Holy Spirit in guiding believers, while inspiration is the process by which God guides the writers of Scripture.
	  31. What liberty of action do the Scriptures allow for the reason and choice of men in prudentially ordering matters that concern religion?
		- The Scriptures allow for the use of reason and choice in matters of prudence.
    The Perspicuity of Scripture  
	  32. What is meant by affirming that the Scriptures are perspicuous?
		- That the Bible is clear and easy to understand.
	  33. What do Protestants admit and what do they affirm on this subject?
		- Protestants admit that the Bible is perspicuous, but affirm that it is not always easy to understand.
	  34. Prove that the Scriptures are perspicuous.
		- Through the evidence of the Bible itself and the early Christian fathers.
    The Integrity of the Text  
	  35. What propositions are affirmed in the eighth section?
		- The integrity of the text of the Hebrew and Greek Scriptures is established through the evidence of the manuscripts and the early Christian fathers.
    The Interpretation of Scripture  
	  36. By what means is the integrity of the text of our modern copies of the Hebrew and Greek Scriptures established?
		- Through the evidence of the manuscripts and the early Christian fathers.
	  37. What propositions do the ninth and tenth sections affirm?
		- Scripture must be interpreted by Scripture.
	  38. Show that Scripture must be interpreted by Scripture.
		- Through the evidence of the Bible itself and the early Christian fathers.
    The Authority of the Church  
	  39. What is the Romish doctrine as to the authority of the Church in questions of faith and practice?
		- The Church has authority in matters of faith and practice.
	  40. What is the difference between a "rule" and a "guide"?
		- A rule is a binding authority, while a guide is a helpful but non-binding authority.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Nature of God  
	  There is only one true God (1)
	  This God is a free personal Spirit (2)
	  He is without bodily parts or passions (3)
    God's Perfections  
	  He possesses all absolute perfections in and of himself (4)
	  He possesses all relative perfections with respect to his creatures (5)
    God's Independence  
	  He is self-existent and absolutely independent (6)
	  He is the sole support, proprietor, and sovereign disposer of all his creatures (7)
    God's Sovereignty  
	  He has all life, glory, goodness, and blessedness in and of himself (8)
	  He is the alone fountain of all being, of whom, through whom, and to whom are all things (9)
    God's Knowledge and Power  
	  His knowledge is infinite, infallible, and independent upon the creature (10)
	  He has most sovereign dominion over all things (11)
    God's Holiness  
	  He is most holy in all His counsels, works, and commands (12)
	  To Him is due from angels and men, and every other creature, whatever worship, service, or obedience He is pleased to require (13)
    God's Attributes  
	  He is infinite in being and perfection (14)
	  He is eternal, incomprehensible, almighty, most wise, most holy, most free, and most absolute (15)
    God's Relationship with Creatures  
	  He is not in need of any creatures which He has made (16)
	  He derives no glory from creatures, but only manifests His own glory in, by, unto, and upon them (17)
    God's Judgment  
	  He is most just and terrible in His judgments (18)
	  He hates all sin and will by no means clear the guilty (19)
    Scriptural Proof  
	  The Bible provides numerous proof texts to support these propositions (20)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. The Unity of God  

  The existence of one God is proved by various arguments, including:
	  The First Cause argument
	  The Designing Intelligence argument
	  The Moral Governor argument
	  The unity of creation and providence
	  The sense of moral accountability in man

  2. The Indivisible Unity of God  

  The indivisible unity of God is proved by:
	  The fact that an essential division in the Godhead would constitute two Gods
	  The Scriptures, which teach that the Christian Trinity is one undivided God

  3. The Nature of God  

  God is a free personal Spirit, without bodily parts or passions
  This is proved by:
	  The arguments for the existence of God
	  The Scriptures, which set forth God as a personal spirit distinct from the world

  4. The Attributes of God  

  God possesses the attributes of intelligence, feeling, and will
  These attributes are proved by:
	  The argument from design
	  The sense of moral accountability in man

  5. The Personality of God  

  God is a personal spirit, distinct from the world
  This is proved by:
	  The argument from design
	  The sense of moral accountability in man

  6. The Nature of Spirit  

  Spirit is the subject to which the attributes of intelligence, feeling, and will belong
  We know spirit by self-consciousness

  7. The Properties of God  

  God possesses in infinite perfection all the properties that belong to our spirits
  These properties include:
	  Intelligence
	  Feeling
	  Will
	  Self-consciousness

  8. The Denial of Material Properties  

  God does not possess bodily parts or passions
  This is denied because:
	  There is no evidence that God possesses such properties
	  Such properties are inconsistent with God's infinite and absolute perfections

  9. The Use of Metaphorical Language  

  The Scriptures use metaphorical language when describing God's actions, such as:
	  Attributing an ear or hand to God
  This is because spiritual faculties are exercised in a way that is different from material faculties

  10. The Implications of God's Nature  

  The nature of God has implications for our understanding of the world and our relationship with God
  These implications include:
	  The unity and simplicity of God
	  The distinction between God and the world
	  The importance of recognizing God's attributes and personality in our lives.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Nature  
	  God possesses all absolute perfections in and of himself
	  God possesses all relative perfections with respect to his creatures

•   Attributes of God  
	  Absolute attributes: self-existence, immensity, eternity, intelligence, etc.
	  Relative attributes: omnipresence, omniscience, etc.

•   Limitations of Human Knowledge  
	  We can only know properties of God that he has condescended to reveal
	  We can only know as much of these properties as he has revealed

•   God's Infinity  
	  God is infinite in his being, wisdom, power, justice, etc.
	  God's infinity means he can exist without limitations of time or space

•   God's Immensity  
	  God's essence fills all space
	  God's spirit is an absolute unit, without extension or dimensions

•   God's Omnipresence  
	  God is present everywhere at all times
	  God is present to all creatures at all times, but manifests himself differently to different creatures

•   God's Eternity  
	  God's duration has no limit
	  God's existence is an ever-abiding, all-embracing present

•   God's Unchangeability  
	  God is absolutely unchangeable in his being and all modes and states thereof
	  God's knowledge, feelings, purposes, and engagements with creatures are unchanging

•   God's Simultaneous Presence  
	  God is simultaneously present everywhere at all times
	  God's presence is not limited by time or space

•   God's Revelation  
	  God reveals his perfections to us through his Word
	  We can only know God's perfections as he has revealed them to us

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Knowledge  
	  God's knowledge is infinite and perfect
	  God knows things immediately, in their essential nature
	  God knows all things eternally, by one direct intuition
	  God's knowledge is independent, total, and permanent

•   God's Self-Knowledge  
	  God knows himself, his nature, and his purposes
	  God knows the resources of his power and the purposes of his will
	  God's self-knowledge is essential to his infinite and eternal being

•   Wisdom  
	  Wisdom presupposes knowledge and is the excellent practical use of God's infinite knowledge
	  God's wisdom is exercised in the election of ends and the selection of means
	  God's wisdom is illustrated in the perfect system of his works of creation, providence, and grace

•   God's Omnipotence  
	  God's power is infinite and unlimited in its range
	  God's power is infinitely perfect in its mode of action
	  God's power acts directly upon its objects and effects all he intends

•   God's Power and Means  
	  God acts immediately, with or without means as he pleases
	  God's power is absolutely independent of all that is exterior to his own nature
	  God's power is exercised at his will, but there remains an infinite reserve of possibility

•   God's Nature and Power  
	  God's power is the power of his all-perfect, self-existent essence
	  God cannot act unwisely or unrighteously, not for want of power but for want of will
	  God's ultimate principles of reason and moral right and wrong are not products of his power, but of his nature

•   God's Omnipotence and Creation  
	  God's omnipotence is illustrated, but never exhausted, in his works of creation and providence
	  God's power is exercised at his will, but there remains an infinite reserve of possibility

•   God's Moral Perfection  
	  God's moral perfection is one absolutely perfect righteousness
	  God's moral perfection is essential to his nature and cannot be changed

•   God's Goodness and Righteousness  
	  God's goodness and righteousness are essential to his nature and cannot be changed
	  God's goodness and righteousness are illustrated in his works of creation, providence, and grace

•   God's Eternal and Immutable Nature  
	  God's nature is eternal and immutable
	  God's nature is the ultimate standard of reason and moral right and wrong

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Perfection  
	  God's perfection is infinite and eternal
	  He is perfectly merciful and just, not alternating between the two

•   God's Goodness  
	  Includes benevolence (promoting happiness of sensitive creatures)
	  Includes love (promoting happiness of intelligent creatures)
	  Includes mercy (exercised toward the miserable)
	  Includes grace (exercised toward the undeserving)

•   God's Sovereignty  
	  God's grace toward the undeserving is based on his sovereign will
	  Can only be assured through a positive revelation from God

•   God's Truth  
	  God is absolutely true in all his properties and actions
	  His knowledge is accurate, wisdom is infallible, and goodness is perfectly true

•   God's Faithfulness  
	  God is always self-consistent in his actions
	  He is sincere in his promises and threatenings, and faithful in their fulfillment

•   God's Justice  
	  Viewed absolutely, God's justice is the all-perfect righteousness of his being
	  Viewed relatively, it is his infinitely righteous nature exercised in governing his creatures

•   God's Immutable Justice  
	  God's justice is an immutable principle of his divine constitution
	  He cannot deny himself or look on iniquity

•   God's Punishment of Sin  
	  God punishes sin because it is intrinsically hateful and deserving of punishment
	  Punishment is a revelation of righteousness in God and a stimulus to moral excellence in his creatures

•   God's Glory and Excellence  
	  God's goodness is regulated by his wisdom to achieve the supreme ends of his glory and his creatures' excellence

•   God's Reliability  
	  God's reliability is the foundation for rational confidence in the constitution of our natures and the order of the external world
	  It guarantees the validity of our senses, reason, conscience, and human testimony, and the reliability of the inspired Scriptures.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Righteousness  
	  Proved by Scripture (Deut. 32:35, Isa. 59:18, 2 Thess. 1:6, Rom. 1:32)
	  Necessary for God to be just and the justifier of believers (Rom. 3:26)
	  Demonstrated in the vicarious suffering of Christ (Gal. 2:21, Gal. 3:21)

•   Universal Judgment of Sin  
	  Awakened sinners universally agree that sin deserves punishment
	  Immutable righteousness demands punishment for crime
	  Moral sense of enlightened men agrees with this judgment

•   Mosaic Dispensation and Sacrifices  
	  All things in the law are purged with blood (Heb. 9:22)
	  Without shedding of blood, there is no remission (Heb. 9:22)
	  Illustrates the principle of righteousness in all human laws and penalties

•   Infinite Holiness of God  
	  Expresses God's perfect purity and righteousness (Lev. 11:44, Ps. 145:17)
	  Expresses God's transcendent majesty and excellence (Isa. 6:3)

•   God's Self-Existence and Independence  
	  God is self-existent and absolutely independent (eternal and creator of all things)
	  His own being has its cause in itself
	  He is independent of all other beings in his being, purposes, and actions

•   God's Sovereignty  
	  His absolute right to govern and dispose of the world
	  Rests in his adorable person, not just his will
	  Grounds of sovereignty: infinite superiority, absolute ownership, and perpetual dependence on him

•   Unity of the Godhead  
	  Three Persons of one substance, power, and eternity: God the Father, God the Son, and God the Holy Ghost
	  Scripture proof texts: 1 John 5:7, Matt. 3:16-17, Matt. 28:19, 2 Cor. 13:14

•   God the Father  
	  Of none, neither begotten nor proceeding
	  Scripture proof text: John 1:14, 18

•   God the Son  
	  Eternally begotten of the Father
	  Scripture proof text: John 1:14, 18

•   God the Holy Ghost  
	  Eternally proceeding from the Father and the Son
	  Scripture proof text: John 15:26, Gal. 4:6

•   Scripture Proof Texts  
	  Various Scripture passages cited to support the above points

Here are 10 bullets summarizing the text, with sub-bullets:

    The Trinity: Three in Unity  
	  The Christian doctrine of the Trinity asserts that there is one God in three persons: Father, Son, and Holy Ghost.
	  Each person is equally the one God, with the same essence and attributes.
    The Nature of God  
	  God's essence is spiritual and indivisible.
	  God's attributes, such as intelligence and will, are inherent properties of his essence.
    The Father is God  
	  The Father is acknowledged as God in the Scriptures.
	  The Father's divinity is not questioned.
    The Son is God  
	  The Son existed before his birth of the Virgin.
	  The Son is called God in the Scriptures, with titles such as Jehovah and Almighty.
	  The Son has all divine attributes, including eternity, immutability, omnipresence, omniscience, and omnipotence.
	  The Son is credited with all divine works, including creation, preservation, and judgment.
    The Holy Ghost is God  
	  The Holy Ghost is called God in the Scriptures.
	  Divine perfections, such as omniscience and omnipotence, are ascribed to the Holy Ghost.
	  The Holy Ghost is credited with divine works, including creation, miracles, and regeneration.
    Scriptural Evidence  
	  The Scriptures provide evidence for the divinity of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost.
	  The Scriptures attribute divine titles, attributes, and works to each person of the Trinity.
    Worship and Invocation  
	  Divine worship is to be paid to the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost.
	  The name of Jesus is invoked in baptism and the apostolical benediction.
    The Indivisible Essence  
	  The essence of God is indivisible, and each person of the Trinity consists of the same essence.
	  The attributes of God are inseparable from his essence.
    The Distinction between Persons  
	  The Father, Son, and Holy Ghost are distinguished from one another by certain personal properties.
	  The order of subsistence and operation of the persons is revealed in the Scriptures.
    The Trinity is Revealed in Scripture  
	  The doctrine of the Trinity is clearly revealed in the New Testament.
	  The Old Testament may hint at the doctrine, but it is not as clearly stated.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Divine Worship  
	  Divine worship is to be paid to the Holy Spirit.
	  The apostolical benediction invokes the Holy Spirit's gracious influences.
	  Baptism is into the name of the Holy Spirit.
	  Blasphemy against the Holy Spirit is never forgiven.
    The Nature of the Trinity  
	  The titles Father, Son, and Holy Spirit refer to different persons, not different relations of the same person.
	  There is one indivisible and inalienable spiritual essence common to Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.
    Distinct Persons  
	  The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit use personal pronouns when speaking to or about each other.
	  They mutually love one another, act upon and through one another, and take counsel together.
	  They are eternally mutually related as Father and Son and Spirit.
    Eternal Relationships  
	  The Father is the Father of the Son, and the Son is the Son of the Father.
	  The Holy Spirit is the Spirit of the Father and of the Son.
    Harmonious Economy of Operations  
	  The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit work together in a perfectly harmonious economy of operations.
	  The Father creates and sits supreme in general administration.
	  The Son becomes incarnate in human nature and discharges mediatorial functions.
	  The Holy Spirit makes God's grace omnipresent and applies it to believers.
    Distinct Properties  
	  The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit have distinct personal properties.
	  The attributes of God are common to each person, but the properties of each person are unique.
    Order of Subsistence and Operation  
	  The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are revealed in a certain order of subsistence and operation.
	  The personal names by which they are revealed express their peculiar personal properties.
    The Father's Property  
	  The Father's peculiar personal property is expressed by the title Father.
	  As a person, the Father is eternally the Father of his only begotten Son.
    The Son's Property  
	  The Son's peculiar personal property is expressed by the title Son.
	  As a person, the Son is eternally the only begotten Son of the Father.
    The Holy Spirit's Property  
	  The Holy Spirit's peculiar personal property is expressed by the title Spirit.
	  The title Spirit cannot fully express the Holy Spirit's essence.

Here are the 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Nature of God  
	  God is one living and true God
	  The title "God" has been applied to the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit
    The Trinity  
	  The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are one God, identical in essence and divine perfections
	  The Father sends and operates through the Son and Holy Spirit
	  The Son sends and operates through the Holy Spirit
	  The Holy Spirit reveals both the Father and the Son
    The Attributes of God  
	  God is a personal spirit
	  God is infinite, eternal, and unchangeable
	  God's power, wisdom, and goodness are infinite and absolute
	  God's justice and holiness are immutable principles of his nature
    The Distinction between the Absolute and Relative Perfections of God  
	  The absolute perfections of God are inherent and unchanging
	  The relative perfections of God are exercised in relation to his creatures
    The Nature of God's Knowledge and Power  
	  God's mode of knowing is different from ours
	  God's power is infinite and exercised in various ways
	  God's power is limited only by his wisdom and goodness
    The Moral Character of God  
	  God's moral character is absolute and unchanging
	  God's goodness is the foundation of his justice and holiness
    The Trinity and the Divine Essence  
	  The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are one God, identical in essence and divine perfections
	  Each person of the Trinity sustains a distinct relation to the divine essence
    The Distinction between the Absolute and Relative Justice of God  
	  The absolute justice of God is an immutable principle of his nature
	  The relative justice of God is exercised in relation to his creatures
    The Sovereignty of God  
	  God's sovereignty is absolute and unchanging
	  God's sovereignty is the foundation of his justice and holiness
    The Trinity and the Divine Attributes  
	  The Trinity is the foundation of God's attributes, including his power, wisdom, and goodness
	  Each person of the Trinity sustains a distinct relation to the divine attributes

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Chapter Two: Of God  

    The Nature of God  
	  The Son is the true God (proof: [insert text])
	  The Holy Ghost is the true God (proof: [insert text])
    The Distinction between the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost  
	  How may it be proved that Father, Son, and Holy Ghost are distinct persons?
	  What is the distinction between the attributes of God and the personal properties of Father, Son, and Holy Ghost?
    Personal Properties of God  
	  Personal properties of the Father
	  Personal properties of the Son
	  Personal properties of the Holy Ghost

  Chapter Three: Of God's Eternal Decree  

    God's Eternal Plan  
	  God has had from eternity an unchangeable plan with reference to his creatures
	  The plan of God comprehends and determines all things and events of every kind that come to pass
    The Nature of God's Plan  
	  The plan of God is single and all-comprehensive, providing for all means and conditions as well as ends selected
	  The plan of God is not contingent or mutable, but rather established and unchangeable
    Scriptural Affirmations  
	  The Scriptures expressly declare that God's plan determines all things and events
	  Examples of scriptural affirmations:
		- "He worketh all things after the counsel of his own will" (Eph. 1:11)
		- "The king's heart is in the hands of the LORD, as the rivers of water: he turneth it whithersoever he will" (Prov. 21:1)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Sovereignty  
	  God's purpose is to work in us to will and do of his good pleasure (Phil. 2:13)
	  God's purpose is to ordain and determine all things (Eph. 2:10)
    Sinful Actions of Men  
	  God permits sinful acts of men and wicked angels, but does not cause or approve them
	  God overrules sinful acts for his own wise and holy ends (Gen. 50:20)
    God's Purpose is Absolute  
	  God's purpose is not conditional, but depends only on his own will
	  God's purpose is not suspended on any condition, but is absolute
    Conditional vs. Unconditional Decrees  
	  A conditional decree is one that determines an event on condition that another event happens
	  An unconditional decree is one that determines an event without any condition
    Calvinist View
