	  Calvinists believe that God's decrees are unconditional, including those related to free actions of men and angels
	  Calvinists believe that God certainly foresees and determines free actions
    Debate with Arminians and Socinians  
	  Arminians and Socinians believe that God cannot certainly foresee free actions
	  Arminians believe that God determines free actions, but deny that he can certainly foresee them
    Calvinist View Proved  
	  The fact that God's decrees determine all classes of events, including free actions
	  The fact that God's purpose is absolute and not suspended on any condition
    God's Decrees Determine Causes and Conditions  
	  God's decrees determine the cause and conditions of events, as well as the events themselves
	  God's decrees make free actions free in relation to their agents, and contingent events contingent in relation to their conditions
    God's Purpose is All-Comprehensive  
	  God's purpose is not limited to specific events, but encompasses the entire system of events
	  God's purpose is wise and holy, and overrules sinful acts for his own ends
    The Confession Teaches Unconditional Decrees  
	  The Confession teaches that all the decrees of God are unconditional
	  This teaching is in line with the Calvinist view of God's sovereignty and purpose.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Decrees are Sovereign  
	  God is the eternal and absolute Creator of all things.
	  All creatures exist and act under God's plan.
	  Scripture affirms God's sovereignty (Dan. 4:35; Isa. 40:13,14; Rom. 9:15-18; Eph. 1:5).

•   God's Decree Includes Means and Conditions  
	  God determines the means and conditions upon which events depend.
	  Scripture examples: Eph. 1:4, Eph. 2:8, 2 Thess. 2:13, Acts 27:24.

•   Salvation is Conditioned on Faith and God's Decree  
	  Salvation is conditioned upon personal faith.
	  God's decree regarding salvation rests solely on His own will (Rom. 9:11; Eph. 1:11).

•   God's Purpose is Efficacious  
	  God's decrees are certainly efficacious, rendering every event absolutely certain.
	  This is evident from God's nature as an infinitely wise and powerful person (Matt. 16:21; Luke 24:44; 22:22).

•   God's Purpose Must be Consistent with His Nature  
	  God's decrees must be absolutely perfect in wisdom and righteousness.
	  The problem of the permission of sin is insoluble, but God's nature remains consistent with absolute righteousness.

•   God is Not the Cause of Sin  
	  God is absolutely holy and cannot violate His own will.
	  Sin is a violation of God's will, and God is not responsible for it.

•   God Permits Sin for a Higher Purpose  
	  God permits sin for the purpose of overruling it in the interests of righteousness and benevolence.
	  This is for God's own glory and our highest good.

•   God's Purpose is Perfectly Consistent with His Nature  
	  God's purpose is in all things perfectly consistent with His nature and mode of action.
	  This is a self-evident truth from the nature of God as an eternal, absolutely perfect, and unchangeable Being.

•   God's Decrees are Unconditional  
	  God's decrees relate to all events without exception and are sovereign and unconditional.

•   God's Decrees are Certain and Absolute  
	  God's decrees render every event absolutely certain.
	  This is evident from the nature of God as an infinitely wise and powerful person.

•   God's Purpose is for His Own Glory and Our Highest Good  
	  God's purpose is ultimately for His own glory and our highest good.
	  This is the ultimate goal of God's decrees and actions.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Decree  
	  Determines the nature of the agent and their actions
	  Cannot be inconsistent with itself
	  Makes a given event certainly future, but does not cause it
    Free Agents  
	  Can be free and still have their actions be certainly future
	  God's foreknowledge of free actions does not contradict their freedom
	  God's actions are certainly holy, yet free
    Predestination  
	  Some men and angels are predestinated to everlasting life
	  Others are foreordained to everlasting death
	  The number of those predestinated is certain and definite
    Election  
	  God chooses individuals to eternal life, not because of their faith or good works
	  The choice is based on God's sovereign good pleasure
	  The elect are chosen to faith and repentance, not because of them
    Scriptural Evidence  
	  The elect are spoken of as individuals in Scripture
	  Their names are written in heaven and in the book of life
	  The blessings of election are such as pertains to salvation
    Calvinist View  
	  God chooses individuals to eternal life based on his sovereign good pleasure
	  The choice is not based on faith or good works
	  The elect are chosen to faith and repentance, not because of them
    Arminian View  
	  God elects individuals to eternal life based on their faith and repentance
	  The choice is based on God's foreknowledge of who will repent and believe
    Socinian View  
	  God's election amounts to no more than his general purpose to save all believers as a class
	  The free acts of men are uncertain and cannot be foreknown as certainly future
    God's Sovereignty  
	  God's decree determines all events of every class that come to pass
	  His sovereignty is immutable, unconditional, and eternal
    Purpose of God's Decree  
	  The purpose of God's decree is to manifest his glory
	  The decree is for the manifestation of his glory, not for human benefit

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Election is to individuals, not communities  
	  Elected to salvation, adoption, and holiness
	  Not based on class or possession of spiritual qualities

•   Election is unchangeable  
	  Self-evident
	  Not conditioned on foreseen faith or repentance

•   Election is based on sovereign grace and personal love  
	  Not on works, but on God's secret counsel
	  Faith and repentance are gifts of God, not conditions of election

•   Faith and repentance are fruits of election, not conditions  
	  Declared in Scripture as gifts of God
	  Cannot condition God's purpose

•   Election is conditioned on God's good pleasure  
	  Declared in Scripture as the purpose of God's will
	  God claims the right of sovereign, unconditional election

•   God's purpose in election is the praise of his glorious grace  
	  Final end of God's works is the manifestation of his glory
	  Election is a means to this end

•   God has appointed the elect unto glory  
	  Through the eternal and most free purpose of his will
	  Foreordained all means to this end

•   The elect are redeemed by Christ  
	  Effectually called unto faith in Christ by his Spirit
	  Justified, adopted, sanctified, and kept by his power

•   Only the elect are redeemed by Christ  
	  Effectually called, justified, adopted, sanctified, and saved
	  Others are not redeemed, called, justified, adopted, sanctified, or saved

•   God's purpose in election is comprehensive and all-encompassing  
	  Includes all elements necessary to accomplish the end
	  Determines means and ends simultaneously

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. God's Purposes and Means  

  God's purposes are like means to ends
  He uses systems of means to accomplish a great design
  His purposes are the product of an infinite intelligence

  2. God's Eternal Purposes  

  God accomplishes his eternal purposes in creation and providence
  He uses systems of means in subordination to predetermined ends
  All events decreed by God eventuate in the relation of means to ends

  3. God's Decree and Means  

  God explicitly tells us that he determines one thing to accomplish another
  He predestinates men to salvation through sanctification and belief of the truth
  The gift of Christ and the Holy Ghost are means to accomplish God's purpose

  4. The Gift of Christ  

  The gift of Christ to make atonement for sin is a means to accomplish God's purpose
  Christ was eternally appointed to make atonement for the elect
  He has made atonement for none others

  5. The Order of God's Purposes  

  The order of God's purposes is means to ends
  The means are intended to effect the end
  None but the elect are redeemed by Christ or effectually called

  6. The View of the Confession  

  The Confession affirms that God elected certain men to salvation
  He determined to send Christ to die for them and the Holy Ghost to renew and sanctify them

  7. The Inadequacy of the Alternative View  

  The alternative view that God determined to save all men is inadequate
  It would require a purpose to save all and a purpose to save only some to coexist in the divine mind
  This is inconsistent with God's unchangeable, self-consistent, and certain purposes

  8. The Gift of Christ and the Holy Ghost  

  The gift of Christ and the Holy Ghost are means to accomplish God's purpose
  They are intended to effect the end of the salvation of the elect

  9. The Election of Men  

  God elected certain men to salvation out of the mass of the fallen race
  He determined to send Christ to die for them and the Holy Ghost to renew and sanctify them

  10. The Consistency of God's Purposes  

  God's purposes are unchangeable, self-consistent, and certain
  They are not contradictory or inconsistent with one another

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Section 1: God's Sovereignty in Salvation  

    God's purpose in salvation is not in vain  
	  God predestinates only those who are actually saved
	  Christ redeems only those who are redeemed
    Christ's sacrifice is for the elect  
	  Only the elect are redeemed by Christ
	  Only the elect are effectually called, justified, adopted, and sanctified

  Section 2: God's Sovereignty in Reprobation  

    God's decree of reprobation  
	  God has determined to withhold his grace from the rest of mankind
	  This withholding is for the glory of God's sovereign power
    God's justice demands punishment for unexpiated sin  
	  God treats those left in their sins with exact justice
	  God's justice demands the punishment of all unexpiated sin

  Section 3: The Nature of Reprobation  

    Reprobation consists of two elements  
	  Negative: God does not elect the reprobate, but "passes over" them
	  Positive: Reprobation is judicial, treating the reprobate according to their deserts
    Reprobation is not inconsistent with justice  
	  All men are "by nature the children of wrath" and justly obnoxious to the penalty of the law
	  Vicarious satisfaction of divine justice was necessary for salvation

  Section 4: The Sovereignty of God in Salvation  

    Salvation is a matter of divine discretion  
	  The selection of salvation's subjects is inalienably a matter of divine discretion
	  Salvation is declared to be in its very essence a matter of grace
    Scriptural support for God's sovereignty in salvation  
	  Romans 9:18, 1 Peter 2:8, Revelation 13:8, Jude 4
	  God asserts the right to sovereignly choose who will be saved

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Mystery of Predestination  
	  The doctrine of predestination is a high mystery that requires special prudence and care.
	  It is often abused and its proper use is of great importance.
    The Principle of Divine Sovereignty  
	  The principle of divine sovereignty in the distribution of grace is revealed in Scripture.
	  It is not difficult to comprehend and is of great practical use.
    The Philosophy of Predestination  
	  The philosophy of the relation of God's sovereign purpose to human free agency and moral evil is not revealed in Scripture.
	  It cannot be discovered by human reason and should not be rashly meddled with.
    The Election of Particular Persons  
	  The election or nonelection of particular persons is not revealed in Scripture.
	  The preceptive will of God (commands to repent and believe) is the rule of human duty.
    The Relationship Between Duty and Election  
	  Duty and grace are first with man, and the inference of personal election is only consequent upon the possession of grace.
	  The concern of the inquirer is with the fact that grace is offered and assured upon condition of acceptance.
    The Privilege of Believers  
	  Believers have the privilege of making the fact of their eternal calling and election sure by adding to faith virtue, knowledge, etc.
	  If they do these things, they shall never fall (2 Pet. 1:5-10).
    The Importance of Obedience  
	  Obedience to the gospel is necessary for assurance of eternal election.
	  This doctrine affords matter of praise, reverence, and admiration of God, and of humility, diligence, and consolation to all who sincerely obey the gospel.
    The Attributes of God's Plan  
	  God's plan must have had from eternity a definite purpose.
	  The general attributes of that plan must have been wise, just, and good.
    The Decrees of God  
	  The decrees of God are one purpose.
	  They relate to and determine all events of every kind.
    The Relation of God's Purpose to Human Sin  
	  The eternal purpose of God sustains a relation to the sinful acts of men.
	  This relation is not fully understood and should not be rashly meddled with.

Here are the 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Decrees and Perfections  
	  All of God's decrees are certainly efficacious (bullet 17)
	  They are consistent with God's own perfections (bullet 19)
	  God cannot be the author of sin (bullet 20)
	  God's decrees are not inconsistent with the liberty of free agents (bullet 21)
    Election and Salvation  
	  God has chosen individuals, not classes, to eternal life (bullet 28)
	  Election is not conditioned upon the foreseen faith and repentance of the person elected (bullet 29)
	  Election is grounded alone upon the good pleasure of God (bullet 30)
    God's Purposes and Intentions  
	  God purposes one thing in order to another thing (bullet 35)
	  The purpose to give Christ sustains the purpose to secure the salvation of the elect (bullet 36)
    Reprobation and Justice  
	  God's reprobation of the wicked involves a negative element (bullet 42)
	  It also involves a positive element (bullet 43)
    Creation and the Universe  
	  God created the visible universe ex nihilo (from nothing) in six days (Chapter 4, Section 1)
	  The elementary substances of things were created out of nothing (bullet 1)
    The Nature of God's Decrees  
	  The certainty of a free act is not inconsistent with the liberty of the agent in the act (bullet 22)
	  The decrees of God are consistent with the liberty of free agents (bullet 21)
    The Election of Individuals  
	  The Socinian, Arminian, and Calvinistic doctrines on election are stated (bullet 27)
	  Election is grounded alone upon the good pleasure of God (bullet 30)
    God's Ultimate End in Election  
	  God's ultimate end in election is not stated (bullet 31)
    The Order of God's Purposes  
	  The order of God's purposes set forth in this section is both the natural one and the true one (bullet 39)
    The Rule of Human Duty  
	  The rule of human duty is stated (bullet 50)
	  The great concernment of the religious inquirer is stated (bullet 51)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Sovereignty and Creation  
	  God's glory existed before the world was created (John 17:5, 24)
	  God is the absolute sovereign and proprietor of all things (Rom. 11:36; 1 Cor. 8:6; Col. 1:16; Rev. 4:11; Neh. 9:6)
    The Creation Account  
	  The Hebrew word for "create" is used to express the idea of absolute making (Ps. 90:2)
	  God created all things from nothing, with no preexisting matter (Heb. 11:3; Ps. 33:6; 148:5)
    Scriptural Evidence for Creation  
	  The Scriptures attribute creation to God's will, word, and breath (Ps. 33:6; 148:5)
	  God is the creator of substance ex nihilo, as well as the former of worlds and things (Heb. 11:3)
    The Nature of Creation  
	  The creation came before chaos, with God creating all things from nothing (Gen. 1:1-2)
	  The substances of things must have had a beginning as well as their present forms (Ps. 90:2)
    The Role of the Trinity in Creation  
	  The Scriptures attribute creation to God absolutely, without distinction of person (Gen. 1:1, 26)
	  Creation is attributed to the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit in different passages (1 Cor. 8:6; Heb. 1:2; Ps. 104:30; John 1:2, 3; Gen. 1:2; Job 33:4)
    The Duration of Creation  
	  God performed the work of creation in the sense of formation and adjustment of the universe in six days (Ex. 20:11)
	  The science of geology has revealed a process of divinely regulated development over vast periods of time
    The Relationship between Scripture and Science  
	  The conclusions of science must be adjusted with the inspired record found in the first chapter of Genesis
	  Some suppose that the first verse relates to the creation of the elements of things at the absolute beginning

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Creation Account in Genesis  
	  The creation account in Genesis describes God's creation of the world in six days
	  Some interpret the "days" as natural days, while others see them as cycles of vast duration
	  The exact meaning of the creation account is still a matter of debate

•   The Record in Genesis  
	  The record in Genesis is given by divine revelation and is therefore infallibly true
	  The book of revelation and the book of nature (science) are both from God and will ultimately coincide

•   The Science of Geology  
	  The facts upon which geology is based are still imperfectly collected and understood
	  A profitable comparison and adjustment of the two records (Genesis and science) has not yet been attempted

•   The Purpose of the Creation Account  
	  The creation account was designed to lay the foundation for an intelligent faith in Jehovah as the creator and ruler of all things
	  It was not intended to prevent or replace scientific interpretation of the world

•   God's Evaluation of Creation  
	  God pronounced all his works as "very good" (Gen. 1:31)
	  This means that all things in the world were excellent according to their respective kinds and perfectly good with reference to God's design

•   The Final End of God's Creation  
	  Two opinions have been held by theologians: God's ultimate end is either the promotion of human happiness or the manifestation of his own glory
	  Our confession takes the position that God's chief end is the manifestation of his own glory

•   Scriptural Support for God's Glory  
	  The Scriptures explicitly assert that God's chief end is the manifestation of his own glory (Col. 1:16, Prov. 16:4, etc.)
	  This is also the chief end of God's eternal decrees, providential governing, and gracious dispensations

•   The Duty to Pursue God's Glory  
	  It is the duty of all moral agents to adopt the manifestation of God's glory as their personal end in all things (1 Cor. 10:31, 1 Pet. 4:11)
	  This is the highest and worthiest end that God could propose to himself

•   The Intrinsic Value of God's Glory  
	  The manifestation of God's glory is intrinsically the highest and worthiest end that God could propose to himself
	  This is the supreme end that gives character to all God's creative, providential, and gracious dispensations

•   The Importance of Humility and Patience  
	  God requires us to believe and learn, and to approach the study of creation with humility and patience
	  Apparent discrepancies in established truths can have their ground only in imperfect knowledge.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Nature and Motives  
	  God is the absolute creator and sovereign
	  God's actions cannot be subordinate to finite and created ends
	  God's final ends and motives are interior to himself

•   Creation of Man  
	  Man was created immediately by God
	  Man was created last in the order of creation, as the crown of God's creation
	  Man's body was created from earthly materials, and his soul was created from nothing

•   Evidence for Immediate Creation  
	  The hypothesis of development is unsupported by facts
	  No transitional forms have been found among existing or fossil species
	  The Scriptures affirm the fact of man's immediate creation

•   Man's Superiority  
	  Man is vastly superior to lower animals in kind and degree
	  Man is immortal and a joint heir with Christ
	  God is the father of our spirits

•   The Human Pair  
	  God created one human pair, from whom the entire human race descended
	  The human pair was created before the existence of other humans
	  The human pair was created with the ability to fulfill God's law

•   The Law of God  
	  The law of God was written in the hearts of the human pair
	  The human pair had the power to fulfill the law
	  The human pair was given the command not to eat of the tree of knowledge

•   The Fall of Man  
	  The human pair was given the possibility of transgressing the law
	  The human pair was left to the liberty of their own will
	  The human pair's will was subject to change

•   The Consequences of the Fall  
	  The human pair's disobedience led to their separation from God
	  The human pair's dominion over the creatures was lost
	  The human pair's happiness in communion with God was disrupted

•   The Christian Revelation  
	  The Christian revelation affirms the truth of man's immediate creation
	  The Christian revelation affirms the truth of the human pair's creation
	  The Christian revelation affirms the truth of God's law and its relation to humanity

•   Scientific Theories vs. Scripture  
	  Scientific theories about human origins are not supported by facts
	  Scientific theories about human origins are in conflict with Scripture
	  Scripture is the ultimate authority on human origins and the nature of God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Human Variability  
	  The differences between human varieties are no greater than those between individuals of the same species.
	  Human varieties can intermix and produce fertile offspring.

•   Common Human Origin  
	  Archaeological, historical, and philological investigations indicate a common origin for all nations.
	  The Scriptures directly assert a common origin for all nations (Acts 17:26, Gen. 10).

•   Scriptural Support  
	  The Scriptures teach that all nations descended from a common ancestor (Gen. 10).
	  The doctrine of original sin and redemption presuppose a common human origin.

•   God's Creation of Man  
	  God created man in his own image (Gen. 1:26-27).
	  Man was created like God in physical constitution, moral character, and dignity.

•   Man's Moral Character  
	  Man was created with a positive moral character, including knowledge, righteousness, and true holiness.
	  This moral character is essential for moral government and knowing God.

•   Indestructible Likeness  
	  The likeness between God and man is indestructible.
	  This likeness is essential for man's ability to know God and be subject to moral government.

•   Pelagianism  
	  Pelagians believe that a created holiness is an absurdity.
	  They argue that God created Adam as a moral agent with no inherent tendency to good or evil.

•   Absurdity of Pelagianism  
	  A state of moral indifference in an intelligent adult moral agent is an impossibility.
	  Such indifference is itself sin and cannot give rise to a holy moral character.

•   Scriptural Refutation  
	  The Scriptures teach that Adam was created in "righteousness and true holiness" (Eph. 4:24).
	  God proclaimed all his works, including Adam, as "very good" (Gen. 1:31).

•   Consequences of Pelagianism  
	  If Adam was created without a positive holy disposition, his first volition must have been sinful or indifferent.
	  Neither a sinful nor an indifferent volition can give rise to a holy moral character.

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Creation and Image of God  
	  Man was created in the image of God (Gen 1:27)
	  Regeneration restores human nature to its pristine condition (Eph 4:24, Col 3:10)
	  The likeness to God lost by the fall is restored in the new birth (knowledge, righteousness, and true holiness)

•   Christ as the Model Man  
	  Christ is the model Man, created without sin and predetermined to holiness (1 Cor 15:45, 47)
	  He was called "that holy thing" in his mother's womb (Luke 1:35)

•   Adam's Knowledge and Guidance  
	  Adam had sufficient knowledge for guidance as a holy moral agent (implied in Gen 1:27)
	  He had special and direct revelation from God (Gen 2:16-17)
	  He was directed as to the divine will regarding the fruit of the tree of knowledge (Gen 2:16-17)

•   Adam's Moral Condition  
	  Adam was created holy and capable of obedience, but also capable of falling (evident from the event)
	  This moral condition was not intended to be permanent (implied in Gen 3:6-7)
	  It is one of the special elements of human nature that cannot be known from experience or observation

•   Human Nature and Sanctification  
	  Human nature is free, but with a tendency towards holiness or evil (depending on the individual's condition)
	  The imperfectly sanctified Christian is subject to two conflicting tendencies (the law in the members and the law of the Spirit)
	  Security comes from being "kept by the power of God through faith unto salvation" (Rom 8:28-30)

•   Creation and the Elements  
	  The Scriptures refer the work of creation to God (Ps 102:25, Heb 1:10)
	  The elements of creation were created out of nothing (Ps 102:25, Heb 1:10)
	  The time occupied in bringing the world and its inhabitants to their present form is not specified in Gen 1 (implied in Gen 1:1-31)

•   Geology and Creation  
	  The science of geology indicates that the earth and its inhabitants have undergone significant changes over time
	  Adjustments between the inspired record and the conclusions of geology have been proposed
	  The present duty of Christians is to seek a harmonious understanding of the two perspectives

•   The Final End of God in Creation  
	  Two distinct opinions have been entertained regarding the final end of God in creation (glory or happiness of humanity)
	  The doctrine of the Confession teaches that God's chief end is his own glory (WCF 4.1)
	  The importance of this question is great, as it affects our understanding of God's character and purposes

•   The Production of Man  
	  Different opinions have been entertained regarding the production of man (immediate creation or evolution)
	  The evidence for immediate creation includes the biblical account and the nature of human beings

•   The Propagation of the Whole Race  
	  Different opinions have been entertained regarding the fact of the propagation of the whole race from Adam (monogenesis or polygenesis)
	  The evidence for monogenesis includes the biblical account and the nature of human beings

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Chapter Four: Of the Creation and Fall of Man  
	  Discusses the unity of the human race and its descent from Adam and Eve
	  Examines the importance of this fact
	  Explores the elements of the proposition "God created man in his own image"
    The Creation of Adam  
	  Refutes false theories about Adam's creation
	  States the evidence for Adam's creation as a holy being
	  Shows that Adam was created with sufficient knowledge for his guidance
	  Discusses the special characteristic of Adam's condition as a moral agent
    The Fall of Man  
	  Examines the Pelagian doctrine about Adam's moral condition
	  Shows that this doctrine involves an absurdity
	  Proves that Adam was created positively holy
    Chapter Five: Of Providence  
	  Defines God's providence as his control over all creatures and actions
	  Explains that God's providence is the consistent execution of his eternal and immutable purpose
	  Discusses the final end of God's providence as the manifestation of his glory
    God's Relation to the World  
	  Examines three different classes of opinion about God's relation to the world:
		- Deists and Rationalists: God only touches the creation at its commencement and leaves it to its own faculties
		- Pantheists: God is the universal absolute substance, and the world is merely a mode of this substance
		- Some Christian theologians: God's power is constantly exerted in continually creating every individual thing again and again every fraction of duration
    God's Providence in Upholding and Preserving the World  
	  Discusses God's role in sustaining and preserving the things he has made
	  Explains that God's providence extends to all creatures and all their actions of every kind
	  Shows that God's providence is the consistent execution of his eternal and immutable purpose

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   View of God's Power  
	  God is the only real agent in the universe
	  He is the immediate cause of all things, including evil
	  Consciousness is a delusion, and human free agency is an illusion

•   Third View of God's Power  
	  God gave existence to all substances, material and spiritual
	  These substances possess real properties and efficiency as causes
	  They are not self-existent, but their existence is grounded in God
	  God's power is not fully revealed, but it is evident in the universe

•   God's Continued Existence  
	  Creatures depend on God for continued existence
	  This dependence is inherent in the idea of a creature
	  God's power is eternally present in every element of his creation

•   Scriptural Evidence  
	  Scripture teaches that God upholds all things by his power
	  God's government extends to all creatures and their actions
	  Scripture emphasizes God's sovereignty and providence

•   Government of God's Creatures  
	  God governs the actions of his creatures
	  This government is evident in the harmony and order of the universe
	  God's design is evident in human history and the natural world

•   Intelligence in the Universe  
	  The universe exhibits intelligence and design
	  This is evident in the harmony and order of the natural world
	  God's presence is evident in the universe and in human history

•   Scriptural Prophecies and Promises  
	  Scripture contains prophecies and promises that are fulfilled and unfulfilled
	  These prophecies and promises demonstrate God's sovereignty and providence
	  God's government extends to individuals and their conduct

•   God's Providence  
	  God's providence is evident in the universe and in human history
	  God's government extends to all creatures and their actions
	  God's sovereignty is demonstrated in the fulfillment of prophecies and promises

•   Human Dependence on God  
	  Humans sense a dependence on God for continued existence and blessedness
	  This dependence is inherent in the human religious consciousness
	  God's power and sovereignty are evident in human experience

•   Conclusion  
	  God's power and sovereignty are evident in the universe and in human history
	  God's government extends to all creatures and their actions
	  God's sovereignty is demonstrated in the fulfillment of prophecies and promises.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. God's Providential Control  

  God exercises providential control over all his creatures and their actions.
	  This control is exerted over the physical world (e.g. Job 37:6-13, Ps. 104:14)
	  This control is exerted over individual events in the natural world (e.g. Matt. 10:29)
	  This control is exerted over fortuitous events (e.g. Job 5:6, Prov. 16:33)

  2. Scope of God's Control  

  God's control extends to:
	  The brute creation (e.g. Ps. 104:21-27, 147:9)
	  The general affairs of men (e.g. Job 12:23, Isa. 10:12-15)
	  The circumstances of individuals (e.g. 1 Sam. 2:6-8, Prov. 16:9)
	  The free actions of men (e.g. Ex. 12:36, Ps. 33:14,15)
	  The sinful actions of men (e.g. 2 Sam. 16:10, Ps. 76:10)

  3. God's Purpose  

  God's providential control is the consistent execution of his eternal and immutable purpose.
	  This purpose is infinitely wise, righteous, and benevolent (e.g. Eph. 1:11)
	  This purpose is sovereign and absolute (e.g. Rom. 9:17, 11:36)

  4. Efficacy of God's Purpose  

  God's purpose is certainly efficacious and always accomplished.
	  This is evident from his infinite wisdom and power (e.g. Job 23:13, Ps. 33:11)
	  This is explicitly declared in Scripture (e.g. Eph. 1:11, Isa. 28:29)

  5. Means and Ends  

  God's providential control is not limited to means, but can also work without, above, and against them.
	  God can work through means (e.g. Acts 27:31,44, Isa. 55:10,11)
	  God can work without means (e.g. Hos. 1:7, Matt. 4:4)
	  God can work above and against means (e.g. Rom. 4:19-21, 2 Kings 6:6)

  6. Consistency of God's Control  

  God's control is consistent with the nature of the creature and his mode of action.
	  God executes his purpose in a self-consistent and unchangeable plan (e.g. Job 23:13, Ps. 33:11)

  7. God's Power  

  God possesses the power to effect his purpose directly and through his own energy.
	  This power is evident from his infinite wisdom and power (e.g. Job 23:13, Ps. 33:11)
	  This power is explicitly declared in Scripture (e.g. Eph. 1:11, Isa. 28:29)

  8. God's Means  

  God makes use of means in his providential control.
	  God uses means to accomplish his purpose (e.g. Acts 27:31,44, Isa. 55:10,11)
	  God can work without means (e.g. Hos. 1:7, Matt. 4:4)

  9. God's Sovereignty  

  God's providential control is sovereign and absolute.
	  This sovereignty is evident from his infinite wisdom and power (e.g. Job 23:13, Ps. 33:11)
	  This sovereignty is explicitly declared in Scripture (e.g. Rom. 9:17, 11:36)

  10. God's Glory  

  God's chief end in all his providential dispensations is the manifestation of his own glory.
	  This is evident from his infinite wisdom and power (e.g. Job 23:13, Ps. 33:11)
	  This is explicitly declared in Scripture (e.g. Rom. 9:17, 11:36)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Mode of Action  
	  Must be consistent with the natures and modes of action of the creatures he created
	  Is evident in the uniform experience and observation of the natural world

•   Free Agency and Providence  
	  Human beings act freely according to the law of their constitution
	  Even prophets and apostles wrote under divine influence, but still exercised their faculties freely
	  Every agent in the material and brute creation acts according to the uniform law of its nature

•   Adjustments and Combinations  
	  The material world, human lives, and history show evidence of adjustments and combinations of elements and agents to effect purpose
	  This is analogous to human control of natural agents, but infinitely more perfect

•   God's Control  
	  God's perfection means that every event and general result is determined by his intention
	  The human soul acts according to a law of its own, excluding necessity, but not excluding certainty

•   God's Means of Action  
	  God ordinarily effects his purposes through means, or second causes subject to his control
	  This is evident from the fact that he gave them their being and properties, and adjusted their relations in creation

•   Universal Experience and Observation  
	  Universal experience and observation teach us that God accomplishes his purposes through the agency of second causes
	  This is seen in ordinary providence, supernatural economy of grace, and the government of the finished world

•   System of Nature  
	  A system involving an established order of nature is necessary for communication between the Creator and intelligent creation
	  This system allows for the exercise of divine attributes and the intellectual and moral education of the creation

•   God's Power  
	  God possesses the power of effecting his ends immediately, without the intervention of second causes
	  He exercises this power at his sovereign pleasure

•   God's Sovereignty  
	  God's sovereignty means that he can act immediately, without the intervention of second causes
	  This power is exercised at his pleasure, and is a demonstration of his divine authority

•   God's Attributes  
	  God's attributes of wisdom, righteousness, and goodness are exercised and manifested through his system of nature and providence
	  This system allows for the intellectual and moral education of the creation, and demonstrates God's character and will.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Power and Nature  
	  God created all second causes and endowed them with properties.
	  God upholds them in being to be instruments of his will.
	  God's power is derived from him, and he can do directly what he does through them.

•   God's Power in Nature  
	  God's power works in ordinary processes of nature.
	  God's will is expressed in natural law.
	  However, God's power and will are not exhausted in those processes.

•   God's Transcendent Power  
	  God remains infinitely greater than his works.
	  God uses the system of second causes as his instrument.
	  God manifests his transcendent prerogatives and powers through free exercises of his energies.

•   Miracles and Direct Action  
	  Occasional direct exercises of God's power are necessary.
	  Miracles are necessary for the education of finite spirits, such as humans.
	  Miracles are not inconsistent with God's infinite perfections.

•   Miracles as Means to an End  
	  Miracles are part of a plan and serve definite ends.
	  Miracles are fixed in their occurrence by God's eternal plan.
	  Miracles are not a violation of the order of nature, but an interpolation of a new power.

•   The Order of Nature and Miracles  
	  The order of nature is an instrument of the divine will.
	  Miracles are the occasional and eternally pre-calculated interpolation of a new power.
	  The order of nature and miracles are intimately correlated elements of one comprehensive system.

•   God's Providence  
	  God's providence extends to the first fall and all other sins of angels and men.
	  God's providence is not a bare permission, but a wise and powerful bounding.
	  God's providence is a manifold dispensation to his own holy ends.

•   God's Holiness and Righteousness  
	  God is most holy and righteous.
	  God is not the author or approver of sin.
	  Sinfulness proceeds only from the creature, not from God.

•   The Nature of Sin  
	  The text does not attempt to explain the nature of sin.
	  The text only discusses God's providence and control over sin.

•   Scriptural References  
	  The text references various biblical passages to support its claims.
	  The references include Romans, 2 Samuel, 1 Chronicles, 1 Kings, Acts, and others.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. God's Providence and Sin  

  God permits sinful acts, but directs and controls them to achieve his purposes.
	  Sinful actions occur by God's permission and according to his purpose (e.g. Gen. 14:4-5, Acts 2:23).
	  God restrains and controls men in their sins (e.g. Ps. 76:10, 2 Kings 19:28).

  2. God's Role in Sin  

  God is not the author or approver of sin.
	  God forbids sin through positive law, threatens punishment, and restrains sin (e.g. Ex. 7:13, Acts 3:13).
	  God overrules sin against its own nature to achieve good (e.g. Gen. 50:20).

  3. God's Chastisement of His Children  

  God sometimes leaves his children to temptations and corruption to chastise them.
	  This is to humble them and make them more dependent on God (e.g. 2 Cor. 12:7-9, Ps. 73).

  4. God's Treatment of Wicked Men  

  God withholds his grace from wicked men, blinds them, and hardens them.
	  God exposes them to objects that lead to sin and gives them over to their lusts (e.g. Rom. 1:24, 2 Thess. 2:10-12).
	  Wicked men harden themselves even under means that God uses to soften others (e.g. Ex. 7:3, 2 Cor. 2:15-16).

  5. God's Providence and the Church  

  God's providence takes special care of his Church.
	  God disposes all things for the good of the Church (e.g. 1 Tim. 4:10, Rom. 8:28).

  6. The Systems of God's Providence  

  God's providence forms a connected system that comprehends all created things and their actions.
	  This system includes several subordinate systems, such as:
		- The providence of God over the material universe.
		- The general moral government of God over the intelligent universe.
		- The moral government of God over the human family in general.
		- The special gracious dispensation of God's providence toward his Church.

  7. The Relation of Subordination  

  The several systems of providence are related as means to ends in the wider system.
	  Each system serves a specific purpose and is subordinate to the overall system of God's providence.

  8. God's Control over All Creatures  

  God's providence reaches to all creatures, including the material universe.
	  God's providence is not limited to human beings, but extends to all created things.

  9. God's Sovereignty over All Things  

  God's providence is a sovereign and all-encompassing system.
	  God's sovereignty extends to all things, including the actions of wicked men and the fate of the Church.

  10. The Consistency of God's Providence  

  God's providence is a consistent and connected system.
	  The various systems of providence work together to achieve God's eternal and immutable purpose.

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Government of the Universe  
	  The material universe is subordinate to God's moral government
	  God's government is for the benefit of his intelligent creatures
	  The physical universe was created for the residence, instruction, and development of humans

•   Redemption and Human History  
	  The history of redemption is the key to understanding human history
	  The Church is the ultimate goal of human history and development
	  God's providence works together for good to those called according to his purpose (Rom. 8:28)

•   God's Moral Government  
	  Includes external and internal spiritual providence
	  External providence orders outward circumstances of individuals
	  Internal spiritual providence influences hearts and consciences through the Spirit

•   Common and Efficacious Grace  
	  Common grace extends to all men, restraining corruption and impressing hearts with truth
	  Efficacious grace extends only to the elect, working in them at God's sovereign pleasure

•   Discipline and Punishment  
	  God withdraws spiritual influences from his children for discipline and mortification of sin
	  God withdraws restraints of the Spirit from ungodly men as punishment for sin

•   Consequences of Withdrawal  
	  Truths of the gospel become a savor of death to those left to themselves
	  Ungodly men are left to the influence of temptations and the power of Satan

•   God's Sovereignty  
	  God executes his decrees in the universe
	  God's providential control reaches to the physical creation, general affairs of men, and individual circumstances

•   Providence and Free Will  
	  God's providential control does not negate human free will
	  God's sovereignty is not in conflict with human responsibility

•   Eternal Purpose  
	  God's providential government is the execution of his eternal purpose
	  God's plan is to perfect the Church and adorn her for her Husband

•   Scriptural Evidence  
	  Scripture teaches God's providential control over the universe, human affairs, and individual circumstances
	  Scripture affirms God's sovereignty and eternal purpose in all things

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Providence  
	  The chief end of God in providence is the manifestation of his own glory.
	  God's providence is always certainly efficacious.
    God's Control  
	  The manner in which God controls any creature must be consistent with its nature.
	  God's control is consistent with the nature of creation.
	  Universal experience and observation confirm God's control.
    Miracles  
	  Miracles occur according to law.
	  Miracles are not derogatory to the divine perfections.
    Sin and Providence  
	  God controls sinful actions according to his purpose.
	  God restrains and overrules sinful actions for good.
	  Divine providence cannot be charged with causing or approving sin.
    The Fall of Man  
	  Our first parents sinned by eating the forbidden fruit.
	  God permitted this sin according to his wise and holy counsel.
	  The fall of man was part of God's plan to order it to his own glory.
    God's Method of Dealing with Creatures  
	  God introduces newly-created subjects of moral government into a state of probation.
	  The permanent character and destiny of creatures depend on their own action.
	  Creatures are created holy, yet capable of falling.
    The Test of Adam and Eve  
	  The test of Adam and Eve was their abstaining from eating the fruit of a single tree.
	  This test was admirably adapted to be a test of their implicit allegiance to God.
    The Origin of Sin  
	  The origin of sin in this world is not fully explained.
	  The Scriptures make certain points clear about the relation of God to the sins of men.
    God's Purpose in Permitting Sin  
	  God permitted sin according to his wise and holy counsel.
	  God's purpose in permitting sin is to order it to his own glory.
    The Nature of Sin  
	  Sin is a dreadful and twofold sin: unbelief and disobedience.
	  Sin is a manifestation of unbelief and disobedience to God's will.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The origin of sin in Adam's soul  
	  Men ask how sinful desires could originate in Adam's holy soul
	  Adam's will was holy, but his actions were sinful
	  The character of his desires and affections was determined by the moral state of his soul

•   The relationship between the soul and desires  
	  A good man brings forth good things from a good heart
	  An evil man brings forth evil things from an evil heart
	  The tree (soul) determines the fruit (actions)

•   The difficulty of understanding sin  
	  We can't explain the origin of sin in Adam's soul
	  We have not experienced the same conditions of free agency as Adam
	  We are under the bondage of corruption, except for supernatural grace

•   The role of grace in holy volitions  
	  A holy volition must spring from a positively holy affection or disposition
	  We cannot exercise holy volitions without divine grace
	  Adam was in a state of probation, holy yet fallible

•   The origin of sin in defect  
	  Sin can originate in defect, not in positive alienation
	  Sin can arise from the temporary ascendancy of natural appetites or constitutional tendencies
	  The external influences and motives that prompted Adam's sin did not imply sin in him

•   The role of Satan in the origin of sin  
	  Satan's fall is not well understood
	  Satan's temptation was a key factor in Adam's sin
	  The true origin of sin is to be referred to Satan

•   The mystery of God's permission  
	  God's permission of sin is a mystery
	  God foreknew that Adam would sin, but did not cause or approve of it
	  God created Adam holy and capable of obedience, but left him to his trial

•   God's sovereignty and decree  
	  God sovereignly decreed not to intervene to prevent Adam's sin
	  God's decree made Adam's sin certainly future
	  God's sovereignty is not to be confused with approval of sin

•   God's character and desire  
	  God abhors sin and desires the excellence and happiness of his creatures
	  God created Adam holy and fully capable of obedience
	  God presented motives that should have deterred Adam from sin

•   The relationship between God's permission and Adam's sin  
	  God's permission of sin is not the same as causing or approving of it
	  God's sovereignty and decree are distinct from his character and desire

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  The Fall of Man  

    The Sin of Adam  
	  God permitted the sin of Adam to occur for His own glory.
	  Adam's sin was a result of his own free will, but God overruled it for good.
    Consequences of Sin  
	  Adam fell from his original righteousness and communion with God.
	  He became dead in sin and wholly defiled in all his faculties and parts.
	  His corruption extended to all aspects of his being, including his will, conscience, understanding, passions, affections, and appetites.

  The Nature of Sin  

    Spiritual Death  
	  Adam's sin resulted in the withdrawal of the principle of spiritual life.
	  He lost his original righteousness and became a rebel against God.
    Corruption of Nature  
	  Adam's entire nature became depraved, leading to an endless series of sins.
	  His will was at war with his conscience, and his understanding was darkened.

  The Imputation of Sin  

    Guilt of Sin  
	  The guilt of Adam's sin was imputed to all his posterity.
	  This guilt was conveyed to all his descendants through ordinary generations.
    Original Corruption  
	  From Adam's original corruption, all actual transgressions proceed.
	  We are utterly indisposed, disabled, and made opposite to all good.

  The Federal Headship of Adam  

    Adam as Federal Head  
	  Adam was both the natural and federal head of all mankind, except for Christ.
	  God constituted Adam as the moral representative of all his natural descendants.
    Covenant with Adam  
	  The covenant made with Adam will be considered in its appropriate place.
	  The point of attention is that Adam was treated as the moral representative of all his natural descendants.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Covenant with Adam  
	  The covenant was a promise of life to Adam and his posterity upon condition of perfect obedience.
	  The covenant was made with Adam as a public person, not just for himself, but for his descendants.

•   The Nature of the Covenant  
	  The covenant was a trial for Adam, with his confirmed and permanent moral character and destiny depending on his actions.
	  The trial was not just for Adam, but for his entire race, which would be propagated through him.

•   God's Method of Dealing with Moral Agents  
	  God creates moral agents holy, yet capable of falling, and then puts them on trial.
	  The trial is designed to determine their confirmed and permanent moral character and destiny.

•   The Three Possible Plans for Mankind  
	  Plan 1: Confirm the whole race in holiness and happiness without probation.
	  Plan 2: Each individual must stand their own probation from infancy to adulthood.
	  Plan 3: The whole race must have their trial in their natural head and root, Adam.

•   Why God Chose Plan 3  
	  It is more rational, righteous, and merciful than Plan 2.
	  Adam was most advantageously constituted and circumstanced to stand the trial safely.

•   The Benefits and Risks of Adam's Trial  
	  If Adam had maintained his integrity, all his descendants would have been born into an indefeasible inheritance of glory.
	  The covenant headship of Adam is part of a glorious constitution that culminates in the covenant headship of Christ.

•   The Nature of Adam's Headship  
	  Adam was a public person, and the covenant was made with him not just for himself, but for his posterity.
	  Everything God commanded, promised, or threatened Adam related to his descendants as much as to himself personally.

•   The Consequences of Adam's Sin  
	  The penalty denounced and executed upon Adam has been executed upon all his descendants from birth upward.
	  All are born spiritually dead, "by nature children of wrath."

•   The Imputation of Guilt  
	  The guilt of Adam's sin is imputed to all his descendants, and the penalty executed upon them at their birth.
	  By "guilt" is meant the just liability to the punishment that Adam's sin deserved.

•   The Meaning of Imputation  
	  To impute means to lay to the charge or credit of anyone as a ground of judicial punishment or justification.
	  This is the sense in which the phrase "to impute sin" or "righteousness" is used in the Bible.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The concept of imputed sin  
	  The guilt of Adam's sin is imputed to his descendants
	  This means that the penalty of Adam's sin is judicially laid to the charge of each of his natural descendants
    The reason for imputed sin  
	  Adam acted as a public person, representing his posterity in his trial for character and destiny
	  The covenant was made with Adam "not for himself only, but for his posterity"
    The consequences of imputed sin  
	  The penalty of Adam's sin is the judicial withdrawment of the life-giving influences of the Holy Ghost
	  This leads to moral and physical death
    The nature of original sin  
	  Original sin is the corruption of Adam's whole nature, which is passed down to his descendants
	  This corruption includes the guilt of Adam's first sin, the want of original righteousness, and the corruption of his whole nature
    The role of Adam's first sin  
	  Adam's first sin is the only sin that is imputed to his descendants
	  This sin is the reason for the penalty of death and the loss of original righteousness
    The relationship between Adam's sin and our own  
	  We "sinned in him in his first transgression" because he represented us in his trial
	  We are responsible for Adam's sin only in his trial for character and destiny
    The concept of original righteousness  
	  Original righteousness is the state of righteousness that Adam had before his fall
	  This righteousness is lost as a result of Adam's sin
    The concept of original sin  
	  Original sin is the corruption of Adam's whole nature, which is passed down to his descendants
	  This corruption includes the guilt of Adam's first sin, the want of original righteousness, and the corruption of his whole nature
    The relationship between Adam's sin and our own guilt  
	  Our guilt is not imputed to us because of our own personal sins, but because of Adam's sin
	  We are born with a sinful nature and are morally and spiritually dead as a result of Adam's sin
    The concept of justification by faith  
	  The righteousness of Christ is imputed to us without works, before and as the necessary condition of good dispositions or actions upon our part
	  Similarly, the guilt of Adam's sin is imputed to his posterity without personal works of their own, before and as the cause of their loss of original righteousness and acquisition of original sin.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Human Nature is Corrupt  
	  Humans are born with a morally corrupt nature (Ps. 51:5; Eph. 2:3; John 3:6)
	  This corruption makes humans inclined to all evil and opposed to all good (Rom. 3:9-19)
    Consequences of Corruption  
	  Humans sin from the commencement of moral agency (Rom. 1:18-32; 3:1-19)
	  Sinful actions proceed from sinful hearts or dispositions (Matt. 15:19; Luke 6:43-45)
    Nature of Corruption  
	  Corruption involves moral and spiritual blindness of mind, as well as hardness of heart and vile affections (1 Cor. 2:14; Eph. 4:18)
	  Corruption is so radical and inveterate that humans are disabled with respect to right moral action (Rom. 7:14-25)
    Universal Experience  
	  All humans, regardless of age, nation, or circumstance, sin as soon as they become capable of moral action (Rom. 3:9-19)
	  This universal fact must have a universal cause, which is the common depravity of human nature
    Scriptural Teachings  
	  Scripture declares that all humans are sinners (Rom. 1:18-32; 3:1-19)
	  Scripture teaches that sinful actions proceed from sinful hearts or dispositions (Matt. 15:19; Luke 6:43-45)
    Nature of Sin  
	  Sin is a moral corruption that involves guilt and liability to punishment (Rom. 2:15; 3:9-19)
	  Sin brings guilt upon the sinner, making them subject to the wrath of God and the curse of the law (Eph. 2:3; Gal. 3:10)
    Punishments of Sin  
	  Sin brings spiritual, temporal, and eternal miseries (Eph. 4:18; Rom. 8:20; Lam. 3:39)
	  Sin makes humans subject to death and all its consequences (Rom. 6:23)
    Regeneration and Corruption  
	  Corruption remains in the regenerate as long as they live (1 John 1:8, 10; Rom. 7:14-25)
	  Corruption is gradually brought into subjection and mortified by the work of the Holy Spirit in sanctification (Rom. 7:5, 7, 8, 25; Gal. 5:17)
    Guilt and Liability  
	  All sin, both original and actual, brings guilt upon the sinner (Rom. 2:15; 3:9, 19)
	  Guilt makes humans liable to punishment, including spiritual, temporal, and eternal miseries (Eph. 4:18; Rom. 8:20; Lam. 3:39)
    Sanctification and Mortification  
	  The work of the Holy Spirit in sanctification gradually brings corruption into subjection and mortifies it (Rom. 7:5, 7, 8, 25; Gal. 5:17)
	  Sanctification is necessary for the mortification of corruption and the restoration of humans to a right relationship with God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Definition of Sin  
	  Sin is any want of conformity unto or transgression of God's law (Larger Catechism, q. 24; Shorter Catechism, q. 14)
	  Sin is any discrepancy of the creature or his acts with God's law (1 John 3:4)

•   Original Sin  
	  Original sin is the nature's corrupt tendencies and affections of the soul
	  It is a violation of God's law as actual transgression
	  It is called sin in Scripture (Rom. 6:12-17; 7:5-17; Gal. 5:17,24; Eph. 4:18,19)

•   Condemnation of Original Sin  
	  God condemns men for their corrupt natural dispositions and hardness of heart (Mark 16:14; Eph. 2:3)
	  The great burden of pollution and guilt is felt to consist in what we are, not what we have done (Rom. 7:24; Ps. 51:5,6)

•   Curse of the Law  
	  Original sin, as well as actual transgressions, deserves the curse of the law
	  Everything condemned by the law is under its curse (2 Thess. 1:9)
	  Men are "by nature children of wrath" (Eph. 2:3)

•   Consequences of Sin  
	  The sinner is made subject to death, including temporal, spiritual, and eternal miseries
	  Temporal miseries include the curse of God upon creatures and all other evils that befall us (Larger Catechism, q. 28)

•   Temporal Miseries  
	  Temporal miseries include death itself and all other evils that befall us in our bodies, names, estates, relations, and employments
	  These miseries apply only to the still unbelieving, unjustified sinner

•   Spiritual Miseries  
	  Spiritual miseries include blindness of mind, a reprobate sense, strong delusions, hardness of heart, horror of conscience, and vile affections (Eph. 4:18; Rom. 1:28; 2 Thess. 2:11)

•   Eternal Miseries  
	  Eternal miseries include everlasting separation from the comfortable presence of God and most grievous torments (Matt. 25:41; Rev. 20:15)

•   Redemption from Sin  
	  Even infants are redeemed by Christ, who redeemed them from the curse of the law (Gal. 3:13)
	  Christ's redemption is from the curse of the law, not from the consequences of sin

•   Justification and Sanctification  
	  The consequences of sin will be treated under the heads of Justification and Sanctification (Confession of Faith, ch. 11 and 13)

Here are the 57 questions in 10 bullets with sub-bullets:

    The Nature of Sin and God's Plan  
	  1. What is the first proposition taught in the first section?
	  2. What is the second proposition there taught?
	  3. What is the third?
	  4. What is the fourth?
	  5. What is the fifth?
    The Origin of Sin  
	  6. What appears to be God's general plan of dealing with all new-created moral agents?
	  7. With what two orders of beings has he so dealt?
	  8. What was made the "test" in the case of man? and why was it admirably fitted for that purpose?
	  9. What appears to have been the nature of the sin committed by our first parents?
	  10. What is the first element of mystery involved in the "origin of sin"?
    The Nature of Sin and the Human Condition  
	  11. Why is it difficult to conceive how a holy being can begin to sin?
	  12. In what respects did Adam's state as a moral agent differ from ours?
	  13. Why cannot a sinful agent originate a holy volition?
	  14. Is sin in its origin a positive disposition or a defect?
	  15. What appear to have been the motives influencing our first parents?
    The Imputation of Sin  
	  16. To whose action is the true origin of sin to be referred?
	  17. What is the second element of mystery in the origin of sin?
	  18. Prove that Adam's sin was permissively embraced in the divine decrees.
	  19. Prove that God did neither cause nor approve it.
	  20. Prove that God purposed to order it for his own glory.

	  21. What is the first proposition taught in the second section?
	  22. What is the second proposition there taught?
	  23. What is the third?
	  24. What is the fourth?
	  25. Upon what does the human soul depend for spiritual life?
    The Consequences of Sin  
	  26. Show that the life-sustaining influences of the Holy Spirit were immediately withdrawn in punishment of sin.
	  27. What was the immediate consequence of that withdrawal?
	  28. To what extent was the moral and spiritual character of our first parents affected?
