	  29. What is the first proposition taught in the third and fourth sections?
	  30. What is the second proposition there taught?
	  31. What is the third?
	  32. What is the fourth?
	  33. What is the fifth?
    The Covenant of Works and the Covenant of Grace  
	  34. In what sections and in what words do our Standards explicitly teach that in the covenant of works Adam represented all his descendants?
	  35. What three plans were possible with regard to the moral probation of the individual member of the human family?
	  36. Show why the plan of giving us our probation in Adam's was both wise and benevolent.
	  37. Prove the fact that Adam was our federal representative.
    The Nature and Extent of Human Depravity  
	  38. What is the precise sense in which our Standards use the term "guilt"?
	  39. In what sense do the use the term "to impute"?
	  40. In what sections and in what words do our Standards affirm that the guilt of Adam's first sin is charged to the account of his children?
    Temporal and Spiritual Miseries  
	  41. What reason do they assign for this imputation of his sin to us?
	  42. Prove from the Scriptures that Adam's sin is so imputed.
	  43. Why is Adam's first sin alone imputed?
	  44. How is that sin punished in us?
	  45. What is the necessary effect of that punishment?
    The Moral State of Man  
	  46. What do these sections teach as to the moral state of man by nature?
	  47. What are the several points involved in their teaching?
	  48. Prove that the doctrine here taught agrees with the universal experience of men.
    The Nature, Extent, and Time of Commencement of Human Depravity  
	  49. State and prove the several points taught in Scripture as to the nature, extent, and time of commencement of human depravity.
	  50. What subjects are treated of in the fifth and sixth sections?
	  51. What is taught as to the continuance and character of corruption in the regenerate?
    The Innate and Permanent Tendency to Sin  
	  52. Prove that the innate and permanent tendency of the soul to sin is as truly a violation of God's law as actual transgression.
	  53. Prove that this "tendency to sin" and actual transgression are alike worthy of punishment.
    Temporal and Eternal Miseries  
	  54. What temporal miseries are inflicted because of sin?
	  55. What relation do temporal afflictions sustain to the justified believer?
	  56. What spiritual miseries are inflicted because of sin?
	  57. What eternal miseries are inflicted on the same account?

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Duty of a Creature to its Creator  
	  The duty is inalienable and springs from the absolute obligation to obey the Creator's will.
	  The duty is also based on the relation of dependence and obligation involved in being created.
    The Nature of Creation  
	  Creation brings the creature under obligation to the Creator, but not the other way around.
	  The Creator's act of creation does not endow the creature with a claim for more grace.
    The Concept of Covenant  
	  A covenant is a conditional promise made by God to the creature.
	  The first covenant made with man was a covenant of works, promising life to Adam and his posterity upon condition of perfect obedience.
    The Elements of a Covenant  
	  A covenant has four essential elements: parties, promise, conditions, and penalty.
	  The parties involved in a covenant are God and the creature.
    The Promise of God  
	  The promise of God is a gift of his personal love and life-giving society.
	  This promise is offered to the creature upon certain conditions.
    The Conditions of the Covenant  
	  The conditions of the covenant are specified by God and must be met by the creature.
	  In the case of Adam, the condition was perfect and personal obedience.
    The Penalty of the Covenant  
	  The penalty of the covenant is specified by God and is incurred by the creature if the conditions are not met.
	  In the case of Adam, the penalty was death.
    The Sovereignty of God  
	  God's actions are sovereign and not bound by the creature's will.
	  God's condescension in making a covenant with the creature is a matter of infinite grace.
    The Analogy of Covenants  
	  The Bible uses the term "covenant" to describe God's transactions with Noah and Abraham.
	  This suggests that the transaction with Adam in the garden was also a covenant.
    The Importance of Obedience  
	  Obedience to God's will is essential for the creature to enjoy the benefits of the covenant.
	  The creature's failure to meet the conditions of the covenant incurs the penalty.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The First Covenant  
	  Involved God and Adam, representing the human race
	  Adam's actions had equal merit or demerit for his descendants

•   Promise and Condition of the Covenant  
	  The promise was life
	  The condition was perfect obedience

•   Names of the Covenant  
	  "Covenant of Works" (due to its condition of perfect obedience)
	  "Covenant of Life" (due to the promise of life)
	  "Legal Covenant" (due to its demand for literal fulfillment of the moral law)

•   Nature of the Covenant  
	  Was a covenant of grace, promising life as a freely-granted reward
	  Was a covenant of works and law, demanding perfect obedience

•   The Promise of Life  
	  Proved from the nature of the penalty (disobedience linked to death)
	  Taught explicitly in Scripture (Rom. 10:5, Matt. 19:16-17, etc.)

•   The Nature of the Life Promised  
	  Not mere continuance of existence
	  Exclusion from the communion of God
	  Consisted in divine fellowship and happiness

•   The Condition of the Covenant  
	  Perfect obedience
	  Proved from the divine law's demand for absolute obedience
	  Illustrated by the command not to eat the forbidden fruit

•   The Penalty of the Covenant  
	  Death
	  Distinctly stated in Scripture (Gen. 3:17)
	  Denoted a state of existence, physical and moral

•   The Nature of Death  
	  Not the cessation of existence or dissolution of the soul-body union
	  Took effect in Adam and his descendants before physical death
	  Described in Scripture as a state of moral and spiritual death

•   Scriptural Evidence  
	  Paul's statement in Rom. 10:5
	  Passages in Matthew, Galatians, Leviticus, Nehemiah, and Revelation

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Nature of Death  
	  Death is a condition of increasing sin and misery
	  It involves the entire person, soul and body
	  It continues as long as the cause continues

•   The Covenant of Works  
	  The original covenant between God and Adam demanded perfect obedience
	  Adam's failure to obey resulted in the penalty of death
	  The old constitution is left without modification, leaving humanity lost

•   The Need for a New Covenant  
	  A new and gracious intervention by God is necessary to save humanity
	  A new covenant must be introduced with definite conditions
	  The new covenant must be different from the old one

•   The Arminian View  
	  Adam's loss of the promise and penalty of the covenant is made up for by Christ's death
	  The new covenant offers eternal life to all men on the condition of faith and evangelical obedience
	  Faith and obedience secure eternal life in the same way as perfect obedience did in the old covenant

•   The Inconsistency of the Arminian View  
	  The Arminian view is inconsistent with the nature of the gospel
	  Faith is not a work that Christ accepts as the ground of salvation
	  Faith is the hand by which we clasp the person and work of our Redeemer

•   The Calvinist View  
	  God determined to save the elect out of the mass of the race fallen in Adam
	  Christ became incarnate in our nature and entered into a covenant with him and his seed
	  The Mediator assumes the broken conditions of the old covenant on behalf of his elect seed

•   The Calvinist View of the Covenant  
	  The Mediator assumes the broken conditions of the old covenant precisely as Adam left them
	  Adam's failure to obey resulted in the forfeiture of life
	  The Mediator's assumption of the covenant conditions is necessary for the salvation of the elect

•   The Role of Faith in the Calvinist View  
	  Faith is not a work that Christ accepts as the ground of salvation
	  Faith is the hand by which we clasp the person and work of our Redeemer
	  Faith is the means by which we are made sharers in Christ's righteousness and beneficiaries of his grace

•   The Nature of the Gospel  
	  The method of salvation presented in the gospel is no compromise of principle
	  Christ fulfills the old legal covenant absolutely
	  We exercise faith or trust in Christ, and through that trust we are made sharers in his righteousness and beneficiaries of his grace

•   The Covenant of Grace  
	  The covenant of grace is frequently set forth in the Scripture as a testament
	  The testament refers to the death of Jesus Christ the testator and the everlasting inheritance
	  The covenant of grace offers life and salvation to sinners through Jesus Christ.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. The Covenant of Redemption  
  The Father and the Son had a definite understanding in eternity about the salvation of the elect
	  Who were to be saved
	  What Christ must do to save them
	  How their salvation was to be accomplished
	  The blessings and advantages involved in their salvation
	  The official rewards that would accrue to the Mediator

  2. The Promise of the Covenant  
  The promise of the covenant was all needful preparation for Christ's work
	  Preparation of Christ's body and soul for his work
	  Support in his work
  The promise also included a glorious reward for Christ
	  In his own Theanthropic person as Mediator
	  In the administration of all the precious graces and blessings of the covenant
	  In the salvation of the elect

  3. The Covenant of Grace  
  The covenant of grace is the same as the covenant of redemption
  It is administered by Christ to the elect through the gospel and the influences of the Holy Spirit
  The covenant of grace offers life to all men on the condition of faith

  4. Christ's Role as Mediator  
  Christ acts as the principal party with God in the covenant
  He offers the blessings of the covenant to all men on the condition of faith
  He promises that if men lay hold of these blessings by faith, they shall certainly enjoy them

  5. Christ's Obedience  
  Christ rendered a perfect vicarious obedience to the covenant
  This obedience was the condition upon which eternal life was originally offered

  6. The Penalty of Death  
  Christ suffered the penalty of death on behalf of all whom he represented
  This penalty was incurred by the old covenant

  7. The Administration of the Covenant  
  The covenant of grace is administered by Christ to the elect through the gospel and the influences of the Holy Spirit
  Christ offers the blessings of the covenant to all men on the condition of faith

  8. The Surety of the New Covenant  
  Christ is the surety of the new covenant
  He insures that the condition of faith and obedience shall not fail in the case of the elect

  9. The Two Covenants  
  Some Calvinist theologians have set forth the divine method of human redemption as embracing two covenants
	  The covenant of redemption
	  The covenant of grace
  However, the Standards of the church do not mention the covenant of redemption as distinct from the covenant of grace

  10. The Doctrine of the Covenant  
  The doctrine of the covenant is stated in the following propositions:
	  The Father and the Son had a definite understanding in eternity about the salvation of the elect
	  The promise of the covenant was all needful preparation for Christ's work and a glorious reward for Christ
	  The covenant of grace is the same as the covenant of redemption and is administered by Christ to the elect through the gospel and the influences of the Holy Spirit.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  The Covenant of Grace  

    Definition  
	  A covenant between God and Christ, mediated by Christ
	  Involves justification, sanctification, perseverance, and glory
    Conditions of the Covenant  
	  Christ was born of a woman, made under the law (Gal. 4:4-5)
	  Christ assumed and discharged the broken conditions of the covenant of works
		- Rendered perfect obedience (Ps. 40:8; Isa. 13:21; John 8:29; 9:4-5; Matt. 19:17)
		- Suffered the penalty of death incurred by breaking the old covenant (Isa. 53; 2 Cor. 5:21; Gal. 3:13; Eph. 5:2)
    Christ's Administration of the Covenant  
	  Christ administers the benefits of the covenant to his people
	  Uses his providence, Word, and Spirit to cause his people to receive the blessings of the covenant
	  Offers the benefits of the covenant to all men in the gospel
    The Relationship between Faith and Repentance  
	  Faith and repentance are gifts of the Son (Christ)
	  Viewed on our side, they are duties and gracious experiences
	  Viewed in connection with the covenant of grace, they are elements of the promise of the Father to the Son
    The Administration of the Covenant  
	  The covenant was differently administered in the time of the law and in the time of the gospel
	  Under the law, it was administered by promises, prophecies, sacrifices, circumcision, and other types and ordinances
	  Under the gospel, it is administered by the preaching of the Word and the administration of the sacraments of Baptism and the Lord's Supper
    The New Testament  
	  The ordinances of the covenant are the preaching of the Word and the administration of the sacraments
	  These ordinances are simpler and less outwardly glorious than those of the Old Testament, but more full, evident, and spiritually efficacious
    The Covenant of Grace in the New Testament  
	  There is only one covenant of grace, not two
	  The New Testament is the administration of the covenant of grace to all nations, both Jews and Gentiles
    The Relationship between the Covenant and Salvation  
	  Faith and repentance are indices of the commencement of salvation
	  They are conditions sine qua non of the completion of salvation
    The Analogy between the Covenant and a Testament  
	  The present administration of the covenant bears a near analogy to a testament or will executed only upon the death of the testator
	  However, Christ is an ever-living and constantly-acting Mediator, so the word "dispensation" is a more accurate translation than "testament" in most cases
    The Efficacy of the Covenant  
	  The covenant is sufficient and efficacious to instruct and build up the elect in faith in the promised Messiah
	  It is through the operation of the Spirit that the covenant is effective in the lives of believers

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. The Covenant of Grace Remains the Same  

  The covenant of grace has remained the same in all essential respects, despite changes in its mode and administration.
	  Christ was the Savior of men before his advent and saved them on the same principles then as now.
	  He was promised to Adam and Abraham as the Savior of the world.

  2. Faith was the Condition of Salvation  

  Faith was the condition of salvation under the old dispensation in the same sense it is now.
	  The Old Testament believers are set up as an example to those who are called to exercise faith under the New Testament.
	  The same gracious promises of spiritual grace and eternal blessedness were administered then as now.

  3. The Covenant was Administered through Types and Symbolic Ordinances  

  Under the old dispensation, the covenant of grace was administered chiefly by types and symbolic ordinances.
	  From Adam to Abraham, the promise to the woman (Gen. 3:15) and the institution of bloody sacrifices were significant.
	  From Abraham to Moses, the promise to Abraham (Gen. 17:7; 22:18) and the sacrament of Circumcision were important.
	  From Moses to Christ, the temple service and the Passover were significant.

  4. The Present Dispensation is Superior  

  The present dispensation of the covenant is superior to the former one.
	  It is administered visibly and immediately by Christ, a son in his own house.
	  The truth is now revealed in clear history and didactic teaching, rather than being partly hidden and partly revealed.
	  The revelation has been vastly increased and rendered more clear by the incarnation of Christ and the mission of the Holy Ghost.

  5. The Present Dispensation is Spiritual and Global  

  The present dispensation is spiritual, rather than carnal.
  It is not confined to one people, but rather embraces the whole Earth.
  It is final, as far as the present order of the world is concerned, and will give way only to the eternal administration of the covenant in the new heavens and the new earth.

  6. The Covenant was Administered through the Prophets  

  The covenant was witnessed to as the Savior from sin by all the prophets.
  The Old Testament believers are set up as an example to those who are called to exercise faith under the New Testament.

  7. The Covenant Promises Remain the Same  

  The same gracious promises of spiritual grace and eternal blessedness were administered then as now.
  The promises were made to Adam and Abraham, and were fulfilled in Christ.

  8. The Covenant was Administered through the Temple Service  

  The covenant was administered through the temple service, which was a significant development from the simple primitive rite of sacrifice.
  The temple service was a rich and symbolic system that foreshadowed the coming of Christ.

  9. The Covenant is Administered through Christ  

  The covenant is administered visibly and immediately by Christ, a son in his own house.
  Christ is the one who has fulfilled the covenant promises and has made them available to all people.

  10. The Covenant will be Fully Realized in the New Heavens and the New Earth  

  The present dispensation of the covenant will give way only to the eternal administration of the covenant in the new heavens and the new earth.
  In this new creation, all things will be gathered together in one in Christ, both which are in heaven and which are on earth.

Here are the 38 questions and sub-questions summarized in 10 bullets with sub-bullets:

    The Covenant of Works  
	  What is the first proposition taught in the first and second sections? (1)
	  What is the second proposition there taught? (2)
	  What is the third? (3)
	  What is the fourth? (4)
	  What is the fifth? (5)
    The Covenant of Grace  
	  Prove that the duty which an intelligent creature owes to its Creator is essential and inalienable. (6)
	  Prove that the enjoyment of the Creator by the creature is not a natural right, but a gracious privilege. (7)
	  What arrangement did God in the beginning make with men in this respect? (8)
	  Prove that this arrangement is properly called a covenant. (9)
	  What are the several elements of a covenant? (10)
    The Original Covenant  
	  Who were the parties to the original covenant? (11)
	  How is this covenant variously styled? (12)
	  Prove that the promise of the covenant was life. (13)
	  What was involved in the life promised? (14)
	  Prove the last answer. (15)
    The Covenant of Redemption  
	  What was the condition of the covenant? and prove it. (16)
	  What was its penalty? and prove it. (17)
	  If God purposes to save fallen men, what is certain to characterize his method of doing so? (18)
    The Calvinistic View  
	  What is the Arminian view as to the conditions upon which salvation is offered to fallen men? (19)
	  State the fatal objections to that view. (20)
	  What is the Calvinistic view of the condition of human salvation? (21)
    The Covenant of Grace and the Covenant of Works  
	  What distinction do some Calvinists make between the "covenant of redemption" and the "covenant of grace"? (22)
	  In what section and in what words is the doctrine of our Standards upon this point stated? (23)
	  What is the point chiefly set forth by LC 31, and what point is chiefly set forth by the WCF 7.3, and SC 20? (24)
    The Covenant of Grace  
	  On what points is it evident that the Father and the Son had a definite understanding? (25)
	  Prove from Scripture that there was such a covenant between the Father and the Son. (26)
	  Show from Scripture what was the promise of that covenant. (27)
	  Show from Scripture what were its conditions. (28)
    The Administration of the Covenant of Grace  
	  What relation does the covenant of grace sustain to the covenant of works? (29)
	  By whom is the covenant of grace administered? (30)
	  How does Christ administer its blessings to his people? (31)
    The New Testament Dispensation  
	  Where and why is his present administration likened to a testament? (32)
	  What is the first proposition taught in the fifth and sixth sections? (33)
	  What is the second proposition there taught? (34)
	  What is the third? (35)
    The Covenant of Grace Under All Changes  
	  Prove that the covenant of grace is essentially the same under all changes of administration. (36)
	  How was it administered under the Old Testament dispensation? (37)
	  In what respects does the new differ from and excel the old dispensation? (38)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Mediatorial Office  
	  Involves intervening between God and man
	  Christ is the ultimate Mediator, with plenipotentiary power to make peace and reconcile God and man

•   Necessary Functions of the Mediator  
	  To propitiate God's just displeasure and expiate the guilt of sin
	  To supplicate and introduce men to God's acceptance
	  To reveal truth to men about God and their relations to him
	  To persuade and enable men to receive and obey the truth
	  To direct and sustain men in their deliverance from sin and the world

•   Mediatorial Office Involves Three Functions  
	  Prophet: a spokesman sent from God to man to make known the divine will
	  Priest: one taken from among men to represent men before God and make propitiation and intercession
	  King: one who rules and directs men in their deliverance from sin and the world

•   Christ as Prophet  
	  The personal "Word of God" incarnate, known by the Father
	  The original fountain of revelation, from which all other prophets are streams
	  Reveals the whole will of God to the Church through his Spirit and Word

•   Scriptural Evidence for Christ as Prophet  
	  Explicitly called a prophet in Scripture (Deut. 18:15,18; Acts 3:22; 7:37; Heb. 1:2)
	  Executed the functions of a prophet before his incarnation (Isa. 9:6; Mal. 3:1; Job 33:23; 1 Pet. 1:11)
	  Executes the office of a prophet since his incarnation (Matt. 11:27; John 3:2; 6:68; Rev. 7:17; 21:3)

•   Christ as Priest  
	  Taken from among men to represent men before God
	  Must be chosen of God, holy and consecrated to the Lord
	  Must have a right to draw near to Jehovah and bring near (offer sacrifices and intercessions)
	  Must have an acceptable sacrifice to offer

•   Christ's Priestly Office  
	  Executes the office of a priest in his once-for-all offering of sacrifice
	  Offers himself as a sacrifice to propitiate God's just displeasure and expiate the guilt of sin

•   Christ as King  
	  Rules and directs men in their deliverance from sin and the world
	  Directs and sustains men in their deliverance from the powers of an evil world

•   Mediatorial Functions of Christ  
	  Discharged all three functions of prophet, priest, and king in his estate of humiliation and exaltation
	  Each function is essential to the mediatorial office, and gives character to every mediatorial action

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Christ's Priesthood  

    Qualifications of a Priest  
	  Christ became a man to fulfill the priestly role (Heb 2:16, 4:15)
	  He was chosen by God, like Aaron (Heb 5:5-6)
	  He was perfectly holy and had direct access to the Father (Heb 8:6)
    Typical of the Priesthood  
	  The Old Testament order of priests and sacrifices were types of Christ (Zech 6:13, Isa 53:10, Dan 9:24-25)
    Functions of a Priest  
	  Christ made propitiation for sin through sacrifice (Eph 5:2, Heb 9:26, 1 John 2:2)
	  He made intercession and continues to intercede (Rom 8:34, Heb 7:25)

  Christ's Priesthood Compared to Aaron's  

    Inadequate Representations  
	  Aaron's priesthood was inadequate due to its:
		- Limited dignity and excellence (John 1:1-4, 14)
		- Limited value of its sacrifice (Heb 10:1-14)
		- Manner of consecration (Heb 7:20-22)
		- Constant succession of priests (Heb 7:23-24)
		- Limited scope (Heb 9:11, 24)

  Christ's Priesthood Compared to Melchizedek's  

    Similarities  
	  Christ was a royal priest, like Melchizedek (Zech 6:13, Rom 8:34, Heb 8:1-2)
	  Christ had no predecessors or successors (Heb 7:17)
	  Christ was an eternal priest (Heb 7:17)

  Christ's Kingship  

    Head of the Church  
	  Christ is sovereign Head over all things to his Church (Eph 1:22, 4:15, Col 1:18, 2:19)
    Functions of a King  
	  Christ calls out a people to himself and gives them laws and discipline (Eph 1:22, 4:15)
	  Christ bestows saving grace and rewards obedience (Eph 1:22, 4:15)
	  Christ restrains and overcomes enemies (Eph 1:22, 4:15)
	  Christ takes vengeance on those who do not know God (Eph 1:22, 4:15)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Jesus is fully human  
	  Possesses all essential properties of humanity
	  Conceived by the power of the Holy Ghost in the womb of the Virgin Mary
	  Born of Mary, the Virgin Mother of God
    Jesus is fully God  
	  Two whole, perfect, and distinct natures, the Godhead and the manhood, are inseparably joined together in one person
	  This person is very God and very man, yet one Christ
    The nature of Jesus' humanity  
	  He had a true body and a reasonable soul
	  He ate, drank, slept, and increased in stature
	  He loved, sympathized, wept, and shrank from suffering as a man
    The origin of Jesus' humanity  
	  His human nature was generated out of the common life of our race
	  It was produced by the power of the Holy Ghost in the womb of the Virgin Mary
	  He is the seed of Eve and the seed of David
    The relationship between Jesus' natures  
	  The distinction of natures is not taken away by the union
	  The property of each nature is preserved and concurring in one Person and one Substance
    The Council of Chalcedon's statement on Jesus  
	  The Council of Chalcedon (A.D. 451) affirmed that Jesus is one and the same Son, Lord, and Only Begotten
	  He is to be acknowledged in two natures, inconfusedly, unchangeably, indivisibly, and inseparably
    The significance of Jesus' humanity  
	  He is the only Mediator between God and man
	  He died in agony on the cross and rose again to prove his identity
    The significance of Jesus' divinity  
	  He is very God and very man, yet one Christ
	  He is the only Begotten, God the Word, the Lord Jesus Christ
    The importance of understanding Jesus' nature  
	  It is essential to understand the constitution of the person of the Mediator as the God-man
	  This understanding is based on the Bible and the teachings of the early Church
    The implications of Jesus' nature  
	  He is the only one who can reconcile God and man
	  He is the only one who can save humanity from sin and its consequences

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Christ's Sinlessness  
	  Scripture declares Christ to be without sin (Heb. 4:15)
	  Peter and John testify to Christ's sinlessness (1 Pet. 2:22, 1 John 3:5)

•   Christ's Divine Nature  
	  Christ is the eternal Son of God (Heb. 7:26, Luke 1:35)
	  Christ's divine nature is evident from his origin and work (John 1:14, 1 John 4:9)

•   Christ's Humanity  
	  Christ took on a human soul and body (John 1:14, Gal. 4:4)
	  Christ's humanity began to exist in the womb of the Virgin (Luke 1:35)

•   Christ's Single Personality  
	  Scripture presents Christ as a single, indivisible person (1 Cor. 2:8, Acts 20:28)
	  Personal pronouns and attributes are used consistently to refer to Christ as one person

•   Christ's Divine and Human Attributes  
	  Divine and human attributes are predicated of Christ in the same context (Col. 1:13-20, Heb. 1:3)
	  Christ is referred to by titles proper to both his divine and human natures (John 3:13, 1 Cor. 2:8)

•   Christ's Eternal Personality  
	  Christ's person existed from eternity (John 8:58, Phil. 2:6-11)
	  Christ's humanity grew into his eternal personality from the instant of conception

•   Christ's Two Natures  
	  Christ has both a human and divine nature (Heb. 2:14, Phil. 2:6-11)
	  Christ's humanity is impersonal, while his divinity is personal

•   Christ's Human Nature  
	  Christ's humanity began to exist in the womb of the Virgin (Luke 1:35)
	  Christ's human nature never had a separate personal existence of its own

•   Christ's Divine Nature  
	  Christ's divinity is eternal and personal (John 8:58, Phil. 2:6-11)
	  Christ's divine nature is one with his human nature in one person

•   Christ's Single Person  
	  Christ is one single person, with both divine and human natures (Col. 1:13-20, Heb. 1:3)
	  Christ's person is the eternal Son of God, who took on a human soul and body in time.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Christ's Two Natures  
	  Remain separate and distinct
	  Divine and human natures constitute one person
	  Impossible to explain philosophically how two self-conscious intelligences can constitute one person

•   Errorist Views  
	  Some have supposed that Christ had no human soul, but a divine spirit took its place
	  Others have separated the two natures, making Christ two persons (a God and a man)
	  Others have mixed the natures, resulting in a new nature

•   Christ's Essential Properties  
	  Divinity cannot be communicated to humanity
	  Humanity cannot be made infinite, self-eternal, and absolutely perfect
	  God cannot create divinity or make humanity divine

•   Scriptural Affirmations  
	  Scriptures affirm that Christ is both God and man
	  Scriptures never affirm of either nature that which belongs to the other
	  God gave his blood for his Church, but his divinity did not die

•   Christ's Sanctification and Anointing  
	  Christ was sanctified and anointed with the Holy Spirit above measure
	  He had all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge
	  He was holy, harmless, undefiled, and full of grace and truth

•   Christ's Office as Mediator and Surety  
	  Christ was called by his Father to this office
	  He was given all power and judgment
	  He was given commandment to execute the office

•   Christ's Willingness to Undertake the Office  
	  Christ did most willingly undertake the office
	  He was made under the law and perfectly fulfilled it

•   Christ's Sufferings and Death  
	  Christ endured most grievous torments in his soul and most painful sufferings in his body
	  He was crucified, died, and was buried
	  He remained under the power of death, yet saw no corruption

•   Christ's Resurrection and Ascension  
	  Christ arose from the dead with the same body in which he suffered
	  He ascended into heaven and sits at the right hand of his Father
	  He makes intercession and shall return to judge men and angels

•   Christ's Return and Judgment  
	  Christ shall return to judge men and angels at the end of the world
	  He has all power and judgment to execute this office

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Christ's Appointment as Mediator  
	  Christ was voluntarily appointed to the office of Mediator by the Father
	  This appointment was not imposed upon him, but rather he took it upon himself

•   Effect of Hypostatical Union on Human Nature  
	  The human nature of Christ was exalted to a degree of dignity and honor beyond that of any other creature
	  It was filled with a perfection of intellectual and moral excellence beyond that of any other creature

•   Christ's Person and Properties  
	  Christ's person possessed all the properties belonging to absolute divinity
	  He also possessed an all-perfect and incomparably exalted humanity

•   Christ's Mediatorial Functions  
	  Christ discharged the functions of the mediatorial office as God-man
	  He acted in this office upon an authority derived from the Father

•   Christ's Relationship with the Father  
	  Christ was sent by the Father and acted in accordance with the Father's will
	  He was inferior to the Father in his official relations and works

•   Christ's Voluntary Assumption of Office  
	  Christ took the office of Mediator upon himself voluntarily
	  This was evident in his statement that he laid down his life of himself

•   Christ's Motivation for Self-Sacrifice  
	  Christ's motivation for self-sacrifice was a personal love for his people
	  This love was beyond human comprehension

•   Christ's Estate of Humiliation  
	  Christ's estate of humiliation consisted in his being born in a low condition
	  This was an act of infinite condescension on the part of the Godhead of Jesus

•   Purpose of Christ's Humiliation  
	  The purpose of Christ's humiliation was to reveal the fullness of the Godhead in a finite nature
	  This was a transcendent and permanent benefit to the whole intelligent creation

•   Christ's Revelation of the Infinite  
	  Christ's humiliation made it possible for the Infinite to be "seen and known"
	  This was a result of the fullness of the Godhead being contained in him bodily

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Christ's Relationship to the Law  
	  Christ's person was eternal and divine, making him the norm and Lord of the law
	  He owed nothing to the law, as it was conformed to him, not he to the law

•   Christ's Vicarious Obedience  
	  Christ assumed the broken conditions of the old covenant of works
	  He rendered vicarious obedience to secure the promised reward for his people
	  He suffered the penalty of sin to secure remission of sins for his people

•   Christ's Active and Passive Obedience  
	  Christ's fulfilling of the law's precepts purchased an everlasting inheritance for his people
	  His suffering purchased reconciliation for his people

•   Christ's Representative Role  
	  Christ was made under the law as a representative of his people
	  His obedience and suffering were vicarious, on behalf of his people

•   Christ's Suffering and Obedience  
	  Christ's earthly career was a combination of suffering and obedience
	  His suffering was vicarious endurance of the penalty of sin
	  His obedience was the discharge of the condition upon which eternal inheritance is suspended

•   The Essence of Christ's Penalty  
	  The essence of the penalty vicariously borne by Christ was the wrath of God
	  The incidents of it were the miseries of this life
	  The culmination of it was the cursed death of the cross

•   Christ's Burial and Descent into Hell  
	  Christ's soul remained temporarily divorced from his body in the unseen world of spirits
	  Some interpretations suggest that Christ's soul went to the place where souls of those who die for their own sins die the second death forever

•   Different Interpretations of Christ's Descent into Hell  
	  Lutherans teach that Christ's descent into hell was the first step in his exaltation
	  Romanists teach that Christ went to the Limbus Patrum to preach the gospel to believers under the old dispensation

•   Christ's Humiliation and Exaltation  
	  Christ's humiliation included his suffering, obedience, burial, and descent into hell
	  His exaltation included his resurrection, ascension, and glorification

•   The Significance of Christ's Humiliation  
	  Christ's humiliation was necessary to secure the promised reward and remission of sins for his people
	  His humiliation demonstrated his love and obedience to the Father

    The Resurrection of Christ  
	  Predicted in the Old Testament (Ps. 16:10, Acts 2:24-31)

	  Attested by miracles performed by the apostles (Heb. 2:4)
	  Witnessed by the Holy Ghost (Acts 5:32)

	  Pledge for the fulfillment of all prophecies and promises (Rom. 4:25)
	  Proved Christ to be the Son of God (John 2:19, 10:17)
	  Public acceptance of Christ's mediatorial work (Rom. 4:25)
	  Advocate with the Father (1 John 2:1, Rom. 8:34)
	  Secures our resurrection (John 14:19, 1 Pet. 1:3-5, 1 Cor. 15:21,22)
    The Ascension of Christ  
	  Took place 40 days after resurrection (Acts 1:3)
	  Christ ascended as Mediator, triumphing over enemies (Eph. 4:8-12)
	  Gave gifts to his friends (Eph. 4:8-12)
	  Completed his mediatorial work (John 14:2, 3, Heb. 6:20)
	  Filled the universe with manifestations of his power and glory (Eph. 4:10)
    The Session of Christ at the Right Hand of God  
	  Christ sits at the right hand of God the Father (Ps. 16:11, 110:1, Dan. 7:13,14)
	  Intercedes for and reigns over all things in behalf of his people (Rom. 8:34, Eph. 1:20,22)
	  Official exaltation to supreme glory, felicity, and dominion (Phil. 2:9-11)
	  A definite place, where his glory is revealed and authority exercised (Heb. 1:3,4)
    Christ's Intercession  
	  Pleads for those in his covenant with his own blood (Heb. 9:12,24)
	  Pleads for blessings in behalf of those in his covenant (John 17:9, Luke 22:32)
	  Intercession is always prevalent and successful (John 11:42, Ps. 21:2)
    Christ's Coming to Judge the World  
	  Will be discussed in its proper place (Chapter 33)
    The Work of Redemption  
	  Fully satisfied the justice of God's Father (34)
	  Purchased reconciliation and an everlasting inheritance (35)
    The Virtue and Efficacy of Christ's Work  
	  Communicated to the elect in all ages (successively from the beginning of the world)
	  Communicated through promises, types, and sacrifices (wherein he was revealed and signified to be the Seed)
    The Inheritance in the Kingdom of Heaven  
	  Purchased for all those whom the Father has given to him (35)
    The Significance of Christ's Work  
	  Revealed and signified to be the Seed (through promises, types, and sacrifices)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Christ's Satisfaction for Sinners  
	  Christ made satisfaction for those he represented through his obedience and sacrifice.
	  This truth is taught in Lutheran and Reformed Confessions.
    Nature of Christ's Satisfaction  
	  Christ's obedience is imputed to believers.
	  God remits sins and gives eternal salvation due to Christ's obedience.
    Christ's Fulfillment of Divine Justice  
	  Christ met the divine demand for the original covenant of works.
	  Christ met the divine demand for essential justice through his obedience unto death.
    Christ's Representative Suffering  
	  Our sins were laid upon Christ.
	  Christ suffered as the representative of sinners.
    Christ's Propitiation for Sin  
	  Christ is the propitiation (expiation) for our sins.
	  Christ gave his life as a ransom for many.
    Christ's Satisfaction as Equivalent to Eternal Sufferings  
	  Christ's sufferings were a full equivalent for the eternal sufferings of all for whom he suffered.
	  This is the doctrine of the whole Christian Church.
    Christ's Redemption and Satisfaction  
	  The offering of Christ is a perfect redemption, propitiation, and satisfaction for all sins.
	  Christ's sacrifice on the cross fully satisfied God.
    Christ's Covenantal Work  
	  Christ secured remission of sins and propitiation of divine wrath according to the terms of the everlasting covenant.
	  Christ secured an everlasting inheritance in the kingdom of glory.
    Christ's Obedience and Suffering  
	  Christ's active obedience secured the remission of the penalty.
	  Christ's sufferings secured the remission of the penalty.
    Christ's Satisfaction as a Gift of God's Grace  
	  Christ's satisfaction is a gift of God's grace, not a result of human merit.
	  God grants and imputes the perfect satisfaction, righteousness, and holiness of Christ to believers.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Christ's Death and Salvation  
	  Christ's death was not just to make salvation possible, but to actually secure it for those for whom he died.
	  He purchased an inalienable title to heaven for all those in whose stead he rendered obedience.

•   Vicarious Sufferings of Christ  
	  Christ's vicarious sufferings must have been personal and definite in their object.
	  Salvation must be applied to all those for whom it was purchased.

•   Application of Redemption  
	  Christ applies and communicates redemption to all those for whom he purchased it.
	  Those who never receive the inheritance and to whom the purchased grace is never applied are not among those for whom it was purchased.

•   Incarnation and Benefits of Christ's Death  
	  Although Christ's satisfaction was rendered after his incarnation, the full benefits of his death were applied to each of the elect in their successive generations.
	  The Holy Spirit applied these benefits through various forms of truth made known to them.

•   Christ's Mediation  
	  Christ's mediation involves the concurrent activities of both his divine and human natures.
	  Each nature contributes what is proper to itself, with the divine nature being the fountain of revelation and the human nature being the form through which his Godhead is revealed.

•   Role of Human Nature in Christ's Mediation  
	  Christ's human nature was necessary for him to be "made under the law" and to be the subject of his vicarious sufferings and obedience.
	  His human nature is the organ of his vicarious obedience and intercession as our representative Priest and Intercessor.

•   Role of Divine Nature in Christ's Mediation  
	  The supreme dignity of Christ's divine person renders his obedience supererogatory and therefore vicarious.
	  The temporary and finite sufferings of his humanity are a full equivalent in justice-satisfying efficacy for the eternal sufferings of all the elect.

•   Concurrent Activities of Both Natures  
	  The activities of Christ's divinity and humanity are constantly and inseparably connected.
	  The divine nature is the source of all divine knowledge, while the human nature is the form through which his Godhead is revealed.

•   Christ's Person as Incarnate God  
	  Christ's person as incarnate God is the focus of all revelations and the subject as well as the organ of all prophetical teaching.
	  His person is the original source of all divine knowledge and the veil through which his glory is transmitted.

•   Christ's Mediation as Prophet, Priest, and King  
	  Christ's mediation involves his activities as prophet, priest, and king, all of which involve the concurrent activities of both his divine and human natures.
	  His mediation is the means by which he applies redemption to all those for whom he purchased it.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. Christ's Mediatorial Acts  
  Christ's mediatorial acts are attributed to his entire person as the Theanthropos-God-man.
  He is to be obeyed and worshipped by angels and men.

  2. Unity of Natures in Christ  
  The unity of both natures in Christ means that what is proper to either nature belongs to the person.
  Scripture often attributes what is proper to one nature to the person denominated by the other nature.

  3. Christ's Application of Redemption  
  Christ applies redemption to all those for whom he has purchased it.
  He does this through:
	  Making intercession for them.
	  Revealing the mysteries of salvation to them through the Word.
	  Effectually persuading them to believe and obey through the Spirit.
	  Governing their hearts through the Word and Spirit.

  4. Christ's Methods of Applying Redemption  
  Christ uses four methods to apply redemption:
	  Making intercession for the persons concerned.
	  Revealing the mysteries of salvation to them in his Word.
	  Effectually operating on their hearts through the Spirit.
	  Dispensing his providence in necessary ways.

  5. Christ's Certain and Effectual Application of Redemption  
  Christ certainly and effectually applies redemption to all those for whom he has purchased it.
  This is in contrast to the idea that Christ died for all people, regardless of their election.

  6. Christ's Purpose in Dying  
  Christ died on purpose to carry out the eternal purpose of God in the election of certain individuals to eternal life.
  Christ died to save only the elect.

  7. Christ's Design in Dying  
  Christ's design in dying was not simply to make reconciliation, but to purchase an everlasting inheritance in the kingdom of heaven for the elect.

  8. Christ's Session at the Right Hand of God  
  Christ, as mediatorial King, is seated at the right hand of God.
  From this position, he applies the redemption he had effected as Priest to the proper subjects of it.

  9. Christ's Methods of Applying Redemption (continued)  
  Christ's methods of applying redemption include:
	  Making intercession for the persons concerned.
	  Revealing the mysteries of salvation to them in his Word.
	  Effectually operating on their hearts through the Spirit.
	  Dispensing his providence in necessary ways.

  10. Christ's Certain and Effectual Application of Redemption (continued)  
  Christ's certain and effectual application of redemption is a key aspect of his mediatorial work.
  This application is a result of Christ's union with the elect and his role as their Head and Surety.

Here are the 10 bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Plan and Purpose  
	  God has a single, comprehensive plan for all of creation.
	  This plan is fully executed and revealed in the event.
	  God's design is to accomplish what he does accomplish, nothing more and nothing less.
    Sufficiency of Christ's Satisfaction  
	  Christ's satisfaction is sufficient for all men who can possibly be created.
	  It is exactly adapted to the legal relations and wants of every man.
    Removal of Legal Obstacles  
	  Christ's satisfaction has removed all legal obstacles to God's saving any man he wills to save.
    Offer of Redemption  
	  Redemption is freely, authoritatively, and in good faith offered to every man to whom the gospel comes.
    Rightful Possession of Redemption  
	  Redemption is rightfully the possession of any man who accepts it.
	  It is objectively available to one hearer of the gospel as much as to another, upon the single condition of acceptance.
    Effectual Application of Redemption  
	  Redemption is effectually applied precisely to those persons to whom the Father and the Son will to apply it.
	  The Father and the Son will to apply it now precisely to those to whom they designed to apply it from eternity.
    Christ's Design and Purpose  
	  Christ died with the purpose of executing the decree of election.
	  His design in making atonement was definite, having respect to certain definite persons - the elect.
    Christ's Functions as Mediator  
	  Christ is a Mediator who effects reconciliation between God and man.
	  As Mediator, Christ must effect reconciliation and secure the salvation of those for whom he rendered satisfaction.
    Christ's Functions as Prophet  
	  Christ is a prophet who executes the functions of a Mediator.
	  As prophet, Christ reveals God's plan and purpose to man.
    Relation of Christ's Functions  
	  Christ's functions as Mediator and prophet are necessarily connected and interdependent.
