Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Mediatorial Actions  
	  Questions 78-84 ask about the mediatorial actions of Christ
	  These questions are related to the doctrine of redemption

•   Christ's Suffering  
	  Our standards teach that Christ suffered with the design of saving certain definite persons (Q 81)
	  This is taught in three places and three forms

•   Redemption  
	  Our standards teach that the redemption of Christ is sufficient, adaptable, and universally available (Q 82)
	  This redemption was originally designed for all people

•   Design of Redemption  
	  Our standards teach that the Father and Son designed redemption together (Q 83)
	  This design is certain to be applied to all for whom it was originally intended

•   Calvinism and Arminianism  
	  Mr. Calamy, a moderate Calvinist, believed in universal redemption in the sense of the Synod of Dort
	  He believed that Christ paid a price for all, but with different conditions for the elect and reprobate

•   Free Will  
	  Section I of Chapter 9 teaches that God has given man natural liberty to choose
	  This liberty is not forced or determined by absolute necessity

•   Views on Free Will  
	  There are three possible views on free will:
		- Determinism (actions caused by external circumstances)
		- Arminianism (will is self-determining, regardless of understanding and affections)
		- The view taught in this section (human soul decides based on its own instincts, ideas, and affections)

•   Proof of Free Will  
	  The universal consciousness of men proves that they have the power to determine their own actions
	  Observation of the actions of others also shows that they have the power of self-decision

•   Responsibility  
	  Man is held responsible for his actions by his own conscience and by God
	  This could not be the case if his actions were caused by circumstances

•   Critique of Arminianism  
	  The view that a man can choose without respect to his judgments or inclinations is not true
	  This view is not supported by the fact that man is held responsible for his actions.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. Human Freedom and Choice  

  The human will is free to choose, but its desires are determined by the sum-total of one's views, feelings, and tendencies at the time.
	  A person's choice is determined by their internal state, not by external factors.
	  The will is not free to act in opposition to reason and morality.

  II. The Nature of Human Action  

  Human action is determined by the character of the agent.
	  The nature of the fruit is determined by the nature of the tree from which it springs.
	  Changing the character of the action requires changing the permanent character or moral tendency of the heart.

  III. Man's State of Innocency  

  In a state of innocency, man had freedom and power to will and do good.
	  However, this freedom was mutable, meaning man could fall from it.
	  This state is described in Ecclesiastes 7:29, Genesis 1:26, 31, and Colossians 3:10.

  IV. The Fall into Sin  

  Man's fall into sin resulted in the loss of ability to will spiritual good.
	  As a natural man, one is averse to good and dead in sin.
	  This is described in Romans 5:5, 8:7-8, John 6:44, 65, and 15:5.

  V. The State of Grace  

  When God converts a sinner, he frees them from bondage under sin.
	  By God's grace, one is enabled to freely will and do spiritual good.
	  However, this freedom is not perfect, as corruption remains.

  VI. The State of Glory  

  In the state of glory, the will of man is made perfectly and immutably free to good alone.
	  This is described in Hebrews 12:23, 1 John 3:2, Jude 1:24, and Revelation 21:27.

  VII. Common Themes  

  In all four states (innocency, sin, grace, and glory), man is a free and responsible agent.
  A person's volition is determined by their desires, which are in turn determined by their internal state.
  Man's choices are always in line with their overall preferences and tendencies.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Man's Responsibility  
	  Man is responsible for his desires and actions
	  His desires are determined by the nature and permanent characteristics of his soul
	  If his desires are immoral, he and his actions are immoral

•   Definition of Free Agency  
	  Man has the power of originating action
	  He is self-moved and does not only move as he is moved upon from without
	  He always wills what he desires to will, based on his understanding at the time

•   Reason and Conscience  
	  Man is furnished with reason to distinguish between the true and false
	  He has a conscience to distinguish between the right and wrong
	  His desires are formed under the light of reason and conscience

•   Adam's Estate  
	  Adam was a free agent in his estate of innocency
	  He had holy affections and moral tendencies
	  He was capable of obedience, but liable to be seduced by external temptation

•   Man's Present Estate  
	  Man is still a free agent, but with a morally corrupted and spiritually dead soul
	  He is unable to will spiritual good without the grace of God
	  He is not able to convert himself or prepare himself for salvation

•   Loss of Ability  
	  Man has lost all ability to will spiritual good accompanying salvation
	  He is not able to convert himself or prepare himself for salvation
	  He is unable to do good works without the grace of God

•   The Fall of Man  
	  Man is conceived in sin and born children of wrath
	  He is indisposed to all saving good and propense to evil
	  He is dead in sins and the slave of sin

•   Regeneration  
	  Man is unable to return to God or correct his depraved nature without the grace of the regenerating Holy Spirit
	  He is not able to will or do spiritual good without the regenerating Holy Spirit

•   The Role of Grace  
	  Man is unable to do good works without the grace of God
	  The grace of God is necessary for man to have a good will and work with God
	  Man is unable to revive himself spiritually or communicate animal life to himself

•   The Nature of Man  
	  Man is spiritually dead and corrupt in respect to divine and spiritual things
	  He is unable to understand, believe, or will spiritual good without the regenerating Holy Spirit

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Human Nature After the Fall  
	  Humans have no spiritual strength to prepare themselves for God's grace
	  They are unable to apprehend or apply God's grace on their own
	  They are unable to change their nature or increase their power to obey God
    Definition of Liberty and Ability  
	  Liberty: the ability to exercise volition as one pleases
	  Ability: the capacity to will in opposition to one's desires or to make oneself desire something
	  Humans have liberty, but not ability in the sense of being able to change their will
    Inability of Humans  
	  Absolute inability: humans have no power to fulfill the moral law or accept Christ
	  Purely moral inability: humans have the faculties to make moral choices, but are hindered by their wrong moral state
	  Natural inability: humans are born with this inability, which is innate and universal
    Scriptural Evidence  
	  Direct declarations of Scripture: e.g. Jer. 13:23, John 6:44, 65
	  Description of human state by nature: blind, dark, and spiritually dead
	  Description of the necessity of regeneration: e.g. John 3:3, Eph. 2:10
    Regeneration and the New Birth  
	  Regeneration is a new birth, a new creation, and a giving of a new heart
	  God is the agent, and man is the subject
	  Regeneration requires the mighty power of God
    Christian Duties and the Fruits of the Spirit  
	  All Christian duties are declared to be the fruits of the Spirit
	  This implies that humans are unable to produce these fruits on their own
    Experience of True Christians  
	  True Christians experience a burden of sinful deadness and aversion to divine things
	  This burden remains immovable despite their efforts
    Universal Experience of Humanity  
	  No one has ever naturally possessed the ability to perform spiritual duties
	  This is evident from the universal experience of humanity
    The Estate of the Regenerate  
	  The regenerate are introduced to a new estate by God's grace
	  This estate is characterized by a new heart and a new birth
    The Role of God in Regeneration  
	  God is the agent of regeneration, and man is the subject
	  Regeneration requires the mighty power of God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Nature of Human Agency  
	  The regenerated Christian remains a free agent, willing to will as they desire.
	  The Holy Spirit implants a new spiritual principle in the soul, freeing the person from sin's bondage.
	  However, the old corrupt moral habit remains, causing a conflict of tendencies.
    The Estate of Glorified Men  
	  In heaven, glorified men continue as free agents, but with all corrupt tendencies extirpated.
	  They are perfectly free and immutably disposed to perfect holiness.
	  This is in contrast to Adam, who was holy but unstable, and unregenerate men, who are unholy and stable.
    The View of Human Agency  
	  The first view stated is that the will is free, but the second view is that the will is not free due to the influence of sin.
	  The true view is that the will is free, but with limitations due to the lingering effects of sin.
    Consciousness of Human Agency  
	  We are conscious of the power of contrary choice, but this consciousness is limited.
	  Consciousness does not teach that the will of man or the man himself is free when he acts.
    The Nature of Volitions  
	  Volitions derive their rational and moral character from the mental and emotional states at the time.
	  If volitions were decided irrespectively of these states, the results would be unpredictable and potentially evil.
    The State of Man's Moral Freedom  
	  Our Standards teach that man's moral freedom is limited since the fall.
	  The doctrine of our Standards is stated in terms of the conflict between good and evil tendencies.
    The Distinction between Liberty and Ability  
	  Liberty refers to the ability to choose, while ability refers to the capacity to perform.
	  Our Standards affirm the inability of man to choose good due to the influence of sin.
    The Nature of Inability  
	  This inability is said to be "absolute" because it is complete and total.
	  It is said to be "moral" because it is related to the will and the ability to choose.
    The Characteristics of Moral Freedom  
	  Our Standards teach that the believer is introduced to moral freedom through regeneration, characterized by a conflict between good and evil tendencies.
	  The glorified man is introduced to moral freedom after death, characterized by perfect holiness and stability.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  The Effectual Call of God  

    Definition  
	  The effectual call of God is the internal call of the Holy Spirit that regenerates and saves the elect.
	  It is a call that is distinct from the external call of the Word.
    Agent of the Call  
	  The sole agent of the effectual call is the Holy Ghost.
	  The Holy Ghost uses the revealed truth of the gospel as his instrument.
    Subjects of the Call  
	  The subjects of the effectual call are all the elect, and only the elect.
	  They are entirely passive with respect to the special act of the Spirit that regenerates them.
    Nature of the Call  
	  The effectual call is an exercise of the almighty and effectual power of the Holy Ghost.
	  It acts immediately upon the soul of the subject, determining them and effectually drawing them to Christ.
    Effect of the Call  
	  The effectual call works a radical and permanent change in the entire moral nature of the subject.
	  It spiritually enlightens the mind, sanctifies the affections, renews the will, and gives a new direction to action.
    Necessity of the Call  
	  The effectual call is necessary for salvation.
	  Without it, men are unable to receive the truth or do any spiritual good.
    Passivity of the Subject  
	  The subjects of the effectual call are passive with respect to the special act of the Spirit that regenerates them.
	  They are not able to resist the call, but are made willing by the grace of God.
    Freedom of the Subject  
	  The subjects of the effectual call come most freely, being made willing by the grace of God.
	  They are not coerced or forced to obey the call.
    Instrument of the Call  
	  The revealed truth of the gospel is the instrument used by the Holy Ghost in the effectual call.
	  The Word is not the sole agent of the call, but is used by the Holy Ghost to effect regeneration.
    Scriptural Support  
	  The effectual call is supported by numerous scriptures, including Acts 13:48, Romans 4:28, and Ephesians 1:5.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Regeneration is distinct from moral influence  
	  Regeneration is a direct, powerful, and efficient process
	  It is a "new birth" and a "new creation"
	  It is different from the simple truth's influence on the understanding and affections
    The Scriptures distinguish between two calls  
	  "Many are called, but few are chosen" (Matt. 22:14)
	  "Whoever is called, them he also justified" (Rom. 8:30)
    Effectual calling is essential to salvation  
	  Whatever is necessary for regeneration is necessary for salvation
	  "Except a man be born again, he cannot see the kingdom of God" (John 3:3)
    Effectual calling is applied to all the elect  
	  God has determined who shall be saved from eternity
	  God has appointed the elect unto glory and foreordained all means thereunto
    The Scriptures represent the "called" as the "elect"  
	  The "called" are the "elect" (Rom. 8:28, 30)
	  Those with Christ in heaven are "called, elect, and faithful" (Rev. 17:14)
    The calling is based on election  
	  The calling is based on God's purpose and grace (2 Tim. 1:9; 2 Thess. 2:13-14)
    The Holy Spirit is the sole agent in effectual calling  
	  The Holy Spirit uses Gospel truth as his instrument
	  The Holy Spirit regenerates the soul through a divine act
    Sinners are active in resisting the common influences of grace  
	  Sinners resist the influences of the Spirit before regeneration
	  These influences are exerted in the way of heightening the natural moral effect of the truth
    Believers co-operate with sanctifying grace after regeneration  
	  Believers co-operate with the Holy Spirit in sanctification
	  This co-operation involves a change of principle and permanent disposition
    Regeneration is a passive process  
	  The new-created soul is passive with respect to the divine act of regeneration
	  The soul is changed by the Holy Spirit, but it does not actively participate in the process

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Regeneration is a permanent moral state  
	  Arises from a holy disposition of the soul
	  Implies a new governing principle or habit of spiritual affection and action

•   Effectual calling is the act of the Holy Spirit  
	  Causes the soul to become regenerate
	  Implies a new birth, begetting, quickening, or new creation

•   The Holy Spirit is the sole agent in regeneration  
	  Cannot be co-operated with by the unregenerate man
	  Implies a new principle or habit that guides the soul

•   Regeneration is a sovereign act of God  
	  Implies a new birth, begetting, quickening, or new creation
	  Cannot be resisted by the unregenerate man

•   The new-born soul co-operates with sanctifying grace  
	  Faith, repentance, love, and good works are fruits of the Spirit
	  Implies a free action of men, subject to divine influences

•   The Holy Spirit uses the "truth" as an instrument in effectual calling  
	  Implies a knowledge of the truth is necessary for regeneration
	  The Scriptures assert that we are begotten by the truth, sanctified by the truth, etc.

•   The divine action in regeneration is omnipotent and certain  
	  Implies a self-consistent and immutable decree of God
	  Must be certainly efficacious and powerful

•   The divine influence is perfectly congruous to human nature  
	  Implies an all-wise Creator exerting influence on his own work
	  Is called in Scripture "a drawing," "a teaching," "an enlightening," etc.

•   Regeneration restores the faculties of the mind  
	  Implies a restoration of the mind, affections, and will to their proper condition
	  Cannot be inconsistent with human nature

•   The influence of the Holy Spirit is a sovereign and certain action  
	  Implies a self-consistent and immutable decree of God
	  Must be certainly efficacious and powerful, connected with salvation.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Regeneration is a radical change  
	  Consists in the implantation of a new governing principle of life
	  Is a "new birth" and "new creation" wrought by God's power
	  Is necessary for both the morally abandoned and the most moral and amiable

•   Regeneration is permanent  
	  Will be shown in chapter xvii on the Perseverance of the Saints
	  Affects the entire man, including intellect, affections, and will

•   Regeneration affects the entire man  
	  Because of the essential unity of the soul
	  All functions of the soul must be perverted if the moral state is corrupt

•   Scriptures affirm the need for regeneration  
	  Sin is essentially deceiving and involves moral blindness
	  The natural man cannot receive spiritual truths
	  All new-born believers are subjects of spiritual illumination and renewal

•   Regeneration is equivalent to giving a new heart  
	  The phrase "to give a new heart" is equivalent to effect regeneration
	  The heart is used to refer to the entire interior man, including intellect, affections, and will

•   Elect infants are regenerated and saved  
	  Through the Spirit, who works when, where, and how he pleases
	  Also applies to other elect persons who are incapable of being outwardly called by the ministry of the Word

•   Outward call of God's Word presupposes intelligence  
	  The will of God is revealed only as far as it concerns those capable of understanding and profiting by the revelation
	  God's purposes with respect to persons or classes not addressed are not explicitly revealed

•   Infants and others not capable of being called must be regenerated immediately  
	  Without the use of means, God can create Adam holy and new-create believers in righteousness and true holiness
	  The natural depravity of infants lies before moral action, and is rectified by the gracious restoration of the soul to its moral relation to the Spirit of God

•   The phrase "elect infants" is precise and fit for its purpose  
	  Points out the facts that all infants are born under righteous condemnation and have no claim in themselves to salvation

•   God's power and sovereignty are demonstrated in regeneration  
	  God can work when, where, and how he pleases
	  His purposes are not limited by human understanding or capabilities

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Salvation of Infants  
	  The salvation of infants is grounded in God's sovereign election.
	  This is true whether all adults are elected or only some are.
	  The Confession affirms what is revealed and leaves uncertain what is not revealed.

•   Election and Salvation  
	  Salvation is based on a sovereign election by God.
	  All salvation is pure grace, with no human merit involved.
	  The Confession affirms this truth and leaves uncertain the fate of non-elect infants.

•   Non-Elect Individuals  
	  Those not elected will certainly fail to be saved.
	  This is not because salvation is not available to them, but because they refuse to accept Christ.
	  Their radical aversion to God is never overcome by effectual calling.

•   Diligent Profession and Salvation  
	  Diligent profession and practice of natural or other religions cannot promote salvation.
	  This is because all men are born under condemnation and need a personal interest in Christ's redemption.
	  An atonement is necessary, and a personal interest in Christ is essential for salvation.

•   Knowledge of Christ and Salvation  
	  A knowledge of Christ and voluntary acceptance of him are essential for personal interest in his salvation.
	  This is proven by Paul's argument in Romans 10:13-17.

•   Importance of Effectual Calling  
	  The grace of effectual calling extends only to the elect.
	  This is why non-elect individuals will certainly fail to be saved.

•   Role of the Holy Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit may persuade non-elect individuals through common influences.
	  However, their radical aversion to God is never overcome by effectual calling.

•   Condemnation and Salvation  
	  All men are born under condemnation and need salvation.
	  A personal interest in Christ's redemption is necessary for salvation.

•   Denial of Christ  
	  To admit that men can be saved irrespectively of Christ is to virtually deny Christ.
	  This is because Christ's atonement is necessary for salvation.

•   Importance of the Gospel  
	  The gospel must be preached to individuals in order for them to hear and believe.
	  Salvation comes through faith, which comes through hearing, and hearing comes through the Word of God.

Here are the 10 bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Purpose and Means of Salvation  
	  God has revealed no purpose to save anyone except those who obey the gospel.
	  The gospel is the appointed means of saving souls.
	  Whatever lies beyond this circle of sanctified means is unrevealed, unpromised, and uncovenanted.
    The Heathen and Salvation  
	  The heathen in mass are evidently strangers to God.
	  They go down to death in an unsaved condition.
	  There is no recorded example of someone being saved without a knowledge of Christ.
    The Internal Call by the Holy Ghost  
	  The internal call by the Holy Ghost is a call to salvation.
	  It is affirmed that God has revealed no purpose to save anyone except those who receive this call.
	  The agent of this call is the Holy Ghost.
	  The effect of this call is to bring people to salvation.
	  The nature of this call is internal and spiritual.
    Proving the Internal Call  
	  The internal call can be proved by the effects it has on people's lives.
	  It is essential to salvation.
	  It embraces all the elect and only the elect.
    Regeneration and Conversion  
	  Regeneration is the act of God that regenerates the soul.
	  Conversion is the act of the believer cooperating with the influences of the Spirit.
	  Regeneration necessarily must precede conversion.
	  The Holy Spirit uses "the truth" as his instrument in regeneration.
	  The spiritual influence exerted in regeneration is certainly efficacious.
    The Nature of Regeneration  
	  Regeneration involves a radical moral change in the believer.
	  This change involves the whole man, intellect and will as well as the affections.
	  It is exerted in a manner perfectly consistent with the nature of man as a free agent.
    The Outward Call and Means of Grace  
	  The outward call and means of grace are addressed to all people.
	  The revelations of God's will in the Scriptures are made to all people.
	  Infants and others incapable of receiving the outward call are regenerated by God without the use of the means necessary in the case of intelligent adults.
    The Non-Elect and Salvation  
	  The non-elect will infallibly fail of salvation.
	  They will not be saved, even if they profess natural religion or another religion.
	  A knowledge and voluntary acceptance of Christ is essential to salvation for intelligent adults.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Justification is a gift of God  
	  It is not earned by human righteousness
	  It is received through faith in Christ's obedience and satisfaction
	  Faith is a gift from God (Rom. 3:24, 2 Cor. 5:19)

•   Faith is the sole instrument of justification  
	  It is not alone, but accompanied by other saving graces
	  It is not a dead faith, but works by love (James 2:17, 22, 26)

•   Justification is a judicial act of God  
	  It is a declaration of pardon and acceptance as righteous
	  It is not an act of gracious power, but a declaration of status

•   Justification is not sanctification  
	  It is not the making holy of a person
	  It is a declaration of righteousness, not a change in character

•   The true sense of justification  
	  Is a declaration of conformity to the law, not a change in character
	  Is proven by the universal meaning of the English word "justify"
	  Is distinct from sanctification, which is the making holy of a person

•   Justification is not mere pardon  
	  It includes pardon of sin, but also the declaration of righteousness
	  It is a judicial act, not a gracious act

•   Justification is a central principle of the Reformation  
	  It was brought out and vindicated by Luther
	  It is a key doctrine of the Protestant Reformation

•   Justification is distinct from condemnation  
	  The opposite of justification is condemnation, not sanctification
	  Justification is a declaration of righteousness, not a change in character

•   Justification is a necessary step in salvation  
	  It is a necessary step in the execution of God's decree of election
	  It is a necessary step in the salvation of believers

•   Justification is a gift to believers  
	  It is a gift to those who are effectually called by God
	  It is a gift to those who truly believe and are regenerate

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Pardon  
	  Relaxes the claims of law or waives their exaction in a given case
	  Is an act of a sovereign in the exercise of pure prerogative
	  Is free, resting upon considerations of mercy or public policy
	  Simply remits the penalty of sin, securing neither honors nor rewards

•   Justification  
	  Is the act of a judge, not a sovereign
	  Rests purely upon the state of the law and of the facts
	  Pronounces the law fulfilled in its strictest sense
	  Declares the person justified to be justly entitled to all the honors and advantages suspended upon perfect conformity to the law

•   Key differences between Pardon and Justification  
	  Pardon is a sovereign act, while justification is a judicial declaration
	  Pardon is based on mercy, while justification is based on the full satisfaction of divine justice
	  Pardon does not secure honors or rewards, while justification does

•   Grounds of Justification  
	  Proceeds upon the imputation or crediting to the believer of the righteousness of Jesus Christ
	  Is an act of God's free grace unto sinners
	  Is based on the perfect obedience and full satisfaction of Christ, imputed to the believer and received by faith alone

•   Arminian view of Justification  
	  Holds that for Christ's sake the demands of the law are graciously lowered
	  Accepts faith and evangelical obedience in the place of perfect obedience as the ground of justification

•   Reformed and Lutheran view of Justification  
	  Teaches that the true ground of justification is the perfect righteousness (active and passive) of Christ, imputed to the believer and received by faith alone

•   Scriptural basis for Justification  
	  Insists that we are not justified by works (Rom. 4:4-8; 11:6)
	  Declares that good works are possible only as consequences of justification (Rom. 6:14; 7:6)
	  Declares that the obedience and suffering of Christ is the true ground of justification (Rom. 5:19)

•   Effects of Justification  
	  Brings peace with God and assurance of salvation (Rom. 5:1-10)
	  Secures inheritance among those who are sanctified (Acts 26:18)

•   Imputation of Righteousness  
	  Is the crediting to the believer of the righteousness of Jesus Christ
	  Is received by faith alone

•   Importance of Faith  
	  Is the means by which the believer receives the imputed righteousness of Christ
	  Is the instrument by which the believer is justified

•   Nature of Justification  
	  Is a judicial declaration that the law is satisfied
	  Is a declaration that the person justified is possessed of righteousness, which satisfies the law

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. The Righteousness of One Man  
  The righteousness of one man, Jesus Christ, has a profound impact on humanity.
  His obedience leads to the justification of many (Rom. 5:18-19).

  2. Imputation of Sin and Righteousness  
  The phrase "to impute sin" or "righteousness" means to set to one's account or lay to one's charge.
  Our sins are imputed to Christ, and His righteousness is imputed to believers (Isa. 53:6, 12; Gal. 3:13).

  3. The Doctrine of Imputation  
  This doctrine is shared by the Reformed and Lutheran Churches.
  It is based on the idea that Christ's righteousness is credited to the believer's account (Rom. 4:4-8; 2 Cor. 5:19-21).

  4. Justification by Faith  
  A person is justified by faith when they lay hold of Christ's righteousness (Calvin, Institutes, 3.11.2).
  The Heidelberg Catechism and Lutheran Form of Concord affirm this doctrine.

  5. The Condition of Justification  
  The essential condition for the imputation of Christ's righteousness is faith in or on Christ (Rom. 4:9; Acts 16:31).
  Faith is the necessary instrument or means of justification.

  6. Faith as the Instrument of Justification  
  Faith is not the ground of justification, but rather the means by which we apprehend the true ground (Gal. 2:16; Rom. 3:20-28).
  Faith is a work, and justification is not based on works (Rom. 3:20-28; Gal. 2:16).

  7. Faith is a Gift of God  
  Faith is a gracious gift of God, not something we can earn or produce (Eph. 2:7-8; Acts 14:27).

  8. Justification is not Based on Faith  
  We are justified "through" or by means of faith, not on account of or for the sake of faith (Rom. 5:1; Gal. 2:16).

  9. The Role of Faith in Justification  
  Faith is not the foundation for a legal justification, but rather the means by which we apprehend the true ground (Rom. 4:9; Acts 16:31).

  10. The Importance of Understanding Justification  
  A clear understanding of justification is essential for a proper understanding of the Christian faith (Rom. 3:20-28; Gal. 2:16).

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Faith Alone Justifies  
	  Faith is the sole instrument of justification
	  It is never alone, but always accompanied by other Christian graces
	  Faith alone justifies, but a faith that is alone (dead faith) will not justify

•   Paul and James' Teachings  
	  Paul teaches that faith alone justifies (against Pharisees and legalists)
	  James teaches that a dead faith will not justify (against nominal Christians)
	  Paul and James are speaking of different things: God's justification of the sinner vs. proving true or real faith

•   Justification by Faith  
	  Faith is the prerequisite for God's justification of the sinner
	  Works are not taken into consideration when it comes to justification
	  True faith will produce good works, just as the sun gives light

•   Christ's Obedience and Death  
	  Christ's obedience and death fully discharged the debt of the justified
	  He made a proper, real, and full satisfaction to God's justice
	  This satisfaction is a manifestation of God's free grace

•   Free Grace Justification  
	  Justification is only of free grace, not based on anything in the justified
	  Both God's justice and grace are glorified in the justification of sinners
	  The cross of Christ is the focus of divine justice and grace

•   Christ's Righteousness  
	  Christ's righteousness is the ground of justification
	  His righteousness fully satisfies the demands of the divine law
	  This righteousness is a manifestation of God's love and free grace

•   The Cross of Christ  
	  The cross of Christ is the highest reach of justice and grace
	  It is the focus of divine justice and grace
	  The self-assumption of the penalty by the eternal Son of God is a vindication of justice and an expression of infinite love

•   God's Decree to Justify the Elect  
	  God decreed to justify all the elect from all eternity
	  This decree is a manifestation of God's free grace and love
	  The justification of the elect is a sovereign act of God's will

•   The Gift of God's Beloved Son  
	  The gift of God's beloved Son is a manifestation of free grace
	  The Son was given for the service of vicarious suffering
	  The sovereign election of the persons to be represented by the Son is a manifestation of God's free grace

•   The Glorious Rewards  
	  The glorious rewards that accrue to the elect on condition of Christ's representation are a manifestation of God's free grace
	  These rewards are a result of the sovereign election of the persons to be represented by Christ

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Justification and the Role of Christ  

    Christ's Death and Resurrection  
	  Christ died for the sins of his people (Gal. 3:8, 1 Pet. 1:2, 19, 20)
	  Christ rose again for their justification (Rom. 8:30)
    Justification is not Immediate  
	  Justification does not occur until the Holy Spirit applies Christ to the believer (Gal. 4:4, 1 Tim. 2:6)
	  The elect are not justified until they receive saving faith in Christ (Rom. 4:25)

  The Nature of Christ's Satisfaction  

    Christ Paid the Penal Debt  
	  Christ's satisfaction was not a payment of a monetary debt, but a payment of the penal debt of his people
	  The satisfaction was a matter of free grace, and its effects eventuate as agreed upon in the covenant between God and Christ

  God's Forgiveness and the State of Justification  

    God Forgives the Sins of the Justified  
	  God continues to forgive the sins of those who are justified (Matt. 6:12, 1 John 1:7, 9)
    The State of Justification is Permanent  
	  The justified cannot fall from the state of justification (Luke 22:32, John 10:28, Heb. 10:14)
    The Possibility of Falling Under God's Displeasure  
	  The justified may fall under God's fatherly displeasure through sin (Ps. 89:31-33, 51:7-12)
	  Restoration of God's favor requires humility, confession, and renewed faith and repentance (Matt. 26:75, 1 Cor. 11:30, 32)

  The Relation of the Justified to God and the Law  

    The Law Demands Favor  
	  After justification, the law demands that the subject be regarded and treated like a son (Gal. 3:9, 13, 14)
	  God, as a loving Father, executes kind offices towards the justified (Heb. 13:8)

  The Nature of Suffering  

    Suffering After Justification  
	  Suffering after justification is fatherly chastisement, designed to correct and improve the offender (Ps. 89:31-33, 51:7-12)
	  The justified must continue to return to God in repentance and faith to have their pardon renewed (Matt. 26:75, 1 Cor. 11:30, 32)

	  43. Of what kind is all the suffering of the justified?
	  44. What is taught in the sixth section, and where has it been previously considered?

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Regeneration and Justification  
	  Regeneration is an act of God that creates a new spiritual life in the heart of the believer.
	  Justification is the instant act of God that declares the believer free from condemnation and gives them a legal right to the benefits of the covenant.

•   Faith and Regeneration  
	  Faith is the first act of the new creature, consequent upon regeneration.
	  Faith apprehends the perfect righteousness of Christ and is the ground for justification.

•   Sanctification  
	  Sanctification is the progressive growth of the new life implanted in regeneration.
	  It is the process of becoming more like Christ and maturing in one's faith.

•   Adoption  
	  Adoption presents the new creature in their new relations with God.
	  It includes both justification and sanctification, and is the divine sonship into which the believer is introduced.

•   Elements of Divine Sonship  
	  Derivation of spiritual nature from God (2 Pet. 1:4; John 1:13; James 1:18; 1 John 5:18)
	  Being born in the image of God (Col. 3:10; Rom. 8:29; 2 Cor. 3:18)
	  Bearing God's name (1 John 3:1; Rev. 2:17; 3:12)
	  Being the objects of God's peculiar love (John 17:23; Rom. 5:5-8)
	  Indwelling of the Spirit of God (Gal. 4:6)
	  Filial spirit, obedient to God (1 Pet. 1:14; 2 John 6)
	  Freedom from guilt, legal bondage, and fear of death (Rom. 8:15-21; Gal. 5:1; Heb. 2:15)
	  Holy boldness and royal dignity (Heb. 10:19,22; 1 Pet. 2:9; 4:14)
	  Present protection, consolation, and abundant supplies (Ps. 125:2; Isa. 66:13; Luke 12:27-32; John 14:18; 1 Cor. 3:21-23; 2 Cor. 1:4)
	  Present fatherly chastisements for good (Ps. 51:11,12; Heb. 12:5-11)
	  Certain inheritance of the riches of God's glory (Rom. 8:17; James 2:5; 1 Pet. 1:4; 3:7)

•   Sanctification and the Christian Life  
	  Sanctification is the process of becoming more like Christ and maturing in one's faith.
	  It is the result of the virtue of Christ's death and resurrection, and is accomplished through the Word and Spirit dwelling in the believer.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. Sanctification  

    Definition  : The process of being made holy and pure in heart and life.
	  Involves the destruction of sin and the strengthening of saving graces.
	  Leads to the practice of true holiness, without which no one can see the Lord.
    Scriptural basis  : John 17:17, 19; Eph. 5:26; Rom. 8:13-14; II Thess. 2:13.

  II. Extent of Sanctification  

    Whole man  : Sanctification involves the entire person, including intellect, affections, and will.
	  Involves the soul and body.
    Imperfect in this life  : Sanctification is never perfect in this life, but there is a constant struggle between the flesh and the Spirit.
	  The flesh lusts against the Spirit, and the Spirit against the flesh.

  III. Means of Sanctification  

    Inward means  : Faith is the instrument of sanctification.
	  Faith is the organ of our union with Christ and fellowship with his Spirit.
	  Faith brings the inward experiences of the heart and outward actions of the life into obedience to the truth.
    Outward means  : The outward means of sanctification are not specified in the text.

  IV. Progress of Sanctification  

    Gradual progress  : Sanctification is a gradual process that continues throughout life.
	  The regenerate part of the believer overcomes the remaining corruption.
	  The saints grow in grace and perfect holiness in the fear of God.
    Constant supply of strength  : The believer receives a constant supply of strength from the sanctifying Spirit of Christ.

  V. Perfection of Sanctification  

    Not perfect in this life  : Sanctification is never perfect in this life, but it is completed at death.
	  The believer is made perfect at death, and the gracious element in their nature prevails.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text on sanctification:

•   Means of Sanctification  
	  The truth as revealed in the inspired Scriptures (John 17:17, 19; 1 Peter 1:22, 2:2)
	  The sacraments (Matthew 3:11; 1 Corinthians 12:13; 1 Peter 3:21)
	  Prayer (John 14:13, 14)
	  The gracious discipline of God's providence (John 15:2; Romans 5:3, 4; Hebrews 12:5-11)

•   Cooperation with the Holy Spirit  
	  The soul exercises the grace given by the Holy Spirit
	  The soul is both bound and encouraged to use means for spiritual renovation
	  The soul forms habits of resisting evil and right action

•   Fruits of Sanctification  
	  Good works are the fruits of sanctification
	  Good works must have their origin in a holy principle in the heart
	  Good works must be confirmed to the law of God (Galatians 5:22, 23; Ephesians 2:10; John 14:21)

•   Work of Sanctification  
	  Involves the destruction of the old body of sin
	  Involves the development of the grace implanted in regeneration
	  Is first inward and spiritual, then outward and practical

•   Conflict between Good and Evil  
	  The tendencies to evil remain after regeneration
	  The tendencies to good and evil are in active antagonism
	  The one must lose as the other gains in prevalence (Galatians 5:24; Colossians 3:5)

•   Sanctification Begins in the Heart  
	  The moral character of actions is derived from inward moral dispositions and affections
	  The Scriptures assert that the heart governs the life (Luke 6:45; Matthew 12:33)
	  Truly good works can be produced only by a heart in living union with Christ (John 15:4)

•   Sanctification Involves the Entire Man  
	  Includes the intellect, affections, and will
	  Involves the development of the entire person in Christ

•   Sanctification is a Duty  
	  The soul is bound and encouraged to use means for spiritual renovation
	  The soul must form habits of resisting evil and right action

•   Sanctification is a Grace  
	  The Holy Spirit gives the grace and prompts and directs in its exercise
	  The soul exercises the grace given by the Holy Spirit

•   Sanctification is Essential to Salvation  
	  Good works are absolutely essential to salvation
	  Good works are the necessary consequences of a gracious state of soul
	  Good works are perpetual requirements of the divine law

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Definition of Sanctification  
	  Involves spiritual illumination and enlightenment of the mind
	  Leads to a state of being where the will is controlled by God
	  Results in the body being made an instrument of righteousness

•   Necessity of Sanctification  
	  Our natural, sinful condition involves blindness and hardness of heart
	  We are sanctified by means of the truth, which involves spiritual illumination

•   Scriptural Basis for Sanctification  
	  Ephesians 1:17-18, Colossians 3:10, 2 Corinthians 4:6, 1 Thessalonians 5:23
	  These passages emphasize the importance of spiritual illumination and enlightenment

•   Perfectionism  
	  Different parties of Perfectionists have varying views on the possibility of perfection in this life
	  Pelagians believe that perfection is possible through human effort alone
	  Arminian and Papist Perfectionists believe that perfection is possible through divine grace, but not absolute perfection

•   Arminian and Papist Perfectionism  
	  Believe that men can do nothing morally right without divine grace
	  Believe that even with divine grace, no man is able to perfectly keep the original Adamic law of absolute perfection
	  Believe that it is the privilege and duty of all men to attain to a state of perfect love and sincere obedience to the gospel law

•   Papist View of Concupiscence  
	  Make a distinction between voluntary transgressions of known law and concupiscence (involuntary first movements of corruption)
	  Deny that concupiscence is properly of the nature of sin

•   John Wesley's View of Concupiscence  
	  Teaches that concupiscence is not properly of the nature of sin
	  Believes that concupiscence is a result of the remains of corruption within the regenerate

•   Reformed View of Concupiscence  
	  Affirms that concupiscence (involuntary first movements of evil desire) is of the nature of sin
	  Believes that this is true because all men judge that the moral state of the soul which determines evil action is itself essentially evil

•   Nature of Sin  
	  Every deficiency from the whole measure of moral excellence is of the nature of sin
	  Nothing short of absolute conformity to the Adamic law of absolute holiness is of the nature of sinless perfection

•   Practical Implications of Sanctification  
	  Involves the control of the will and the subjection of the body to the control of the sanctified will
	  Results in the believer being made an instrument of righteousness unto God

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Nature of Sin in Christians  
	  Concupiscence (remaining spontaneous tendency to evil) is an occasion for self-abhorrence and confession.
	  It needs forgiveness and the constant application of Christ's atoning blood.
	  Concupiscence is of the nature of sin.
    Scriptural Evidence  
	  Paul calls concupiscence "sin" (Rom. 7:7).
	  The sin that dwelt in Paul worked against his will, and wrought in him all manner of concupiscence (Rom. 7:14-25).
	  Concupiscence is also called the "old man" and the "body of sin" (Col. 2:11, 3:9).
    Scripture Saints and Sinless Perfection  
	  The biographies and testimonies of Scripture saints make it impossible to attribute sinless perfection to any one of them.
	  Paul and John disclaim sinless perfection (Rom. 7:14-25, Phil. 3:12-14, 1 John 1:8).
    Perfectionism  
	  Perfectionism is in conflict with the universal experience and observation of God's people.
	  The personal profession of perfectionism is generally judged to be just ground for serious suspicions.
    Sanctification and Perfection  
	  Sanctification is a work of God's free grace in execution of his eternal purposes of salvation.
	  The believer's nature, upon the whole, prevails, and he gradually advances in holiness until he is rendered perfect at death.
    The Author and Means of Sanctification  
	  The Author of sanctification is God.
	  The inward means of sanctification is the sanctifying Spirit of Christ.
	  The outward means of sanctification is not specified.
    The Fruits of Sanctification  
	  The fruits of sanctification include holy actions and a change in the permanent inward state of the soul.
    The Work of Sanctification  
	  The work of sanctification involves the gradual "mortification" of the "old man."
	  It involves the development of the graces implanted in regeneration.
    The Nature of Concupiscence  
	  Concupiscence is a remaining spontaneous tendency to evil in the heart of the perfect Christian.
	  It is of the nature of sin.
    The View of Concupiscence in Different Theologies  
	  Pelagianism views perfection as attainable in this life through human effort.
	  Arminianism and Papism view concupiscence as a moral failing, but not necessarily a sin.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Chapter Thirteen: Of the Mortification of Sin  

    23. State the proof derived from a consideration of the essential nature of virtue and the moral law  
	  Virtue and the moral law require perfection
	  Human nature is imperfect and prone to sin
    24. The same from the devotional literature and admissions of evangelical Arminians  
	  Devotional literature emphasizes the need for perfection
	  Evangelical Arminians acknowledge the importance of striving for perfection
    25. The same from the declarations of Scripture and from the biographies of scriptural characters  
	  Scripture teaches that believers should strive for perfection
	  Scriptural characters, such as Paul, exemplify the pursuit of perfection
    26. In what sense is the epithet "perfect" applied to men in the Scriptures?  
	  "Perfect" refers to moral perfection, not physical or spiritual completeness
	  Believers are called to strive for moral perfection
    27. To what is Perfectionism opposed?  
	  Perfectionism is opposed to the idea that believers can achieve moral perfection through their own efforts
	  It emphasizes the need for God's grace and assistance
    28. What is the certain issue of this warfare between the "law in the members" and the "law of the mind"?  
	  The issue is certain because of God's sovereignty and the believer's new nature
	  The believer's new nature will ultimately prevail over the flesh
    29. What is the ground of this certainty as to the result?  
	  The ground is God's promise and the believer's union with Christ
	  The believer's new nature is empowered by the Holy Spirit

  Chapter Fourteen: Of Saving Faith  

    I. The grace of faith, whereby the elect are enabled to believe to the saving of their souls  
	  Faith is a gift from God, wrought by the Holy Spirit
	  It is ordinarily wrought by the ministry of the Word
    Faith, in the most general sense of the word, is the assent of the mind to the truth of that of which we have not an immediate cognition  
	  Faith demands evidence and rests upon it
	  It differs from reason and knowledge in its object and scope
    Religious faith, in the most general sense of that word, is the assent of the mind to the general truths of religion  
	  This faith is common to all religions, true or false
	  Saving faith, on the other hand, is a spiritual discernment of the excellence and beauty of divine truth
    Of this saving faith it is alleged in this section:  
	  It is wrought in our hearts by the Holy Ghost
	  It is ordinarily wrought by the means of the Word of God
	  It is strengthened by the use of the sacraments and prayer

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. Effectual Calling  

    Faith is a moral act  
	  Must be grounded in spiritual congeniality with the truth
	  Unbelief is denounced as a sin, not intellectual weakness
    Faith is a gift of God  
	  Wrought in the soul by the Holy Spirit
	  Essential element in saving faith
    Faith is not natural  
	  Men are spiritually blind and incapable of discerning spiritual things
	  Must be enlightened by the Holy Spirit to discern spiritual things

  II. The Ministry of the Word  

    Faith comes through hearing  
	  Scripture asserts that faith comes through hearing the Word of God
	  Preaching of the gospel is the ordinary way to bring truth to bear on men's hearts
    The Word is the instrument of faith  
	  The Spirit uses the revealed truth of God as his instrument in regeneration and sanctification
	  Adult men never come to the experience of Christ's salvation without knowledge of his person and work

  III. Nourishing Faith  

    Faith must be nourished  
	  By the truth, prayer, the sacraments, and every means of grace
	  Whatever promotes sanctification promotes the strength of faith

  IV. The Acts of Saving Faith  

    Faith accepts and receives God's Word  
	  Believes to be true whatsoever is revealed in the Word
	  Acts differently on each particular passage, yielding obedience to commands, trembling at threats, and embracing promises
    Principal acts of saving faith  
	  Accepting, receiving, and resting upon Christ alone for justification, sanctification, and eternal life
	  By virtue of the covenant of grace

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Authority of God's Word  
	  The Bible is the ultimate authority in matters of faith
	  God's Word is the foundation of all Christian doctrine and practice
	  The Bible is the inspired Word of God, without error or contradiction

•   The Testimony of God  
	  God's testimony is borne through the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit
	  The Holy Spirit bears witness to the soul of the believer
	  God's testimony is corroborated by signs and wonders

•   Saving Faith  
	  Saving faith receives all of God's Word as true, without exception
	  Faith is not limited to certain parts of the Bible, but includes the whole
	  Saving faith excludes all traditions, doctrines of men, and private revelation

•   The Nature of Faith  
	  Faith is a complex state of mind that varies with the nature of the truth believed
	  Faith involves cordial, realizing assent to the truth presented
	  Faith includes recognition, assent, acquiescence, submission, and trust, as appropriate

•   Trust as an Element of Faith  
	  Trust is an integral and inseparable element of saving faith
	  Trust is especially important in justifying faith, which unites to Christ
	  Trust is not always necessary, but is essential in cases where it is legitimate

•   Justifying Faith  
	  Justifying faith involves two essential elements: assent and trust
	  Assent includes intellectual recognition and cordial embrace of the truth
	  Trust is an act of the whole man, embracing the truth with intellect, affection, and will

•   The Object of Faith  
	  The object of faith is the person, offices, and work of Christ
	  Faith in Christ is the sole condition and instrument of justification
	  Rejection of Christ is the ground of reprobation

•   The Role of Scripture  
	  Scripture is the ultimate authority in matters of faith
	  Scripture reveals the person, offices, and work of Christ
	  Scripture is the foundation of all Christian doctrine and practice

•   The Importance of Understanding Scripture  
	  Understanding Scripture is essential for faith and practice
	  The original text of Scripture is the foundation of all Christian doctrine and practice
	  The inspired canon of Scripture is the ultimate authority in matters of faith

•   The Relationship Between Faith and Practice  
	  Faith and practice are inseparable
	  Faith is the foundation of all Christian practice
	  Practice is the outworking of faith in daily life

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    What is faith?  
	  Faith is an active assent to and cordial embrace of the truth
	  It involves intellectual recognition and trust in Christ
    Elements of saving faith  
	  Trust or implicit reliance upon Christ for salvation
	  Intellectual recognition of the truth of Christ's work and promises
    Trust in Christ  
	  To believe in or on Christ implies trust as well as credence
	  Trust is the foundation upon which expectation and hope are based
    Fruits of faith  
	  Faith leads to persuasion, obtaining, embracing, and working righteousness
	  Faith is not just intellectual conviction, but a hearty embrace of Christ
    Degrees of faith  
	  Faith can be weak or strong
	  It can be assailed and weakened, but ultimately gains the victory through divine grace
    Growth of faith  
	  Faith can grow up to the measure of a full assurance through Christ
	  This growth is possible through the ministry of the Word and the use of sacraments and prayer
    Saving faith  
	  It is different from general faith in that it involves trust in Christ for salvation
	  It is the work of the Holy Spirit, wrought through the ministry of the Word
    Faith and the Word  
	  Saving faith rests upon the truth of the testimony which God bears in his Word
	  It receives all the contents of God's Word, without exception
    Faith and the Holy Spirit  
	  Saving faith is the work of the Holy Spirit
	  It continues to increase and is strengthened by the use of sacraments and prayer
    Faith and assurance  
	  Faith can grow up to the measure of a full assurance through Christ
	  This assurance is possible through the ministry of the Word and the use of sacraments and prayer

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Chapter 14: Of Saving Faith  

    What is saving faith?  
	  Varies in elements depending on the passage of God's Word
    Is truth an integral element of saving faith?  
	  Yes
    What is the object of saving faith?  
	  The sole instrument of justification and condition of salvation
    What are the first two elements of saving faith?  
	  Assent and trust
    What relation do faith, hope, and trust sustain to one another?  
	  Mutually supportive
    What truths are taught about saving faith?  
	  1. It involves assent
	  2. It involves trust
	  3. It is essential for salvation

  Chapter 15: Of Repentance Unto Life  

    What is repentance?  
	  An evangelical grace that is essential for salvation
    What are the grounds of repentance?  
	  1. A true sense of sin
		- Includes consciousness of guilt, pollution, and helplessness
	  2. A bright apprehension of the mercy of God in Christ
		- Necessary for true repentance
    What is the essence of true repentance?  
	  1. Sincere hatred of sin and sorrow for our own sin
	  2. A desire to turn from sin and walk with God in obedience to his commandments

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. Definition of Repentance  
	  Repentance is a turning from sin unto God
	  It involves a change of mind, including thought, feeling, and purpose
	  It is a voluntary forsaking of sin as evil and hateful

  2. Essence of Repentance  
	  Repentance consists of actual turning from sin unto God
	  It is a practical turning, or "conversion", from sin unto God
	  It involves sincere sorrow, humiliation, and confession

  3. Marks of Repentance  
	  A sincere purpose of, and a persevering endeavor after, new obedience
	  A change of mind, including thought, feeling, and purpose
	  A turning unto God as our reconciled Father

  4. Relationship between Repentance and Regeneration  
	  Repentance can only be exercised by a soul after, and in consequence of, its regeneration by the Holy Spirit
	  God regenerates, and we repent in the exercise of the new gracious ability thus given

  5. Difference between Conversion and Repentance  
	  Conversion is a more general term, including all experiences involved in commencing the divine life
	  Repentance is more specific, emphasizing the work of the law upon the conscience and the turning from sin

  6. False Repentance  
	  There is a false repentance experienced before regeneration, and by those never regenerated
	  This false repentance arises from the common operations of the truth and the Spirit upon the natural conscience

  7. Genuineness of True Repentance  
	  True repentance is proved by its being conformed perfectly to the requirements and teachings of Scripture
	  It is also proved by its fruits, and infallibly springs from regeneration and leads to eternal life

  8. Repentance as an Evangelical Grace  
	  Repentance is an evangelical grace, given to us for Christ's sake, as well as a duty obligatory upon us
	  It is a gift of God, involving true conviction of sin, holy hatred of sin, and faith in the Lord Jesus and his work

  9. Importance of Preaching Repentance  
	  Repentance should be diligently preached by every minister of the gospel
	  This is self-evident from the essential nature of the duty, and was included in the commission Christ gave to the apostles

  10. Biblical Affirmation of Repentance  
	  Repentance is directly affirmed in Scripture, as a gift of God and a necessary experience for salvation
	  It is mentioned in various passages, including Zechariah 12:10, Acts 5:31, and 2 Timothy 2:25.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. Repentance is not a satisfaction for sin  
	  Repentance is not a substitute for God's pardon
	  It is not a cause of pardon, but rather a response to God's free grace in Christ

  2. Repentance is necessary for pardon  
	  None may expect pardon without repentance
	  Repentance is a necessary condition for pardon, but not a sufficient one

  3. No sin is too small to deserve damnation  
	  All sin deserves punishment and damnation
	  No sin is too small to be punished by God

  4. No sin is too great to be forgiven  
	  True repentance can forgive even the greatest sins
	  God's mercy and forgiveness are available to all who truly repent

  5. General repentance is not enough  
	  Men must repent of their particular sins specifically
	  General repentance is not sufficient to bring about true forgiveness

  6. Repentance is a gift of God  
	  Internal repentance is a gracious gift of God
	  It is not something that can be earned or merited

  7. Repentance is not a supplement to Christ's sacrifice  
	  The external penance of the Roman Catholic Church is an attempt to supplement Christ's sacrifice
	  This is an impertinent attempt to add to God's perfect work

  8. Repentance is a response to God's grace  
	  Repentance springs from the application of Christ's grace to the soul
	  It leads to the application of Christ's grace to the soul

  9. Repentance is necessary for justification  
	  Repentance is a necessary condition for justification
	  It is not a sufficient condition, but rather a response to God's free grace

  10. Repentance is not a means of earning pardon  
	  Repentance is not a means of earning God's pardon
	  It is a response to God's free grace and mercy, not a way of earning forgiveness.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Repentance and Its Importance  

    Definition of Repentance  
	  Repentance is the natural and instant sequence of the grace of regeneration
	  It involves faith in Christ and is the instrument of justification
    Design of Christ's Work  
	  To "save his people from their sins" (Matt. 1:21)
	  To free people from the guilt of their sins through pardon and from the power of their sins through repentance
    Repentance as a Duty and a Grace  
	  Repentance is a duty as well as a grace
	  Ministers are commanded to preach it as essential to forgiveness (Luke 24:47; Acts 20:21)

  The Nature of Sin and Repentance  

    The Consequences of Sin  
	  The least sin deserves punishment (James 2:10)
	  True repentance includes faith, which unites us to Christ and secures the imputation of his righteousness
    The Importance of Repenting from Specific Sins  
	  Men ought to repent not only in general but also of every particular sinful action
	  They should redress the wrong done by their actions when possible (Luke 19:8; 1 John 1:9)

  Confession and Repentance  

    Private Confession to God  
	  Every man should make private confession of all his sins to God
	  God will certainly pardon him when his sorrow and renunciation of his sins are sincere (1 John 1:9)
    Public or Private Confession to Those Offended  
	  When a Christian has personally injured a brother or scandalized the Church, they should be willing to declare their repentance
	  This involves a public or private confession, depending on the situation (James 5:16; Luke 17:3,4; Josh. 7:19)

Here are the 10 bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Duty to Forgive  
	  It is the duty of the brethren or Church to forgive the offending party upon repentance (Matthew 18:15-18)
	  This is a dictate of natural conscience and Scripture
    Forgiveness and Repentance  
	  Forgiveness is a gift of Christ, and its exercise is a certain indication of forgiveness by Christ (Luke 17:3-4)
	  The Church is bound to forgive public scandals when the Lord has done so (Matthew 6:12)
    Roman Catholic Teaching on Penance  
	  The Roman Catholic Church teaches that confession to a priest is an element of penance and evidence of true repentance
	  They maintain that the priest absolves judicially, not merely declaratively, from the penal consequences of sins confessed
    Scriptural Teaching on Confession  
	  The Bible commands believers to confess their faults to one another, not to a priest (James 5:16)
	  Believers have immediate access to Christ and God through Christ (1 Timothy 2:5, John 14:6)
    The Power of Forgiveness  
	  The power of absolute forgiveness of sin belongs to God alone (Matthew 9:26)
	  No class of men has been granted this power as a matter of fact
    The Authority to Bind or Loose  
	  The authority to bind or loose, committed to the Church by Christ, conveys the power of declaring the conditions on which God pardons sin
	  This authority is not the power to forgive sins, but to declare the conditions of forgiveness
    Genuine Repentance  
	  Genuine repentance includes a true hatred of sin and sorrow for our own sin
	  It includes an actual turning from all sin unto God
	  It includes a sincere purpose of, and a persevering endeavor after, new obedience
    False Repentance  
	  False repentance is a pretended or hypocritical repentance
	  It can be discriminated from genuine repentance by its lack of true hatred of sin and sorrow for our own sin
    The Importance of Repentance  
	  Repentance is an evangelical grace
	  It should be diligently preached as a necessary part of the Christian life
    The Relationship between Repentance and Pardon  
	  Repentance is no cause whatever of the pardon of sin
	  None are ever pardoned without repentance

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Good Works  
	  Are only those commanded by God in His Word (Micah 6:8, Rom. 12:2, Heb. 13:21)
	  Not those devised by men without warrant (Matt. 15:9, Isa. 29:13, I Peter 1:18)

•   Characteristics of Good Works  
	  Must be commanded by God (1)
	  Must spring from faith and love in the heart (2)

•   Effects and Uses of Good Works  
	  Express gratitude and manifest God's grace (1)
	  Glorify God (2)
	  Develop grace and strengthen assurance (3)
	  Edify the brethren (4)
	  Stop the mouths of adversaries (5)
	  Necessary for eternal life (6)

•   Obedience to God's Will  
	  Must be performed in conformity to God's revealed will (1)
	  Not self-development, but obedience to a personal authority (2)

•   The Law of Moral Perfection  
	  Is an all-perfect rule of righteousness (1)
	  Has its ground in the eternal nature of God (2)

•   The Importance of Obedience  
	  The good man is the obedient man (1)
	  The sinner is guilty of disobedience to the Supreme Lawgiver (2)

•   The Role of Scripture  
	  Provides a perfect rule of faith and practice (1)
	  Every principle, motive, and end of right action can be learned from Scripture (2)

•   The Abhorrence of Uncommanded Service  
	  God abhors uncommanded service (1)
	  Warns against adding to or diminishing from His commands (2)

•   The Catholic Church's Teaching on Confession  
	  Teaches that confession of sins is necessary for salvation (1)
	  Teaches that priests have the power to absolve sins (2)

•   The Error of the Catholic Church's Teaching  
	  Is wrong in teaching that confession of sins is necessary for salvation (1)
	  Is wrong in teaching that priests have the power to absolve sins (2)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Good works must spring from a principle of faith and love  
	  Determined by the moral character of the principle or affection that prompts it
	  Love to God is the foundation-principle upon which all moral duties rest
	  Any action performed without love to God will lack the essential element of true obedience

•   The effects and uses of good works in the Christian life are manifold  
	  Express the gratitude of the believer and manifest the grace of God in them
	  Adorn the profession of the gospel and render manifest the excellent working of the Spirit
	  Glorify God and manifest the excellency of his grace

•   Good works glorify God  
	  Manifest the excellency of God's grace and excite all who behold them to appreciate and proclaim his glory
	  Edify the brethren and confirm the truth of Christianity and the power of divine grace

•   Good works are necessary for salvation  
	  Not as a prerequisite to justification, but as essential elements of sanctification and glorification
	  A saved soul is a holy soul, and a holy soul is one whose faculties are all engaged in works of loving obedience

•   Good works are the fruits of sanctification  
	  Having their root in regeneration and the present exercise of trust in God and love for him
	  Faith is shown by works, and the kind of works of which James speaks springs only from a believing heart

•   The ability to do good works is not of ourselves, but from the Spirit of Christ  
	  We are enabled to do good works by the actual influence of the Holy Spirit
	  We are not to grow negligent, but rather diligent in stirring up the grace of God that is in us

•   Good works exercise and increase the graces of the heart  
	  By the universal law of habit, grace grows by its exercise
	  The assurance of our own gracious state naturally increases with the strength and evidence of those graces unto which the promise of salvation is attached

•   Good works are essential to the Christian life  
	  They are necessary to the attainment of salvation and the growth in holiness
	  A holy soul is one whose faculties are all engaged in works of loving obedience

•   Good works edify the brethren and disprove the cavils of wicked men  
	  Edify others as confirmatory evidence of the truth of Christianity and the power of divine grace
	  Disprove the cavils and render nugatory the opposition of wicked men

•   Heaven could not exist except as a society of holy souls  
	  Good works are the means of sanctification and glorification
	  A saved soul is a holy soul, and a holy soul is one whose faculties are all engaged in works of loving obedience

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Regeneration and Sanctification  
	  Regeneration is the implantation of a permanent holy principle in the soul by the Holy Spirit.
	  Sanctification is the continued sustenance and guidance of this principle by the Holy Spirit.

•   The Role of the Holy Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit enables the Christian to do good works.
	  The Holy Spirit's influence is necessary for the Christian to will and do good works.

•   The Christian's Responsibility  
	  The Christian must actively co-operate with the Holy Spirit.
	  The Christian must use their faculties of reason, conscience, and free will to obey God's will.

•   The Means of Grace  
	  The Christian must use the means of grace, such as the written Word, to honour the Holy Spirit.
	  The Christian must not wait for special motions from the Holy Spirit, but rather seek guidance and assistance.

•   The Nature of Good Works  
	  Good works are not done by the Christian's own strength, but by the enabling power of the Holy Spirit.
	  Good works are done in response to the Holy Spirit's prompting and guidance.

•   The Limitations of Good Works  
	  Even the best works are imperfect and mixed with weakness and imperfection.
	  Good works cannot merit pardon of sin or eternal life at the hand of God.

•   The Disproportion between Good Works and God's Glory  
	  The value of good works is vastly disproportionate to the glory of God.
	  Good works cannot satisfy the debt of our former sins or profit God in any way.

•   The Christian's Status as a Servant  
	  The Christian is an unprofitable servant, even when doing their best.
	  The Christian's good works are defiled and mixed with imperfection.

•   The Importance of Humility  
	  The Christian must not rely on their own strength or merit, but rather on the enabling power of the Holy Spirit.
	  The Christian must recognize their limitations and imperfections.

•   The Relationship between the Holy Spirit and the Christian  
	  The Holy Spirit works through the Word and the Christian's faculties of reason, conscience, and free will.
	  The Christian must co-work with the Holy Spirit to do good works and obey God's will.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. Works of Supererogation are Impossible  

  Definition: Works that go beyond what is demanded or commanded
  Impossibility:
	  Even the most sanctified saint cannot fully discharge all obligations
	  The moral law is intrinsically obligatory, making supererogation impossible

  2. The Romish Church's Error  

  Teaches that good works after baptism merit increase of grace and eternal felicity
  Distinguishes between commands and counsels of Christ
	  Commands are binding on all, while counsels are optional

  3. The Nature of the Moral Law  

  What is right is obligatory
  What is not obligatory is not moral
  Supererogation is not moral and has no value or merit

  4. Christ's Commands  

  To love God and neighbor is the absolute and universal command
  No "counsels" are given as distinct from commands

  5. Increase of Grace and Eternal Felicity  

  Secured by Christ's blood and given freely or reserved for eternal inheritance

  6. Fruits of the Romish System  

  Celibacy, voluntary poverty, and monastic vows have produced immoral and false principles

  7. The Impossibility of Sinless Perfection  

  Christians cannot attain sinless perfection in this life
  Therefore, it is impossible to do more than is commanded

  8. The Nature of Works  

  Works are accepted because of union with Christ, not because of their own merit
  Works are rewarded for Christ's sake, not for their own sake

  9. The Acceptance of Believers' Works  

  Works of sincere believers are accepted in spite of imperfections
  Accepted because of union with Christ, not because of their own merit

  10. The Reward of Believers' Works  

  Works are rewarded for Christ's sake, not for their own sake
  Reward is based on sincerity and union with Christ, not on merit.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Judgment and Moral Law  
	  The moral law is based on God's nature and character.
	  What is not obligatory is not moral, and what is not moral cannot be rewarded.
    Value of Human Works  
	  The best human works are infinitely unworthy compared to God's favor.
	  Human works cannot be compared in value to God's rewards.
    God's Sovereignty and Superiority  
	  God's sovereignty and proprietorship over us exclude the possibility of our actions deserving a reward.
	  Our actions cannot profit God or lay him under obligation to us.
    Imperfection of Human Works  
	  Our works, even if perfect, could not merit anything.
	  Our imperfect works need to be atoned for by Christ's blood.
    Acceptance of Good Works  
	  Good works of sincere believers are accepted because of their union with Christ Jesus.
	  Our approaches to God are made through Christ.
    Relation of Good Works to Rewards  
	  In a strict sense, no work can merit a reward from God due to its intrinsic value or worthiness.
	  All human faculties are granted and sustained by God, making us already in debt to him.
    Merit in a Covenant Sense  
	  If Adam had obeyed in his original probation, he would have "merited" the reward conditioned upon it.
	  This merit is based on the terms of the covenant, not the intrinsic value of obedience.
    No Merit in This Life  
	  Our works in this life have no merit intrinsically due to their imperfections.
	  Our works have no merit by covenant concession because we are not standing in the covenant of works.
    Gracious Reward in the Gospel  
	  The gracious reward of the believer is a branch of the same gracious root as the infusion of grace in the heart.
	  The covenant of grace provides for the exercise of grace in the life and the reward of the exercised grace.
    Christ as the Meritorious Ground  
	  The righteousness of Christ, received by faith alone, constitutes the sole meritorious ground for salvation.
	  This is the basis for our salvation in all its stages.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. The Relationship Between Grace and Works  

    A. Grace as a Reward  
	  A reward added to a work of obedience
	  A suitable stimulus to duty, like a father rewarding a child
    B. The Proportion of Grace  
	  A gracious proportion established between the grace given in reward and the grace given in holy exercises
	  The more obedience, the more reward; the more earthy grace, the more heavenly glory

  II. Works Done by Unregenerate Men  

    A. Characteristics of Unregenerate Works  
	  Imperfect and ungodly, even if they are good in themselves
	  Not done from a principle of divine love or with the glory of God as the object
    B. Distinctions Between Good and Sinful Works  
	  An action can be good in itself but sinful in its motives or object
	  A rebel can do good things, but they are still sinful in relation to God's authority

  III. The Sinfulness of Unregenerate Works  

    A. The Sinfulness of Unregenerate Works  
	  All unregenerate works are sinful, even if they are good in themselves
	  The ploughing of the wicked is said to be sin (Prov. 21:4)
    B. The Displeasure of God  

  IV. God's Displeasure with Unregenerate Works  

    A. God's Displeasure with Sinful Works  
	  God is more displeased with sinful works than with the neglect of commanded duties
    B. God's Displeasure with Neglect  

  V. The Importance of Obedience  

    A. Obedience as a Duty  
	  Unregenerate men have a bounden duty to obey God's commands
    B. The Sin of Rebellion  

  VI. The Sin of Rebellion  

    A. Rebellion as a Personal Attitude  
	  Rebellion is a personal attitude of defiance against God's authority
    B. The Absence of Proper Motives and Objects  

  VII. The Absence of Proper Motives and Objects  

    A. The Importance of Divine Love  
	  True good works spring from a principle of divine love
    B. The Importance of the Glory of God  

  VIII. The Glory of God  

    A. The Glory of God as the Object  
	  The glory of God should be the object of all good works
    B. The Revealed Will of God  

  IX. The Revealed Will of God  

    A. The Importance of Following God's Will  
	  Good works should be done according to the revealed will of God
    B. The Consequences of Disobedience  

  X. The Consequences of Disobedience  

    A. The Consequences of Sinful Works  
	  Sinful works cannot please God or merit grace
    B. The Consequences of Neglect  

  XI. The Consequences of Neglect  

    A. The Sin of Neglect  
	  Neglecting to obey God's commands is a sin
    B. The Importance of Obedience  

  XII. The Importance of Obedience  

    A. Obedience as a Means of Grace  
	  Obedience is a means of receiving grace from God
    B. The Relationship Between Obedience and Grace  

  XIII. The Relationship Between Obedience and Grace  

    A. The Proportion of Grace  
	  The more obedience, the more reward; the more earthy grace, the more heavenly glory
    B. The Importance of Obedience  

  XIV. The Importance of Obedience  

    A. Obedience as a Duty  
	  Obedience is a bounden duty
    B. The Sin of Rebellion  

  XV. The Sin of Rebellion  

    A. Rebellion as a Personal Attitude  
	  Rebellion is a personal attitude of defiance against God's authority
    B. The Absence of Proper Motives and Objects  

  XVI. The Absence of Proper Motives and Objects  

    A. The Importance of Divine Love

    B. The Importance of the Glory of God

    A. The Glory of God as the Object  
	  The glory of God should be the object of all good works

Note: The text is quite dense and complex, and the summary above tries to break it down into manageable bullet points. However, some of the points may still be a bit convoluted or require further clarification.
