Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  The Nature of Salvation  

    The Decree of Election  
	  Depends on God's free and unchangeable love
	  Immutability of the decree ensures salvation
    The Role of Jesus Christ  
	  Merit and intercession of Jesus Christ are essential
	  Efficacy of Christ's work ensures salvation
    The Covenant of Grace  
	  Nature of the covenant ensures salvation
	  Certainty and infallibility of salvation arise from the covenant
    The Holy Spirit  
	  Abiding of the Spirit ensures salvation
	  Seed of God within believers ensures salvation

  The Possibility of Falling Away  

    Causes of Falling Away  
	  Temptations of Satan and the world
	  Remaining corruptions of human nature
	  Neglect of the means of grace
    Effects of Falling Away  
	  God's displeasure and the Holy Spirit's grief
	  Deprivation of graces and comforts
	  Hardening of the heart and wounding of the conscience
	  Temporal judgments and scandalization of others

  The Perseverance of Believers  

    Certain Perseverance  
	  True believers will certainly persevere to the end
	  Perseverance is not due to human free will
    Principles of Perseverance  
	  Immutability of the eternal decree of election
	  Provisions of the eternal covenant of grace
	  Merits and intercession of Christ
	  Constant indwelling and preserving power of the Holy Ghost

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Calvinistic vs. Arminian doctrine  
	  Calvinistic doctrine: God causes every true believer to persevere in faith and obedience till death.
	  Arminian doctrine: Perseverance in faith and obedience depends on the free will of every individual.

    Objections to Calvinistic doctrine  
	  Objection 1: Inconsistent with man's free will
		- Response: God controls the free wills of his people without limiting their liberty.

    Security of the saints  
	  Arminians believe that saints will be secure from falling from grace in heaven, but not on earth.
	  Calvinistic doctrine: Saints are secure from falling from grace both on earth and in heaven.

    Carnal security  
	  Calvinistic doctrine is not liable to the charge of fostering a spirit of carnal security.
	  True doctrine: Perseverance in holiness is certain if one has truly believed.

    Certainty of salvation  
	  Certainty of salvation is known through perseverance in holiness.
	  Threats of the law and invitations of the gospel are more applicable to those who are not in a gracious state.

    Effort and perseverance  
	  Calvinistic doctrine: Effort and perseverance are necessary, but certain of success.
	  God works in believers to will and to do of his good pleasure.

    Scriptural basis  
	  Scripture asserts the fact of certain perseverance.
	  Examples: 1 Peter 1:5, Philippians 1:6, John 10:28.

    God's power and control  
	  God controls the free wills of his people without limiting their liberty.
	  God works in believers to will and to do of his good pleasure.

    Arminian confession  
	  Arminian confession: It is possible for believers to fall away totally and finally from grace.
	  Calvinistic doctrine: This is not possible, as God causes every true believer to persevere.

    Romish Church doctrine  
	  Romish Church doctrine: A man once justified cannot lose grace, and those who fall away were never truly justified.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Ground of perseverance  
	  Not in the free will of saints
	  But in the inherent immutability of God's eternal decree of election
	  Election is immutable and certainly efficacious

•   Election  
	  Respects individuals
	  Chooses them to salvation and all means thereof
	  Not conditioned on the use of grace, but founded on God's will
	  Immutable and certainly efficacious

•   Covenant of grace  
	  A personal counsel from eternity between Father and Son
	  Determines who are to be saved, what Christ will do, and how redemption will be applied
	  Those in the covenant cannot fail of benefits provided

•   Christ's merits and intercession  
	  Christ wrought out perfect righteousness in the stead of his people
	  Makes effectual intercession in heaven for those he purchased redemption for
	  Impossible for those for whom he was substituted to fail of salvation

•   Holy Spirit's role  
	  Constantly indwells the soul
	  Acts in accordance with the laws of the soul's constitution
	  Secures the ultimate victory of new spiritual principles and tendencies

•   Perseverance as a duty and grace  
	  Wrought by the Holy Spirit, not irrespective of, but through the free will of the man
	  A duty as well as a grace
	  Should be preached for the encouragement of the diligent and the slothful

•   Arminian doctrine  
	  Election is not unconditional, but based on God's foreknowledge of human response
	  Christ's death is designed to save all people, not just the elect
	  Free will of the sinner is necessary for regeneration

•   Consequences of Arminian doctrine  
	  Renders the conclusion inevitable that the true believer may totally and finally fall away
	  Leads to a denial of the perseverance of the saints

•   Importance of perseverance  
	  A duty and necessity for salvation
	  Should be preached to quicken the slothful and increase the sense of obligation felt by all

•   Implications of perseverance  
	  Those in the covenant cannot fail of benefits provided
	  Those for whom Christ was substituted cannot fail of salvation

Here are the 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Doctrine of Falling from Grace  
	  The Romish Church teaches that a believer can fall from grace through sin.
	  The Protestant Arminians teach that a believer can fall from grace through sin, but can also regain it through repentance.
	  The Calvinistic doctrine teaches that a true believer cannot fall from grace.

•   Freedom of the Human Will  
	  The Calvinistic doctrine does not deny the freedom of the human will.
	  The doctrine emphasizes God's sovereignty in salvation.

•   Carnal Security  
	  The Calvinistic doctrine does not foster a spirit of carnal security among believers.
	  Believers are still accountable for their actions and must strive to live a holy life.

•   Scriptural Teaching  
	  The Scriptures explicitly teach that true believers will not be allowed to fall from grace.
	  This certainty is based on God's decree of election, the eternal covenant of grace, Christ's death and intercession, and the indwelling of the Holy Spirit.

•   Sources and Occasions of Falling  
	  True believers are liable to fall due to various sources and occasions, such as:
		- Worldly temptations
		- Sinful desires
		- Spiritual apathy
		- False doctrine

•   Effects of Falling  
	  Falling from grace can lead to:
		- Spiritual darkness
		- Loss of joy and peace
		- Increased sinfulness
		- Separation from God

•   Assurance of Salvation  
	  True believers can have an infallible assurance of their salvation.
	  This assurance is based on:
		- The divine truth of the promises of salvation
		- The inward evidence of God's grace in their lives
		- The testimony of the Spirit of adoption

•   False Assurance  
	  Unregenerate men can have a false assurance of salvation.
	  This false assurance is based on:
		- Human desire for happiness
		- Self-love and self-confidence
		- Spiritual pride

•   Distinguishing True from False Assurance  
	  True assurance begets humility, while false assurance begets spiritual pride.
	  True assurance is based on a deep understanding of God's promises and the evidence of His grace in one's life.

•   Sources of True Assurance  
	  True assurance comes from:
		- The divine truth of the promises of salvation
		- The inward evidence of God's grace in one's life
		- The testimony of the Spirit of adoption

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The True and False Paths  
	  The true path leads to increased diligence in practicing holiness
	  The false path leads to sloth and self-indulgence
    Characteristics of the True Path  
	  Leads to candid self-examination and a desire to be searched and corrected by God
	  Leads to a desire for more intimate fellowship with God
    Attaining Certainty of Personal Salvation  
	  Directly affirmed in Scripture (Rom. 8:16, 1 John 2:3, 1 John 3:14)
	  Commanded as a duty in Scripture (Heb. 6:11, 2 Pet. 1:10)
    Examples of Attaining Certainty  
	  Paul's testimony (2 Tim. 1:12, 4:7-8)
	  John's testimony (1 John 2:3, 4:16)
	  Unquestionable instances in modern times, including Protestant Reformers
    The Protestant Reformation's View on Assurance  
	  The Reformers taught that faith involves trust in God's favor towards us
	  They identified assurance with faith, making it essential to salvation
    The Roman Catholic View on Assurance  
	  Held that faith is mere intellectual assent to the truth, not involving trust
	  Believed that no one could attain to certainty of personal salvation without an extraordinary revelation
    The Basis of Infallible Assurance  
	  Rests on the divine truth of the promises of salvation
	  Includes trust in God's promises, which strengthens our assured hope
    The Relationship between Trust and Assurance  
	  Trust rests upon the divine truth of the promises
	  In turn, supports our assured hope of salvation
    The Importance of Assurance  
	  Exhorted to show the same diligence to attain full assurance of hope (Heb. 6:11)
	  Commanded to give diligence to make our calling and election sure (2 Pet. 1:10)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Nature of Hope  
	  Hope is a gift from God
	  The fullness of hope is assurance
	  Assurance is based on the inward evidence of graces

•   The Inward Evidence of Graces  
	  The Scriptures promise eternal life to those who believe
	  The believer has a distinct evidence in their consciousness that they believe
	  This evidence leads to the conclusion that they shall have eternal life

•   The Promises of Scripture  
	  Promises are made to those who love God, keep his commandments, and love their brethren
	  When these graces are possessed, the conclusion is immediate and irresistible that we are in union with Christ

•   The Testimony of the Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit witnesses with our spirits that we are children of God (Rom. 8:16)
	  This witnessing is not a direct revelation, but rather a series of particulars that the Spirit conveys

•   The Authorship of the Spirit  
	  The Spirit is the author of the promises of Scripture
	  The Spirit is the author of the graces of the saints
	  The Spirit gives spiritual illumination to the believer

•   The Role of the Spirit in Faith  
	  The Spirit is the direct author of faith in all its degrees
	  The Spirit gives origin to the grace of full assurance

•   The Office of the Holy Ghost  
	  The Holy Ghost induces full assurance in our minds in connection with the evidence of our gracious character
	  Full assurance is a legitimate and undoubting conclusion from appropriate evidence

•   The Presence of the Holy Spirit  
	  The presence of the Holy Spirit is the first installment of the benefits of Christ's redemption
	  The Holy Spirit is the pledge and earnest of the completion of that redemption

•   The Induction of Full Assurance  
	  The Holy Spirit works in us to will and to do of his own good pleasure (Phil. 2:13)
	  The Holy Spirit sheds abroad the love of God in our hearts (Rom. 5:5)
	  The Holy Spirit begets us again to a lively hope (1 Pet. 1:3)

•   The Nature of Full Assurance  
	  Full assurance is not a blind and fortuitous feeling, but a legitimate and undoubting conclusion from appropriate evidence
	  Full assurance is a state of mind that it is the office of the Holy Ghost to induce in our minds

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. Infallible Assurance  

    Definition  : Infallible assurance is the certainty of salvation that comes from knowing God's promise.
	  Ephesians 1:13-14, 2 Corinthians 1:22, 5:5

  II. Attaining Infallible Assurance  

    Not essential to faith  : Infallible assurance is not a necessary part of faith, but rather a fruit of it.
	  1 John 5:13, 1 Corinthians 2:12, 1 John 4:13
    Attainable through ordinary means  : True believers can attain infallible assurance through the use of ordinary means, such as prayer and scripture.
	  2 Peter 1:10, Ephesians 3:17-19
    Duty to seek assurance  : It is the duty of every believer to seek infallible assurance through diligence and effort.
	  2 Peter 1:10, Romans 5:1-2, 14:17

  III. Shaken or Diminished Assurance  

    Causes of shaken assurance  : Assurance can be shaken or diminished due to negligence, special sin, sudden temptation, or God's temporary withdrawal of light.
	  Psalm 31:22, 51:8, 12, 14, Ephesians 4:30-31
    Never utterly destitute of God's favor  : True believers are never left without any token of God's favor, even in times of shaken assurance.
	  1 John 3:9, Luke 22:32, Psalm 51:8, 12
    Assurance can be revived  : Infallible assurance can be revived through the operation of the Spirit, even in times of shaken or diminished assurance.
	  Micah 7:7-9, Jeremiah 32:40, Isaiah 54:7-14

  IV. Conclusion  

    Infallible assurance is not of the essence of faith  : True faith exists in various degrees of strength, and infallible assurance is not a necessary part of it.
	  1 John 5:13, 1 Corinthians 2:12, 1 John 4:13
    Duty to seek infallible assurance  : It is the duty of every believer to seek infallible assurance through diligence and effort.
	  2 Peter 1:10, Romans 5:1-2, 14:17

•   Definition of Assurance  
	  Assurance is not always a full assurance
	  It relates to the certainty of one's personal relation to Christ and eternal salvation, not the truth of the object of faith

•   Types of Assurance  
	  Assurance of faith: strong faith in the truth of Christ
	  Assurance of hope: certain persuasion that one is a true believer and therefore safe

•   Essence of Faith  
	  Direct act of faith: faith in Christ and his promise
	  Reflex act of faith: inference from one's own spiritual experiences

•   Scriptural Evidence  
	  The offer of salvation in Christ is set forth in the Scriptures as a matter of trust, not certainty
	  The Bible promises salvation to classes (believers, saints), not individuals

•   Examples from Scripture

	  The Bible contains exhortations to believers to seek full assurance

•   Attainment of Assurance  
	  Assurance is not of the essence of faith, but its fruit
	  It can be attained in this life through ordinary means, without extraordinary revelation

•   Duty to Seek Assurance  
	  Attaining assurance is a duty as well as a grace
	  All that leads to it should be diligently sought, and all that prevents it should be carefully avoided

•   Characteristics of Genuine Assurance  
	  Depends on evidence of diligence in duties and strength of graces
	  Depends on the approving witness of the Holy Spirit

•   Discerning False and Genuine Assurance  
	  A false and presumptuous assurance should be discriminated from a genuine assurance

•   Consequences of Assurance  
	  Genuine assurance cannot lead to looseness and indifference in the cultivation of grace and performance of religious duties

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Moral Law  
	  Introduced to Adam as a covenant of works
	  Requires perfect obedience
	  Promises life upon fulfilling and threatens death upon breach

•   The Law After the Fall  
	  Continued to be a perfect rule of righteousness
	  Delivered by God on Mount Sinai in 10 commandments
	  Written in two tables: 4 commandments to God, 6 to man

•   The Nature of God's Law  
	  Grounded in God's all-perfect and unchangeable moral nature
	  Absolute standard of righteousness
	  Infallible judge of righteousness is the divine intelligence

•   The Binding Nature of God's Law  
	  Binds man's conscience and requires perfect obedience
	  Gives moral obligation to duty
	  Rooted in the changeless nature of God

•   The Four Classes of Divine Laws  
	  Belong to one of four classes
	  (Text does not specify what these classes are)

•   The Covenant with Adam  
	  Special covenant with Adam as the natural head of the race
	  Required perfect obedience from Adam and his descendants
	  Promised confirmation in holiness and eternal felicity upon obedience

•   The Role of the Law in Salvation  
	  Ceased to offer salvation on the ground of obedience after the fall
	  Continued to be the revealed expression of God's will
	  Binds all human consciences as the rule of life

•   The Ten Commandments  
	  Delivered by God on Mount Sinai
	  Written in two tables of stone
	  Record of the 20th chapter of Exodus

•   The Duty to God and Man  
	  The first four commandments contain our duty to God
	  The other six contain our duty to man

•   The Importance of Obedience  
	  Requires perfect obedience
	  Promises life upon fulfilling and threatens death upon breach
	  Essential for confirmation in holiness and eternal felicity

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Classification of Moral Laws  
	  There are four types of moral laws:
		- (a) Grounded in the divine nature, immutable, and irrepealable
		- (b) Based on the permanent nature and relations of men
		- (c) Based on changing relations of individuals and communities
		- (d) Dependent on the positive command of God

•   Universal Obligation  
	  Moral laws of type (a) and (b) are universally binding
	  They are obligatory because of their natural propriety and the will of God

•   Temporary Obligation  
	  Moral laws of type (c) are only binding for a specific time and place
	  They are intended to be followed only as long as the special conditions exist

•   Positive Command  
	  Moral laws of type (d) depend on the positive command of God
	  They are not universal or perpetual and only bind those to whom God has addressed them

•   Innate Moral Law  
	  The moral law is written on the heart of every person
	  It is part of Adam's original endowment and remains clear despite sin

•   Revelation of Moral Law  
	  The moral law was revealed in the human constitution
	  However, this revelation is insufficient for guiding humans in their present circumstances

•   Inspired Scriptures  
	  God has made a more full and explicit revelation of his law in the inspired Scriptures
	  The Scriptures are the only and all-sufficient rule of faith and practice

•   Scope of Moral Obligation  
	  Whatever is revealed in the Scriptures as the will of God is part of the moral law
	  Whatever is not revealed is not part of our moral obligation

•   Covenant Relation  
	  God introduced Adam into a covenant relation to the law
	  Adam's obedience to the law was the condition of his character and destiny

•   Fulfillment of the Law  
	  God sent his Son to fulfill the requirements of the legal covenant
	  The Son secured the benefits of the covenant for his elect

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Covenant of Works and the Law  
	  The Covenant of Works has been fulfilled by Christ, freeing believers from the law in that capacity.
	  However, the law remains a rule of action and standard of character.
    Christ's Fulfillment of the Law  
	  Christ fulfilled the law vicariously as the condition of salvation.
	  This does not mean that Christ was vicariously conformed to the law as a rule of conduct or moral character.
    The Holy Spirit's Work in Believers  
	  The Holy Spirit fulfills the law in believers by sanctifying them into complete conformity to it.
	  Believers bring forth good works as a result of their obedience to the law.
    The Ten Commandments as a Summary of the Law  
	  The Ten Commandments summarize the moral law of God.
	  They are not exhaustive, but rather a general outline of the principles of the law.
    The Significance of the Ten Commandments  
	  The Ten Commandments were placed under the mercy-seat, representing the all-perfect law of righteousness.
	  They teach love to God and to man, and are the foundation of God's throne.
    The Relationship Between the Decalogue and Other Scriptures  
	  Every specific duty taught in Scripture can be referred to one or more of the general precepts taught in the Decalogue.
	  The Decalogue is a summary of the moral law of God.
    The Original Writing of the Decalogue  
	  The Decalogue was written by the finger of God himself on two tables of stone.
	  The first four commandments relate to duties owed to God, and the remaining six relate to duties owed to fellow-men.
    Interpreting the Decalogue  
	  The Decalogue is the law of God, not the law of man.
	  It requires conformity of the governing affections and dispositions of the heart as well as outward actions.
    The Rules of Interpretation  
	  The law is perfect and requires perfect obedience.
	  It is spiritual, respecting thoughts, feelings, motives, and inward states of hearts as well as actions.
	  Every command implies a corresponding prohibition, and every prohibition implies a corresponding command.
    The Relationship Between Commands and Promises  
	  Every command implies a corresponding promise, and every promise implies a corresponding command.
	  Every prohibition implies a corresponding promise, and every promise implies a corresponding prohibition.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Moral Law  
	  Is binding on all people, regardless of their justification or status
	  Is not just a matter of moral duty, but also a reflection of God's authority as Creator
	  Is strengthened, not weakened, by the gospel of Christ
    Ceremonial Laws

    Christ's Relationship to the Law

	  Fulfilled the law as our representative

    The Original Laws

    The Spirit of Christianity  
	  Exalts and expands the letter of the law
	  Is an example of how the moral law is strengthened, not weakened, by the gospel
    The Relationship Between the Law and the Gospel  
	  The law and the gospel are not mutually exclusive
	  The law is a reflection of God's authority and moral standards
	  The gospel is a means of redemption and regeneration, which strengthens the obligation to obey the law
    The Continuing Authority of the Moral Law  
	  Remains unchanged under the Christian economy
	  Is a reflection of God's authority and moral standards
	  Is strengthened, not weakened, by the gospel of Christ

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Determining Abrogated Law  
	  The principles for determining which elements of the law are abrogated and which remain in force are:
		- Continued obligation of a commandment is asserted or recognized in the New Testament
		- Explicit repeal or implied abrogation in the New Testament
		- Careful examination of the reason for the law's enactment

•   Moral Law  
	  The moral law is constantly recognized in the New Testament
	  Its provisions remain in full force under the new dispensation

•   Ceremonial Law  
	  The ceremonial law was typical of Christ and his work
	  It was a "shadow of things to come" and has been superseded by Christ's work

•   Reason for Law  
	  If the original reason for a law's enactment is universal and permanent, it remains in force
	  If the reason is transient, the law's binding force is also transient

•   Mosaic Institute  
	  The Mosaic institute can be viewed in three aspects:
		- National and political covenant
		- Legal covenant
		- System of symbols

•   National and Political Covenant  
	  The Israelites became the people of Jehovah under the theocratic government
	  The Church and State were identical

•   Legal Covenant  
	  The moral law was set forth in the Ten Commandments
	  Obedience to the law was the condition of life in the Adamic covenant

•   System of Symbols  
	  The ceremonial system was a rule of works
	  The vast majority of symbols were types or prophetic symbols

•   Judicial Laws  
	  The judicial laws of the Jews have ceased to have binding obligation
	  The peculiar relations of the people to God and to one another no longer exist

•   Believers and the Law  
	  True believers are not under the law as a covenant of works
	  The law is not a means of justification for believers

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Law's Purpose  
	  To inform people of God's will and their duty
	  To direct and bind people to walk in accordance with God's will
    The Law's Uses  
	  To reveal the sinful nature of people's hearts and lives
	  To lead people to conviction, humiliation, and hatred of sin
	  To show people the need for Christ and the perfection of his obedience
    The Law's Role in Regeneration  
	  To restrain corruptions in regenerate people
	  To forbid sin and show people the consequences of their actions
	  To reveal God's approbation of obedience and the blessings of following the law
    The Law and the Gospel  
	  The law is not contrary to the gospel, but complements it
	  The Spirit of Christ enables people to do what the law requires
    The Law's Uses for Different Groups  
	  For all men: a revelation of God's character and will, a standard of moral excellence
	  For unregenerate men: to convince them of their guilt and pollution, and to lead them to Christ
	  For incorrigible sinners: to restrain their evil passions and to vindicate God's justice
	  For regenerate men: to guide their sanctification and to show them their obligations to Christ
    The Law's Relationship to Righteousness  
	  No man can attain righteousness through obedience to the law
	  The law demands perfect and perpetual obedience, which is impossible for humans
    The Law and the Gospel of Christ  
	  Those who have embraced the gospel of Christ are no longer under the law as a covenant of life
	  The law is still useful under the gospel dispensation, but in a different way
    The Law's Role in Sanctification  
	  The law remains an indispensable instrument of the Holy Spirit in the work of sanctification
	  The law shows people the extent of their obligations to Christ and how far short they are
    The Law's Purpose in Relation to the Gospel  
	  To lead people to Christ and to show them the need for his obedience
	  To reveal God's will and to direct people to walk in accordance with it
    The Law's Relationship to the Gospel's Principles  
	  The law is in perfect harmony with the principles of the gospel
	  The Spirit of Christ enables people to do what the law requires, freely and cheerfully.

Here are the 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Purpose of the Law  
	  To set up in the regenerate the habit of conviction of sin and repentance and faith
	  To present motives deterring from sin and assuring of grace
	  To lead the soul onward to a blissful attainment when the law of God becomes the spontaneous law of the spirit
    The Law and the Divine Nature  
	  The divine will sustains a relation to the divine nature
	  The law is a reflection of God's character and nature
    Classes of Divine Laws  
	  Class 1: Moral laws (e.g. the Ten Commandments)
		- Characteristics: eternal, unchanging, and universal
	  Class 2: Ceremonial laws (e.g. dietary laws, sacrifices)
		- Characteristics: temporary, abolished by Christ
	  Class 3: Judicial laws (e.g. laws governing civil relationships)
		- Characteristics: temporary, abolished by Christ
	  Class 4: Civil laws (e.g. laws governing government and society)
		- Characteristics: temporary, abolished by Christ
    The Revelation of the Law  
	  The moral law was first revealed to Adam in the Garden of Eden
	  The complete revelation of the will of God is found in the Scriptures
    The Law and the Christian  
	  The law remains binding on the elect, but they are delivered from its condemning power
	  The law is summarized in the Ten Commandments
    The Church of Rome and the Decalogue  
	  The Church of Rome has tampered with the Decalogue
	  The great principle to bear in mind in interpreting the Decalogue is to understand it as a summary of the moral law
    The Uses of the Law  
	  The law is useful for unregenerate men in view of the offers of grace in the gospel
	  The law is useful for incorrigible sinners as a means of conviction
	  The law is useful for the regenerate as a means of growth and sanctification
    Christian Liberty and Liberty of Conscience  
	  Christian liberty consists in freedom from the guilt of sin, the condemning wrath of God, and the curse of the moral law
	  Christian liberty also consists in being delivered from the present evil world, bondage to Satan, and dominion of sin

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Christian Liberty  
	  Implies an inward spiritual condition of soul that enables a person to desire and will as they ought to do in conformity to God's law.
	  Involves a change of nature through regeneration and sanctification, and a change of relation through justification.
	  Is a main element in the grace of adoption and a privilege of all children of God.
    Purchased by Christ  
	  Was purchased for us by Christ's sacrifice.
	  Is attributed to Christ (Gal. 5:1).
    Applied by the Holy Spirit  
	  Is applied and effectually wrought in us by the Holy Ghost.
	  Is attributed to the Holy Spirit (2 Cor. 3:17).
    Common to All Believers  
	  Consists of being delivered from the guilt of sin and the curse of the moral law.
	  Is common to all believers at all times.
    Delivered from Guilt  
	  The guilt of sin is cancelled when the believer is justified.
	  The demands of the law are satisfied by crediting the perfect righteousness of Jesus Christ to the believer's account.
    Delivered from Sin  
	  The believer is delivered from the bondage of sin as an inherent principle of their nature.
	  This deliverance is commenced in regeneration and perfected in sanctification.
    Contrast with Old Dispensation  
	  Believers under the new dispensation have greater liberty and freedom from the yoke of the ceremonial law.
	  Have greater boldness of access to the throne of grace and fuller communications of the free Spirit of God.
    New Testament Liberty  
	  Is further enlarged under the new testament.
	  Involves freedom from the constraining motives of fear and the coercive influences of Satan and evil men.
    Privileges of New Testament Believers  
	  Have greater knowledge of their privileges and gracious aid in availing themselves of them.
	  Have greater access to the throne of grace and fuller communications of the free Spirit of God.
    Characteristics of Christian Liberty  
	  Involves a childlike love and willing mind.
	  Is characterized by a desire to obey God's law, not out of slavish fear, but out of love and hope.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Victory and Peace with God  
	  Secures believers in their faith
	  Includes God's reconciliation to us and our reconciliation to Him
	  Delivers us from fear and bondage

•   Peace with God  
	  Includes the benefits of God's reconciliation and our reconciliation to Him
	  Fills our hearts with filial, submissive, and confiding love
	  Casts out all fear

•   Delivered from Satan and the World  
	  The power of Satan and the world depends on the corrupt state of our hearts
	  Christ's act of justification consecrates us to God
	  The work of sanctification breaks the power of temptation

•   Delivered from Afflictions and Death  
	  The sting of death is sin, and Christ has delivered us from the curse of the law
	  Believers are no longer under the law as the basis of their salvation
	  Death and its consequences are elements of God's chaste ning grace

•   Delivered from the Grave and Everlasting Damnation  
	  The first effect of redemption is the forgiveness of sins
	  The penal consequences of sins are removed
	  There is no condemnation to those in Christ Jesus

•   Christian Liberty  
	  New Testament believers enjoy a higher degree of Christian liberty than Old Testament believers
	  They are delivered from the obligation of the ceremonial law

•   Delivered from the Ceremonial Law  
	  The ceremonial law was a revelation of the gospel of the Son of God
	  It was obscured with material symbols and ceremonies
	  New Testament believers enjoy the clear light shed from Christ's person and work

•   Sanctification and Temptation  
	  The work of sanctification breaks the power of temptation
	  God enables us to resist temptation and come out conquerors
	  Satan is subject to Christ's power

•   The Holy Spirit as Earnest  
	  The Holy Spirit is the earnest of our inheritance
	  He witnesses with our spirits that we are children of God
	  He helps us to resist Satan and put him to flight

•   The Power of Christ  
	  Christ was tempted like us, yet without sin
	  He has delivered us from the curse of the law
	  He has given us the power to resist temptation and come out conquerors

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. The New Covenant  

  The new covenant provides immediate access to the Father, unlike the old covenant which required a medium of priests and an outward sanctuary.
	  This results in greater boldness in approaching God.
	  Believers have fuller communications of the Holy Spirit.

  II. The Holy Spirit  

  The new dispensation is characterized by a clearer and fuller revelation of the Holy Spirit's office and influence.
	  Christ promised the gift of the Holy Spirit in this pre-eminent measure after his ascension.
	  The Holy Spirit was not yet given until after Jesus' ascension.

  III. God's Sovereignty over the Conscience  

  God alone is Lord of the conscience, and it is free from the doctrines and commandments of men that are contrary to his Word.
	  To believe such doctrines or obey such commands out of conscience is to betray true liberty of conscience.
	  Requiring implicit faith and absolute obedience destroys liberty of conscience and reason.

  IV. Christian Liberty  

  Those who practice sin or cherish lust under the pretense of Christian liberty destroy the end of Christian liberty.
	  Christian liberty is meant to enable us to serve the Lord without fear, in holiness and righteousness.
	  This liberty is not intended to destroy, but to uphold and preserve, the powers ordained by God.

  V. Opposition to Lawful Power  

  Those who oppose lawful power or the lawful exercise of it, under the pretense of Christian liberty, resist the ordinances of God.
	  This includes opposing civil or ecclesiastical power.
	  Such actions may be lawfully called to account and proceeded against by the censures of the church.

  VI. Church Discipline  

  Those who publish or maintain opinions or practices contrary to the light of nature or the known principles of Christianity may be lawfully called to account and proceeded against by the censures of the church.
	  This includes opinions or practices that are destructive to the external peace and order established by Christ in the church.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Authority over Conscience  
	  God has set the human conscience free from obligation to believe or obey doctrines or commandments of men that contradict Scripture.
	  Believing or obeying such doctrines or commandments is a sin of betraying the liberty of conscience.
    Christian Liberty  
	  Christian liberty is not absolute, but has its end and limits.
	  Its end is to allow every person to serve God according to His will without hindrance from others.
	  Its limits are:
		- The authority of God, the Lord of conscience.
		- The equal liberties and rights of fellow-men in organized societies.
    Obedience to Authorities  
	  Obedience to legitimate authorities of the Church and State is essential to obedience to God.
	  The Church has the right to exercise discipline on those who maintain or practice opinions or actions contrary to Scripture or the peace and welfare of the Christian community.
    The Role of Scripture  
	  Scripture is the only perfect rule of faith and practice in spiritual matters.
	  Scripture is a complete and perspicuous rule that judges all controversies.
	  Scripture is the supreme judge of all human doctrines and commandments.
    The Standard of God's Will  
	  The Roman Catholic Church claims the authority to bind and loose consciences, but this is based on tradition and perverted interpretations of Scripture.
	  Protestants insist that Scripture is the only standard of God's will and that it sets the human conscience free from obligation to believe or obey doctrines or commandments of men.
    The Power of Scripture  
	  Scripture is addressed directly to believers, making them personally responsible for interpreting it and judging human doctrines and commandments.
	  Scripture is perspicuous, meaning it is clear and understandable, and therefore every believer can interpret it for themselves.
    The Limitations of Human Authority  
	  Human authorities, including the Church and State, have limitations on their power to bind and loose consciences.
	  Human authorities must respect the equal liberties and rights of fellow-men in organized societies.
    The Importance of Conscience  
	  The human conscience is a precious liberty that must be respected and protected.
	  Betraying the liberty of conscience is a sin that must be avoided.
    The Role of the Church  
	  The Church has the right to exercise discipline on those who maintain or practice opinions or actions contrary to Scripture or the peace and welfare of the Christian community.
	  The Church must respect the liberties and rights of fellow-men in organized societies.
    The Relationship between Church and State  
	  The Church and State have distinct spheres of authority, and obedience to one does not necessarily imply obedience to the other.
	  The Church and State must respect each other's liberties and rights in organized societies.

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. Scripture and Authority  
  The Bible is the ultimate authority for Christians (e.g. Dent. vi. 4 - 9; Luke i. 3; Rom. i. 7)
  All Christians are commanded to search the Scriptures and give a reason for their faith (e.g. Joh n v. 39; Acts xvii. 11; 2 Tim. iii. 15 - 17)

  2. The Church and Authority  
  The Church is not a hierarchy or body of officers, but the body of believers as such (e.g. 1 John ii. 20, 27; 1 Tim. iii. 15)
  The Church is guided by God and preserved from error (e.g. Matt. xvi. 18; Eph. v. 27; 1 Pet. ii. 5)

  3. Apostolic Authority  
  Those who claim apostolic authority are destitute of the signs of an apostle (e.g. 2 Cor. xii. 12; 1 Cor. ix. 1; Gal. i. 1, 12)
  There is no provision for the perpetuation of the apostolate (e.g. Acts i. 21, 22)

  4. Civil Magistrate and Authority  
  The civil magistrate has the right to impose religious articles of faith or rules of worship (e.g. chapter xxiii., section 3)
  However, this right is limited by the authority of Scripture and the conscience of individuals

  5. Christian Liberty  
  Christian liberty is not an absolute liberty to do as we choose, but a regulated liberty to obey God without hindrance from man (e.g. 1 Thess. iv. 3)
  Christian liberty is limited by the principles of natural right and revealed truth

  6. Limitations of Christian Liberty  
  Christian liberty must not be used to harm or injure others (e.g. 1 Cor. viii. 9)
  The liberty to eat or not eat meat offered to idols is an example of this limitation

  7. Authority of Officers  
  The authority of officers in the Church and State represents the authority of God and binds the Christian to obedience (e.g. 1 Tim. iii. 1 - 13)
  Both civil and ecclesiastical courts have the right to enforce obedience through discipline

  8. Enforcing Obedience  
  The mode of discipline used by civil and ecclesiastical courts must be appropriate to their respective spheres of authority
  The enforcement of obedience is necessary to maintain order and promote the well-being of individuals and society

  9. Sin of Disobedience  
  It is a sin to disobey God's authority and to yield to human imposition (e.g. Rom. i. 7)
  Disobedience to God's authority is a violation of our own nature as moral and rational beings

  10. Importance of Understanding Christian Liberty  
  Clearly understanding Christian liberty is of the highest importance for Christians (e.g. 1 Cor. viii. 9)
  Misunderstanding Christian liberty can lead to licentiousness and harm to others.

    What is the subject of this chapter?  
	  The subject of this chapter is Christian liberty and its various aspects.
    How does it differ from that of WCF 9?

	  This liberty includes freedom from the guilt of sin, the curse of the moral law, the bondage of sin, and the dominion of Satan and the world.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Object of Worship  
	  The only proper objects of worship are the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost.
	  Since the fall, worship must be approached through a Mediator, and only through Christ alone.
    The Nature of Worship  
	  Worship is a dictate of natural reason and conscience.
	  It is a response to God's absolute perfection, superiority, and relation to us as Creator, Preserver, and moral Governor.
    The Importance of Scripture  
	  God has given us an infallible, authoritative, complete, and perspicuous rule of faith and practice in the Holy Scriptures.
	  Scripture prescribes the mode in which we are acceptably to worship and serve God.
    The Consequences of Ignoring Scripture  
	  Neglecting Scripture's way of worship is an offence to God and a sin in us.
	  Attempting to serve God in ways not prescribed by Scripture is also an abomination to God.
    The Role of Reason and Conscience  
	  Natural reason and conscience may have sufficed to direct worship and service before the fall.
	  However, since the fall, an explicit, positive revelation is necessary to prescribe the principles and methods of worship.
    The Limits of Human Judgment  
	  Human judgment and will-worship are not acceptable to God.
	  Only the principles and general rules laid down in Scripture should guide our worship and service.
    The Application of Scripture  
	  Some circumstances concerning worship and church government can be ordered by the light of nature and Christian prudence.
	  However, these should be guided by the general rules laid down in Scripture.
    The Importance of Mediation  
	  Since the fall, worship must be approached through a Mediator, and only through Christ alone.
	  Christ is the only proper Mediator between God and humanity.
    The Nature of Religious Worship  
	  Religious worship is not to be rendered to angels, saints, or any other creature.
	  Worship is to be directed solely to the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost.
    The Supreme Worship of God  
	  The obligation to render supreme worship and devoted service to God is a dictate of nature as well as a doctrine of revelation.
	  Worship is a response to God's infinite and absolute perfection, and our absolute dependence on him.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Limits of Human Judgment  
	  Human judgment should not go beyond the clear warrant of Scripture.
	  Decisions should be made based on the varying times and circumstances of the case.

•   The Trinity and Worship  
	  The divine worship should be addressed equally to Father, Son, and Holy Ghost.
	  This is because Father, Son, and Holy Ghost are distinct persons, yet equally the one supreme God.

•   The Need for a Mediator  
	  God can now be acceptably approached only through a Mediator.
	  This is due to the guilt of man by nature and in consequence of habitual transgression.
	  It is also due to the justice of God and the fact that God has determined to deal with men through a mediator.

•   The Role of Christ as Mediator  
	  Christ represents the elect before the Father in obeying and suffering vicariously in their stead.
	  Christ makes intercession in their behalf and is the medium through which all gracious benefits come to us from God.

•   The Proper Object of Worship  
	  God is the only proper object of worship.
	  Christ is the only Mediator through whom we may approach God.

•   The Error of Worshiping Angels and Saints  
	  Religious worship should not be offered to angels, nor to saints, nor to any other creature.
	  Worship should not be offered to God through any other mediator save Christ alone.

•   The Practices of the Church of Rome  
	  The Church of Rome teaches that the Virgin Mary and saints and angels are to receive true religious worship.
	  They are to be invoked to help us in our times of need and to intercede with God or with Christ for us.

•   The Distinctions Made by the Church of Rome  
	  The Church of Rome distinguishes between Latria (highest religious worship due to God alone) and Doulia (inferior religious worship due to saints and angels).
	  They also mark a middle degree of worship, Hyperdoulia, due to the Virgin Mary alone.

•   The Objections to the Church of Rome's System  
	  The system has no support in Scripture.
	  The reasons for worshiping God do not apply to the worship of other beings.
	  Reason and revelation unite in teaching us that God stands apart from all other objects and is not to be classed as an object of worship with any other.

•   The Sin of Idolatry  
	  The sin of worshipping other gods and angels is explicitly forbidden in Scripture.
	  The worship of images, or of God, Christ, or saints by images, is forbidden in the Second Commandment.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. Critique of Roman Catholicism  

  The Roman Catholic Church's practices are not unique to them, but are common to all idolaters.
	  The sins forbidden in the First and Second Commandments are still committed by Roman Catholics.
  The invocation of saints is absurd, as they cannot hear or help us unless they are omnipresent and omniscient.
	  The Roman Catholic explanation that God may tell the saints what we pray is unconvincing.

  II. Mediators between God and Man  

  Christ is the only Mediator between God and man (1 Tim. 2:5).
	  Christ has exhaustively discharged every requisite mediatorial function.
  We are complete in Christ and should come directly to God through Christ.
	  We should come with boldness and liberty, not supplementing Christ's work with other mediators.
  The saints and angels are not fit to be mediators, as they are absent, dependent, and busy worshipping Christ.
	  They have no supererogatory merits and cannot lay a foundation for our acceptance with God.

  III. Prayer  

  Prayer is a principal part of religious worship, required by God.
	  It should be made in the name of the Son, by the help of the Spirit, and according to God's will.
  Prayer should be made with understanding, reverence, humility, fervency, faith, love, and perseverance.
	  If vocal, it should be in a known tongue.

  IV. Prayer and Intercession  

  Prayer should be made for things lawful and for all sorts of men living or that shall live hereafter.
	  However, it should not be made for the dead or for those who have sinned the sin unto death.
  We should pray for things that are lawful and for the benefit of others.
	  We should not pray for things that are not lawful or for the benefit of the dead.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Prayer is a universal duty  
	  Required of all men, regardless of knowledge or moral ability

	  Maintains harmony between Christ's intercession in heaven and our prayers on earth (Rom. 8:26-27)

•   The heart of the worshipper must be in a proper state  
	  Humility and reverence for God's majesty and moral perfections
	  Submission to God's will and confidence in his ability to help
	  Intelligent apprehension of our relations to God and the nature of our service
	  Real earnestness and fervency of heart

•   Prayer must be expressed in a common language  
	  When praying with others, use a language understood by all
	  Avoids the Romish custom of praying in Latin, which is unknown to many
	  Forbidden in Scripture (1 Cor. 14:1-33)

•   Objects of petition should cover all desirable and lawful things  
	  Depend on God for all things and should ask for everything we need
	  Give precedence to the "best things" and seek God's righteousness first (1 Cor. 12:31, Matt. 6:33)
	  Desires for unlawful things should be laid aside and repented of

•   Uncertainty about God's will should be considered  
	  Even lawful desires may be uncertain whether they align with God's will
	  Pray for God's guidance and wisdom in making decisions

•   Prayer should be offered with importunity and perseverance  
	  Luke 18:1-8 teaches us to pray with persistence and importunity
	  This is essential for effective and acceptable prayer

•   Prayer is a form of worship  
	  Includes all direct acts of worship, such as hymns and psalms
	  Prayer is a way to express our devotion and reverence for God

•   Prayer is a natural duty and a means of receiving God's blessings  
	  We are told to pray for the Holy Spirit and to pray after receiving him
	  Prayer is a way to receive God's blessings and guidance in our lives

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Intercession and Prayer  
	  Petitions should be conditional upon God's will
	  Pray for all men living or to live, but not for those already dead or those who have committed the unpardonable sin

•   The Romish Church's Doctrine on Prayers for the Dead

•   Scriptural Teachings on Death and Intercession

	  Occasional acts of worship include singing, prayer, and thanksgiving

•   The Importance of Public Worship

	  Scripture is the foundation of worship and should be read and preached regularly

•   The Importance of Sacraments and Oaths  
	  Sacraments, such as baptism and the Lord's Supper, are essential to worship
	  Oaths and vows should be made with reverence and solemnity

    The Nature of Worship  
	  Worship is a vital part of a Christian's life
	  It involves the Word, prayer, singing of psalms, and sacraments
