Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. Introduction to the Sabbath  

  The Sabbath is a divine institution that is to be continued to the end of the world.
  It is based on the Christian Sabbath and is observed as a holy day.

  II. Scriptural References  

  Exod. 20:8-11; Isa. 56:2-7 (references to the Sabbath in the Bible)
  Gen. 2:2-3; I Cor. 16:1-2; Acts 20:7 (additional references)

  III. Keeping the Sabbath Holy  

  The Sabbath is kept holy by:
	  Observing a holy rest from worldly employments and recreations
	  Engaging in public and private worship
	  Performing duties of necessity and mercy

  IV. Reasons for the Sabbath  

  The Sabbath has its ground in:
	  The universal and permanent needs of human nature
	  The economy of redemption
	  The creation of the world and all its inhabitants
	  The ascension of the crucified Redeemer
	  The eternal Sabbath of the saints

  V. Purpose of the Sabbath  

  The Sabbath is designed to:
	  Keep in remembrance the creation of the world and all its inhabitants
	  Keep in remembrance the ascension of the crucified Redeemer
	  Be a perpetual type of the eternal Sabbath of the saints
	  Afford a suitable time for public and private worship
	  Provide a suitable period of rest from labor

  VI. Universality of the Sabbath  

  The Sabbath is intended to be universal and permanent
  It is applicable to all men of all nations and in all stages of intellectual and moral development

  VII. History of the Sabbath  

  The Sabbath was introduced as a divine institution at the creation of the race
  It was observed by the Jews and Gentile nations from the earliest ages
  The law with respect to the Sabbath was incorporated into the Decalogue

  VIII. Significance of the Sabbath  

  The Sabbath is one of the ten requirements in the Decalogue
  It is put side by side with the commandments that require us to love God and honor his name

  IX. Conclusion  

  The Sabbath is a divine institution that is to be continued to the end of the world
  It is based on the Christian Sabbath and is observed as a holy day.

  X. Classification of the Sabbath  

  The Sabbath comes partly under the fourth and partly under the second class of laws
  The fourth class includes laws that have their ground in the universal and permanent state and relations of men in this world
  The second class includes laws that have their ground in the divine will and are binding only so long as commanded.

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Sabbath is a pre-existent institution  
	  Implied by the commandment to "remember the Sabbath-day, to keep it holy"
	  Originated thousands of years before the Jewish history
	  Underlying all the relations God sustains with the entire race

•   The Sabbath was made for mankind  
	  Christ says, "The Sabbath was made for man"
	  Refers to the creation of the world and the resurrection of Christ

•   The law of the Sabbath is a positive commandment  
	  Grounded in the will of God as supreme Lord
	  Dictate of reason to set apart time for worship and rest

•   The Sabbath has multiple purposes  
	  To commemorate the creation of the world
	  To commemorate the resurrection of Christ
	  To provide a permanent and recurring type of the rest of heaven

•   The time of observance was changed from the seventh to the first day of the week  
	  In the age of the apostles
	  Sanctioned by the apostles
	  Observed as "the Lord's day" in the Christian Church

•   The change was according to the will of God  
	  Because of its apostolic origin
	  Because of the importance of the resurrection of Christ
	  Because of the universal consent of Christians

•   The observance of the Christian Sabbath should be in the spirit of the gospel  
	  Not in the spirit of the law
	  In accordance with the ends for which it is instituted

•   The whole day should be consecrated to the purposes of the Sabbath  
	  Rest from worldly labor
	  Worship of God
	  Religious instruction of fellow-men

•   Nothing should interfere with the consecration of the day  
	  Except evident and reasonable demands of necessity
	  Except demands of mercy for fellow-men and dependent animals

•   The Christian Sabbath should be observed diligently  
	  To avoid all that hinders the most profitable application of the day
	  To lead fellow-men to avoid such hindrances

Here are the 56 points in 10 bullets with sub-bullets:

    The Obligation to Worship God  
	  1. God should be worshipped (dictate of natural conscience)
	  2. The grounds of the obligation are not specified
	  3. We are shut up to worship God in the ways he has prescribed
    The Manner of Worship  
	  4. Our manner of worshipping God is left open to some discretion
	  5. The only proper object of worship is God
	  6. God can be approached by men only through a Mediator
    The Worship of Saints and Angels  
	  7. The Romish Church teaches the worship and invocation of saints and angels
	  8. The Romish Church distinguishes between worship due to God and creatures
	  9. The Romish Church distinguishes between worship of images and the person represented
	  10. The worship of saints and angels is not commanded or approved by reason
    The Invocation of Saints  
	  11. The invocation of saints is a pure absurdity
	  12. Saints and angels are not mediators between us and God or Christ
    Prayer  
	  13. Prayer is a necessary act of worship
	  14. Prayer must be offered through Christ
	  15. Prayer must be offered with the help of the Holy Ghost
	  16. A state of mind is necessary on the part of one approaching God in prayer
    The Objects of Prayer  
	  17. We may pray for ourselves and others
	  18. We may pray for those not yet born
	  19. The Romish doctrine on the intermediate state and prayers for the dead is false
    Regular Worship  
	  20. Regular public worship consists of reading, preaching, and singing
	  21. Regular public worship does not depend on the acceptability of the worship
	  22. Regular public worship depends on the sincerity of the worshipper
    Secret and Family Worship  
	  23. Secret worship is a necessary act of devotion
	  24. Family worship is a necessary act of devotion
	  25. The Directory of Worship teaches on secret and family worship
    The Sabbath  
	  26. The Sabbath is a law of God
	  27. The Sabbath is designed to subserve several ends
	  28. The Sabbath is perpetual and universal
    The Observance of the Sabbath  
	  29. The Sabbath was originally enjoined upon mankind in general
	  30. The Sabbath was not an institution peculiar to the Jews
	  31. The time of observance was changed from the seventh to the first day of the week
    The Manner of Observing the Sabbath  
	  32. The first general principle determining the manner of observing the Sabbath is not specified
	  33. The second general principle determining the manner of observing the Sabbath is not specified
	  34. The whole day should be devoted to the special observance of the Sabbath

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    What is a lawful oath?  
	  A part of religious worship
	  A solemn call to God to witness the truth of what is affirmed
	  An implied imprecation of God's disfavor if one lies or breaks their promise
    When is it lawful to swear?  
	  On just occasion
	  When the person swearing is fully persuaded of the truth
	  When the oath is imposed by lawful authority
    What is the only name in which it is lawful to swear?  
	  The name of God
	  Swearing by any other name is sinful and idolatrous
    What is the purpose of an oath?  
	  To recognize God's omnipresence, omniscience, and sovereignty
	  To appeal to God's judgment as final
    What is the nature of a lawful oath?  
	  An act of supreme religious worship
	  A solemn promise to do something in the future
	  A recognition of God's authority and judgment
    What is the extent of a lawful oath's binding obligation?  
	  It cannot oblige to sin
	  It binds to performance, even if it causes harm to the person
	  It cannot be violated, even if made to heretics or infidels
    What are the exceptions to the rule of not swearing?  
	  When the oath is imposed by lawful authority
	  When the oath is taken in a matter of weight and moment
    What is the proper way to take an oath?  
	  Without equivocation or mental reservation
	  In the plain and common sense of the words
	  With all holy fear and reverence
    What is the consequence of refusing an oath?  
	  It is a sin to refuse an oath touching anything that is good and just
	  It is a sin to refuse an oath imposed by lawful authority
    What is the significance of swearing by the name of God?  
	  It recognizes God's sovereignty and authority
	  It acknowledges God's judgment as final
	  It is a sin to swear by any other name or thing.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The True God Can Be Sworn By  
	  A person of integrity can swear by a false god without compromising their integrity
	  This is evident in Scripture, where God is sworn by as the true God

•   Atheists and Infidels Should Not Swear  
	  It is dishonest for an atheist to swear at all
	  It is also dishonest for an infidel to swear on Christian Scriptures, as they do not believe in the God they are invoking

•   Scriptural Recognition of Swearing  
	  Scripture commands us to swear by the true God (Isaiah 45:23, 16:16, Deuteronomy 6:13)
	  Scripture forbids swearing by false gods (Jeremiah 5:7, Joshua 23:7)

•   The Third Commandment  
	  The literal meaning is "Thou shalt not take the name of thy God in that which is false" (Exodus 20:7)
	  This commandment prohibits taking a false oath or swearing on a trifling occasion

•   The Oath as a Religious Institution  
	  The oath is a recognized religious institution in the Old Testament (Deuteronomy 6:13, 10:20)
	  Christ and Paul both used oaths to confirm their statements (Matthew 26:63-64, Romans 1:9, 2 Corinthians 1:23)

•   Proper Occasions for Swearing  
	  Swearing is proper on serious and lawful occasions where an appeal to God's witness is necessary
	  Swearing is also proper when imposed by competent authority

•   The Oath as an Act of Divine Worship  
	  The oath should be administered and taken in a reverent manner
	  The oath should be taken with outward actions that signify the true God is being invoked

•   Interpreting and Keeping the Oath Sacred  
	  The oath should be interpreted and kept sacred by the person taking it
	  The person taking the oath should honestly believe that it is understood by the person who imposes it

•   The Obligation of the Oath  
	  The obligation of the oath arises from the original and universal obligation to speak the truth and keep faith
	  The obligation of the oath also arises from the duty to honour God and avoid dishonouring him

•   Perjury and Deception  
	  Intentionally deceiving others by invoking God's witness while lying is a sin of perjury
	  The person taking the oath must be honest in their interpretation and keep the oath sacred.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Oaths and their validity  
	  An oath cannot bind to something that is inherently unlawful.
	  The obligation of the law takes precedence over all other obligations.
	  The sin is in taking the oath, not in breaking it.

•   Oaths and impossibility  
	  An oath to do something impossible cannot bind, as it is an expression of God's will.
	  This is because the oath is not binding if it is impossible to fulfill.

•   Oaths and morality  
	  An oath to do something right and binding imposes an additional obligation to perform it.
	  This obligation is imposed by the law and voluntarily assumed by oneself.

•   Oaths and the parties involved  
	  The obligation of an oath does not depend on the goodness or badness of the person imposing it.
	  An oath to an infidel or heretic binds just as much as an oath to a saint.

•   Oaths and disadvantage  
	  The obligation of an oath binds even when a person swears to their own disadvantage.
	  This is because the obligation is to truth and reverence, not to personal gain.

•   Oaths and coercion  
	  The obligation of an oath is not impaired by violence or fraud.
	  Oaths imposed by conquerors on the vanquished are still binding.

•   Vows and their nature  
	  A vow is of the same nature as a promissory oath.
	  Vows should be made with the same religious care and performed with the same faithfulness.

•   Vows and to whom they are made  
	  Vows should be made to God alone, not to any creature.
	  Vows should be made voluntarily, out of faith and conscience of duty.

•   Vows and their purpose  
	  Vows should be made in a way that is thankful for mercy received or to obtain something desired.
	  Vows should bind oneself to necessary duties and other things that fitly conduce to them.

•   Vows and their limitations  
	  No one may vow to do something forbidden in the Word of God or what would hinder a duty commanded.
	  Vows should not be made to do something that is not in one's power or for which there is no promise of ability from God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    What is a vow?  
	  A vow is a promise or commitment made to God
	  It can be made formally or informally, orally or in writing
    Scriptural sanction for vows  
	  Ecclesiastes 5:4, Psalm 76:11, 1 Samuel 1:11, and Acts 18:18 all mention vows
	  Receiving sacraments like Baptism and the Lord's Supper involves vows
    Types of vows  
	  Vows can be general or specific, binding to loyal obedience or a particular action
	  Vows cannot bind to do something unlawful or impossible
    When a vow is not binding  
	  If made by a child or someone under authority and unable to bind themselves
	  If continued observance is inconsistent with spiritual interests
    The nature of vows  
	  Vows are binding when the matter is morally indifferent
	  However, accumulating multiple vows can be injurious and dishonor God
    Restricting vows  
	  Vows should be restricted to duties imposed by God and revealed in Scripture
	  Self-imposed duties can be a burden and endanger safety
    The importance of vows  
	  Vows are a form of religious worship and should be taken seriously
	  They should be made in the name of the true God
    Who can make vows  
	  Only those who are able to bind themselves can make vows
	  Children and those under authority cannot make vows
    When a vow fails  
	  If the matter of the vow is unlawful or impossible
	  If continued observance is inconsistent with spiritual interests
    The lesson of experience  
	  Experience teaches that multiplying vows can be injurious
	  Vows should relate to things indifferent or duties antecedently binding
    The principle of vows  
	  The obligation of a vow rests on the principle of promise and commitment
	  Vows should be made with the help of divine grace

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. God's Ordination of Civil Magistrates  

  God has ordained civil magistrates to be over the people for his own glory and the public good.
	  They are armed with the power of the sword to defend and encourage the good, and to punish the evil.
	  This is based on Romans 13:1-4 and 1 Peter 2:13-14.

  II. Christians and Civil Magistrates  

  It is lawful for Christians to accept and execute the office of a magistrate.
	  They should maintain piety, justice, and peace according to the laws of each commonwealth.
	  They may lawfully wage war on just and necessary occasions.
	  Examples include Genesis 41:39-43, Nehemiah 12:26, and 1 Timothy 2:2.

  III. The Duty of Obedience  

  Civil government is a divine institution, and obedience to legitimate rulers is a duty owed to God as well as to fellow men.
	  The right or legitimate authority of human government comes from God's will, not from human consent or social compact.
	  This is based on the following reasons:
		- God is the Creator and absolute Possessor of all men.
		- He has formed humans as intelligent, morally responsible, and free agents.
		- He is the supreme moral Governor of all moral agents.
		- He has constituted humans as social beings in families and communities, making civil government necessary.
		- He uses civil government as an instrument to promote the ends of redemption and build his kingdom in the world.

  IV. God's Providence in Civil Government  

  God has not prescribed a particular form or order of succession for civil government.
	  He has laid the general foundation for the duty and necessity of government in human consciences and social natures.
	  He has left every people free to choose their own form of government, according to their circumstances and as instructed by his Word.
	  God has established civil government among men from the beginning, and among all peoples and nations, of all ages and generations.
	  The God-man as mediatorial King has assumed the government of the universe, and his headship extends to the whole and every part.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Sovereignty and Providence  
	  God's ultimate goal is to bring good and glory to his people
	  All things work together for good to his people and for his glory
	  Enemies will be subdued and judged

•   Jesus as Ruler of the Universe  
	  Jesus is the present providential Governor of the physical universe
	  He is the King of the Jews and Lord of Lords
	  All nations and rulers should acknowledge and serve him

•   Purpose of Magistrates  
	  The proximate end of magistrates is to promote the public good
	  The ultimate end is to promote God's glory
	  Magistrates should make God's glory their governing purpose

•   Promoting God's Glory  
	  Magistrates should promote the good of the community in temporal concerns
	  This includes education, morals, physical prosperity, and protection of life and property
	  Magistrates should also promote piety and order

•   Christian Magistrates  
	  Christian magistrates should promote piety as well as order
	  They should do this through example and impartial protection of the Church
	  They should enact and enforce laws in the true spirit of the Gospel

•   Accepting and Executing Magisterial Office  
	  It is lawful for Christians to accept and execute magisterial office
	  Christians have a greater obligation to be qualified for such roles
	  Magistrates should be Christians to discharge their duties for God's glory

•   Waging War  
	  Christian magistrates may lawfully wage war on just and necessary occasions
	  The right and duty of self-defense is established by nature, reason, and the Word of God
	  War is an incalculable evil, but sometimes necessary

•   General Principles of War  
	  War is an evil that destroys lives, occasions misery, and causes moral degradation
	  Deciding when to go to war is difficult and not our place to consider
	  Principles of war should be guided by the Gospel and the will of God

•   Limitations of Magistrates  
	  Magistrates should not assume the functions of the Church
	  They should not attempt to patronize or control the Church
	  They should give impartial protection and facility to the Church

•   God's Glory as Chief End  
	  God's glory is the chief end of every man, nation, and community
	  It should be the governing purpose of every individual in all relations and actions
	  All other objects and designs are subordinated to God's glory

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   War and National Existence  
	  War is only justified if it's necessary to preserve national existence.
	  The enemy must aim to do the nation a wrong that threatens national life.
	  War must be the only means to avert the threat.

•   Just War Principles  
	  A war may be defensive in spirit and intent, but aggressive in conduct.
	  The question of right depends on the purpose, not the manner or location of the attack.
	  Every other means of securing justice and maintaining national safety should be exhausted before war.

•   Civil Magistrates and the Church  
	  Civil magistrates should protect the church without interfering with matters of faith.
	  They should ensure the liberty of ecclesiastical persons to discharge their sacred functions.
	  No law should interfere with the regular government and discipline of the church.

•   Protection of People and Assemblies  
	  Civil magistrates should protect the person and good name of all their people.
	  They should prevent indignity, violence, abuse, or injury to any person.
	  All religious and ecclesiastical assemblies should be held without molestation or disturbance.

•   Duty to Magistrates  
	  People should pray for magistrates, honor their persons, and pay tribute.
	  They should obey lawful commands and be subject to authority for conscience' sake.
	  Infidelity or difference in religion does not exempt people from obedience.

•   Limitations on Magistrates  
	  Magistrates should not assume the administration of the Word and sacraments.
	  They should not interfere in matters of faith or the government of the church.
	  The pope has no power or jurisdiction over magistrates or their people.

•   Relationship between Church and State  
	  The Church and State are divine institutions with different objects and spheres of action.
	  They are independent of each other and owe mutual good offices.
	  This is opposed to the Papal doctrine of the relation of the State to the Church.

•   Independence of Church and State  
	  The Church has its own government and officers, separate from the State.
	  The State has its own government and officers, separate from the Church.
	  This independence is essential for the proper functioning of both institutions.

•   Papal Doctrine vs. Reformed View  
	  The Papal doctrine sees the State as subordinate to the Church.
	  The Reformed view sees the Church and State as equal and independent institutions.

•   Consequences of Papal Doctrine  
	  The Papal doctrine would lead to the subordination of the State to the Church.
	  This would undermine the independence of the State and the Church.
	  It would also lead to the concentration of power in the hands of the Pope.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Church and State Relationship  
	  The Church has authority over civil rulers for certain ends
	  The Pope has historically intervened in civil matters, imposing interdicts and deposing sovereigns
	  The Church's authority is subordinate to the great end of the Church's existence

•   Erastian Doctrine  
	  Regarded the State as a divine institution responsible for spiritual and temporal matters
	  The civil magistrate was seen as responsible for supporting the Church and appointing its officers
	  This doctrine prevailed in European State Churches

•   Our Confession's Teaching  
	  The State is independent of the Church and has civil jurisdiction over ecclesiastical persons
	  The Church has no authority to apply civil pains or disabilities to coerce men to obey its laws
	  The civil magistrate has no official jurisdiction in Church affairs

•   Religious Liberty  
	  Every man has the right to worship God according to his own conscience
	  Ecclesiastical rulers are not allowed to coerce men to obey Church laws
	  The civil magistrate is bound to protect Church members and organizations

•   Separation of Church and State  
	  The same person can be a civil magistrate and a Church member or officer
	  Their jurisdictions are entirely independent and separate
	  The civil magistrate's jurisdiction is not affected by their Church office

•   Obedience to Civil Magistrates  
	  Obedience to civil magistrates is a duty binding on all subjects for conscience' sake
	  This obedience is to God, as civil government is an ordinance of God
	  Obedience should be from the heart and for conscience' sake, not of constraint

•   Limitations on Obedience  
	  Obedience is limited when commanded to do something contrary to God's authority
	  Obedience is also limited when the civil government has become corrupt and no longer accomplishes its ends

•   Rebellion  
	  Rebellion is a grievous sin, as it is disobedience to God
	  Rebellion works permanent physical ruin and social demoralization

•   Civil Government's Purpose  
	  Civil government is an ordinance of God, established for certain ends
	  The powers that be are ordained of God for these ends

•   Principles of Our Confession  
	  Our Confession teaches that the State is independent of the Church
	  Our Confession teaches that religious liberty is an inalienable prerogative of mankind
	  Our Confession teaches that the civil magistrate has no official jurisdiction in Church affairs

Here are the 30 questions and answers summarized in 10 bullets with sub-bullets:

    The Role of Civil Government  
	  The first proposition taught in the first and second sections of this chapter is that civil government is a divine ordinance.
	  Civil government is ultimately founded on God's sovereignty over the world and its inhabitants.
	  The proof from general facts and Scripture is that civil government is a divine ordinance.
    The Form of Government  
	  God has left the decision of the particular form of government to be adopted by any people to the people themselves.
	  The circumstances and rule that determine the choice of government are not specified.
	  Civil government was originally instituted by God as Creator.
    The Supreme Ruler  
	  The divine person who is now the supreme Ruler among the nations and head of all governments is God the Father.
	  The proof is from Scripture, specifically Psalm 2:7-8.
    The Purpose of Civil Government  
	  The ultimate end to promote which the civil magistrate is appointed is the glory of God.
	  The proximate end is the promotion of peace, justice, and piety.
	  The civil magistrate is to promote the public good in the special sphere of civil government.
    War and Obedience  
	  It is lawful for Christians to accept civil office.
	  The only proper excuse for war is self-defense against a legitimate attack.
	  A Christian people should attempt to resolve conflicts peacefully before appealing to war.
    The Church and State  
	  The Papal doctrine is that the State is subject to the Church.
	  Our Confession teaches that the State is not subject to the Church.
	  The Erastian doctrine is that the Church is subject to the State.
    Marriage and Divorce  
	  Marriage is a divine institution ordained by God.
	  The ends designed to be promoted by marriage are specified: mutual help, increase of mankind, and prevention of uncleanness.
	  It is lawful for all sorts of people to marry, but Christians should marry only in the Lord.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Marriage is a divine institution  
	  Ordained by God
	  Based on the creation of man and woman
	  Governed by the law of God

•   Marriage is a religious and civil contract  
	  Pledges vows to God, each other, and society
	  Involves civil obligations and consequences (e.g. property, children)

•   The State must define the nature and effects of marriage  
	  Must not contravene the laws of God
	  Must be made in conformity with God's laws

•   Christians must obey God rather than men  
	  When laws conflict with God's laws, Christians must follow God's laws
	  Examples of conflict in Great Britain and America

•   The main ends of marriage are  
	  Mutual help of husband and wife
	  Increase of mankind with legitimate issue
	  Increase of the Church of Christ with holy seed
	  Prevention of uncleanness

•   Marriage is a contract for life between one man and one woman  
	  Based on God's original law and nature
	  Proven to be physically, economically, and morally adapted to achieve the ends of marriage

•   Polygamy is inconsistent with human nature and the relations of the sexes  
	  Defeats the ends of marriage
	  Not adapted to achieve the goals of marriage

•   The law of God is dispensable in special cases  
	  Can be dispensed by the Lawgiver
	  Ceases to be binding and its non-observance ceases to be sin

•   Moses and other biblical figures were exceptions to the law  
	  Dispensed with the law in special cases
	  Did not set a precedent for others to follow

•   Christians must follow God's laws in matters of marriage  
	  Must not follow human laws that conflict with God's laws
	  Must act in accordance with God's laws, even if it means taking consequences.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's original intention for marriage  
	  God created man and woman in paradise
	  Marriage was ordained as a good and pure institution
	  It is a type of the mystical union between Christ and the Church

•   The law of monogamy  
	  God's law of monogamy was disregarded by the ancestors of the Israelites
	  Christ restored the law of marriage to its original basis

•   Adultery in marriage  
	  Adultery occurs when a person marries another while their spouse is still alive
	  The sole exceptions are divorce due to fornication or the death of a spouse

•   Marriage and spiritual elevation  
	  The Roman Catholic Church teaches that celibacy is meritorious and conducive to spiritual elevation
	  Protestants deny this, arguing that marriage is a good and pure institution

•   The purpose of marriage  
	  Marriage is a means of good in itself, except when abused by man
	  It is the best condition for bringing out and educating moral instincts and faculties

•   The experiment of celibacy  
	  The Roman Catholic Church's experiment with celibacy has shown its degrading tendency
	  Protestants argue that marriage is not a hindrance to spiritual elevation

•   The role of marriage in the Christian life  
	  Marriage is not essential for Christian service, but may be a hindrance in certain circumstances
	  Some types of service, such as missionary work, may be more efficiently accomplished without marriage

•   Intermarriage with false or ungodly religions  
	  The principle of not intermarrying with false or ungodly religions touches on the perfection of marriage, not its validity
	  Paul teaches that a Christian remains bound by a previous marriage unless the ungodly partner abandons them

•   The validity of marriage  
	  The validity of marriage is not affected by the faith of the parties involved
	  A Christian remains bound by a previous marriage unless the other partner voluntarily abandons them

•   The importance of marriage  
	  Marriage is a good and pure institution, ordained by God
	  It is a means of bringing out and educating moral instincts and faculties, and is essential for human flourishing

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Marriage between Christians and non-Christians  
	  Marriages between converted and unconverted individuals are valid and should be respected
	  However, true Christians should not contract such alliances due to spiritual incompatibility
	  This can lead to desecration of the marriage ordinance or spiritual depression for the believer

•   Marriage within degrees of consanguinity or affinity  
	  Marriage within forbidden degrees of consanguinity or affinity is incestuous and invalid
	  The Word of God prohibits such marriages (Lev. 18:6-17, 24-30; Lev. 20:19; 1 Cor. 5:1; Amos 2:7)

•   Incestuous marriages  
	  No law of man or consent of parties can make an incestuous marriage lawful
	  The man may not marry any of his wife's kindred nearer in blood than his own, and the woman may not marry any of her husband's kindred nearer in blood than her own

•   Adultery or fornication after a contract  
	  Detection of adultery or fornication before marriage gives just occasion to dissolve the contract
	  In the case of adultery after marriage, it is lawful for the innocent party to sue out a divorce and marry another

•   Divorce  
	  Nothing but adultery or willful desertion that cannot be remedied by the church or civil magistrate is cause sufficient for dissolving the bond of marriage
	  A public and orderly course of proceeding is to be observed in such cases

•   Incest  
	  Incest consists of sexual intercourse between parties forbidden by the divine law to marry
	  Marriage between these parties is impossible and void ab initio

•   Degrees of incest  
	  The crime of incest differs according to the varying degrees of relationship
	  The obligation to avoid intermarriage between near blood-relations is a dictate of nature as well as of the Word of God

•   Levitical law on incest  
	  The Levitical law recorded in Lev. 18:6-23 and 20:10-21 is the only law on this subject in the Scriptures
	  If this law is still binding, it carries with it the principle that it is incest for a man to cohabit with any one of his deceased wife's relations nearer in blood than it is lawful for him to do of his own

•   Churches' views on incest  
	  The Greek and Roman Catholic Churches agree that the Levitical law on incest is still binding
	  All branches of the Protestant Church (Episcopal, Lutheran, and Presbyterian) have maintained the same principle

•   Marriage and spiritual compatibility  
	  True Christians should not contract marriages that may lead to spiritual incompatibility or depression
	  Spiritual compatibility is essential for a healthy and fulfilling marriage

•   Divorce and remarriage  
	  In the case of adultery after marriage, it is lawful for the innocent party to sue out a divorce and marry another
	  The innocent party is free to remarry as if the offending party were dead (Matt. 19:9; Rom. 7:2-3)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Law of Marriage  
	  Marriage is a divine institution (Rom. vii. 2, 8)
	  It is a contract for life between one man and one woman
    Causes for Divorce  
	  Adultery is a valid cause for divorce (Matt. v. 31, 32; xix. 9)
	  Willful, causeless, and incurable desertion is also a valid cause (1 Cor. vii. 15)
    Effect of Divorce  
	  Divorce does not dissolve the marriage bond, but gives the right to demand dissolution (Rom. vii. 2, 8)
	  The innocent party must seek vindication of their rights through public authorities
    Civil Law and Divorce  
	  Civil law has no authority to grant divorces on grounds other than adultery and willful desertion
	  Civil authorities that grant divorces on other grounds are in conflict with the law of God
    Null and Void Divorces  
	  Unlawful divorces are null and void
	  Parties to such divorces are considered to be living in sin if they remarry
    Marriage as a Religious and Civil Contract  
	  Marriage is both a religious and civil contract
	  This means that both divine and human laws apply to marriage
    Conflict between Divine and Human Law  
	  In cases of conflict, divine law should take precedence over human law
	  Human laws that contradict divine law are considered null and void
    Civil Laws of Marriage  
	  Civil laws of marriage in England have erred by allowing divorces on grounds other than adultery and willful desertion
	  Civil laws in this country have also erred in similar ways
    Main Ends of Marriage  
	  Marriage is designed to promote the well-being and happiness of the parties involved
	  It is also designed to promote the well-being and happiness of children
    Polygamy and Celibacy  
	  Polygamy is not lawful according to the original law of marriage
	  Celibacy is not considered a superior state of life by Protestants, but rather a personal choice.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Church  
	  The Church is the universal body of Christ, composed of all believers throughout history.
	  The Church is invisible, consisting of all the elect who have been, are, or will be gathered under Christ.
	  The Church is the spouse, body, and fullness of Christ, who fills all in all.
    The Visible Church  
	  The visible Church is the collective body of all those who profess the true religion throughout the world.
	  The visible Church includes the children of believers.
	  The visible Church is the kingdom of the Lord Jesus Christ, the house and family of God.
    The Ministry and Ordinances of God  
	  Christ has given the ministry, oracles, and ordinances of God to the Church for the gathering and perfecting of the saints.
	  The Church has been given the power to make the ordinances of God effectual through Christ's presence and Spirit.
    The Definition of the Church  
	  The word "catholic" means universal, and is the proper title of the true Church of Christ.
	  The word "church" is a collective term including the whole body of the "called" or "elect".
    The Promises of the Gospel  
	  All the promises of the Gospel are addressed to the Church, the collective body of the "effectually called".
	  The Church is the pillar and ground of the truth, the body and fullness of Christ, and the Bride, the Lamb's wife.
    The Judgment of the Church  
	  The Church is the only means of salvation, and there is no ordinary possibility of salvation outside of it.
	  The Church is the kingdom of the Lord Jesus Christ, and the house and family of God.
    The Judgment of the Christian Church  
	  Historically, the Christian Church has judged that the Levitical law is no longer binding.
	  The prevailing opinion and practice of the Church in recent times is that the Levitical law is not binding.
    Marriage and Divorce  
	  The Biblical law of incest is found in Leviticus and teaches that marriage within the forbidden relationship is impossible.
	  The law of God prohibits marriage within certain degrees of affinity and relationship.
    The Dissolution of Marriage  
	  Only certain causes justify the dissolution of a marriage by human tribunals.
	  No other causes justify divorce.
    Obtaining a Divorce  
	  A divorce must be obtained through the proper channels, and the civil authorities must be informed.
	  Christian and church courts must act in accordance with the law of God, even if the civil authorities have granted a divorce.

•   The Church  
	  Refers to a collective body of believers
	  Can be referred to as a particular church or a group of churches
	  Can also refer to a Christian family or household

	  Is called "invisible" because its true members are not always visible

	  Many who really belong may not be manifest

	  To become a more perfect manifestation of the invisible Church

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Visible Church  
	  The visible Church is composed of all organized Christian bodies
	  Despite imperfections, they are all one visible Church
	  This is evident because they are all visible parts of the true spiritual Church, which is the body of Christ

•   Membership in the Visible Church  
	  A credible profession of the true religion makes a person a member of the visible Church
	  A credible profession is one that is sufficiently intelligent and corroborated by daily life
	  This profession is ground for the presumption that the person is a member of the true Church

•   Nature of the Visible Church  
	  The visible Church is called "the kingdom of heaven" on earth
	  Its nature and progress are described in various parables in Matthew 13

•   Children of Professors  
	  The children of all professors of the true religion are fellow-members with their parents in the visible Church
	  This principle will be discussed and proved in chapter 28, section 4

•   Gifts and Agencies of the Visible Church  
	  The visible Church has been given the inspired Scriptures as an infallible oracle and rule of faith and practice
	  The Gospel ministry is an order indicated by the gifts and graces of the Holy Ghost
	  The ordinances of the visible Church include preaching, prayer, singing, baptism, the Lord's Supper, and discipline

•   Purpose of the Visible Church  
	  The great end designed to be accomplished by the visible Church is the gathering in of the elect and the perfecting of the saints
	  This is to be accomplished through the agencies of the visible Church, including the Scriptures, the Gospel ministry, and the ordinances

•   Success of the Visible Church  
	  The success of these agencies in attaining their end is secured by the promise of Christ to be with them and to render them effectual until the end of the world
	  This promise is found in Matthew 28:20

•   Ordinary Possibility of Salvation  
	  Out of the bounds of the universal visible Church, there is no ordinary possibility of salvation for adults
	  This proposition applies only to adults who are out of the pale of the visible Church
	  Members of the human race who die in infancy are believed to be saved through the merits of Christ

•   Conditions of Salvation  
	  To be saved, a person must confess Christ before men, i.e., become a public, visible professor of the true religion
	  The conditions of salvation are laid down in Romans 10:9-10

•   Importance of Public Profession  
	  Every man who hears the gospel is commanded to confess Christ before men
	  This is the only way to be saved, according to the conditions laid down in Romans 10:9-10

•   The Importance of Confessing Christ  
	  Confessing Christ is a necessary step in salvation
	  This confession can take many forms, but typically involves joining the community of Christ's people and taking sacraments
	  Failure to confess Christ publicly can be equivalent to rejecting Him, especially in the face of shame or persecution

•   The Nature of the Visible Church

	  The church is more or less visible and pure depending on the doctrine, worship, and membership of its constituent parts

	  Some churches have degenerated to the point of becoming "synagogues of Satan"
	  Despite these imperfections, there will always be a church on earth that worships God according to His will

•   The Head of the Church  
	  The Lord Jesus Christ is the only head of the church
	  The Pope of Rome is not the head of the church and is, in fact, the Antichrist

•   The Nature of the Church's Purity  
	  The purity of the church is determined by its doctrine, worship, and membership
	  The church is more or less pure depending on the zeal and spiritual character of its members
	  The church is also more or less pure depending on its discipline and ability to maintain a pure membership

•   The Visibility of the Church  
	  The church is more or less visible depending on its purity and the degree to which it is distinguished from the world
	  The church is more visible when its doctrine, worship, and membership are more pure and its members are more zealous and spiritual

•   The Impermanence of the Church's Purity  
	  The church's purity is a matter of degree and can vary over time and in different sections
	  The church's purity is also affected by the imperfections of its members and the presence of error and mixture

•   The Role of Discipline in the Church  
	  Discipline is an important factor in maintaining the purity of the church's membership
	  The church's ability to discipline its members and maintain a pure membership is a key factor in its overall purity

•   The Importance of Zeal and Spiritual Character  
	  The zeal and spiritual character of the church's members are key factors in its overall purity
	  The church is more or less pure depending on the degree to which its members are zealous and spiritual

•   The Universality of the Church  
	  The church is universal and consists of all professing Christians and their organizations
	  The church is more or less visible and pure depending on the doctrine, worship, and membership of its constituent parts.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Church's Potential for Corruption  
	  The Church can become corrupt and lose its character as a true church of Christ.
	  This has happened in the past, such as during the reign of Ahab and in the ancient churches founded by the apostles.

•   The Church's Deterioration  
	  The ancient churches in the East and Northern Africa have undergone significant deterioration.
	  The Church of Rome has abandoned the faith and obscured the glory of Christ, while Protestant churches have made an equal apostasy.

•   The Church's Infallibility  
	  The Church of Rome claims that the promise of Christ secures the infallible orthodoxy and purity of the visible organization.
	  However, this is not true, as the true Church is the invisible body of the elect or true believers of all nations and ages.

•   The Promise's Fulfillment  
	  The promise of Christ has been fulfilled in the sense of a spiritual, invisible Church, but not in the sense of a visible, organized Church.
	  The Roman Church has led the defection from the faith and practice of the apostolic Church.

•   The Church's Attributes  
	  The attributes ascribed to the true Church in Scripture prove it to be spiritual and invisible.
	  Examples of these attributes include being called, saints, and the body of Christ.

•   The Church's Visibility  
	  The true Church can never entirely fail from the earth, according to divine promise.
	  The visible Church can be obscured by heresies or lessened by defection, but it can never be entirely wanting.

•   The Church's Headship  
	  The Lord Jesus Christ is the only absolute and supreme Head of the Church.
	  This is self-evident and abundantly asserted in Scripture.

•   The Church's Governance  
	  Many have maintained that the visible Church needs an earthly visible head to administer its government and laws.
	  However, this is not necessary, as the Church can be governed by the Spirit of Christ.

•   The Church's Representative  
	  The Church of Rome claims that the Pope is the vicar and minister of Christ's power.
	  However, this is not supported by Scripture, which shows that Christ is the only Head of the Church.

•   The Church's True Nature  
	  The true Church is the invisible body of the elect or true believers of all nations and ages.
	  It is not an outward, organized succession, but a spiritual and invisible entity.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Erastian State Churches  
	  The Church of England and the Church of Germany have acknowledged their sovereigns as supreme heads of the Church and State.
	  The Church of England recognized Henry VIII as the supreme head of the Church of England.

•   Supremacy of the Sovereign  
	  The supremacy of the sovereign over the Church is an article of faith in the Church of England.
	  The Queen's majesty has the chief power in the realm of England and other dominions.

•   Claims to Ecclesiastical Supremacy  
	  Claims to ecclesiastical supremacy are a matter of fact and evidence.
	  If Christ delegated his authority to the Pope or national sovereigns, they should be obeyed.
	  If they have no such authority, their claims are blasphemous and treasonous.

•   The Invisible Head of the Church  
	  In the absence of a visible head of the Church, we are forced to depend on the great invisible Head.
	  The invisible Head presides over and governs the Church through:
		- His inspired Word (the Bible)
		- Apostolical institutions (ministry, sacraments, ordinances)
		- His own spiritual presence

•   The Antichrist  
	  The word "Antichrist" occurs in the New Testament in 1 John and 2 Thessalonians.
	  Interpreters have differed as to whether it refers to a personal opponent or a system antagonistic to Christ.
	  The authors of the Confession likely meant that the Papal system is antic hristian in spirit and form.

•   The Invisible Church  
	  The promises of the Gospel are addressed to the invisible spiritual Church.
	  The Church is called "invisible" because it is not a physical institution, but a spiritual body.

•   The Word "Catholic"  
	  The true sense and right application of the word "catholic" is a matter of debate.
	  The word "catholic" means "universal" or "general".

•   The Word "Church"  
	  The word "church" in the New Testament refers to the assembly of believers.
	  The word "church" has different senses and usages in the New Testament.

•   The Universality of the Invisible Church  
	  Our Confession teaches that the invisible Church is universal and includes all believers.
	  The invisible Church is not limited to a particular institution or denomination.

•   The Reasons for the Church's Invisibility  
	  The Church is called "invisible" because it is not a physical institution, but a spiritual body.
	  The Church is invisible because it is not limited to a particular time or place.
