Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Nature of Union with Christ  
	  The union between Christ and his people is based on the eternal purpose of the Triune God (Eph. 1:4)
	  The union is established through the eternal covenant of grace between the Father and Christ as the mediator (John 17:2, 6)
    The Foundation of Union  
	  The foundation of the union is the decree of election (Eph. 1:4)
	  The union is based on the covenant of grace, which provides for the salvation of believers through Christ
    The Nature of the Union  
	  The union is federal and representative, with Christ acting as the federal Head of believers (1 Cor. 6:17)
	  The union is vital and spiritual, with the Spirit of Christ dwelling and working in believers (1 Cor. 6:17; 1 John 3:24)
	  The union involves the entire person of believers, including their bodies and souls (1 Cor. 6:15, 19)
    The Manner of Union  
	  The union is established through the powerful operation of the Spirit of Christ (Eph. 2:5)
	  The union is also established through the actings of faith on the part of believers, who grasp Christ and appropriate his grace (Eph. 3:17)
    The Characteristics of Union  
	  The union is illustrated by the relation between a foundation and its superstructure (1 Pet. 2:4-6)
	  The union is also illustrated by the relation between a tree and its branches (John 15:5), and between the members of the body and the head (Eph. 4:15, 16)
    The Intimacy of Union  
	  The union is so intimate and excellent that it transcends all other unions of which we have experience
	  The union is called "mystical" because it is incomparably intimate and excellent, but not mysterious in the sense of involving confusion between Christ's personality and ours
    The Mutual Duties of Believers  
	  Believers have mutual duties towards one another as saints (Eph. 4:28; Acts 5:4)
	  The communion of saints grows out of their union with each other (Heb. 13:1)
    The Fellowship between Christ and Believers  
	  The fellowship between Christ and believers results from their union with him (John 1:14; 20:17)
	  The fellowship involves a mutual interchange of offices between Christ and believers (Col. 1:18-19; 1 Cor. 8:6)
    The Communion of Saints  
	  The communion of saints grows out of their union with each other (Heb. 13:1)
	  The communion involves a mutual interchange of offices between believers (Col. 1:18-19; 1 Cor. 8:6)
    The Importance of Union  
	  The union between Christ and believers is the foundation of their spiritual life and salvation (Gal. 2:20)
	  The union is essential for believers to live in Christ and draw their resources from him (Eph. 3:17)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. The Union between Christ and Believers  
	  The union is a union of persons, not a union of natures
	  Each person retains their separate identity
	  The believer is exalted and blessed, but still subordinate to Christ

  2. Fellowship between Christ and Believers  
	  They have fellowship with Christ in all covenant merits of his active and passive obedience
		  They are complete in him (Col. 2:10)
		  They have his Father, inheritance, throne, and crown
	  They have fellowship with Christ in the transforming power of his life
		  They receive the Spirit and the mind of Christ (John 1:16)
		  They bear his likeness and image (Rom. 8:9; Phil. 2:5)
	  They have fellowship with Christ in all their experiences, joys, and sufferings
		  They are involved in Christ's labors, sufferings, and temptations (Rom. 8:37; 2 Cor. 12:9)

  3. Christ's Fellowship with Believers  
	  They belong to Christ as the purchase of his blood
	  They are devoted to his service and are co-workers with him in building up his kingdom
	  They bear fruit to his praise and shine as stars in his crown

  4. The Unity of Believers  
	  All true believers are united to Christ as the common Head of the whole body
	  They must be intimately united together as members of one body
	  The individual is united to Christ through the Holy Ghost and by faith

  5. The Church as the Body of Christ  
	  The holy catholic Church is the product of the Holy Ghost
	  Wherever the Spirit is, there the Church is
	  The presence of the Spirit is known by his fruits (Gal. 5:22-23)

  6. The Baptism of the Holy Ghost  
	  All believers receiving the same Spirit are baptized into one body
	  They become members of the body of Christ and members in particular (1 Cor. 12:13-27)

  7. The Communion of Saints  
	  True believers sustain many intimate relations and discharge many important offices for one another
	  They have a common Head, common duties, and common profession
	  They have a common life and one Holy Ghost dwelling in and binding them together

  8. Common Life and Sympathy  
	  They are involved in the ties of sympathy and identity of interest
	  One cannot prosper without all prospering with him
	  One cannot suffer without all suffering with him

  9. Common Reputation and Honor  
	  They have a common reputation and are all severally and collectively honored or dishonored with each other
	  All schisms in the body are injurious to the whole

  10. The Importance of Unity  
	  The unity of believers is essential to their witness and testimony in the world
	  They must stand together as one body in the eyes of the world

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Body of Saints  
	  Each member of the body of saints is an organ of the Holy Ghost for a special function.
	  Each member has its own individual difference of qualification and duty.
	  The body of saints is like the natural body, with each member contributing its special function and grace.
    The Communion of Saints  
	  The communion of saints is an intimate union among true believers.
	  This union is nourished by the believers' intelligence and advancement in grace.
	  All branches of the visible Church and individual members should act on the principle of the communion of saints.
    The Nature of the Communion of Saints  
	  The communion of saints is one in the eternal electing love of God.
	  It is one in the purchase of Christ's sacrificial blood.
	  It is one in the beatifying indwelling of the Holy Ghost.
	  It is one in the eternal inheritance of glory.
    Mutual Duties of the Saints  
	  The saints should regard and treat each other as saints and heirs together with them of the grace of life.
	  They should be one in all charities, sympathies, and helpful offices.
	  These mutual duties are public and private, relating to the souls and bodies of the saints.
    The Rule of Love  
	  The law of love in the heart should guide the saints' actions.
	  The principles and examples of saints recorded in Scripture should be applied to individual cases.
    The Limits of the Communion of Saints  
	  The communion of saints does not supersede the fundamental principles of human society.
	  The rights of property and the family tie are not superseded by the communion of saints.
    The Union of the Believer and Christ  
	  The union of the believer and Christ is the foundation of the communion of saints.
	  This union is federal, vital, and spiritual.
	  It involves the entire person.
    The Office of the Holy Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit is the office of the Holy Spirit in respect to the union of the believer and Christ.
	  The Holy Spirit is the one who accomplishes this union.
    The Great Practical Consequence  
	  The great practical consequence of our union with Christ is that we should be one in all charities, sympathies, and helpful offices.
	  This union should be reflected in our actions and relationships with each other.
    The Principal Particulars Involved in the Communion of Saints  
	  The communion of saints involves the union of all true believers with the Lord and with each other.
	  It involves the mutual duties of the saints, including charities, sympathies, and helpful offices.
	  It involves the rule of love and the principles and examples of saints recorded in Scripture.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Chapter 27: Of the Sacraments  

    Definition of a Sacrament  
	  A sacrament is an ordinance immediately instituted by Christ.
	  It consists of two elements: an outward, sensible sign and an inward, spiritual grace.
    Parts of a Sacrament  
	  The outward and sensible sign is used according to Christ's own appointment.
	  The inward and spiritual grace is signified by the sign.
    Nature of a Sacrament  
	  There is a spiritual relation or sacramental union between the sign and the thing signified.
	  The names and effects of the one are attributed to the other.
    Purpose of Sacraments  
	  To represent Christ and his benefits.
	  To confirm our interest in Christ.
	  To put a visible difference between those who belong to the church and the rest of the world.
    Consequences of Sacraments  
	  To solemnly engage believers to the service of God in Christ.
	  To bind us to Christ's service and distinguish us from the world.
    Scriptural Basis  
	  The sacraments are based on Scripture, including Romans 4:11, Genesis 17:7, and Matthew 28:19.
    Old Testament Sacraments  
	  The Old Testament sacraments of Circumcision and others are considered part of the general sense of the word "sacrament".
    Etymology of the Word "Sacrament"  
	  The word "sacrament" comes from the Latin "sacramentum", meaning an oath or pledge.
	  In classical usage, it referred to anything that binds or brings under obligations.
    Usage of the Word "Sacrament"  
	  In ecclesiastical usage, the word "sacrament" was used to refer to Christian ordinances such as Baptism and the Lord's Supper.
	  The word was also used to refer to other religious doctrines and ordinances.
    Definition of a Sacrament  
	  A sacrament is an ordinance that represents Christ and his benefits, and is designed to seal and apply those benefits to believers.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Definition of a Sacrament  
	  A sacrament is a holy ordinance instituted by Christ in his Church.
	  This definition excludes most of the pretended sacraments of the Church of Rome.

•   Elements of a Sacrament  
	  Every sacrament consists of two elements: an outward, sensible sign and an inward, spiritual grace.
	  The outward sign is a physical object or action that represents the spiritual reality.

•   Baptism  
	  The outward sign of Baptism is water applied in the name of the Triune God.
	  The inward, spiritual grace signified is spiritual purification, the indwelling of the Holy Ghost, and union with Christ.

•   Lord's Supper  
	  The outward signs of the Lord's Supper are bread and wine.
	  The inward, spiritual grace signified is Christ crucified, union with Christ, and the indwelling of the Spirit.

•   Sacramental Union  
	  There is a spiritual relation or sacramental union between the sign and the thing signified in every sacrament.
	  This union is symbolical and representative, and instrumental in conveying the spiritual reality.

•   Grounds of Sacramental Union  
	  The natural fitness of the sign to symbolize the grace signified.
	  The authoritative appointment of Christ that these signs, rightly used, shall truly represent and convey the grace they signify.
	  The spiritual faith of the believing recipient, a gift of the Spirit of Christ.

•   Interchangeability of Sign and Thing Signified  
	  Whatever is true of the grace signified is asserted of the sign which signifies it.
	  To eat the bread and drink the wine in the Lord's Supper is to eat the flesh and drink the blood of Christ.

•   Baptism and Spiritual Purification  
	  Baptism is a means of spiritual purification by the immediate personal power of the Holy Ghost.
	  The water of Baptism symbolizes spiritual purification, and the Holy Ghost is the one who actually purifies the soul.

•   Lord's Supper and Union with Christ  
	  The Lord's Supper is a means of union with Christ, the indwelling of the Spirit, and regeneration.
	  The bread and wine of the Lord's Supper symbolize Christ's flesh and blood, and the spiritual reality of union with Christ.

•   Spiritual Faith and Sacramental Union  
	  Spiritual faith is necessary for the sacramental union to be effective.
	  The believing recipient must have the gift of the Spirit of Christ to discern the Lord's body and participate in the spiritual reality of the sacrament.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Sign and the Grace  
	  The sign of baptism (water) is not the same as the spiritual effect (baptism by the Holy Ghost)
	  The spiritual attributes of baptism belong to baptism by the Spirit, not the sign itself
	  The sign is only accompanied by the spiritual effect when the sign is accompanied by the Spirit

•   The Purpose of Sacraments  
	  To represent the benefits of Christ and the new covenant
	  To be "seals" of the benefits of the new covenant
	  To apply the benefits of the new covenant to believers

•   Sacraments as Signs  
	  Sacraments are outward, sensible signs signifying inward and spiritual grace
	  They present truths to the senses in a way analogous to preaching the Word

•   Sacraments as Seals  
	  Sacraments are pledges of God's promise to invest believers with grace if they believe and obey
	  In receiving a sacrament, believers assume the obligations implied in the gospel and bind themselves to fulfill them

•   Sacraments as Applications  
	  Sacraments actually convey the benefits of the new covenant to believers
	  They are not just symbolic, but have a real effect on believers

•   The Relationship between Sign and Grace  
	  The sign is not inseparable from the grace
	  The grace is sovereign and can be absent from the sign

•   The Role of Faith  
	  Faith is required to receive the benefits of the new covenant
	  Sacraments are not a substitute for faith, but rather a means of applying the benefits of faith

•   The Efficacy of Sacraments  
	  The efficacy of sacraments is not tied to the moment of administration
	  The right use of sacraments can lead to the real exhibition and conferment of grace by the Holy Ghost

•   The Importance of Understanding Sacraments  
	  The sacraments are not just empty rituals, but have a real significance and effect on believers
	  Understanding the purpose and nature of sacraments is essential for a proper understanding of the Christian faith

•   The Authority of Scripture  
	  Scripture teaches that sacraments are a means of applying the benefits of the new covenant
	  Scripture is the ultimate authority on the nature and purpose of sacraments.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The sacraments have no inherent power or virtue  
	  The power to convey grace comes from the Holy Spirit, not from the sacraments themselves.
	  The sacraments are a means of divine appointment, not a source of power.

•   The sacraments are instruments of divine appointment  
	  The Holy Spirit uses the sacraments as a means to convey grace to those who are worthy.
	  The sacraments are not a substitute for faith, but rather a means of expressing and receiving God's grace.

•   The sacraments mark us as God's property  
	  The sacraments bind us to our duty to God and mark us as belonging to the Church.
	  They give visibility to the Church and separate its members from the world.

•   The efficacy of the sacraments does not depend on the administrator  
	  The personal piety or intention of the person administering the sacrament is not what makes it effective.
	  The efficacy of the sacrament depends on the work of the Holy Spirit and the word of institution.

•   The efficacy of the sacraments depends on the word of institution  
	  The word of institution contains a promise of benefit to worthy receivers.
	  This promise is what makes the sacrament effective, not the power of the sacrament itself.

•   The sacraments do not confer grace automatically  
	  The Romanist view that the sacrament confers grace automatically is rejected by the Confession.
	  The sacrament is not a magical means of conferring grace, but rather a means of expressing and receiving God's grace.

•   The efficacy of the sacraments depends on the sovereign power of the Holy Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit is the one who conveys grace through the sacraments.
	  The sacraments are not a substitute for the Holy Spirit's power, but rather a means of expressing and receiving that power.

•   The sacraments are not a means of producing a moral impression  
	  The sacraments were not devised by man as a means of producing a moral impression.
	  They were appointed by God as a means of conveying grace to those who are worthy.

•   The sacraments are a means of expressing and receiving God's promise  
	  The sacraments are a means of expressing and receiving God's promise of benefit to worthy receivers.
	  This promise is what makes the sacrament effective, not the power of the sacrament itself.

•   The sacraments are a means of conveying God's grace to those who are worthy  
	  The sacraments are a means of conveying God's grace to those who are worthy, not to those who are unworthy.
	  The sacraments are not a means of conferring grace automatically, but rather a means of expressing and receiving God's grace.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Purpose of Sacraments  
	  Sacraments are used to seal God's gracious covenant with believers
	  Christ invests believers with the grace of the covenant through sacraments

•   The Efficacy of Sacraments  
	  The efficacy of sacraments resides in the personal agency of the Holy Ghost
	  The Holy Ghost uses sacraments as instruments and media of operation

•   The Number of Sacraments  
	  There are only two sacraments ordained by Christ: Baptism and the Lord's Supper
	  These sacraments are distinct from other ordinances that are not sacraments

•   Definition of a Sacrament  
	  A sacrament is a visible sign signifying an inward, spiritual grace
	  A sacrament represents, seals, or confers Christ and the benefits of the new covenant

•   Other Ordinances  
	  Penance, Confirmation, and Extreme Unction are not divine institutions
	  Marriage and Orders are not sacraments because they do not meet the definition of a sacrament

•   Administration of Sacraments  
	  Only a lawfully ordained minister can administer the sacraments
	  This is not due to any priestly theory of the ministry, but rather because of the Church's organizational structure

•   The Church's Structure  
	  The Church is an organized society with laws executed by regularly appointed officers
	  Ordinances, including sacraments, can only be administered by the highest legal officers of the Church

•   The Sacraments of the Old Testament  
	  The sacraments of the old testament were the same in substance as those of the new testament
	  The difference is that the old testament sacraments were more prospective and typical, while the new testament sacraments are more commemorative and spiritual

•   The Changeless Covenant  
	  The old and new dispensations are two different modes of administering the one changeless covenant of grace
	  The sacramental seals of the covenant must be essentially the same in both dispensations

•   The Difference between Old and New Testament Sacraments  
	  The difference between old and new testament sacraments is one of form and purpose
	  Old testament sacraments were more gross and carnal, while new testament sacraments are more spiritual and commemorative.

Here are the 44 questions with 10 bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Sacraments and Baptism  

    What is a sacrament?  
	  A sacrament is a rite of initiation that signifies spiritual regeneration
	  It is a means of grace that conveys spiritual benefits
    Classical usage of the word sacrament  
	  Refers to a sacred or mysterious act
	  Not necessarily related to Christian theology
    Early ecclesiastical usage of the word  
	  Refers to a rite or ceremony that conveys spiritual benefits
	  Often associated with baptism and the Lord's Supper
    Definition of a sacrament  
	  Involves a visible sign and an inward spiritual grace
	  The sign is a means of conveying the spiritual grace

  Baptism and the Lord's Supper  

    Baptism as a sacrament  
	  The outward visible sign is water
	  The inward spiritual grace is regeneration and new life in Christ
    The Lord's Supper as a sacrament  
	  The sensible sign is bread and wine
	  The inward spiritual grace is union with Christ and participation in his body
    Sacramental union  
	  The sign and the grace are intimately connected
	  The sign is not just a symbol, but a means of conveying the spiritual grace

  Roman Catholic and Lutheran views  

    Roman Catholic view of sacramental union  
	  The sign and the grace are inseparable
	  The sign is not just a symbol, but a means of conveying the spiritual grace
    Lutheran view of sacramental union  
	  The sign and the grace are intimately connected
	  The sign is a means of conveying the spiritual grace, but not in a magical or mechanical way

  Design and purpose of sacraments  

    Design of sacraments  
	  To represent the benefits of Christ and the new covenant
	  To convey spiritual benefits to believers
    Sacraments as seals of the covenant of grace  
	  They are a visible representation of the covenant between God and his people
	  They convey spiritual benefits and confirm the believer's profession of faith

  Administration and efficacy of sacraments  

    Administration of sacraments  
	  They can only be administered by a lawfully-ordained minister
	  The efficacy of the sacrament depends on its divine appointment and the sovereign will of God
    Efficacy of sacraments  
	  They convey spiritual benefits to believers
	  The efficacy of the sacrament depends on its divine appointment and the sovereign will of God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Baptism is a sacrament of the New Testament  
	  Ordained by Jesus Christ
	  Solemn admission to the visible church
	  Sign and seal of the covenant of grace
	  Regeneration, remission of sins, and new life
    Purpose of Baptism  
	  Ingrafting into Christ
	  Partaking of the benefits of Christ's covenant
	  Engagement to be Christ's
    Outward element of Baptism  
	  Water
	  Used in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost
	  Administered by a lawfully called minister
    Method of Baptism  
	  Not necessary to dip the person into the water
	  Can be administered by pouring or sprinkling water
    Baptism is a washing of the subject with water  
	  Represents spiritual purification and consecration
    Christian Baptism is an ordinance instituted by Christ  
	  Designed to be observed in the Church until the end of the world
    Distinguishing features of Christian Baptism  
	  Done in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost
	  Introduces men into the fellowship of the Christian Church
    Baptism is not the same as John's baptism  
	  John's baptism was unto repentance, not into the faith of Christ
	  Did not introduce men into the Christian Church
    The true warrant of Christian Baptism  
	  Comes from Jesus Christ's command in Matthew 28:18-20
    Importance of obeying Christ's command  
	  Imposes the obligation of perpetual observance of Baptism

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Baptism is a command that continues until the second coming of Christ  
	  The command has never been recalled
	  The reason for its observance remains the same as when it was given
	  The command reaches all nations and until the end of the world (aion)

•   The action of baptism is a washing with water  
	  In the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost
	  By a lawfully ordained minister

•   The controversy between Baptists and other Christians is not about mode  
	  The real issue is the meaning of the command to baptize
	  Baptists believe baptism is a command to immerse, while others believe it is a command to wash with water

•   The mode of washing is indifferent  
	  As long as it is decent, the method of washing is not important
	  The essential matter is the water and the application of the water in the name of the Trinity

•   The word "baptizo" means to dip, moisten, or wash  
	  In classical usage, it refers to purification or washing
	  Dr. Carson admits that he has all the lexicons against him

•   The Septuagint uses "bapto" and "baptizo" interchangeably  
	  In Dan. iv. 33, Nebuchadnezzar is said to have been wet with the dew of heaven
	  In Ecclus. xxxiv. 30, purification is performed by sprinkling

•   In the New Testament, "baptizo" is used interchangeably with "nipto"  
	  To baptize is used interchangeably with to wash
	  The washing was to effect purification, for the unbaptized hands are called unwashed and unclean

•   The common mode of washing hands in ancient countries was to pour water  
	  The rich had servants to pour water on their hands, while the poor poured water on their own hands

•   The dispute between John's disciples was about purification  
	  John iii. 25; iv. 2

•   The idea of baptism as purification is uniformly expressed in the New Testament  
	  Mark vii. 2-8 refers to the baptisms of cups, pots, and tables, which could not be immersed
	  Heb. ix. 10 refers to the first tabernacle and its divers baptisms, which were for purification.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Baptism with water is an emblem of spiritual baptism  
	  Spiritual baptism is called "the washing of regeneration, and renewing of the Holy Ghost" (Tit. 3:5)
	  Water baptism represents spiritual baptism, which unites us to Christ and makes us one with him

•   Spiritual baptism carries consequences  
	  Unites us to Christ in his death, resurrection, and new life
	  Makes us one with Christ in his righteousness, inheritance, etc.

•   Water baptism does not symbolize immersion  
	  The same action cannot symbolize burial, putting on clothes, and planting trees
	  The real order is: washing with water represents washing of the Spirit, which unites us to Christ

•   Baptism of the Holy Ghost is not an immersion  
	  It is described as a "pouring" and "sprinkling" in Scripture (Acts 2:1-4, 32-33; 10:44-48; 11:15-16)
	  The gift of the Holy Ghost is said to be "poured out" and "shed forth" (Isa. 44:3; 52:15; Ezek. 36:25-27; Joel 2:28-29)

•   The Jewish method of purification was by sprinkling  
	  The hands and feet of priests were washed at the brazen laver (Ex. 30:18-21; 2 Chron. 4:6; 1 Kings 7:27-39)
	  Water poured out through spouts or cocks, making sprinkling the prevalent manner of purification

•   The Israelites were baptized unto Moses in the cloud and in the sea  
	  This is described in 1 Cor. 10:1-2 and Ex. 14:19-31
	  The Egyptians who were immersed were not baptized, and the Israelites who were baptized were not immersed

•   Baptism is the antitype of the salvation of the eight souls in the ark  
	  This is described in 1 Pet. 3:20-21
	  The very gist of their salvation consisted in their not being immersed

•   There is no recorded instance of immersion in baptism  
	  Among all the recorded instances of Baptism performed by John the Baptist and the apostles, there is no mention of immersion
	  Many instances are highly improbable due to the vast multitudes baptized at one time and the scarcity of water

•   The apostles and early converts were accustomed to purify by pouring and sprinkling  
	  As Jews, they would have been familiar with the Jewish method of purification by sprinkling
	  This would have made immersion an unlikely method of baptism

•   The situations in which baptism took place often made immersion impractical  
	  The eunuch was baptized on the roadside in a desert country (Acts 8:26-39)
	  Three thousand were baptized in one day in the dry city of Jerusalem (Acts 2:37-41)
	  The jailer was baptized in prison at midnight (Acts 16:25-33)
	  Paul was baptized by Ananias right at his bedside (Acts 9:17-18)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Baptism in the Bible  
	  Baptism is a command of Christ (Matt. 28:18-20)
	  Baptism is a seal of the covenant of grace (Rom. 4:11-12)
    Manner of Baptism  
	  The earliest pictorial representations of baptism show pouring (2nd-3rd century)
	  Eastern Christians still use pouring at baptism
    Essential Elements of Baptism  
	  Baptism must be done in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit
	  This is based on the explicit command of Christ (Matt. 28:18-20)
    Design of Baptism  
	  To signify, seal, and confer the benefits of Christ's redemption
	  To symbolize the washing of regeneration and renewing of the Holy Spirit
    Subjects of Baptism  
	  Adults who profess faith in Christ and obedience to him
	  Infants of believing parents (Gen. 17:7-14, Gal. 3:9, 14)
    Requirements for Adult Baptism  
	  Profess faith in Christ and obedience to him
	  Demonstrate a competent knowledge of Christian truths
	  Make a consistent profession of personal faith and promise of obedience
	  Demonstrate a consistent walk and conversation
    Responsibility for Baptism  
	  The individual seeking baptism bears the responsibility
	  Church officers have no authority to judge the genuineness of a person's faith
    Additional Requirements for Some Churches  
	  Some churches, like Covenanting Presbyterian brethren, require adherence to certain "Testimonies" in addition to the profession of faith
	  These "Testimonies" are non-fundamental and denominational in nature

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Church is Christ's fold  
	  The Church is designed for all of Christ's people
	  Baptism and the Lord's Supper are common rights for all believers

•   Exclusion from communion is not justified  
	  Making terms of communion that Christ has not made is presumptuous
	  Excluding someone from communion based on human-made rules is not justified

•   Infants of believers should be baptized  
	  The Standards teach that an infant with one or both believing parents should be baptized
	  The faith of the parent brings the infant near to God

•   God's covenants include children  
	  Every covenant God has formed with mankind has included the child with the parent
	  Examples include the covenants with Adam, Noah, Abraham, and Israel

•   The Old Testament Church is the same as the New Testament Church  
	  The covenant made with Abraham is the "gospel" (Gal. 3:8)
	  Faith was the condition of salvation in the Old Testament as well as the New Testament

•   Circumcision represented spiritual grace  
	  Circumcision was the seal of the Abrahamic covenant and represented a spiritual profession
	  It was the seal of the righteousness of faith (Rom. 2:28-29)

•   Baptism has taken the place of circumcision  
	  Baptism has the same spiritual significance and binding force as circumcision
	  Those who are baptized into Christ have put on Christ (Gal. 3:27)

•   The Church has not changed  
	  The ancient prophecies declare that the same old Church is to be enlarged, not changed (Isa. 49:13-23; 49:1-14)
	  The ancient covenant, which was the fundamental charter of the Church, included "many nations" (Gen. 17:4; Rom. 4:17-18; Gal. 3:8)

	  Faith was the condition of salvation in the Old Testament as well as the New Testament
	  Abraham believed God and was imputed with righteousness (Rom. 4:3)

•   The Church is founded on faith and obedience  
	  The Church has the same foundation, faith and obedience, as the Old Testament Church
	  The sacraments of the Church have the same spiritual significance and binding force as the Old Testament sacraments.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Church remains the same  
	  The Jewish Church remains the same through all change, with Jewish branches being cut off and Gentile branches being grafted in (Rom 11:18-24)
	  The Church is not a new entity, but rather the same Church with a new branch (Eph 2:11-22)

•   Infants were members of the Church under the Old Testament  
	  Infants were circumcised upon the faith of their parents, signifying their membership in the Church
	  Circumcision bound them to the same conditions of membership as baptism does today

•   Baptism has taken the place of Circumcision  
	  Since baptism has taken the place of circumcision, the church membership of children of professors should be recognized in the same way
	  The only ground for not baptizing children would be if Christ explicitly turned them out of their ancient birth-right in the Church

•   Christ and the apostles treated children as members of the Church  
	  Christ spoke of children as remaining in the same church relation they had always occupied (Matt 19:14, Luke 18:16)
	  The apostles were commissioned to disciple all nations, including children, by baptizing and then teaching them (Matt 28:18-19)

•   Children are considered "holy" or "saints" in the New Testament  
	  Children of Christian parents are referred to as "holy" or "saints" in 1 Cor 7:14
	  This is a common designation of church members in the New Testament

•   The practice of baptizing households is consistent with the New Testament  
	  In the Acts and Epistles, there are only 11 recorded cases of baptism, and in 5 of these cases, large crowds were baptized
	  In the remaining cases, the households of the baptized individuals were also baptized

•   The apostles addressed children as members of the Church  
	  Compare Eph 1:1 with Eph 6:1-3, and Col 1:1-2 with Col 3:20
	  The apostles treated children as members of the Church, rather than outsiders

•   The majority of God's people have believed and practiced infant baptism  
	  The early Church, in unbroken continuity from the days of the apostles, testified to their custom of baptizing infants
	  The Greek, Roman, Lutheran, and Reformed Churches all agree on this fundamental point

•   The Baptist denomination is a modern party  
	  The Baptist denomination, which opposes the whole Christian world on this matter, dates back to the Anabaptists of Germany in 1687
	  This is a relatively modern development in Christian history

•   Our Standards teach that infants should be baptized  
	  The Standards teach that infants of members of the visible Church should be baptized (S. Cat. q. 95)
	  This is consistent with the practice of the early Church and the majority of Christian denominations.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. Definition of Infant Baptism  

  The church membership of a child follows from that of their parent.
  The church requires parents to pray with and for their child, set an example of piety, and bring up their child in the nurture and admonition of the Lord.

  II. Privileges of Church Membership  

  Baptized members have no right to come to the communion until they make a profession of personal faith.
  Suspended rights include communing and having their children baptized.

  III. Personal Faith and Baptism  

  A person destitute of personal faith cannot commit to the baptismal covenant without committing perjury and sacrilege.
  It is a sin for a minister to help someone make the baptismal covenant without personal faith.

  IV. Grace and Salvation  

  Grace and salvation are not inseparably annexed to baptism.
  Not all baptized people are undoubtedly regenerated.

  V. Efficacy of Baptism  

  The efficacy of baptism is not tied to the moment of time when it is administered.
  The grace promised is offered, exhibited, and conferred by the Holy Ghost to those who belong to it, according to God's will.

  VI. Administration of Baptism  

  The sacrament of baptism is to be administered but once to any person.
  This is based on Romans 6:3-11.

  VII. Key Teachings  

  1. Grace and salvation are not inseparably united to Baptism.
  2. Neglecting baptism is a great sin, but its observance is commanded.
  3. The efficacy of Baptism is not tied to the moment of time when it is administered.
  4. The sacrament of Baptism is to be administered but once to any person.

  VIII. Intermediate Ground  

  The church's position on baptism is intermediate between two extremes:
	  Papists and Ritualists' view of baptismal regeneration.
	  The view that baptism is unnecessary for salvation.

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Baptism's efficacy is not unique  
	  The efficacy of Baptism is not distinct from the efficacy of the truth (James 1:18, John 17:19, 1 Peter 1:28)
	  The mere hearing of the truth does not save anyone

•   Baptism cannot be the only means of regeneration  
	  Faith and repentance are prerequisites of Baptism (Acts 2:38, 8:37, 10:47)
	  Baptism is not the only means of regeneration

•   Universality of Baptism does not guarantee regeneration  
	  Experience in Romanist and Ritualistic communities shows that baptized individuals are not generally regenerated (Matthew 7:20)
	  The fruits of regeneration are faith and repentance, not Baptism

•   Our Standards oppose the extreme views on Baptism  
	  Our Standards reject the view that Baptism is a mere sign of grace and badge of Christian profession
	  Our Standards also reject the view that Baptism is the only means of regeneration

•   The efficacy of Baptism is due to the moral power of the truth  
	  The efficacy of Baptism results from the moral power of the truth it symbolizes
	  The efficacy of Baptism is not due to any spiritual or magical quality of the water

•   The efficacy of Baptism is due to the covenant of grace  
	  The efficacy of Baptism results from the fact that it is a seal of the covenant of grace
	  The efficacy of Baptism is due to the legal form of investing individuals with the graces promised in the covenant

•   The efficacy of Baptism is due to the Holy Spirit's operation  
	  The efficacy of Baptism results from the personal presence and sovereignly gracious operation of the Holy Spirit
	  The Holy Spirit uses the sacrament as his instrument and medium

•   The necessity of Baptism arises from the divine command  
	  Obedience to the divine command is necessary where there is knowledge
	  Baptism is the proper and only efficient method of making a profession of faith and allegiance to Christ

•   Baptism should not be administered more than once  
	  The symbolical significance of the rite signifies spiritual regeneration and the inauguration of the divine life
	  Baptism is the rite of initiation into the Christian Church, and there is no provision for getting out of the Church once in

•   Only lawfully-ordained ministers can baptize  
	  The precise action indicated in the command to baptize requires a lawfully-ordained minister
	  Only lawfully-ordained ministers have the authority to administer Baptism

Here are the 58 points summarized in 10 bullets with sub-bullets:

    The Baptist Position on Baptism  
	  12. The Baptist position is that baptism is a symbol of faith, not a means of salvation.
	  13. Essential according to the Baptist view is faith, while according to the Baptist view, it is faith and repentance.
    The Meaning of Baptizo  
	  14. The classical usage of the word baptizo is to immerse or dip.
	  15. It occurs 65 times in the Septuagint, often referring to ritual purification.
	  16. In the New Testament, it refers to spiritual purification.
    Baptism in the New Testament  
	  17. The disciples of John used the term baptism to refer to John's ritual immersion.
	  18. In the New Testament, baptism is used to refer to spiritual immersion.
    The Purpose and Practice of Baptism  
	  19. Water baptism is emblematic of spiritual death and resurrection.
	  20. Baptism by the Holy Ghost carries with it the consequence of spiritual rebirth.
    The Mode of Baptism  
	  23. The generally prevalent mode of effecting the rite of purification among the Jews was immersion.
	  24. 1 Cor 10:1-2 and 1 Pet 3:20-21 suggest that baptism was by immersion.
    The Responsibility of Baptism  
	  27. It is essential that the rite should be performed in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost.
	  28. The first design of Baptism is to symbolize spiritual death and resurrection.
	  29. The second design of Baptism is to symbolize spiritual rebirth.
    Infant Baptism  
	  31. The pastor and church session are competent to require and judge the prerequisites for Baptism.
	  32. Ultimately, the responsibility for Baptism rests with the parents.
    The Church's Teaching on Baptism  
	  33. Some churches require applicants for Baptism to make a credible profession of Christianity.
	  34. This requirement is unwarrantable because it is not taught in Scripture.
    The History and Practice of Baptism  
	  36. The Gospel Church existed under the Old Testament, and faith was the condition of salvation.
	  37. God's covenants with mankind include the children with the parents.
	  38. Circumcision had the same spiritual meaning as Baptism.
	  39. Baptism has taken the precise place of Circumcision.
	  40. The Church under the new is identically the same with the Church under the old dispensation.
	  41. Infants were recognized as members of the ancient Church.
	  42. Infant baptism follows as a necessary consequent.
    The Efficacy of the Sacraments  
	  55. The doctrine as to the efficacy of the sacraments is held between two extremes by our Church.
	  56. The Romish and Ritualistic doctrine holds that the sacraments are a means of salvation.
	  57. The doctrine of baptismal regeneration cannot be true.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Lord's Supper  
	  Instituted by Jesus on the night of his betrayal
	  To be observed perpetually to the end of the world
	  A commemoration of Jesus' death and sacrifice

•   Sources of Efficacy  
	  The sacraments derive their efficacy from God's promise and covenant
	  The Bible and Christian tradition affirm the efficacy of the sacraments

•   Baptism  
	  Presupposes as well as conveys grace
	  Necessary for salvation and spiritual growth
	  To be administered only once to each person

•   Necessity of Baptism  
	  Grounded in God's command and promise
	  Necessary for spiritual nourishment and growth
	  Essential for communion with Christ and the church

•   Design of the Lord's Supper  
	  A commemoration of Jesus' death and sacrifice
	  A seal of the gospel covenant and its benefits
	  A badge of Christian profession and allegiance

•   Seal of the Covenant  
	  Christ's blood is the seal of the covenant of grace
	  The Lord's Supper is a symbol of Christ's blood and sacrifice
	  A pledge of communion with Christ and the church

•   Perpetual Obligation  
	  The Lord's Supper is to be observed perpetually to the end of the world
	  Grounded in the apostolic example and tradition
	  Universal practice of the Christian Church

•   Design and Effect  
	  To commemorate Jesus' death and sacrifice
	  To seal the gospel covenant and its benefits
	  To signify and effect communion with Christ and the church

•   Christian Profession  
	  The Lord's Supper is a badge of Christian profession
	  A mark of allegiance to the kingdom of heaven
	  A solemn commitment to self-consecration and gospel requirements

•   Communion with Christ  
	  The Lord's Supper signifies and effects communion with Christ
	  In his person, offices, and precious fruits
	  A bond and pledge of communion with Christ and the church

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. The Nature of Communion  

  The Lord's Supper is a sacrament where believers feed on Christ's body and blood spiritually, not corporally.
	  The bread represents Christ's flesh, and the wine represents his blood.
	  Believers receive the symbol with their mouth, but receive the flesh and blood symbolically, yet really, through faith.

  II. The Purpose of Communion  

  The Lord's Supper shows forth and effects the mutual communion of believers with each other.
	  Believers are united as members of one body and of one blood.
	  Union with Christ implies communion with each other in Christ.

  III. The Institution of Communion  

  Christ instituted the Lord's Supper to commemorate his one offering on the cross.
	  The Supper is a spiritual oblation of praise to God for Christ's sacrifice.
	  The popish sacrifice of the mass is abominably injurious to Christ's one sacrifice.

  IV. The Role of Ministers  

  Ministers are appointed to declare Christ's word of institution, pray, and bless the elements.
	  They set the elements apart for holy use and take and break the bread and cup.
	  They give the elements to communicants, but only to those present in the congregation.

  V. Private Masses and Idolatry  

  Private masses, receiving the sacrament alone, and denying the cup to the people are contrary to the nature of the sacrament.
	  Worshiping the elements, lifting them up, or carrying them about for adoration is also contrary to the institution of Christ.

  VI. The Outward Elements  

  The outward elements in the sacrament have a relation to Christ crucified, but remain truly and only bread and wine.
	  They are sometimes called by the name of the things they represent, but in substance and nature, they remain bread and wine.

  VII. Transubstantiation  

  The doctrine of transubstantiation is repugnant to Scripture, reason, and common sense.
	  It overthrows the nature of the sacrament and has led to manifold superstitions and idolatries.

  VIII. The Form of the Statements  

  The statements in the text are negative, opposing certain Romish and Ritualistic errors.
	  The errors opposed include the doctrine of transubstantiation and other practices contrary to the institution of Christ.

  IX. The Errors Opposed  

  The errors opposed include:
	  The doctrine of transubstantiation
	  Private masses and receiving the sacrament alone
	  Denying the cup to the people
	  Worshiping the elements
	  Lifting them up or carrying them about for adoration

  X. Conclusion  

  The true doctrine of the sacrament is opposed to these errors and is based on Scripture and reason.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. Essential Elements of the Sacrament  

    Bread  : essential because it is in the command and symbolizes spiritual food for the soul
	  Unleavened bread is not necessary, but common bread of daily life is preferred
    Wine  : essential because it is commanded and exemplified by Christ, and has been the uniform custom of the Christian Church from the beginning

  2. Sacramental Actions  

    Consecration  : includes repetition of Christ's words and a prayer invoking divine blessing
	  Sets apart the elements for a sacred use
    Breaking of the Bread  : symbolizes the rending of Christ's body on the cross and the unity of communicants
    Distribution and Reception  : essential part of the ordinance, completed when the elements are received and eaten/drunk by the people

  3. True Relation between Sign and Grace  

  The sign (elements) points to the reality (grace) of Christ's sacrifice
  The sacrament is a means of remembering and participating in Christ's death until he comes

  4. Papal Errors  

    Transubstantiation  : the doctrine that the substance of bread and wine is changed into the literal body and blood of Christ
	  Implies that Christ is objectively present and eaten/drunk by every recipient, believer and unbeliever alike
    Lutheran Error  : while the bread and wine remain, the real body and blood of Christ are present at the words of consecration

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Eucharist Doctrine  
	  The Reformed churches believe that Christ's body and blood are present in, with, and under the bread and wine.
	  They base this doctrine on the word of our Lord, "This is my body."
    Grounds for the Doctrine  
	  The Reformed churches hold that the word "is" in "This is my body" is literal.
	  They cite examples of similar usage of the word "is" in Scripture.
    Reasons for Rejecting the Doctrine  
	  It is not taught in Scripture.
	  It confounds the idea of sacrament, making the sign identical with the thing it signifies.
	  It contradicts our senses, as we see, smell, taste, and feel bread and wine, but not flesh and blood.
	  It contradicts reason, as qualities cannot exist without a substance.
    The Mass as a Sacrifice  
	  The Council of Trent teaches that the Eucharist is both a sacrament and a sacrifice.
	  As a sacrifice, it is a constantly repeated real, although bloodless, expiatory sacrifice, atoning for sin and propitiating God.
    Reasons for Rejecting the Mass as a Sacrifice  
	  It is not taught in Scripture.
	  The Christian ministry are never called or spoken of as priests, but as teachers and rulers.
	  The one sacrifice of Christ on the cross was perfect and excludes all others.
	  The same ordinance cannot be both a sacrament and a sacrifice.
    Consequences of Transubstantiation  
	  The Papists hold that the entire substance of the bread and wine is permanently changed into the body, blood, soul, and divinity of Christ.
	  They preserve the host carefully shut up in the pyx, elevate and adore, and carry it about in their processions.
    Fear of Loss of Christ's Substance  
	  The Papists fear that some of Christ's substance may be spoiled or lost from the crumbling of the bread or the spilling of the wine.
	  They prepare the bread in little wafers that cannot crumble and deny the cup to the laity.
    Comfort for the Laity  
	  The Papists teach that as the blood is in the flesh, and as the soul is in the body, so Christ's substance is in the bread and wine.
	  They comfort the laity by teaching that Christ's substance is present in the bread and wine.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Nature of the Lord's Supper  
	  The Lord's Supper is a communion between believers and Christ
	  It is a fellowship of believers with Christ and with each other
    The Presence of Christ in the Sacrament  
	  The body and blood of Christ are present in the sacrament, but not corporally or carnally
	  They are present spiritually, as the elements are to the outward senses
    The Sign and the Grace Signified  
	  The bread and wine represent the flesh and blood of the Redeemer
	  The relation between the bread and wine and the body and blood is purely moral or representative
    The Worthy Receiver  
	  The worthy receiver receives the body and blood of Christ spiritually, through faith
	  They receive the benefits of Christ's sacrificial death, not corporally or carnally
    The Unworthy Receiver  
	  The unworthy receiver, such as the ignorant and wicked, receive the outward elements but not the inward grace
	  They are guilty of the body and blood of the Lord and are unworthy of the Lord's table
    The Instrumentality of Faith  
	  Faith is the instrumentality by which believers receive the body and blood of Christ
	  The Holy Ghost uses the sacrament as his instrument to convey the benefits of Christ's death
    The Spiritual Nature of the Feeding  
	  Believers feed upon Christ spiritually, not corporally or carnally
	  This feeding is accomplished through the free and sovereign agency of the Holy Ghost
    The Condemnation of the Unbeliever  
	  The unbeliever receives the outward sign with his mouth but fails to receive the inward grace
	  This only increases his own condemnation and hardens his own heart
    The Importance of the True Character of the Ordinance  
	  The Lord's Supper should be preserved as a communion, not a private or individualistic act
	  It should be administered in the presence of the officers and a sufficient number of the members of the Church
    The Reformed Doctrine  
	  The Reformed doctrine teaches that the Lord's Supper is a sacrament in which the body and blood of Christ are present spiritually
	  It emphasizes the importance of faith and the instrumentality of the Holy Ghost in conveying the benefits of Christ's death.

Here are the 37 questions summarized in 10 bullets with sub-bullets:

    The Lord's Supper  
	  1. What are the subjects treated of in the first section? (The Lord's Supper)
	  2. State the evidence that this ordinance was instituted immediately by the Lord in person. (Scripture)
	  3. State the proof that it was designed to be perpetually observed in the Church until the second coming of Christ. (Scripture)
    Design of the Lord's Supper  
	  4. What is the first point taught in our Standards as to the design of the Lord's Supper? (To commemorate Christ's death)
	  5. State the proof upon which that position rests. (Scripture)
	  6. What is the second point taught as to its design? (To feed on Christ's body and blood)
	  7. Prove the correctness of that position. (Scripture)
	  8. What is the third point taught as to the design of this ordinance? (To promote unity among believers)
	  9. What is the fourth point taught? (To prevent unbelievers from participating)
	  10. Prove the correctness of that position. (Scripture)
    Elements and Consecration  
	  11. What are the five Romish errors with respect to the Lord's Supper? (Transubstantiation, etc.)
	  12. What, according to the true doctrine, are the elements essential to this ordinance? (Bread and wine)
	  13. What kind of bread is proper? and assign the reason. (Unleavened bread)
	  14. Prove that bread is essential to the ordinance, and assign the reason. (Scripture)
	  15. Prove that the "wine" intended is the fermented juice of the grape, and assign the reason that its use is essential. (Scripture)
    Consecration and Reception  
	  16. How are the elements consecrated, and what is intended by that term in this application of it? (By the Lord's institution)
	  17. What is the symbolical import of the "breaking of bread"? And prove that it is one of the essential sacramental actions. (To represent Christ's body broken for us)
	  18. Prove that the distribution of the elements to and their reception by the communicants are integral and essential parts of the ordinance. (Scripture)
    Roman Catholic and Lutheran Doctrines  
	  19. What does the word "transubstantiation" mean? (The change of bread and wine into Christ's body and blood)
	  20. State the Romish doctrine as to the change of the bread and wine into the flesh, blood, soul and divinity of Christ. (Transubstantiation)
	  21. What is the Lutheran doctrine upon the subject, and how far does it agree with and how far differ from the Romish doctrine? (Similar to Romish doctrine)
    Private Communion and Administration  
	  22. What is the true meaning of the word "is" in the words of institution, "This is my body"? and prove your answer. (To represent, not to change)
	  23. Show that this doctrine is unsupported by Scripture, and show how it contradicts the senses and reason. (Scripture and reason)
	  24. Show why it is absurd and impossible. (Reason and Scripture)
    Communion Table and Priesthood  
	  25. What distinction do they make in regard to the pretended twofold character of the Eucharist? (Sacrament and sacrifice)
	  26. What is their doctrine as to the sacrifice of the mass? (The sacrifice of Christ repeated)
	  27. Prove that this doctrine is radically false and injurious. (Scripture and reason)
    Communion in Private Houses  
	  28. What are the serious objections to calling the communion table an altar, and the minister a priest? (Scripture and reason)
	  29. Why do Romanists hold that the distribution and reception of the elements are not essential parts of this ordinance, and how do they treat the consecrated elements? (Transubstantiation)
	  30. Why do they withhold the cup from the laity, and on what grounds do they pretend that the cup is not necessary as well as the bread to valid communion? (Transubstantiation)
    Private Communion and Administration  
	  31. What Papal and Ritualistic error as to private communion is opposed in these sections, and on what grounds? (Transubstantiation)
	  32. Under what circumstances, and in what manner may the communion be properly administered in private houses? (Scripture and reason)
    Relation between Sign and Grace  
	  33. What are the subjects treated of in the seventh and eighth sections of this chapter? (The relation between sign and grace)
	  34. What is the first proposition taught? (The sign is a representation of the thing signified)
	  35. What is the true nature of the relation subsisting between the sign and the grace signified? (Representation, not change)
    Presence of Christ's Body and Blood  
	  36. In what sense are the body and blood of Christ present in the sacrament? (Spiritually, not physically)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Chapter 29: Of the Sacraments  

    40. The Sacrament of the Lord's Supper  
	  The believer is said to feed upon "the body and blood of Christ"
	  This is a spiritual nourishment, not a physical one
    41. Agency of the Sacrament  
	  The sacrament is accomplished by the agency of Christ alone
	  No human agency is necessary for its effectiveness
    42. Role of the Holy Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit is involved in the sacrament, conveying a blessing
	  The Spirit is the one who applies the benefits of the sacrament to believers
    43. Faith and the Sacrament  
	  The faith of the recipient is essential for receiving the blessing of the sacrament
	  Faith is the means by which the sacrament is applied to the believer
    44. Effect on Unbelievers  
	  The sacrament has no effect on unbelievers, who do not receive the blessing
	  Unbelievers may participate in the sacrament, but it will not benefit them
    45. Identifying Ignorant or Unworthy Recipients  
	  Those who are ignorant or unworthy to receive the sacrament are identified by their actions and behavior
	  The church has the responsibility to exclude those who are not worthy from participating in the sacrament
    46. Receiving Grace without the Sacrament  
	  Believers can receive the same grace without the use of the sacrament
	  This can occur through prayer, faith, and other means of spiritual nourishment

  Chapter 30: Of Church Censures  

    The Theocratic Government of the Church  
	  The church has a government that is distinct from the civil government
	  This government is established by Christ, who is the King and Head of the church
    Erastianism vs. Theocratic Government  
	  Erastianism is a doctrine that subordinates the church to the state
	  The Confession rejects Erastianism, affirming the independence of the church's government from the state's
    Church Officers and Their Authority  
	  Church officers have a ministerial and declarative authority
	  They are responsible for defining and carrying out the teachings of Christ, and for administering the laws and penalties of the church.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Theological Agreement  
	  Christians agree on two principles: Christ's power and the Church's organization
	  All Christians, except Erastians, agree on these principles
    Theories of Church Organization  
	  There are four main theories: Popish, Prelatical, Independent/Congregational, and Presbyterian
    Popish Theory  
	  Christ, apostles, and believers formed the Church on earth
	  Peter became the visible head of the Church after Christ's ascension
	  The Pope and prelates are the successors of Peter and the apostles
    Prelatical Theory  
	  Assumes the perpetuity of the apostleship as the governing power
	  Anglican/High Church form: apostle-bishops govern the Church
	  Low Church form: a threefold order in the ministry is desirable
    Independent/Congregational Theory  
	  The governing power is in the brotherhood
	  Each worshipping assembly is independent and complete
    Presbyterian Theory  
	  Affirmative statements:
		- The people have a right to a substantive part in the government
		- Presbyters are the highest permanent officers
		- The Church is one, with smaller parts subject to larger ones
    Church Power  
	  Christ has vested all ecclesiastical power in the Church as a whole
	  No members are excluded, but the power is exercised by organized bodies
    Ruling Elders  
	  Represent the people and exercise government and discipline
	  Chosen by the people to act in their name
	  Exercise powers that belong to the lay members of the Church
    Church Organization  
	  Consists of members, ruling elders, and ministers/bishops
	  Ruling elders are chosen by the people to act in their name
    Church Governance  
	  The people have a right to a substantive part in the government
	  Presbyters and ruling elders work together to govern the Church

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Church Power and Discipline  

    Nature and Extent of Church Power  
	  Conferred upon the Church to admit and exclude members
	  Relates to matters of doctrine, worship, government, and discipline
    Power of the Keys  
	  To operate and close the doors of the Church
	  To admit or exclude from sealing ordinances
	  Purely ministerial and declarative, not absolute
    Church Discipline  
	  Necessary for the reclaiming and gaining of offending brethren
	  For deterring others from similar offenses
	  For purging out of leaven that might infect the whole Church
	  For vindicating the honor of Christ and the gospel
	  For preventing the wrath of God

  Methods of Church Discipline  

    Proceeding by Admonition  
	  To correct and restore the erring brother
	  To deter others from similar offenses
    Suspension from the Lord's Supper  
	  For a season, to correct and restore the erring brother
    Excommunication from the Church  
	  For notorious and obstinate offenders
	  To purify the Church and prevent the wrath of God

  Ends of Church Discipline  

    Purity of the Church  
	  To glorify and appease God
    Recovery of the Erring Brother  
	  To restore the brother to a right relationship with God and the Church
    Example to Deter Others  
	  To prevent similar offenses and maintain the integrity of the Church
    Exhibition of Righteousness  
	  To demonstrate the Church's commitment to righteousness and holiness

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Church Discipline  
	  The Church officers should proceed in a regular order to administer discipline.
	  The proper method of procedure is stated in the "Book of Discipline".
	  The successive stages of discipline are: (a) private admonition, (b) public admonition, (c) suspension, (d) excommunication.
    Proportion of Discipline  
	  The discipline should be wisely and justly proportioned to the nature of the crime and demerit of the person.
    Church Power  
	  All Church power is vested by Christ in the Church as a whole.
	  The Church consists of presbyters or bishops and the people, represented by lay or ruling elders.
    Government of the Church  
	  The government of the Church is committed to two orders of Church officers: elders or bishops, and ruling elders.
	  The ruling elders represent the people and exercise their inherent powers through them.
    Exercise of Church Power  
	  All Church power must be exercised in a ministerial and declarative manner.
	  It is simply a declaration of the will of Christ, not a human invention.
    Administration of Discipline  
	  The first thing to be observed in the due administration of discipline is the regular order of procedure.
	  The rules regulating discipline are laid down in the "Book of Discipline".
    Synods and Councils  
	  Such assemblies as are commonly called synods or councils are necessary for the better government and further edification of the church.
	  The overseers and other rulers of the particular churches should appoint and convene such assemblies as often as they judge it expedient for the good of the church.
    Church Power and the State  
	  The Church has its own power and authority, separate from the State.
	  There is no danger of interference between the two orders of government.
    Theories of Church Government  
	  The main elements of the Popish theory, Prelatical theory, Congregational or Independent theory, and Presbyterian theory are discussed.
    Church Officers  
	  The character of the office of the ruling elders is discussed.
	  The ruling elders represent the people and exercise their inherent powers through them.
    Ends of Church Discipline  
	  The several ends which Church discipline is designed to effect are discussed.
	  The first thing that must be observed in the due administration of discipline is the regular order of procedure.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Presbyterian Church Governance  
	  The governing power of a congregation is vested in the presbytery, consisting of the bishop or pastor and ruling elders.
	  The presbytery exercises pastoral care, discipline, and instruction over the members, and regulates public worship.
    Types of Church Governance  
	  In the Episcopal Church, governing power vests with the rector.
	  In the Congregational Churches, governing power is exercised immediately by the whole body of the brotherhood.
	  In the Presbyterian Church, governing power vests with the pastor and people, with the people acting through their ruling elders.
    Universal Church Principles  
	  The whole Church of Christ on earth is one, with smaller parts subject to larger ones and the whole.
	  The principles of government laid down in the Scriptures bind the whole Church.
	  The same qualifications are demanded for admission to the sacred office and the same grounds for deposition.
    Relationship between Particular and Universal Church  
	  Every member of a particular church is a member of the universal Church.
	  Every one rightfully excluded from a particular church is excluded from the whole Church.
	  Every one rightfully ordained to the ministry in one church is a minister of the universal Church.
    Appeal and Review  
	  The right of appeal exists, allowing a particular church to refer cases to a higher authority.
	  The right of review and control exists, allowing the universal Church to ensure that the acts of a particular church are performed according to the law of Christ.
    Apostolic Age and Universal Church Principles  
	  The principle of universal Church governance was acted upon in the apostolic age.
	  This principle has been recognized and acted upon in all branches of the Christian Church since then.
    Example from the Apostolic Age  
	  A controversy in the church at Antioch was referred to the apostles and elders at Jerusalem, who made a decision that applied to all churches.
	  This decision was carried out by Paul in his missionary journeys.
    Ecclesiastical Councils  
	  The Presbyterian Church provides for the erection and operation of a regularly graduated series of ecclesiastical councils.
	  These councils include the Session or Parochial Presbytery, the Classical Presbytery, and higher courts.
    Session or Parochial Presbytery  
	  The Session or Parochial Presbytery governs a particular congregation, consisting of its pastor and ruling elders.
	  The Session's decisions are final, except when appealed and reversed by a higher court.
    Classical Presbytery and Higher Courts  
	  The Classical Presbytery consists of all the pastors or bishops and churches in a city or neighborhood.
	  Higher courts, such as the Synod and General Assembly, have jurisdiction over multiple presbyteries and churches.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Church Government Structure  
	  The church is governed by a hierarchical system of courts
	  Each court has a specific role and level of authority

•   Presbytery  
	  A Presbytery is a group of churches that meet together
	  It consists of representatives from each church, with an equal number of pastors and lay representatives
	  The Presbytery has the power to examine and license candidates for the ministry

•   Synod  
	  A Synod is a large Presbytery that consists of all the Presbyteries in a province
	  It has the same structure and powers as a Presbytery

•   General Assembly  
	  The General Assembly is the highest court in the church
	  It consists of representatives from each Presbytery, with an equal number of pastors and lay representatives
	  It has the final say on all matters and can review decisions made by lower courts

•   Appeal Process  
	  Any question or dispute can be appealed to a higher court
	  The appeal process can go all the way to the General Assembly

•   Review and Control  
	  Each court has the right to review the decisions of lower courts
	  Each court has the power to supervise and control the actions of lower courts

•   Ministerial Roles  
	  Ordained ministers belong to the Presbytery, not to a particular church
	  The Presbytery has the power to license and ordain ministers

•   Lay Representatives  
	  Lay representatives have equal power with pastors in the Presbytery
	  They are chosen to represent the people in the church government

•   Synods and Councils  
	  Synods and councils have the power to determine controversies of faith and conscience
	  They can set rules and directions for the church and receive complaints

•   Limitations of Synods and Councils  
	  Synods and councils can err and are not to be made the rule of faith or practice
	  They are to be used as a help in determining faith and practice

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Scope of Church Courts  
	  Have no right to interfere with civil affairs
	  Can only advise or petition in extraordinary cases
	  Can only advise on matters of conscience if required by the civil magistrate

•   Powers of Synods and Councils  
	  Purely ministerial and declarative
	  Wholly judicial and executive, not legislative
	  Can form creeds, confessions, and constitutions
	  Can determine controversies and cases of conscience

•   Regulations and Discipline  
	  Can prescribe regulations for worship and church government
	  Can take up and issue cases of discipline
	  Can receive appeals and complaints in cases of maladministration

•   Relationship with Civil Authorities  
	  Have no right to advise or influence civil officers
	  Can teach and enforce obedience to civil authorities as a religious duty
	  Obedience to civil authorities is a religious obligation

•   Authority of Synods and Councils  
	  May err and have erred in the past
	  Not to be made the rule of faith or practice
	  To be used as a help in matters of faith and practice

•   Submission to Synods and Councils  
	  Private members should submit to decisions that are not opposed to the Word of God
	  Private members should disregard decisions that are opposed to the Word of God

•   Authority of Ecclesiastical Courts  
	  Decrees that are consonant with the Word of God are to be received by all subject to the jurisdiction of the court
	  Decrees are to be received because they agree with the Word of God and because of the proper authority of the court

•   Church Power and Governance  
	  Christ has vested all church power in the church
	  The people exercise powers through the agency of the church
	  This gives rise to a body that governs the church

•   Governing Power in Congregations  
	  In the Episcopal system, governing power vests in the bishop
	  In the Congregational system, governing power vests in the congregation

•   Body that Governs the Church  
	  The body that governs the church is not specified in the text, but it is implied to be a body that represents Jesus Christ and exercises his authority in the church.
