Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Nature of Human Beings  
	  Composed of a soul and a body
	  Death is the temporary separation of these two elements
    The Soul's Condition After Death  
	  Believers are immediately made perfect in holiness
	  Continue conscious, active, and happy
	  In the presence of Christ
    The Soul's Condition After Death (continued)  
	  Wicked souls continue conscious and active, but in penal torment
	  Reserved for the judgment of the great day
    Irreversibility of Conditions  
	  Believers will not be lost, and wicked will not be saved
    Scriptural Grounds  
	  No support for the Roman Catholic doctrine of multiple places or conditions
    The Duality of Human Nature  
	  Scripture implies a soul and body with distinct attributes and subsistence
	  Examples: Gen 2:7, Matt 10:28, Ecc 12:7, 2 Cor 5:8, Phil 1:22-24
    The Body's Condition After Death  
	  Resolved into its original chemical elements
	  Eventually incorporated into the Earth's matter
    The Resurrection  
	  The real identity of our bodies is preserved
	  Will be brought to a glorious resurrection as members of Christ
    The Intermediate State  
	  Believers and wicked souls continue conscious and active, but separate from their bodies
	  Believers are in the presence of Christ, while wicked souls are in penal torment
    The Location of the Soul's Intermediate State  
	  Scripture gives no clue about the location of the wicked souls' suffering
	  Possibly not the same location as their permanent dwelling after the final judgment

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Intermediate State  
	  The Bible teaches that the intermediate state of souls is one of conscious activity
	  The redeemed are perfectly holy and happy with Christ, while the reprobate are with the devil and his angels in torment

•   Location of the Wicked  
	  The location of the place where the wicked are tormented is not revealed by God
	  The terms "up" or "down" are metaphorical and do not indicate absolute direction

•   Location of the Redeemed  
	  The location of the place where the redeemed are gathered is not revealed, except that it is wherever the glorified humanity of Christ is
	  They are with Christ and behold his glory

•   Christ's Location  
	  Christ is at the right hand of God, a locality that marks a definite place
	  However, the phrase "right hand of God" refers to Christ's condition of honour and power as mediatorial King

•   The Earth's Future  
	  There is a strong probability that the Earth will be burned with fire and then gloriously replenished
	  This could be the location of Christ and his glorified spouse's central home throughout eternity

•   Scriptural Evidence  
	  The reappearance of Samuel in a conscious state (1 Sam. 28:7-20)
	  The appearance of Moses and Elias at the transfiguration of Christ (Matt. 17:3)
	  Christ's address to the thief on the cross (Luke 23:43)

•   The Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus  
	  Lazarus is conscious and active in Abraham's bosom
	  The rich man is in conscious torment in Hell (Hades)

•   The Death of Stephen  
	  Stephen saw the heavens opened and Jesus Christ standing at the right hand of God (Acts 7:55-59)
	  He cried out, "Lord Jesus, receive my spirit," and so died

•   Paul's Teachings  
	  To be "at home in the body" is to be "absent from the Lord" (2 Cor. 5:1-8)
	  To be "absent from the body" is to be "present with the Lord" (2 Cor. 5:1-8)

•   The Church's Unity  
	  The Church is declared to be one whole family, with part in heaven and part on earth (Eph. 3:15)
	  The sleep of death is a "living together with Christ" (1 Thess. 5:10)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Intermediate State  
	  The souls of the just are made perfect and happy with the angels in heaven (Heb. 12:23)
	  The souls of martyrs are under the altar in heaven, praying for punishment of their persecutors (Rev. 6:9-11)
	  The souls of believers are with Christ and the holy angels (Rev. 5:9, 7:9, 14:1, 3)
    Papal Doctrine on the Intermediate State  
	  The Papists believe in a Limbus Infantum for unbaptized infants
	  The Limbus Patrurm for Old Testament believers
	  Permanent Hell for unbaptized adults and those who have lost the grace of baptism
	  Heaven for Christians who have attained Christian perfection
	  Purgatory for partially sanctified Christians
    Purgatory  
	  The Council of Trent teaches that there is a purifying fire and that souls in purgatory can be benefited by prayers and masses
	  This doctrine is false because it is not taught in Scripture and is opposed to its teaching on the intermediate state
	  It rests on anti-Christian principles regarding the atonement of Christ and the efficacy of temporary suffering
    The Resurrection  
	  The dead will be raised up with the same bodies, although with different qualities (John 5:25-29, Acts 24:15)
	  The bodies of the just will be raised to honor and made conformable to Christ's glorious body (Acts 24:15, John 5:25-29, I Cor. 15:43)
	  The bodies of the unjust will be raised to dishonor (Acts 24:15, John 5:25-29, I Cor. 15:43)
    The Resurrection of the Just and Unjust  
	  There will be a simultaneous resurrection of all the dead, both just and unjust (Dan. 12:2, John 5:25-29)
	  Those who remain living on earth will not die, but be changed (I Thess. 4:17, I Cor. 15:51-52)
	  The very same bodies that are buried will be raised and reunited to their souls (John 5:25-29, Acts 24:15)

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  The Resurrection of the Dead  

    The Judgment of the Two Classes  
	  The righteous and the wicked will be judged simultaneously after their resurrection.
	  The sheep will stand on the right side, and the goats on the left.
	  The righteous will go to eternal life, while the wicked will go to everlasting punishment.
    The Timing of the Resurrection  
	  The Lord will descend from heaven with a shout, with the voice of the archangel, and with the trumpet of God.
	  The dead in Christ will rise first, and then the living will be caught up together with them in the clouds.
    The Nature of the Resurrection Body  
	  The same bodies that are buried in the earth will be raised and reunited to their souls.
	  The identity of the body will be preserved, although its qualities will be changed.
    The Changes in the Resurrection Body  
	  The body will be changed from a corruptible to an incorruptible state.
	  The body will be made "like unto Christ's glorious body."
    The Differences between the Resurrection Bodies  
	  The bodies of believers will be raised to glory, while the bodies of the reprobate will be raised to dishonor.
	  The spiritual body will be suited to the new wants and stage of development of the believer.
    The Purpose of the Resurrection  
	  The resurrection is a change from a corruptible to an incorruptible state.
	  The body will be suited to the new physical conditions of the "new heavens and the new earth."
    The Identity of the Resurrection Body  
	  The body will be the same, although its qualities will be changed.
	  The body will be material and identical with the body that was once animal.
    The Changes in the Material Elements  
	  The body will undergo changes in its material elements and form between birth and death.
	  The body will be changed from a natural to a spiritual state.
    The Analogy of the Resurrection  
	  The resurrection of believers will be analogous to the resurrection of Christ.
	  The body of Christ was raised in its identical form, with the print of the nails and the spear.
    The Authority of Scripture  
	  The resurrection is explicitly declared in Scripture.
	  The authority of God's Word is the basis for believing in the resurrection.

Here are the 34 bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  The Nature of Death and the Afterlife  

  1.   Scripture assumes the duality of human nature  
	  Humans have a spiritual and physical aspect
  2.   Definition of death  
	  Death is the separation of the soul from the body
  3.   What happens to the body after death  
	  The body returns to dust
  4.   Scriptural teachings on death  
	  Death is a transition to an intermediate state
  5.   Change in believers' souls after death  
	  Believers immediately go to a state of happiness and presence with Christ

  The Intermediate State  

  6.   State of believers and reprobates between death and resurrection  
	  Both are conscious and active, but in different states
  7.   Place to which believers go immediately after death  
	  A state of happiness and presence with Christ
  8.   Place to which reprobates are introduced after death  
	  A state of torment and presence with devils
  9.   Will the conditions of believers and reprobates be reversed?  
	  No, their states are fixed
  10.   Why these states are not final  
	  They are intermediate, awaiting the resurrection

  Scriptural Teachings on the Afterlife  

  11.   Proof that believers' souls are conscious and happy  
	  Scripture passages (e.g. Luke 23:43, 2 Corinthians 5:8)
  12.   Proof that reprobates' souls are conscious and in torment  
	  Scripture passages (e.g. Luke 16:19-31, Matthew 25:41)
  13.   Absolute location of the place of suffering  
	  Scripture teaches that it is a real place, not just a state of mind
  14.   Interpretation of "up" and "down" in relation to the afterlife  
	  These terms refer to a real, physical location
  15.   Location of Christ and the blessed dead  
	  They are gathered in a place of bliss, awaiting the resurrection

  Roman Catholic Teachings on the Afterlife  

  16.   Roman Catholic teachings on Limbus Patrum and Limbus Infantum  
	  These are places where the souls of the righteous and infants go before the resurrection
  17.   Who goes to heaven and who to hell immediately after death  
	  According to Roman Catholic teaching, some souls go directly to heaven or hell
  18.   Roman Catholic teachings on purgatory  
	  Purgatory is a place where souls are purified before entering heaven
  19.   Reasons that disprove the Roman Catholic doctrine on purgatory  
	  Scripture passages (e.g. 1 Corinthians 3:15, Hebrews 12:14)

  The Resurrection  

  20.   First proposition: the resurrection of the just and unjust will be simultaneous  
	  Scripture teaches that the resurrection will occur at the same time for all
  21.   Second proposition: those living at the time of Christ's second coming will not die  
	  Scripture teaches that they will be "changed" and not experience death
  22.   Third proposition: the same body that is placed in the earth will rise again  
	  Scripture teaches that the resurrection body will be the same as the natural body
  23.   Fourth proposition: Christ rose with the same body  
	  Scripture teaches that Christ's resurrection body was the same as his natural body
  24.   Fifth proposition: changes to the form and material elements of the body do not impair its identity  
	  Scripture teaches that the resurrection body will be the same as the natural body, despite changes
  25.   Nature of the resurrection body  
	  It will be made like Christ's glorified body
  26.   Proof that the resurrection body will be made like Christ's  
	  Scripture passages (e.g. 1 Corinthians 15:49, Philippians 3:21)
  27.   Meaning of "natural body" and "spiritual body"  
	  These terms refer to the body before and after the resurrection
  28.   Proof that the bodies of the reprobate will be raised to dishonor  
	  Scripture passages (e.g. 1 Corinthians 15:43, 2 Thessalonians 1:9)

  The Last Judgment  

  29.   God has appointed a day for judgment  
	  Scripture teaches that God will judge the world in righteousness
  30.   Purpose of the Last Judgment  
	  To manifest God's mercy and justice, and to reward or punish individuals
  31.   Scripture passages that teach the Last Judgment  
	  Acts 17:31, John 5:22, 27, Jude 1:6, 2 Peter 2:4, 2 Corinthians 5:10, Ecclesiastes 12:14, Romans 2:16, 14:10, 12, Matthew 12:36-37

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Appointment of Judgment  
	  God has appointed a day of general judgment (Acts 17:31, Rom. 2:5-6)
	  This judgment will be conducted on a predetermined day in the future
    The Judge  
	  The Judge is the God-man in his office as mediatorial King (John 5:22, 27)
	  He is called "the Son of man" and "the man ordained by God" (Matt. 25:31, 32)
    Subjects of Judgment  
	  The entire human race of every generation will be judged (Matt. 25:31-46)
	  Each individual will appear immediately after their resurrection, in the completeness of their reintegrated person (1 Cor. 15:52-54)
    Nature of Judgment  
	  All persons will be judged as to all their thoughts, words, and deeds (Rom. 2:5-6)
	  The judgment will be conducted on a predetermined day in the future
    Purpose of Judgment  
	  The great end of God in the appointment of this day is the manifestation of his glorious justice and grace (Rom. 2:5-6)
	  The righteous will be awarded admission to the presence of the Lord, while the reprobate will be awarded a place with the devil and his angels
    Reward for the Righteous  
	  The righteous will be awarded admission to the presence of the Lord (Matt. 25:21, 31-46)
	  They will consciously enjoy a state of unending holiness, happiness, and honor
    Punishment for the Reprobate  
	  The reprobate will be awarded a place with the devil and his angels (Matt. 25:31-46)
	  They will endure conscious torment and shame through a ceaseless eternity
    The Role of the God-man  
	  The God-man will conduct the judgment as mediatorial King (Matt. 25:31-46)
	  He will consummate his work in the destruction of his enemies and the complete redemption of his friends
    The Timing of Judgment  
	  The judgment will be conducted on a predetermined day in the future
	  The exact timing is not specified, but it is clear that it will be a future event
    The Importance of Repentance  
	  God commands all men everywhere to repent, because he has appointed a day in which he will judge the world in righteousness (Acts 17:30, 31)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Judgment  
	  Will be a public unveiling of secrets and manifestation of character
	  Will involve the judgment of all evil angels
	  Will be concerned with the state of the heart and the motives behind actions

•   The Basis of Judgment  
	  Will not be based on appearances, testimony, or partial knowledge
	  Will be based on the law written on the heart (for those without the law)
	  Will be based on the gospel for those who have lived under the dispensation of the gospel

•   The Role of Saints in the Judgment  
	  Will not be acquitted on the ground of their own good deeds
	  Will be acquitted because their names are found in the book of life
	  Will have their good deeds publicly cited as evidence of their union with Christ

•   The Purpose of the Judgment  
	  Is to manifest God's glorious excellencies as moral Governor and Redeemer
	  Is to show God's righteousness in the condemnation of his enemies
	  Is to show God's glorious grace in the sanctification of his people

•   The End of the Judgment  
	  The righteous will be awarded admission to the presence of the Lord
	  The righteous will continue in a state of conscious and exalted happiness, excellence, and honor for eternity

•   The Blessed Estate of the Saints  
	  Will be perfectly free from sin and evil of every kind
	  Will involve every form of blessedness in an inconceivably great degree
	  Will be characterized by perfect freedom and joy

•   The Nature of the Blessed Estate  
	  Will be characterized by perfect freedom from sin and evil
	  Will involve sharing the glory of Christ as joint heirs with him
	  Will be a state of conscious and exalted happiness, excellence, and honor

•   The Duration of the Blessed Estate  
	  Will be an absolutely unending eternity
	  Will be a state of continuous and unbroken joy and happiness

•   The Role of Christ in the Judgment  
	  Will be the basis of the saints' justification
	  Will be the instrument of the saints' union with God
	  Will be the one who manifests his glorious excellencies in the judgment

•   The Importance of Faith and Works  
	  Faith is the instrument of the saints' union with Christ
	  Works are the evidence of the saints' union with Christ
	  Faith and works are shown to be connected in the judgment

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Definition of Eternal Life  
	  Refers to an unending eternity
	  Called "eternal life", "everlasting life", "eternal weight of glory", etc.
	  Found in passages such as Matt. xix. 16, 29; Rom. ii. 7; 2 Cor. iv. 17

•   Duration of Eternal Life  
	  Lasts for an absolutely unending eternity
	  Contrasts with the temporary nature of earthly life

•   The New Heaven and New Earth  
	  After the great conflagration, the Earth will be reconstituted
	  Will be gloriously adapted to be the permanent residence of Christ and his Church

•   The Reprobate  
	  Will be immediately driven to the place prepared for the devil and his angels
	  Will continue in conscious torment and shame for an unending eternity

•   Penal Torments of the Lost  
	  Described using the strongest terms in the Greek language
	  Include "fire that shall not be quenched", "worm that never dies", etc.

•   Unpardonable Sin  
	  Christ says it shall never be pardoned, neither in this world nor in the next
	  Found in Matt. xii. 32

•   Christian Church's Teaching  
	  The entire Christian Church has agreed that the penal sufferings of the lost are to last forever
	  Includes Greek, Roman, Lutheran, and Reformed churches

•   Hypotheses of Annihilation  
	  Some individuals and heretical societies have denied eternal torment
	  Suggest that the "second death" in Rev. xx. 14 involves total destruction of being

•   Flaws in Annihilation Hypothesis  
	  Contradicts Scripture's consistent description of the lost as enduring conscious suffering
	  Ignores the atoning and purifying efficacy of Christ's sacrifice

•   Flaws in Restoration Hypothesis  
	  Suggests that suffering can purify the soul, which is not supported by Scripture
	  Ignores the atoning and sanctifying power of Christ's sacrifice
	  Lacks any scriptural basis for ultimate restoration of the reprobate

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Nature of Judgment  
	  The Bible teaches that men will be judged on their actions, motives, feelings, and thoughts (Qs 15-17)

    Scriptural Proof

    Historical and Theological Context  
	  The doctrine of eternal conscious punishment has been held by the Church throughout history (Q 30)
	  The opposing hypotheses of annihilation and restitution have been disproven (Qs 31-34)

    The Importance of the Future Judgment

	  The constant pendancy of the future judgment should affect believers' lives and motivate them to live in obedience to God (Q 39)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Interpretation of the Confession of Faith  
	  The Confession of Faith is a declaration of the essential doctrines of Christianity.
	  The system contained in the Confession is meant to be the essential doctrines, and nothing more.
    Objections to the interpretation  
	  The phrase "for substance of doctrine" is ambiguous and cannot be substituted for "system of doctrine".
	  The phrase "system of doctrine" has a definite sense and refers to a specific scheme of doctrine.
    The mind of the Church  
	  The Church has made repeated official declarations on the subject of the Confession of Faith.
	  The adopting act of the original Synod in 1729 is a key document in understanding the Church's mind on this issue.
    Key points from the adopting act  
	  The Synod does not claim authority to impose its faith on others, but does take care to keep the faith pure and uncorrupt.
	  Ministers of the Synod are required to declare their agreement with the Confession of Faith and the Larger and Shorter Catechisms.
	  Candidates for the ministry must also declare their agreement with the essential articles of the Confession.
    Procedure for admitting ministers  
	  Presbyteries must take care to admit only candidates who declare their agreement with the essential articles of the Confession.
	  Candidates with scruples about specific articles may be admitted if their scruples are deemed non-essential.
    Treatment of those who differ  
	  The Synod agrees to treat those who differ from them in non-essential points with friendship, kindness, and brotherly love.
	  The Synod will not traduce or use opprobrious terms against those who differ from them.
    Record of the adopting act  
	  The adopting act was recorded in the Synod's minutes on the same day it was adopted.
	  All ministers of the Synod present at the time declared their agreement with the Confession of Faith, except one who was not prepared.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. The Westminster Confession of Faith and Catechisms  

    1.1. Adoption of the Confession  
	  The Synod unanimously agreed to adopt the Confession of Faith and Catechisms of the Assembly of Divines at Westminster.
	  The only exceptions were clauses in chapters 20 and 23, which were not received in a way that would imply a controlling power of the civil magistrate over Synods.
    1.2. Terms of Christian Communion  
	  The terms of Christian communion are competent knowledge and a credible profession of faith and repentance.
	  The condition of ministerial communion is the adoption of the system of doctrine contained in the Westminster Confession of Faith and Catechisms.
    1.3. Exceptions to the Confession  
	  The only exceptions allowed were those related to matters outside the system of doctrine.
	  The rejection of these exceptions left the system of doctrine in its integrity.

  II. The Synod's Declaration  

    2.1. Declaration in 1730  
	  The Synod declared that they understood the clauses respecting the admission of intrants or candidates in a way that obliged them to receive and adopt the Confession and Catechisms.
    2.2. Declaration in 1736  
	  The Synod reaffirmed their adoption of the Westminster Confession and Catechisms without variation or alteration.
	  They declared that this was their true intent in adopting the Confession.
    2.3. Reunion of the Synods in 1758  
	  The reunited Synods approved and received the Westminster Confession, Larger and Shorter Catechisms, as an orthodox and excellent system of doctrine.
	  They adhered to the plan of worship, government, and discipline contained in the Westminster Directory.

  III. Objections to the Interpretation of the Ordination Formula  

    3.1. Lack of Definite Meaning  
	  The phrase "substance of doctrine" has no definite assignable meaning.
    3.2. Disorder and Confusion  
	  The system has been tried and found to produce the greatest disorder and confusion.
    3.3. Impracticability  
	  The principle of receiving and adopting every proposition in the Confession as part of one's own faith is impracticable and would lead to the ruin of the Church.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Church's Office is Ministerial  
	  The Church's office is purely ministerial and should be exercised cautiously and humbly.
	  The Church has no right to alter the evidence of a call to ministry or to salvation.
    Inordinate Demands  
	  The Church's demands for adherence to the Westminster Confession and Catechisms are inordinate.
	  Making ministers profess to adhere to every proposition in the Confession is a great sin that hurts their conscience.
    The Westminster Confession  
	  The Westminster Confession contains three distinct classes of doctrines:
		- Common to all Christians
		- Common to all Protestants
		- Unique to the Reformed churches
    Interpretation of the Formula  
	  The true interpretation of the formula prescribed for the adoption of the Confession is the "via media".
	  This interpretation recognizes the Confession as containing the Reformed system of doctrine.
    Arguments for the Via Media  
	  The via media interpretation is in accordance with the literal meaning of the words.
	  It corresponds with the known intention of the Church in requiring the adoption of the Confession.
	  It is the only interpretation consistent with a good conscience and the peace and union of the Church.
    New School Presbyterian View  
	  The New School Presbyterians agreed to the "system-of-doctrine" view.
	  They condemned the "every-proposition theory" as inconsistent with the plain terms of the adopting act.
    The System of Doctrine  
	  The system of doctrine taught in the Holy Scriptures is the Reformed or Calvinistic system.
	  This system is distinct from the Lutheran, Arminian, Antinomian, Pelagian, and Roman Catholic systems.
    Subscription to the Confession  
	  Subscription to the Confession implies acceptance of the Reformed system of doctrine.
	  Honest and candid men agree that there is no real difference between the Old School and the New School on this point.
    The Auburn Declaration and The Declaratory Act  
	  The Auburn Declaration was adopted by a representative body of New School Presbyterians in 1837.
	  The Declaratory Act was a statement of the New School Presbyterian view on the Confession.
    Consensus on the Confession  
	  There is a consensus among honest and candid men that subscription to the Confession implies acceptance of the Reformed system of doctrine.
	  This consensus is based on a simple and honest interpretation of the ordination formula.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the Auburn Declaration:

•   I. Remission of Sin  
	  God permitted sin for wise and benevolent reasons, not because He was unable to prevent it.
	  Election to eternal life is a sovereign act of God's mercy, not based on human faith or obedience.

•   II. Election  
	  Election is a gracious purpose that takes effect in conjunction with faith and a holy life.
	  It does not violate human will or liberty.

•   III. Fall of Adam  
	  Adam's transgression made all mankind morally corrupt and liable to death.
	  This corruption is a result of Adam's representative role as the head of the human race.

•   IV. Hereditary Sin  
	  Infants are born with a nature inclined to evil and only evil.
	  They are not endowed with knowledge, righteousness, or true holiness.

•   V. Infants Involved in Moral Ruin  
	  Infants are part of the human family and suffer and die due to their involvement in the general moral ruin of the race.
	  Their suffering and death are not due to any personal sin, but rather the consequences of Adam's transgression.

•   VI. Universal Need of Redemption  
	  All infants, as well as adults, need redemption by the blood of Christ and regeneration by the Holy Ghost to be saved.
	  Original sin is a natural bias to evil that leads to actual transgression.

•   VII. Imputation of Sin and Righteousness  
	  The sin of Adam is not imputed to his posterity in a literal sense, but rather the race is treated as if they had sinned.
	  The righteousness of Christ is not imputed to his people in a literal sense, but rather they are treated as if they were righteous.

•   VIII. Atonement of Christ  
	  The sufferings of Christ were truly vicarious, serving as a substitute for the punishment due to transgressors.
	  Christ's sacrifice was a full equivalent for the punishment due to sin, and it secures salvation for all who believe.

•   IX. Moral Ability  
	  The Auburn Declaration does not address the concept of moral ability in detail.
	  However, it implies that humans are morally corrupt and unable to save themselves through their own efforts.

•   X. Conclusion  
	  The Auburn Declaration is an authoritative explanation of the Westminster Confession of Faith from the perspective of the New School branch of the Presbyterian Church.
	  It demonstrates the essential unity of Presbyterian churches despite their organic differences.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Sinners and Moral Agency  
	  Sinners have the faculties necessary for moral agency and accountability
	  However, their love of sin and opposition to God's law prevent them from complying with God's commands
    Intercession of Christ  
	  Christ's intercession is for the elect, both before and after their regeneration
	  Christ's intercession is based on Scripture, specifically John 17:9 and 20
    Saving Faith  
	  Saving faith is an intelligent and cordial assent to God's testimony about Christ
	  Saving faith implies reliance on Christ alone for pardon and eternal life
	  Saving faith is an effect of the Holy Spirit's special operations
    Regeneration  
	  Regeneration is a radical change of heart produced by the Holy Spirit
	  Regeneration is instantaneous and determines the sinner to good
    Salvation by Grace  
	  Salvation is by God's grace and Spirit, not by human merit or power
	  God's decision not to save all is based on his wisdom, not a lack of power
    Liberty and Will  
	  God can influence the human will according to his pleasure
	  God determines the human will to good in all cases of true conversion
    Justification  
	  Believers are justified solely on the ground of Christ's obedience and death
	  Justification is not based on personal merit, but on Christ's righteousness
    Freedom in Faith and Unbelief  
	  Those who reject the gospel do so freely, not by coercion
	  Those who accept the gospel do so freely, not by coercion
	  God's decision to save some and not others is based on his sovereignty
    The Declaratory Act  
	  The United Presbyterian Church of Scotland adopted the Westminster Confession of Faith and Catechisms in 1847
	  The church passed a declaratory act in 1879 to clarify the sense in which the Confession is to be understood
    The Church's View of Scripture  
	  The church views the Standards as imperfect and subject to exception
	  The church has clarified its view of Scripture on several subjects, including redemption and salvation

Here are 10 concise bullets summarizing the text, with sub-bullets:

•   The Importance of Gospel Truth  
	  The Church regards certain doctrines as vital to the system of gospel truth.
	  These doctrines should be given due prominence.

•   Divine Decrees and Election  
	  The doctrine of divine decrees is held in connection with the truth that God wants all to come to repentance.
	  God has provided a salvation sufficient for all, offered in the gospel.

•   Man's Total Depravity  
	  Man's total depravity does not imply a loss of responsibility under the law of God and the gospel.
	  Man can still perform actions that are good, but not spiritually good without a renewed heart.

•   Salvation through Christ  
	  None are saved except through the mediation of Christ and the grace of the Holy Spirit.
	  The duty of sending the gospel to the heathen is clear and imperative.

•   Civil Magistrate and Religion  
	  The Church disapproves of compulsory or persecuting principles in religion.
	  The Church does not require approval of anything in its Standards that teaches such principles.

•   Christ's Obligation on the Church  
	  Christ has laid a permanent and universal obligation on the Church to maintain its ordinances.
	  The Church is also obligated to preach the gospel to every creature.

•   Providing for the Gospel  
	  The Church is to provide for the fulfillment of its obligation to preach the gospel through freewill offerings.

•   Liberty of Opinion  
	  Liberty of opinion is allowed on certain points in the Standards, such as the interpretation of the "six days" in the Mosaic account of creation.
	  The Church guards against the abuse of this liberty to the injury of its unity and peace.

•   The Role of the Holy Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit works when and where and how it pleases him.
	  The Spirit restrains and influences man's actions, but does not negate man's responsibility.

•   The Church's Stance on Infant Salvation  
	  The Church does not require the belief that any who die in infancy are lost.
	  God may extend his grace to those who are without the pale of the ordinary means of salvation.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Theories of Church Polity  
	  4 main theories: Popish, Prelatical, Independent/Congregational, and Presbyterian
    Popish Theory  
	  Christ, Apostles, and believers established the Church, with a perpetual organization
	  Peter became the visible head of the Church after Christ's ascension
	  The Pope and Prelates are successors to Peter and the Apostles
    Prelatical Theory  
	  Assumes the perpetuity of the apostleship as the governing power in the Church
	  Anglican/High-Church form: the Church consists of those who profess the true religion and are subject to apostle-bishops
	  Low-Church form: a three-fold order in the ministry is desirable, but not essential
    Independent/Congregational Theory  
	  The governing and executive power in the Church is in the brotherhood
	  Each worshipping assembly is independent and complete in itself
    Presbyterian Theory  
	  Denies: all church power vests in the clergy, the apostolic office is perpetual, and each individual congregation is independent
	  Affirms: the people have a right to a substantive part in the government of the Church, presbyters are the highest permanent officers, and the Church is one in the sense that smaller parts are subject to larger ones
    Church Power and Rights  
	  The Church is a theocracy, with Jesus Christ as its head
	  All power is derived from Christ and his word is the written constitution
	  The Church has the right to:
		- Set forth a public declaration of doctrine
		- Frame creeds or confessions of faith
		- Select, ordain, and send forth teachers
		- Set down rules for public worship
		- Make rules for its own government

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The question of Church power  
	  Does Church power vest in the clergy or the whole body of the faithful?
	  This question determines the essence of things and the destiny of men
    Consequences of clergy-only power  
	  The people would be bound to passive obedience in matters of faith and practice
	  All right of private judgment would be denied
    Consequences of Church power in the people  
	  The people would have a right to a substantive part in decision-making
	  This would lead to the end of Church tyranny and the introduction of liberty
    The connection between Church and civil power  
	  The theory that Church power vests in a hierarchy begets the theory of divine right in kings and nobles
	  The theory that Church power vests in the people begets the theory of civil power in the people
    The relationship between Church and State  
	  The absence of despotic power in the Church leads to the absence of despotic power in the State
	  This is why Charles of England said "No bishop, no king"
    The principle of Presbyterianism  
	  This principle is one of liberty and order
	  It is subject to the infallible authority of the word
    The exercise of Church power  
	  It is in the hands of duly constituted officers
	  It is restricted by the word of God
    The analogy between individual and Church experience  
	  The believer is delivered from sin to serve righteousness
	  The Church is delivered from illegitimate authority to serve a legitimate and divine authority
    The Reformers' goal  
	  To deliver the Church from bondage to prelates
	  Not to create a tumultuous multitude, but to subject the Church to a legitimate and divine authority
    The outcome of the Reformation  
	  The end of Church tyranny and the introduction of liberty
	  The opening of the prison to those who were bound, and the introduction of the people of God into liberty.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Church's Power is Limited and Guided by Scripture  
	  The Church's power is limited by the written law of God as recorded in the Bible.
	  The Church's exercise of power is guided by Scripture.

•   The Church is Not a Democracy  
	  The Church is not a vast democracy where everything is decided by the popular voice.
	  The Church has a divinely appointed government with legitimate officers.

•   The Principle of Liberty and Order are Harmonious  
	  The principle of liberty and the principle of order are perfectly harmonious.
	  The Church's power vests in the Church itself, while also being exercised through divinely appointed officers.

•   Ruling Elders are Representatives of the People  
	  Ruling elders are chosen by the people to act in their name in the government of the Church.
	  The functions of ruling elders determine the power of the people.

•   Powers of Ruling Elders  
	  Ruling elders have an equal voice with the clergy in the formation and adoption of all symbols of faith.
	  Ruling elders have an equal vote with the clergy in selecting preachers, judging their fitness for the sacred office, and deciding whether they shall be ordained.

•   The Church's Power is Not Exclusively in the Clergy  
	  The Church's power does not vest exclusively in the clergy.
	  The people have a role in the government of the Church through their representatives.

•   The Authority of the Civil Magistrate is Jure Divino  
	  The authority of the civil magistrate is divinely appointed.
	  This is not inconsistent with the doctrine that power is in the hands of legitimate officers.

•   The Church's Power is Exercised through Legitimate Officers  
	  The Church's power is exercised through legitimate officers, such as ruling elders.
	  These officers are chosen by the people to act in their name.

•   The Church's Power is Not a Condition of Christian Communion  
	  The Church's power is not a condition of Christian communion.
	  The people have a role in the government of the Church, and their consent is required for certain decisions.

•   The Church's Power is Expressed through Confessions and Symbols of Faith  
	  The Church's power is expressed through confessions and symbols of faith.
	  These confessions and symbols are adopted by the people through their representatives.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Church has authority  
	  The Church cannot frame rituals or liturgies without the people's consent
	  The people have the same authority as the clergy in matters of constitution, rules, and canons
	  The people have a decisive voice in exercising the power of the keys (opening and shutting the door of communion)

•   Presbyterianism and Church power  
	  Presbyterians believe that Church power vests in the Church itself, not just the clergy
	  The people have a right to a substantive part in the Church's discipline and government

•   Scriptural basis for Church power  
	  The question is whether the clergy or the people are the Church
	  If the people are the Church, then power vests in them, not just the clergy

•   Historical definitions of the Church  
	  For the first 15 centuries after Christ, definitions of the Church did not mention the clergy
	  Romanists and Anglicans define the Church as those who are subject to the Pope or Prelates, respectively

•   Protestant definitions of the Church  
	  Protestant symbols define the Church as the company of faithful men
	  This definition does not mention the clergy, indicating that they are not essential to the Church

•   Argument 1: The people constitute the Church  
	  The people, according to Scripture and Protestant Confessions, constitute the Church
	  This means that the people have a right to a substantive part in the Church's government

•   Argument 2: Church power arises from the indwelling of the Spirit  
	  The Spirit dwells in the whole Church, not just the clergy
	  Therefore, the whole Church is the seat of Church power, not just the clergy

•   The Spirit's promise and gift  
	  Romanists hold that the Spirit was promised and given to bishops as a class
	  However, this is not supported by Scripture, and the Spirit dwells in all believers, not just the clergy

•   The people's role in the Church  
	  The people are not just passive listeners, but active participants in the Church's government
	  They have a right to a substantive part in the Church's discipline and government

•   The clergy's role in the Church  
	  The clergy are not the only mediators between God and humanity
	  All believers are priests and kings, with a role to play in the Church's government and discipline.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Holy Spirit's role in the Church  
	  The Holy Spirit is given to the apostles and their successors to guide them into the knowledge of the truth.
	  The Spirit animates the whole body of Christ, making it the representative of Christ on earth.

•   Church power and authority  
	  If the Spirit dwells in the whole Church, then Church power vests in the Church itself, not exclusively in the clergy.
	  The Church's teaching and judgment are ministerially the teaching of the Spirit.

•   The Spirit's presence in all believers  
	  The Spirit dwells in every believer, guiding them into the knowledge of the truth.
	  Every believer has the witness of the Spirit within them and does not need external teaching.

•   The fulfillment of the Spirit's promise  
	  The promise of the Spirit was fulfilled on the day of Pentecost, not just for the apostles, but for the whole assembly.
	  The Spirit filled the whole assembly, and they began to speak with other tongues.

•   The distribution of spiritual gifts  
	  The Spirit gives gifts to believers, including prophecy, ministry, teaching, and other manifestations.
	  Every believer has a unique gift according to the measure of the gift of Christ.

•   The nature of Church power  
	  Church power arises out of the presence of the Spirit.
	  The Spirit animates, guides, and instructs the whole Church, not just the clergy.

•   The commission of Christ to the Church  
	  Christ commissioned the Church to go into all the world and preach the gospel.
	  This commission includes the duty to spread and maintain the gospel, the power to teach, rule, and exercise discipline, and the promise of Christ's perpetual presence and assistance.

•   The duty and promise of the commission  
	  The duty is to extend and sustain the gospel in its purity over the whole earth.
	  The promise is the assurance of Christ's perpetual presence and assistance.

•   The role of the clergy  
	  The clergy are not the exclusive possessors of Church power or the Spirit.
	  The clergy are merely the ministers of the Spirit's teaching and judgment.

•   The anti-Christian doctrine of clericalism  
	  The doctrine that the Spirit of God resides in the ministry to the exclusion of the people is anti-Christian.
	  This doctrine undermines the unity and equality of the Church, and reduces the people to a mere passive recipient of the clergy's teaching.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The duty and promise of Christ's presence belong to the whole Church  
	  The command to "go teach all nations" is addressed to the whole Church, not just the clergy
	  Every Christian feels a personal obligation to discharge this duty

•   The right of the people to a substantive part in the government of the Church is recognized  
	  The apostles recognized the people's right to participate in the government of the Church
	  The people had a decisive voice in the appointment of Matthias to replace Judas

•   The people had a role in appointing deacons  
	  The whole multitude chose the seven men to be invested with the office of deacon

•   The people were involved in making authoritative decisions  
	  The apostles and elders, with the whole Church, sent chosen men to Antioch to address the question of the Mosaic law
	  The brethren were associated with the ministry in this decision

•   Apostolic epistles were addressed to churches, not just the clergy  
	  Epistles were addressed to churches in Corinth, Ephesus, Galatia, and Philippi
	  The people were assumed to be responsible for the orthodoxy of their teachers and the purity of church members

•   The people were required to try the spirits and sit in judgment  
	  The people were required to try the spirits and determine whether teachers were sent by God
	  The Galatians were censured for giving heed to false doctrines

•   The people had the power to excommunicate and restore members  
	  The Corinthians were commanded to excommunicate an incestuous person and later restore him to fellowship

•   The power of the people was exercised through appropriate organs  
	  The commands to watch over the orthodoxy of ministers and the purity of members were addressed to the people, not just the clergy

•   The people had a substantive part in the government of the Church  
	  The fact that commands were addressed to the people proves that they were responsible and had a part in the government

•   The Church was a democratic society  
	  The Church was a society in which the powers inherent in the society were exercised through appropriate organs, involving the people in the government.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Church Power Belongs to the Whole Church  
	  The power of the Church is vested in the people, not just the clergy.
	  The people have a right and are bound to take part in the government of the Church.
	  The power of the Church is exercised through legitimate officers.

•   The Office of Ruling Elders is Essential  
	  The office of ruling elders is not a matter of expediency, but an essential element of Presbyterianism.
	  Ruling elders are the representatives of the people in the Church.
	  Their office arises out of the nature of the Church and rests on the authority of Christ.

•   The Ministry is an Office, Not a Work  
	  The ministry is a station to which the incumbent must be appointed.
	  The appointment implies certain prerogatives that must be recognized and submitted to.
	  Not every man who has the qualifications for the work of the ministry can assume the office of the ministry.

•   Qualifications for the Ministry are Specified in Scripture  
	  The titles given to ministers in the Scriptures imply official station.
	  The qualifications for the ministry are specified in the word of God.
	  The mode of judging those qualifications is prescribed in Scripture.

•   Ministers are Appointed by Christ, Not the People  
	  Ministers derive their authority from Christ, not from the people.
	  Christ has ordained that there shall be such officers in his Church.
	  Christ gives the requisite qualifications and calls those thus qualified.

•   The Church's Role in Appointing Ministers  
	  The Church's role is not to confer the office, but to sit in judgment on the question of whether the candidate is called of God.
	  The Church expresses its judgment in the public and solemn manner prescribed in Scripture.

•   Ministers Derive Authority from Christ  
	  Ministers derive their authority from Christ, not from the people.
	  This follows from the theocratical character of the Church and the relation which Christ sustains to it.

•   Theocratical Character of the Church  
	  The Church is a theocratical institution, with Christ as its king.
	  Christ is the source of all authority and power in the Church.

•   Ministers are Given Official Authority by Christ  
	  Christ gives the requisite qualifications and calls those thus qualified.
	  Christ gives them their official authority.

•   The Authority of Ministers is Recognized in Scripture  
	  The official authority of ministers is ascribed to them in the Scriptures.
	  The command is given that such authority should be duly recognized.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Ministers are appointed by Christ  
	  Ministers are called the servants, messengers, and ambassadors of Christ
	  They speak in Christ's name and by his authority
	  They are sent by Christ to the Church to reprove, rebuke, and exhort

•   Ministers derive their authority from Christ, not the people  
	  The Holy Ghost appoints ministers, not the Church
	  Ministers are servants of the Church, but not in the sense of deriving their commission from the Church

•   The Church is the body of Christ  
	  Christ dwells in the Church by his Spirit
	  The Church has powers that vest ultimately in the Church as a whole
	  Ministers are given gifts, qualifications, and functions by the Holy Ghost

•   Ministers are not appointed by the Church  
	  Ministers are not appointed by the hands and feet of the Church, but by the indwelling Spirit
	  This is the representation that pervades the New Testament

•   Ministers have specific functions  
	  They are charged with preaching the word and administering the sacraments
	  They are the organs of the Church in executing the great commission

•   Ministers are rulers in the house of God  
	  They are invested with the power of the keys, opening and shutting the door of the Church
	  They are clothed with all these powers in virtue of their office

•   Ministers exercise their powers in different contexts  
	  When alone, they exercise their functions alone in gathering and organizing churches
	  When associated with other ministers and the representatives of the people, they exercise their powers in concert

•   The apostolic age illustrates the ministerial office  
	  The apostles and those ordained by them acted in virtue of their ministerial office, singly in founding churches
	  They later acted in connection with other ministers and elders

•   Presbyterianism is based on the scriptural view of the presbyterial office  
	  Presbyters are invested with the powers mentioned above
	  This is the theory of the ministerial office included in the whole system of Presbyterianism

•   The Church has ultimate power and authority  
	  Powers vest ultimately in the Church as a whole
	  The Church has the authority to govern and discipline itself, with ministers acting in concert with other ministers and the representatives of the people.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Titles and Qualifications of Presbyters  
	  They are called teachers, rulers, shepherds, stewards, overseers, builders, watchmen, ambassadors, and witnesses.
	  They must be apt to teach, well instructed, and able to rightly divide the word of God.
	  They must be sound in the faith, able to resist false teachings, and able to rule their own families.
    Duties of Presbyters  
	  They are to preach the word, feed the flock of God, and guide it as a shepherd.
	  They must labor for the edification of the saints and watch for souls as those who must give an account.
	  They must guard the Church against false teachers and exercise episcopal supervision.
    Permanence of the Office of Presbyters  
	  The gift of teaching and ruling is permanent, so the office of presbyter is also permanent.
	  The Church must always have officers to accomplish its divinely appointed work.
	  The apostles ordained presbyters in every city and gave directions for their ordination.
    Continuity of the Office of Presbyters  
	  The office of presbyter has continued to the present time.
	  There is no dispute about this among those concerned.
    Highest Permanent Officers of the Church  
	  Presbyters are the highest permanent officers of the Church.
	  They are charged with preaching the gospel, extending, continuing, and purifying the Church.
	  They have episcopal powers and oversight.
    Relationship to Apostles and Prophets  
	  Apostles and prophets were officers of a higher grade than presbyters during the apostolic age.
	  The apostleship was designed to be perpetual, and prelates are the official successors of the original apostles.
	  If prelates have the office, they must have the gifts of an apostle.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Attributes of Apostles  
	  Must have the same qualities as the original messengers of Christ
	  Inward qualifications are necessary for every office, including apostleship

•   The Consequences of False Apostles  
	  A false apostle is one who lacks the "word of wisdom"
	  A teacher without the "word of knowledge" is not a teacher
	  A worker of miracles without the gift of miracles is a magician
	  A deceiver is one who pretends to speak with tongues without the gift of tongues

•   The Pope's Claim to Christ's Prerogatives  
	  If the Pope is the vicar of Christ, he must have Christ's attributes
	  He cannot have the one without the other
	  If he claims universal dominion, he must also claim divine attributes

•   The Consequences of Idolizing the Pope  
	  Romanists enthrone the Pope and address him with blasphemous titles
	  They offer incense and pronounce anathemas against those who do not acknowledge his authority

•   The Reason for Protestant Opposition to Popery  
	  Opposition to Popery is a religious feeling because it involves a claim to divine attributes
	  Submission to the Pope's authority would involve apostasy from God

•   The Principle Applies to Apostleship  
	  If any set of men claim to be apostles, they must also claim apostolic endowments
	  If they have not the latter, their claim to the former is an usurpation and pretence

•   The Qualifications of the Apostles  
	  They received the word of wisdom, or a complete revelation of the gospel
	  They received the gift of the Holy Ghost, making them infallible in communicating the truth
	  They received the gift of working miracles in confirmation of their mission

•   The Prerogatives of the Apostles  
	  Absolute authority in all matters of faith and practice
	  Authority equally absolute in legislating for the Church
	  Universal jurisdiction over the Church

•   The Importance of Apostolic Qualifications  
	  The apostles were men immediately commissioned by Christ
	  They were qualified to make a full and authoritative revelation of his religion

•   The Implications of Apostolic Authority  
	  If any set of men claim to exercise apostolic authority, they must also claim apostolic endowments
	  If they have not the latter, their claim to the former is an usurpation and pretence

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Apostles' Claims and Authority  
	  Paul claimed to be an apostle with immediate commission, revelation, and absolute authority.
	  He appealed to the signs of an apostle, including patience, signs, wonders, and mighty deeds.

•   Necessity of Agreement with Apostles  
	  Agreement with the apostles in faith and subjection to them was necessary for salvation.
	  The apostle John said that those who know God will hear the apostles, and those who are not of God will not.

•   Infallibility of Apostles' Writings  
	  The writings of the apostles were regarded as infallible and authoritative in all matters of faith and practice.
	  This was due to their possession of apostolic gifts, including inspiration and infallibility.

•   Prelates as Apostles  
	  The argument is that if prelates are apostles, they must have apostolic gifts.
	  However, prelates do not possess these gifts, including special revelation, inspiration, and the power of working miracles.

•   Lack of Apostolic Gifts in Prelates  
	  Prelates have no special revelation, are not inspired, and do not have the power of working miracles or conferring miraculous gifts.
	  They are therefore not apostles.

•   Inseparability of Office and Gifts  
	  The connection between an office and its gifts is inseparable.
	  Prelates claiming to be apostles must show possession of apostolic gifts, but they do not.

•   Collective and Individual Fallibility of Prelates  
	  The historical fact that prelates collectively and individually are uninspired and fallible is palpable.
	  Those of one age differed from those of another, and those of one Church pronounced accursed those of another.

•   Consequences of Prelates Being Apostles  
	  If prelates are apostles, then there can be no religion and no salvation among those not subject to their authority.
	  This conclusion is a reductio ad absurdum, as it would mean that there is no religion beyond the pale of prelatical churches.

•   Prelatical Churches and the Nature of Religion  
	  Prelatists maintain that religion consists in external and formal things, rather than in the fruits of the Spirit.
	  This assumption leads to the conclusion that submission to prelates' teaching and jurisdiction is essential to salvation.

•   Conclusion  
	  The assumption that prelates are apostles leads to the conclusion that they have the gifts of the apostles and that submission to their teaching and jurisdiction is essential to salvation.
	  This conclusion is a perversion of the nature of religion, which is an inward state rather than an external relation.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Perpetual Apostleship Theory  
	  Leads to making religion ceremonial and divorcing it from piety and morality
	  Is a speculative error with destructive consequences
	  Is not supported by historical or biblical evidence

•   The Temporary Nature of the Apostolic Office  
	  The apostles, including the 12 and John, disappeared from history
	  Their gifts, functions, and title ceased when John ascended to heaven
	  This is a plain historical fact

•   The Dangers of Idolizing Men  
	  Putting the Pope in the place of Christ is a fearful thing
	  Putting erring men in the place of infallible apostles is also a fearful thing
	  Faith in their teaching and submission to their authority should not be the condition of grace and salvation

•   The Authority of Christ and His Apostles  
	  We bow to the authority of Christ
	  We submit to the infallible teachings of his inspired apostles
	  We deny that the infallible is continued in the fallible or the divine in the human

•   The Role of Presbyters  
	  Presbyters are the highest permanent officers of the Church
	  They hold their preeminence by human, not divine authority
	  They are not apostles, but rather presbyters

•   The Government of the Church  
	  The Church is governed by judicatories composed of presbyters and elders
	  This is in opposition to the theory of the Independents
	  The Church is one in such a sense that a smaller part is subject to a larger, and the larger to the whole

•   The Unity of the Church  
	  The Church has one Lord, one faith, one baptism
	  The principles of government laid down in the Scriptures bind the whole Church
	  The terms of demission and legitimate grounds of exclusion are the same everywhere

•   The Qualifications for Admission to the Ministry  
	  The same qualifications are everywhere demanded for admission to the sacred office
	  The same grounds are everywhere required for deposition

•   The Relationship Between Particular Churches and the Universal Church  
	  Every particular church has a right to manage its own affairs and administer its own discipline
	  However, it cannot be independent and irresponsible in the exercise of that right
	  Its members are members of the Church universal, and those whom it excommunicates are cut off from the communion of the saints

•   The Importance of Presbyterianism  
	  Presbyterianism is based on the principles of the government of the Church by judicatories composed of presbyters and elders
	  This is in contrast to the theory of the Independents, which holds that each church is independent and responsible only to itself

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Church is one body  
	  Represented as one in Scripture (e.g. one body, one family, one fold)
	  United by the indwelling of the Holy Spirit
	  Produces subjective and objective union among members

•   The Church is a divinely constituted society  
	  Derives power from its charter, not the consent of the governed

	  Obligations of communion and obedience terminate on the whole Church

	  Subject to the authority of the whole Church or an authority that extends over all
