	  Did not consist of isolated, independent congregations
	  Separate churches were subject to an authority that extended over all

•   The Church is governed by the same laws  
	  The same laws govern all members of the Church
	  These laws are derived from the word of God

•   The Church is pervaded by the same Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit unites all members of the Church
	  Produces unity of faith and love among members

•   The Church has a right of appeal and review  
	  The whole Church has the right to see that decisions are made according to the law of Christ
	  This is the Presbyterian theory on the subject

•   The Church is not limited to a particular congregation  
	  Obligations of communion and obedience terminate on the whole Church
	  A Christian is bound to obey his brethren, not just those in his local congregation

•   Independent churches would be impossible  
	  Would be like an independent finger or branch of a tree
	  Would lead to the destruction of the very nature of the Church

•   The Church is a living body  
	  United to the same head (Christ)
	  Governed by the same laws and pervaded by the same Spirit
	  If one part suffers, all suffer with it; if one part is honored, all rejoice with it

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Early Church was United  
	  The Church in Jerusalem was united in worship and subject to the same rulers.
	  The believers were associated in separate churches, but remained subject to a common tribunal.

•   The Apostles were the Bond of Union  
	  The apostles exercised general jurisdiction over the whole body of believers.
	  They had universal authority, which arose from their commission and inspiration.
	  They exercised the powers of teachers and rulers, and their word was law.

•   The Apostles' Authority was Universal  
	  Paul wrote with full authority to the Church in Rome, despite never having visited the city.
	  Peter addressed his epistles to churches in various regions, including those in Paul's field of labor.
	  The apostles' authority was not limited to their own churches, but was universal.

•   The Council at Jerusalem Demonstrated Unity  
	  A controversy arose in the church at Antioch, and the case was referred to the apostles and elders in Jerusalem.
	  The Council at Jerusalem made a decision that was authoritative for all churches.
	  Paul delivered the decrees of the Council to other churches on his missionary journeys.

•   The Church's Organic Nature  
	  The Church has an inward force, or nisus formativus, that impels it to assume a unified form.
	  This force has been manifest throughout the Church's history, leading to the formation of councils and the exclusion of heretics.

•   The Law of Unity in the Church  
	  Believers are impelled to express their inward unity by outward union.
	  This law has been manifest in all ages and under all circumstances.
	  It led to the exclusion of those who denied the common faith and refused to acknowledge their subjection to the Church as a whole.

•   The Early Church's Understanding of Heresy and Schism  
	  The Church understood heresy and schism as a denial of the common faith.
	  Those who denied the common faith were excluded from all churches.
	  This understanding led to the formation of councils and the exclusion of heretics.

•   The Reformation and the Idea of Unity  
	  The churches formed during the Reformation ran together as naturally as plants or animals.
	  The idea of unity was still strong, even in the face of division and schism.

•   The Church's Inward Impulse towards Unity  
	  The Church's inward impulse towards unity is a fundamental aspect of its nature.
	  This impulse has been manifest throughout the Church's history, leading to the formation of councils and the exclusion of heretics.

•   The Importance of Unity in the Church  
	  Unity is essential to the Church's nature and purpose.
	  The Church's inward impulse towards unity is a fundamental aspect of its being.
	  The Church's history demonstrates the importance of unity in its life and practice.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Church is one  
	  It is a kingdom, family, and body with one head, one faith, and one Spirit.
	  This unity is evident in the Church's nature and the command of Christ to obey brethren.

•   The Church's unity is not optional  
	  It is a law of the Spirit that the Church strives for unity and mutual subjection.
	  This is evident in the Church's history, where unity and subjection have been the normal state.

•   The Church's unity is not a human invention  
	  Presbyterianism is not a device of man, but a real growth of the Church's being.
	  It is the outward expression of the inward law of the Church's unity.

•   The Church's government is based on the Holy Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit dwells in the people of God and gives them the ability and right to govern.
	  This is why the people should have a substantive part in the government of the Church.

•   Presbyters are the highest permanent officers of the Church  
	  This is because the gifts of the apostles and prophets have ceased.
	  Presbyters are the natural leaders of the Church, based on the Church's inward life.

•   Congregations are not independent  
	  The Church is one body, and all its parts are mutually dependent.
	  This means that separate congregations are not independent, but part of a larger whole.

•   The Church's form should correspond to its inward life  
	  The outward form of the Church should be the natural expression and product of its inward life.
	  This means that the Church's government and structure should be based on its inward unity and mutual subjection.

•   Scriptural liberty elevates the people  
	  In systems of scriptural liberty, the people are proportionally elevated.
	  This is because such systems demand intelligence and produce it.

•   Despotism degrades the people  
	  In systems of despotism, whether civil or ecclesiastical, the people are degraded.
	  This is because such systems deny the people's right to govern and participate in the Church's life.

•   The Church's growth and development depend on its form  
	  The Church's form should be conducive to its progress and development.
	  If the Church's form is based on its inward life, it will tend to produce the gifts and graces that qualify its members to govern.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Presbyterianism and its goals  
	  Presbyterian institutions aim to educate and elevate the people
	  Successful operation requires constant exertion to attain this end
	  The Church organization has higher aims, including the extension and establishment of the gospel
    The importance of liberty and order  
	  The combination of liberty and order in the Presbyterian system is key to civil liberty
	  This system is designed to balance the rights of the people with subjection to legitimate authority
    The Church's role in governance  
	  The Church has the right to call and appoint ministers
	  This right belongs to the whole Church, not just individual leaders
    Scriptural basis for Church governance  
	  The Bible teaches that the Church has the power to call and appoint ministers
	  This power is based on the keys of the kingdom, which were given to the Church by Christ
    Historical support for Church governance  
	  Turrettin and other theologians have argued that the right to call and appoint ministers belongs to the whole Church
	  This view is supported by the writings of Augustine, Calvin, and other Church fathers
    The role of the Church in appointing ministers  
	  The Church has the right to appoint ministers, not just individual leaders
	  This right is based on the Church's authority to discern true doctrine and distinguish it from false teaching
    The importance of Church autonomy  
	  The Church should be free from external control and subject only to the authority of Christ
	  This autonomy is essential for the Church's ability to govern itself and appoint its own ministers
    The Westminster Confession and its significance  
	  The Westminster Confession is a key document in Presbyterian theology
	  It outlines the Church's governance and the role of the Church in appointing ministers
    The importance of Church unity  
	  The Church should strive for unity and harmony in its governance and decision-making
	  This unity is essential for the Church's ability to fulfill its mission and extend the gospel
    The Presbyterian system as a divine institution  
	  The Presbyterian system is not a human invention, but a divine institution based on the Bible
	  It is designed to promote the extension and establishment of the gospel and the edification of the Church.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Interpretation of the Westminster Confession  
	  There are three ways to interpret the requirement to adopt the Westminster Confession and Catechism.
	  Some interpret it to mean every proposition in the Confession is included in the profession made at ordination.
	  Others say it means adopting the "system of doctrine" as taught in the Reformed Churches.
	  A third view is that it means adopting the essential doctrines of Christianity and nothing more.
    First interpretation is incorrect  
	  The first interpretation is not the meaning of the words.
	  Many propositions in the Westminster Confession do not belong to the integrity of the Augustinian or Reformed system.
    Practical implications of the first interpretation  
	  It would be impossible to carry out this rule of interpretation without dividing the Church into innumerable fragments.
	  A body of several thousand ministers and elders cannot think alike on all topics.
    Historical context of the Church's interpretation  
	  The Church has never adopted the first interpretation as a rule.
	  Individuals have held this view, but the Church as a body has not.
    Doctrinal errors and the Church's stance  
	  The Church has never prosecuted for doctrinal error except for errors that reject the doctrines themselves.
	  For example, denying the doctrine of original sin would reject the Reformed system.
    Original sin and its explanation  
	  The doctrine of original sin is essential to the Reformed system.
	  However, there are different ways to account for this fact, including:
		- The scriptural explanation of representation.
		- The view that humanity acted and sinned in Adam.
		- The view that the fact of original sin is to be accounted for in the general law of propagation.
    Church view of original sin  
	  The Church view is that Adam's sin is the judicial ground of our condemnation.
	  This view is held in the Latin, Lutheran, and Reformed Churches.
    Non-essential aspects of the doctrine  
	  The principle of representative responsibility is not essential to the doctrine of original sin.
	  Realists and others may accept the doctrine but reject this principle.
    Accounting for original sin  
	  The Church view is that Adam's sin is the judicial ground of our condemnation.
	  Other views, such as humanity acting and sinning in Adam, are also accepted by some.
    General law of propagation  
	  Some assert that the fact of original sin is to be accounted for in the general law of propagation, like begets like.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Doctrine of Original Sin  
	  Humans are born in a state of sin or with a sinful nature
	  This view is not equally scriptural or harmonious with the Confession, but it does not reject the system of doctrine

•   The Doctrine of Inability  
	  Man is rendered utterly indisposed to all spiritual good due to the fall
	  This doctrine is essential to the Reformed Church's system of doctrine

•   Calvinism vs. Arminianism  
	  The denial of the inability of fallen man rejects Calvinism
	  The question of whether man can cooperate in regeneration or resist God's converting grace separates Calvinists from Arminians

•   The Essence of the Doctrine  
	  The inability of fallen man is not affected by whether it is called natural or moral
	  The perverseness of the will or the blindness of the understanding are not essential to the doctrine

•   God's Sovereignty  
	  God foreordains all that comes to pass
	  He executes his decrees in creation and providence, controlling all creatures and their actions

•   Election and Salvation  
	  God has elected some to everlasting life from eternity
	  Salvation is certain for God's people due to the incarnation and mediatorial work of Jesus Christ and the effectual working of the Spirit

•   The Reason for Salvation  
	  Some are saved and others not due to God's election, not their faith or repentance
	  God sends the Holy Spirit to those he has elected, rendering their repentance, faith, and holy living certain

•   The Augustinian System  
	  The system consists of certain doctrines that cannot be omitted without destroying its identity
	  These doctrines include the plenary inspiration of Scripture, the doctrine of the Trinity, and the doctrine of decrees and predestination

•   The Importance of the System  
	  Ministers are required to adopt the system, not every proposition in the Standards
	  The system consists of essential doctrines that cannot be omitted

•   The Doctrines of the Augustinian System  
	  The plenary inspiration of Scripture and the consequent infallibility of its teachings
	  The doctrine of the Trinity, with one God subsisting in three persons
	  The doctrine of decrees and predestination, including election and salvation
	  The doctrine of creation, with the universe and all its contents owing their existence to God's will

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Nature of God  
	  God is infinitely wise, holy, and benevolent
	  God governs all creatures and their actions
    The Doctrines of the Covenants  
	  The first covenant (of works): life was promised to Adam and his descendants on condition of perfect obedience
	  The second covenant (of grace): God offers life and salvation to sinners through Jesus Christ, requiring faith and promising the Holy Spirit
    The Doctrine of Christ  
	  Christ is the Mediator, Prophet, Priest, and King of the Church
	  Christ is the Head and Savior of the Church, the Heir of all things, and Judge of the world
	  Christ took on human nature, uniting Godhead and manhood in one person
    The Doctrine of Free Will  
	  Man was created a free agent with the ability to choose good or evil
	  Man lost this ability to choose spiritual good through the fall
	  God enables sinners to repent and believe through conversion
    The Doctrine of Effectual Calling  
	  God predestinates some to life and effectually calls them to salvation
	  Effectual calling is a free and special act of God's grace
	  It is not based on anything foreseen in man
    The Doctrine of Justification  
	  Justification is a free act of God's grace
	  It is not based on anything done by man, but on Christ's obedience and sufferings
	  It includes declaring and accepting the sinner as righteous
    The Doctrine of Adoption  
	  Those who are justified are received into the family of God
	  They are made partakers of the Spirit and privileges of God's children
    The Doctrine of Sanctification  
	  Those who are regenerated are made holy through the power of the Holy Spirit
	  Sanctification is a work that is always imperfect in this life, but perfected at death
    The Doctrine of Saving Faith  
	  Saving faith is the gift of God and the work of the Holy Spirit
	  It involves receiving and resting on Christ alone for justification, sanctification, and eternal life
    The Doctrine of Repentance  
	  Repentance involves a change of heart and mind, recognizing the danger and odiousness of sin
	  It involves a sense of the mercy of God in Christ and a desire to turn away from sin

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Importance of Good Works  
	  Good works are the fruits of faith
	  They are indispensable for adults as the uniform products of the indwelling Holy Spirit
	  They are not necessary as the ground of justification, but are a result of faith
    The Perseverance of the Saints  
	  Those once called and sanctified by the Spirit can never totally or finally fall from a state of grace
	  This is due to the immutable decree of election, Christ's infinite merit, and the Spirit's abiding presence
	  The covenant of grace secures the salvation of all who believe
    Assurance of Salvation  
	  Assurance of salvation is desirable, possible, and obligatory
	  It is not of the essence of faith, but rather a result of faith
    The Law  
	  The law is a revelation of God's will and a perfect rule of righteousness
	  It is perpetually obligatory on justified persons, but not as a covenant of works
    Christian Liberty  
	  Christian liberty includes freedom from the guilt of sin, the condemnation of the law, and the bondage of Satan
	  It also includes free access to God as his children
    The Role of the Conscience  
	  God alone is the Lord of the conscience
	  The conscience is set free from the doctrines and commandments of men that are contrary to God's Word
    The Presbyterian System of Doctrine  
	  The Presbyterian system of doctrine is a matter of history and has been taught by the Church from the beginning
	  It is the life and soul of the Church, taught by Christ and his inspired servants
    The Importance of the Confession of Faith  
	  The Presbyterian Church is pledged to profess, defend, and teach the system of doctrine outlined in the Confession of Faith
	  A breach of faith to God and man occurs if the Church fails to require a profession of this system by its teachers and guides
    The Old School's Contention  
	  The Old School has always contended that the Confession of Faith should be adopted in its entirety
	  This is a matter of conscience and a requirement for those who teach and guide the people of the Church
    The Alternative Understanding of the System of Doctrine  
	  Some have understood the system of doctrine to mean only the essential doctrines of Christianity
	  This understanding is not in line with the Presbyterian system of doctrine and has been rejected by the Old School

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Westminster Confession and Subscription  
	  The Synod adopted the Westminster Confession in its strict meaning.
	  However, President Davis said candidates only needed to adopt it as to "the articles essential to Christianity".
	  This led to controversy and withdrawal of some members.

•   The Adopting Act  
	  The Synod called the Adopting Act "their Preliminary Act", not the actual adoption of the Confession.
	  The Confession was adopted in all its articles, except for the 23rd chapter on the power of the civil magistrate.

•   The General Assembly and Constitution  
	  In 1787, the General Assembly declared the Westminster Confession of Faith part of the Church's constitution.
	  No one has argued that the Constitution was accepted only as embracing general principles of government.

•   The Broad Church Principle  
	  The Old School has always protested against the Broad Church principle of subscription.
	  This principle is seen as immoral, as it allows people to claim to adopt a Calvinistic confession while rejecting its distinctive features.

•   Arguments Against the Broad Church Principle  
	  1. It is immoral to claim to adopt a confession while rejecting its distinctive features.
	  2. It is contrary to the principle on which the Church was founded.
	  3. It would lead to the relaxation of discipline and the introduction of conflict or indifference.

•   The Duty of the Church  
	  The Old School believes it is the duty of the Church to hold fast to the great system of truth contained in the Confession.
	  This system has been the faith of the great body of the people of God in all ages.

•   The Consequences of the Broad Church Principle  
	  It would lead to the relaxation of all discipline and the introduction of conflict or indifference.
	  It would destroy the purity of the Church.

•   The Duty of Men of Faith  
	  Men who feel it their duty to profess and teach the system of doctrine contained in the Confession in its integrity cannot consent to the Broad Church principle.
	  Any attempt to establish this principle would tend to produce division and dissent.

•   The Choice  
	  Either the faith of the Church should conform to its creed, or the creed should conform to the faith.
	  The Church cannot adopt a principle that is seen as immoral and destructive.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Apostles' Creed as a basis for Church organization  
	  Some people believe the Apostles' Creed is a broad enough basis for Church organization
	  However, they should not try to persuade others to sacrifice their consciences or adopt a more extended formula of faith that is not sincerely embraced

•   The Westminster Confession of Faith  
	  The Presbyterian Churches in Great Britain and Ireland have not altered the substance of the Confession of Faith
	  However, they have given explanations of the sense in which they adhere to certain passages that have been subject to doubt

•   Passages of concern  
	  Three passages in the Confession of Faith have been the subject of difficulty and doubt
	  These passages are:
		- WCF 20.2 (assertion of the rights of conscience)
		- WCF 23.3 (civil magistrate may not assume the power of the keys of the kingdom of heaven)
		- WCF 25.6 (government of the Church is in the hand of church officers, distinct from the civil magistrate)

•   Explanations and qualifications  
	  Various explanations and qualifications have been offered to reconcile the seeming inconsistency between these declarations and the passages in question
	  These explanations have been offered by Presbyterian Churches in Great Britain and Ireland

•   Presbyterian Churches and the Westminster Confession  
	  Presbyterian Churches of British origin have universally held to the Westminster Confession of Faith
	  Despite divisions, they have maintained their commitment to the Confession

•   The right to examine and revise the Confession  
	  The right to examine and revise the Confession has always been claimed and exercised by Presbyterian Churches
	  Important explanations and qualifications have been issued as a result

•   The General Assembly of the Church of Scotland  
	  In 1647, the General Assembly of the Church of Scotland issued an explanation of the sense in which they understood and held binding one portion of the Confession
	  This explanation clarified the sense in which certain passages were intended

•   The role of the civil magistrate  
	  The civil magistrate may advise with synods of ministers and ruling elders, but should not assume the power of the keys of the kingdom of heaven
	  The government of the Church is in the hand of church officers, distinct from the civil magistrate

•   The importance of Christian liberty  
	  The Confession asserts the rights of conscience and the independence of the Church
	  This assertion is a noble and important declaration of Christian liberty

•   The bond of union with Presbyterians of all countries  
	  The Westminster Confession of Faith has served as a link connecting the modern Church with the past
	  It has constituted a bond of union with Presbyterians of all countries, despite divisions and differences.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Ordinary Assemblies  
	  The church can assemble without the magistrate's consent if necessary for the good of the church.
	  The magistrate's consent is not required if it would be detrimental to the church.
    Formula for Ordination  
	  The United Presbyterian Church uses a formula for ordination that asks ministers, missionaries, and elders to acknowledge the Westminster Confession of Faith.
	  The formula clarifies that the church does not approve of compulsory or persecuting principles in religion.
    Free Church Position  
	  The Free Church guards against misconstruction of its position on civil magistracy and religion.
	  The church declares that it maintains scriptural principles on the duties of nations and rulers, but disclaims intolerant or persecuting principles.
    Formula Questions  
	  One question in the formula asks if the Lord Jesus Christ has appointed a government in the church that is distinct from and not subordinate to civil government.
	  The question also asks if civil government has jurisdiction or authoritative control over the church.
    Reformed Presbyterian Church Position  
	  The Reformed Presbyterian Church gives force to the Act of 1647 and uses a Testimony to illustrate its principles.
	  The Testimony declares that the church is not pledged to defend every sentiment or expression in the Confession.
    Reformed Presbyterian Church Testimony  
	  The Testimony asserts that civil coercion is incompatible with true religion and must prove ineffectual in practice.
	  The Testimony emphasizes the importance of interpreting and explaining disputed passages in the Confession.
    United Original Secession Church Position  
	  The United Original Secession Church uses a Testimony to explain its views on civil magistracy and religion.
	  The Testimony defends the doctrine on civil rulers in the Westminster Confession on the principles of Scripture and reason.
    Interpretation of the Confession  
	  The Westminster Confession can be defended on the principles of Scripture and reason.
	  A fair and candid interpretation of the whole doctrine in the Confession will not reveal intolerant or persecuting principles.
    Church Positions  
	  The United Presbyterian Church, Free Church, Reformed Presbyterian Church, and United Original Secession Church have different positions on civil magistracy and religion.
	  Each church has its own formula, Testimony, or declaration that clarifies its stance on these issues.
    Key Principles  
	  The church maintains scriptural principles on the duties of nations and rulers.
	  The church disclaims intolerant or persecuting principles and emphasizes the importance of liberty of conscience and private judgment.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Context of the Westminister Confession of Faith  
	  The Confession is a statement of faith for the Presbyterian Church
	  It is a key document in the history of the Church
    Persecution and Civil Magistrate  
	  The Confession addresses the issue of persecution for conscience' sake
	  It rejects the idea of subjecting religious matters to civil authority
    Church and State  
	  The Confession does not allow for civil magistrates to have power over the Church
	  It does not grant civil magistrates any power within the Church
    Method of Resolving Difficulties  
	  The main body of American Presbyterians proposed a solution to difficulties with the Confession
	  They suggested deleting one clause and altering two paragraphs
